Epistolae et decreta
by Innocent I

## [nt: This is one of those letters which Holstenius published in the first part of the *Roman Collection*, inserted in the acts of the Roman synod under Boniface II. Since Innocent calls this letter the *first-fruits* of his episcopate, which he entered upon *at once* on the death of Anastasius, we may not without reason conclude that it was written about the month of May of the year 402.] EPISTLE I, OF POPE INNOCENT I TO ANYSIUS, BISHOP OF THESSALONICA. *Innocent confirms to Anysius that authority over the Churches of Illyricum which Anastasius and his predecessors had granted him.*

[0463D]

*To his most beloved brother ANYSIUS, INNOCENT.*

Since our God Christ has deigned to call to himself the man of holy memory, Bishop Anastasius, albeit swiftly, [0464D] reckoning his merits to be so great and of such a kind that they already exceeded the fellowship of human conversation, and that age, by reason of the purity of his life and the abundance of his teaching, with which he ruled the people of God with the whole rigour of ecclesiastical authority: lest his Church should remain even a little while without the helm of a ruler, at once, according to his mercy, with the consent of the holy priests and of all the clergy and people, with the peace which God, returning to heaven, [0465A] deigned to bestow on his Church, it was fitting to recognize me ordained in his place, dearest brother [n: ( f. te cognoscere)]; and to announce my first-fruits swiftly to none but a man [nt: The same Anysius is honoured by Palladius, in the *Dialogue on the Life of Chrysostom*, ch. 3, with the encomium of *an excellent old man*, τοῦ χαλογήρου. There it is also recorded that he entrusted his own letter as well to Eulysius, bishop of Apamea in Bithynia, who was carrying to Rome the letters of fifteen bishops in support of Chrysostom.] most excellent and ever faithfully labouring in God [n: (V. epist. 2 Nicolai I)]. To whom also my predecessors in the episcopate before me, men so great and of such a kind, that is, Damasus of holy memory, Siricius, and the man mentioned above, so deferred, that they committed to thy holiness, which is full of justice, the cognizance of all things that were done in those parts; it becomes thee to recognize that my littleness also holds this judgment and has the same will. For it was not right either that I should attempt to go against the judgment of men so great and so good, into whose place I am known to have succeeded, or that anything should seem to be taken away from thy merit, on which illustrious men [0465B] are seen to have conferred so great a favour of this authority. I therefore openly declare that I acknowledge this very thing to be reserved to my littleness also: that with equal judgment and in like form I grant, both to the good alike and to thy charity, that which thou deservest.

## NOTICE ON THE FOLLOWING LETTER.



## 1.

*The things in it copied from Siricius do not make its authenticity doubtful.* --Many things in this letter are copied from Letter 4 of Siricius. But, as Tillemont observed, this ought not to seem at all surprising, since Innocent, asked by Victricius to hand on to him the rules customarily kept in the Roman Church, could not have done this more happily than by copying out the decrees of his predecessors. Hence, therefore, no fit reason is found why either the aforesaid letter should be taken away from Siricius, or this one from Innocent. Nor are the grounds lighter which assign this one [0465C] to Innocent than were those which assign that one to Siricius. It is cited under his name by the Second Council of Tours, c. 20, by Cresconius in the *Breviarium canonum* passim, and by Pope Zacharias in Sirmond, tome I of the *Concilia Galliae*, Epistle 9, cc. 14 and 20. Moreover, of the old collections, of which we have examined a great many, in none is it lacking, in none is it not presented inscribed to Innocent. It is ascribed to him by the Corbie exemplar copied in the times of Pope Vigilius, by the Colbert one written in Lombardic capital letters, by the old Codex of canons published by Quesnel, by the exemplars of the Dionysian and Hadrianic collections, and also by the Spanish collection, the Isidorian, and many others.

## 2.

*That it is not to be put back to the year 405.* --In all these collections [nt: (if you except the Dionysian, in which the consular date is omitted)] the ending of the letter is entirely the same, and with the greatest agreement it is dated *Honorio aug.* VI *et Aristeneto conss.* Hence no room is left for the conjecture of a learned man, by which he suspects that this consular date was added to this letter of Innocent [0465D] by another hand, or at any rate that *post consulatum Honorii*, etc., should be read. For though we grant him that the words *post consulatum*, which copyists are accustomed to express by the first letters *P. C.*, could easily have fallen out; in that case surely *Honorii et Aristeneti*, not *Honorio et Aristeneto*, would have been retained in at least some old codex, nor ought the word *conss.* to have been added in any. Nor shall we be any more compelled to yield to that conjecture if we weigh what cause there may be why he thinks this letter written in the year 405 rather than 404. He is moved, namely, solely by the words of no. 5, in which Innocent mentions what he had endured on account of the curials, with the emperor present, and with Victricius placed beside him. From which he also thinks it follows that this letter was written not only after the emperor had come into the City, [0466A] but also after Victricius had returned from Rome to Rouen. And yet, he says, the Emperor Honorius came to Rome only in the year 403, in the month of December, and had not come there before, as is gathered from Claudian's poem: and Victricius, in so small an interval as runs from the emperor's arrival in the City to the 15th day of February of the year 404, could not return from Rome to Rouen, and send a letter thence to Innocent, so that it was delivered to him before that day. From which he concludes that this letter was dated not in the consulship of Honorius Augustus, but after the consulship of Honorius Augustus, that is, in the year 405, not 404. But if, on account of a fact of which no certain knowledge is had, it be lawful to deny credit to titles so many, so diverse and so ancient, what will be certain in history? Let it be, then, that Honorius came to Rome at the close of the year 403, and that Victricius was in the City at that time: what compels us to agree at the same time that the same bishop had returned to Rouen before he received this letter of Innocent? Innocent mentions no one sent to him in Victricius's name, no [0466B] letter of his delivered to him. But, says the learned man, the word *misi*, which Innocent uses in the opening, implies one who is absent. But if it implies one absent, it certainly does not signify one returned to Rouen, not one gone out of Rome. For what prevents Victricius, about to depart [sic: *asbcessurus*] from Rome, from having asked of him a book of the ecclesiastical rules while he was bidding Innocent farewell, and him, not having the book at hand at that time, from having taken care that it should be put together as soon as possible and delivered to Victricius before his departure from the City. Nor indeed is it necessary that those to whom we are said to send something should be far from us. Friends daily send one another little gifts, books, letters, etc., even when contained within the walls of the same city. Let Damasus and Jerome be an example of this, above, p. 579 and following.

## 3.

*It is called the book of rules. The division of the chapters.* --Further, the book of rules mentioned does not seem to be reckoned anything other than this very letter, inasmuch as in it Innocent, after a brief preface, sets out several chapters of discipline [0466C] by which Churches are to be ruled. These chapters, moreover, are presented summarily in most manuscripts, though not in all, with titles set before them. But neither in those is this division or summary of the chapters one and the same; rather it varies according to the bent and judgment of those who collected the pontifical letters. More ample than the rest, however, is the one which the exemplars of the Codex published by Quesnel present. Wherefore we should have held it enough to represent that one alone here, were it not worth the labour to add to it the other, from the collection of Dionysius Exiguus, with which the Spanish collection too, from which the Isidorian was copied, agrees in nearly everything. For since Cresconius and other collectors of canons are accustomed to cite the decrees of the Roman pontiffs according to the division into titles arranged by Dionysius, it is necessary, in order that their citations may be found, to know beforehand what that division is. Now Dionysius Exiguus comprised in twenty-nine titles all the decrees which he collected from Innocent, among which the first place is held by this pope's letter to Decentius, divided into eight [0466D] titles. Then this one to Victricius follows it, which he divides into twelve titles. Wherefore the first decree of this letter is called title 9 in Dionysius, and the last title 20, as is to be seen below.



## 4.

*The laws which forbade curials to move out of the curia.* --In those decrees, moreover, that which is said in cap. 11 about the curials must be explained here somewhat more fully than is permitted in the annotations, because mention of the same matter will occur again and again in other letters of other pontiffs as well. Those were called curials, or decurions, who discharged the municipal magistracies and offices. Of them the Emperor Majorian, at the beginning of Novel 1, speaks thus: « No one is unaware that the curials are the sinews [0467A] of the commonwealth and the vitals of the cities: whose body antiquity rightly called the lesser Senate. » But, as the same emperor observes, the judges or governors of the provinces reduced many by their unjust exactions to such a point that they deserted their native place. To the curials belonged the charge of taxes, of buildings, of public shows or games, etc. For this reason the laws of the emperors were severe, by which no one was permitted to be freed from the curia unless he had discharged all the offices of his curia. The Emperor Constantine ordained [n: (Cod. Th. lib. XII, tit. 1, leg. 11)]: « Let no judge attempt to grant to any curial exemption from civil duties, or free anyone from the curia at his own discretion: » Of those too who, leaving their curias, take refuge in the garrisons of the army, he orders [n: (Leg. 1)] « all who are not yet found liable to the primipilate to be released from the army and to return to the curia. » Likewise he laid down [n: (Leg. 12)], as the interpreter Anianus puts it, « that whatever curial wishes to pass from the city in which he was born to another, may in no way [0467B] . . . nor by any plea of necessity escape the condition owed to the curia, or the services of his own city, on the ground that he wished to change his city. » By another law too [n: (Leg. 20)] he commands « that no curial, unless all the offices of the curia have been performed in order (Anianus adds, *which are owed to the native place*), ought to undertake the office of curator or defensor. » Nor was the indulgence of the emperors any greater towards those who migrated from the curia to the clergy. Ambrose, Epistle 41, to the Emperor Theodosius, n. 29, thought he must remonstrate with him on this matter in this manner: « How shall I excuse myself before the bishops, who now lament grievously because certain men who have discharged the rank of presbyter, or ministers of the Church, through thirty and innumerable years, are dragged back from the sacred office and assigned to the curia? » And that remonstrance was all the more just because Constantine had earlier [n: (Leg. 13)] wished the curias to be admonished that henceforth « they should drag back to the curia only those whom they found, within twenty years' service in the offices, either to have fled their origin, [0467C] or to have spurned nomination and thrust themselves into the service. » Indeed Julian had afterwards decreed [n: (Leg. 56)]: Those who, born of curial origin, have loved the military office, when the service of ten years has been completed, shall by the authority of our commands be exempt from the curia.

## 5.

*On what condition curials were permitted to give their names to the clergy.* --Curials were not so removed from the clergy that every approach to it was closed to them. Valentinian and Valens permit them to give their names to the clergy on a certain condition, which they explain thus in a law given in the year 364, on the 10th day of September [n: (Leg. 59)]: « He who chooses the part of the Church, let him either, by conferring his own goods on a kinsman, make him a curial in his stead, or cede his means to the curia which he has left, he who has done neither being of necessity recalled: » that is, so that he who did not cede his goods either to a kinsman or to his curia is by all means to be recalled to the curia. On the same matter there is also this constitution of Theodosius, dated the year 384, on the 7th day of November [n: (Leg. 104)]: « Curials who prefer to serve the churches rather than the curias, if they wish [0467D] to be what they feign, let them despise those things which they withdraw. For we free them on no other terms than that their patrimonies be despised. » Ambrose had this in view when he wrote in Epistle 18, to Valentinian, n. 13: « If a priest seek the privilege of declining the curial burden, he must give up the possession of his paternal and ancestral property and of all his means. » Constantius had been somewhat more indulgent: for in the year 361, on the 29th day of August, he first decreed in favour of the bishops [n: (Leg. 49)]: « Let no one compel a bishop only to make over his means to the curia, as had formerly been laid down. » Then he wills that those should enjoy the same privilege « who have attained the ranks of presbyters, and of deacons also, or subdeacons and the rest, with the curia assisting, and giving its consent under the eye of the judge . . . above all if they are asked for by the voices of the whole people. » Otherwise he orders [0468A] that two portions of their substance be handed over, either to their kinsmen substituted for the curial duties, or to the curia; but reserves the third to themselves. And of those who had failed of the conditions set out above, what is added at the end of the same constitution seems to have been said: « If the superintendents of the granaries, and those who are about to undertake a magistracy, the superintendents of the peace also, or the receivers of the various kinds of goods, have thought they should aspire to the Church (that is, to the ministry of the Church), after they have entered upon the offices of the charge or honour laid on them; it will be fitting for the prelates of the heavenly law first to resist them, and, they themselves being the first to strive for it, for these to be recalled to the same duties: or if they neglect this, they are to be dragged back by the curials, with the judgment of the office supporting. » Indeed Arcadius, derogating from his father's law, issued a constitution dated the year 398, on the 27th day of July, and reported in the *Codex Justinianus*, book I, tit. 3, law 12, by which he orders that a curial, if he has been ordained a cleric, be restored at once upon prior summons: « For we do not suffer, he says, that law to profit clerics any longer which, upon cession of patrimony having followed, did not forbid decurions to be clerics. » That this, however, was said of the minor [0468B] clerics alone is proved by what the same emperor laid down at the close of the year 399 [n: (Leg. 163)]: « If any, from our father's second consulship, leaving the curia, have made themselves over to the fellowship of the clergy, if they have already been found worthy to be bishops, or presbyters, or deacons, let them indeed persevere in the sacred and more secret mysteries; but let them be compelled either to offer substitutes to the curia, or, according to the law long since passed, to hand over their means to the curia. All the rest, readers, subdeacons, or clerics to whom the privileges of clerics are not owed, are to be presented forthwith to the duties owed to their native place. » With laws so many and so diverse, therefore, the judges did not lack an occasion by which to reclaim importunately curials admitted into the clergy, and thereby to make trouble for the prelates of the churches.

## EPISTLE II. From an old Colbert exemplar, no. 932.






## I.

*That no one of the bishops dare to ordain without the knowledge of the metropolitan [0468C] bishop.* --II. *That he who has chosen to have the belt of military service after the remission of sins be not admitted to the clergy.* --III. *That all contentions or causes arising among clerics of any order be ended before the provincials, that is, their own bishops. That the greater causes be referred to the apostolic see after the episcopal judgment.* -- IV. *That a cleric take a virgin to wife.* --V. *That a layman who has taken a widow to wife, whether before baptism or after baptism, be not admitted to the clergy.* --VI. *That he who has married a second wife ought not to be ordained a cleric, even if he obtained (such) a marriage before baptism.* --VII. *That no one presume to ordain a cleric from another's church.* --VIII. *That those baptized who come from the Novatians or the Montenses be received by [0468D] the imposition of hands only.* --IX. *That priests and levites do not come together with their wives.* --X. *That a monk, if he wishes to strive for the order of the clergy, ought not to turn aside from the prior purpose which he bore as a monk.* --XI. *That he who, being corrupted, wishes to come to the clergy, promise not to take a wife, according to the rule of the ancients.* --XII. *That curials, or those occupied with any public functions whatever, be not made clerics.* --XIII. *That handmaids of God who have been veiled, if they have given themselves up to corruption, whether publicly by marrying or secretly, be not admitted to penance, unless he to whom they had joined themselves has departed from the world.* --XIV. *That handmaids of God not yet consecrated, if afterwards they have wished to marry, be received to penance.*

[0469A]From the old codices of the collection of Dionysius Exiguus. IX. *That a bishop be not ordained without the knowledge of the metropolitan.* --X. [nt: In the Spanish collection and in Isidore: II: *On those who after baptism have taken up the belt of secular military service, that they ought not to be admitted to the order of the clergy* (*cleritatus*). III: *On the causes of clerics which are not justly ended in the province, that they be terminated by the apostolic see.* In the very old Corb. exemplar: IV: *On those who after the remission of sins have had the belt of secular military service.* V: *That it be not lawful for anyone to summon to another Church.*] *Who may not be received into the clergy. And that the causes of clerics be ended by their own bishops.* --XI [nt: The Isidorian collection, with the Spanish collection: IV: *On the wives of clerics, that they be joined to virgins, and do not take a second wife.* Corb. manuscript: VI: *On clerics, that they do not take women as wives.* VII: *On those who take women as wives*: that being understood under the name *woman* who is not a virgin. In which sense we have seen the same word used by Siricius, Epistle 1.] *That a cleric do not take a widow to wife.* --XII. *That if a layman has married a widow, he be not made a cleric.* --XIII. *He who has had a second wife, let him not be a cleric.* -- XIV. *That no bishop presume to ordain another's cleric.* --XV. *That those who come to the Church from the Novatians or the Montenses be not rebaptized.* -- XVI. *That priests and levites ought not to come together with women.* --XVII. *That a monk, if he has been made a cleric, [nt: So the pure Royal exemplar of Dionysius. But others, augmented later, with which the Isidorian and Spanish collections agree: *propositum suum servare debeat*.] do not take a wife.* --XVIII. *That a cleric be not made from curials, on account of the pleasures, invented by the devil, which he is compelled to exhibit.* --XIX. *On [0469B] virgins [nt: In the Spanish collection and the Isidorian: *De virginibus sacratis, si lapsae fuerint*. In the very old Corb. manuscript: *De puellis sanctimonialibus, qualiter debeant velari*.] who have been veiled, if they have gone astray.* --XX. *On virgins not veiled, if they have gone astray.*

*INNOCENT [nt: In some manuscripts, *Victorio*; in certain ones, as in Merlin, *Victorico*; in others, *Victoricio*; in Reg., *Victoritio*; in the rest, *Victricio*. Of what merit this Victricius was, Paulinus, bishop of Nola, has committed to memory in two letters written to him.] to VICTRICIUS, bishop of Rouen, greeting.*

## 1.

Although, dearest brother, by reason of the merit and honour of the priesthood in which thou art most powerful, all things of living and teaching [nt: One Colb. codex and another Fossat., with the Roman edition and Quesnel, as in Crabbe's margin: *secundum ecclesiasticas regulas*, against more than twenty other manuscripts. And this is what it means: Although all the precepts of teaching and living which are contained in the ecclesiastical rule are known to thee.] of the ecclesiastical rule are known to thee, and there is nothing which may seem to have been less gathered by thee from the sacred readings; yet, because thou hast greatly asked for the norm [nt: In the very old Corb. manuscript, and from it Sirmond and Labbe: *normam ad auctoritatem*; a reading not to be despised, in much the same sense as that in which the Council of Carthage, below, Epistle 26, n. 1, writes to Innocent himself that it refers its statutes to him, so that the authority of the apostolic see also may be applied to them.] and authority of the Roman Church, in full compliance with thy wish I have sent, attached to my letter, the disciplines of life and of approved conduct, set in order, by which the peoples of the Churches [0469C] of your region may observe [nt: The Corb. codex with Fossat.: *quibus et regulis*; one Colb.: *quibus regulis*, *rebus* being omitted.] by what things and rules the life of Christians ought to be held within the profession of each, and what discipline is kept in the churches of the city of Rome. It will be for thy love diligently to bring this book of rules, as a teacher and a monitor, to the notice of the neighbouring peoples and of our fellow priests who in those [0470A] regions preside over their own churches; so that they may be able both to know our ways, and by diligence in teaching to form the conduct of those who flock to the faith. For either they will recognize their own practice from this our consonant reading, or, if anything is still wanting, they will easily be able to supply it by good imitation.

## 2.

*The beginning of the apostolate and of the episcopate from Peter.* --Let us begin, then, [nt: Merlin, Crabbe and the Roman edition, with only the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections: *adjuvante Deo et sancto Dei apostolo*. What Innocent teaches here Caesarius of Arles, holding it for certain, writes thus to Pope Symmachus, Epistle 14, n. 2: *As the episcopate took its beginning from the person of blessed Peter the apostle, so it is necessary that your holiness, by fitting disciplines, show plainly to the several churches what they ought to observe.*] with the help of the holy apostle Peter, through whom both the apostolate and the episcopate took their beginning in Christ, so that, since many causes have often arisen which in some cases were not causes but crimes, there may henceforth be a concern for every priest to have a care of this kind in his own Church, [0470B] as the apostle Paul preaches [n: (Ephes. V, 27)], that such a Church is to be presented to God, not having spot or wrinkle, lest our conscience be violated, defiled by the breath of some diseased sheep. On account of those, therefore, who through ignorance or sloth do not hold to the ecclesiastical discipline, and presume many things not to be presumed, thou hast rightly asked that in those parts a form of this kind, which the Roman church holds, be observed. Not that any new precepts be commanded, but that we desire those things to be observed by all which have been neglected through the sloth of some, which things nevertheless have been established by apostolic tradition and that of the fathers. For it is written [nt: The older manuscripts, Colb., Fossat. and Corb.: *ad Timotheum*; but in this last *ad Thessalonicenses* has been restored by a second revision. In the early editions of the Councils, as in the manuscripts of the Hadrianic and Spanish collections, is read *ad Thessalonicenses II, et ad Timotheum*. Shortly after, the old editions of the Councils, with the Roman edition, have *tradidimus* for *tradidi*, against the manuscripts.] to the Thessalonians, with Paul warning [n: (II Thess. II, 14)]: *Stand, and hold our traditions, which I delivered to you, whether by word, or by epistle.* [var: Vulg. *quas didicistis*, "which you have learned"] [0470C] That thought, assuredly, ought to rouse thy mind more vehemently, that thou mayest be found secure before the sight of God, free from every stain of this world. For to whom much is entrusted, more is required of him [n: (Luc. XII, 48)], [nt: These words, *usura poenarum*, are lacking in the manuscripts, Colb., the Dionysian, the Hadrianic, the Spanish, the Isidorian, and also in most editions, as also in Siricius. They are found, however, in Sirmond, Labbe and Quesnel, as in the older manuscripts, two Colb., one Corb. and Fossat., and also, by a second revision, in the Coislin. manuscript of the Spanish collection.] an interest of penalties. Since, therefore, not for ourselves only, [0471A] but for the people of Christ [nt: In Siricius, *populo credito*, which is preferred in the Roman edition after Crabbe, against the other books.] we are compelled to render account, [nt: So the old Corb. codex with Fossat.. But in two Corb. manuscripts, *disciplinam dominicam*. Another Colb. likewise, with the Reg. manuscript of the Dionysian collection and the Roman edition, *disciplina deifica*. In Crabbe, *idcirco disciplinam Dei deificam*. In the manuscripts of the Hadrianic collection, *disciplina sanctifica*, corruptly. Hence in Corb. *disciplina sanctificare* has been substituted by a second revision, which also prevailed in the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections. Hence it is plain that this reading of Merlin, which Quesnel also seized on, is not genuine: *Idcirco disciplina Dei sanctificare populum et erudire debemus*.] we ought to instruct the people in the divine discipline. [nt: So Sirmond and Labbe, following the Dionysian, the Hadrianic and the better manuscripts. But the old editions of the Councils, and also the Roman edition and Quesnel, with the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections: *Quosdam enim asseris exstitisse*; how this reading, even were it not to be rejected, would confirm what the learned man wishes, namely that Innocent is replying not to what Victricius, present at Rome, had said to him, but to what he had written after his departure from the City, altogether escapes us. For since it shows *asseris*, not *scribis*, it signifies of itself not things written and sent from afar, but rather things said face to face. But since it lacks the authority of the older manuscripts, it may rightly be believed to have been added to Innocent.] For there have been some who, not holding to the statutes of the elders, would violate the chastity of the Church by their presumption, following the favour of the people and not fearing the judgment of God. Therefore, lest by our silence we be thought to give consent to these, since the Lord says [nt: In the Corb. manuscript of the Hadrianic collection *per prophetam* has been added by an old hand, which afterwards prevailed in the Spanish and Isidorian collections, and also in Merlin, Crabbe and Quesnel.] [n: (Psal. XLIX, 18)]: *Thou sawest a thief and didst run with him;* these are the things which it is expedient that every catholic bishop henceforth keep, with regard to the divine judgment.

## CHAP. I.



## 3.

First, that no one dare to ordain without the knowledge of the metropolitan bishop [nt: In the Roman edition, and also in the Councils and Quesnel, following the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections, *episcopum* is added here, a word which is absent from the Dionysian and Hadrianic collections and other older ones, as also in Siricius. Only in one of those old manuscripts do we read *metropolitani episcopum* in place of *metropolitani episcopi*. We have already observed that Innocent so borrows the words of Siricius as to adapt them to the discipline to be kept in the Gauls; nor does he likewise enjoin on the Gauls what the former had prescribed for the Italians, *that no one dare to ordain without the knowledge of the apostolic see*.] [n: (Dist. LXIV, c. 5)]; for that judgment is sound which is confirmed by the opinions of many: [nt: Sirmond, Quesnel and some manuscripts: *Ne unus*. Then other editions also: *episcopus ordinare praesumat episcopum*. This last word is absent from most and from the better manuscripts. But here, as just before, it is understood. The twofold part of this decree is shaped to the norm of the fourth canon of Nicaea.] let not one bishop [0472A] presume to ordain; lest a stolen favour seem to have been bestowed. For this too was established and defined in the synod of Nicaea.

## CHAP. II.



## 4.

Likewise [n: (Dist. LVI, c. 61)], if anyone after the remission of sins [nt: In the Colb. codex written in Lombardic letters there is written in the margin, opposite this decree, *De poenitentibus*. He who set it there, thinking that the discourse here is not of the remission of sins which is received through baptism but of that which is granted when penance has been completed, interpreted this decree as concerning not the baptized but those who have done penance. That it is to be understood otherwise, however, was observed above on Epistle 5 of Siricius, p. 654, note d (in our *Patrologia*, tome XIII, col. 1158, note d). More rightly, therefore, in the Spanish collection this decree is rendered in summary thus: *On those who after baptism have taken up the belt of secular military service.*] has had the belt of secular military service, he ought by no means to be admitted to the clergy.

## CHAP. III.



## 5.

But if any causes or contentions have arisen among clerics [nt: The old editions of the Councils, and only the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections, add here *vel inter laicos*. That the discourse here is of clerics alone, however, what follows immediately shows. The fifth canon of the Council of Nicaea gave occasion for this addition. And indeed Pierre de Marca, book I *De Concordia*, ch. 6, n. 10, thinks that this fifth canon and the sixth are explained throughout this passage. Hincmar, Opuscule 47, or the letter to John VIII, ch. 12, p. 773, so supports him as to judge that by this exception, *sine praejudicio tamen Rom. ecclesiae*, etc., the seventh decree of the Council of Sardica is recalled. Paschasius Quesnel, however, in his notes on Leo's Epistle 5, n. 4, judges that the canons of the Council of Sardica alone are regarded here. Yet in those canons nothing is laid down about the causes of clerics of the lower order, except in can. 16, where they are permitted *to appeal to the neighbouring bishops*. Nor is it obscure that the canon recalled here by Innocent is the one which the Africans, to whom the synod of Sardica was unknown, note in their Epistle 3 to Celestine in these words: *The decrees of Nicaea have most plainly committed both the clerics of lower rank and the bishops themselves to their metropolitans.* Wherefore of those three opinions the truer seems the one which Hincmar sets out in the place cited, inasmuch as he teaches that Innocent, *in saying that, according to the synod of Nicaea, judgment on causes that have arisen is to be ended by the bishops of the same province gathered together, explains a chapter* (5) *of the Council of Nicaea: and in forbidding anyone of the ecclesiastical order to fly off to other provinces, explains another chapter* (namely 6) *of the Council of Nicaea, which commands that in the several provinces their privileges be preserved to the Churches.* Certainly those words, *secundum Nicaenam synodum*, which are omitted in the Colb. Langob. manuscript, refer not to the exception enclosed in the parenthesis, but only to what precedes.] of the higher order as well as of the lower [n: (3, q. 6, c. 14)], that, according to the synod of Nicaea, the quarrel be ended by the bishops of the same province gathered together, and that it be not lawful for anyone (without prejudice, however, to the Roman Church, [nt: These words, *cui in omnibus causis debet* (or, as in the manuscripts, *debetur*) *reverentia custodiri*, Nicholas I, Epistle 40 in another numbering, does not read. They are lacking also in the manuscripts of the Dionysian and Hadrianic collections and in many other old codices: moved by whose authority Quesnel expunged them, although they were not wanting to the Oxford exemplar of the Codex published by him. The same learned man tries to persuade, Dissertation 12, ch. 4, n. 5, that they were added by the editors of the Roman Codex (that is, of the one which he published). But two exemplars of the same Codex, which he calls the Roman, older than the Oxford one, namely the Pithou and the Colbert, in which they are not found, ought to have prevented him from persuading himself of this. They are found, however, in the Colb. Langob. manuscript and in another Colbert, no. 3368, containing an ancient collection of canons, and also in the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections, as also in Hincmar, Opuscule 45, ch. 12, and in the book of 55 chapters, ch. 28. I pass over Gratian and others. Finally, in the very old Corb. exemplar they have been added by an old hand, and indeed the same hand that is accustomed to supply omitted words. But whether they are judged to be Innocent's or another's, everyone ought to be persuaded that the mind of this pope is not rashly explained by them, inasmuch as they rest on the words of Hosius in the synod of Sardica, can. 4, saying: *If it please you, let us honour the memory of the holy apostle Peter*, etc.; and also on what Gaudentius, ibid., can. 5, subjoins about a bishop who, deposed by the judgment of the neighbouring bishops, has cried out that his case must be tried in the city of Rome.] to which reverence ought to be kept in all causes), to leave these priests who in the same province govern the Church of God [0473A] by divine will, and fly off to other provinces. And if anyone should perchance presume to do so, let him both be removed from the office of the clergy and be judged by all guilty of wrongs.

## 6.

If greater causes have been brought into the midst, let them be referred to the apostolic see, [nt: So the Roman edition and the old editions of the Councils, with the Oxon. manuscript and others of the Spanish and Isidorian collections, and also Hincmar, Opuscule 45, ch. 12. But in the Dionysian codices and in almost all our other manuscripts, which Sirmond, Labbe and Quesnel followed, *beata consuetudo exigit* is lacking. On the other hand, in the Colb., Langob. manuscript and in another, no. 3368, is found *sicut vetus consuetudo exigit*, and the preceding words, *synodus statuit*, etc., are omitted. This reading is favoured by what Innocent writes, whether in Epistle 29, to the Council of Carthage, n. 1: *Keeping the examples of ancient tradition, and mindful of ecclesiastical discipline, you have strengthened with true reason the vigour of our religion no less now in consulting than before when you pronounced, you who have shown your approval that the judgment should be referred to us*; or in Epistle 30, to the Council of Milevis, n. 2: *Diligently and fittingly do you consult the secret counsels of the apostolic honour . . . concerning anxious matters, what judgment is to be held, following, that is, the ancient rule, which you know has always been kept with me throughout the whole world.* The same is supported both by Cyril to Celestine, Epistle 8, n. 1, speaking thus: *The old custom of the Churches urges that matters of this kind be communicated to thy holiness*; and by Leo, Epistle 5, n. 5, where he asks that the greater causes be referred to him *for the tradition of the ancient institution and the reverence owed to the apostolic see*. Isidore Mercator, in the letter which he forged in the name of Pelagius, copying out this passage of Innocent, alters it thus: *But let the greater and difficult questions, as the holy synod has laid down and blessed custom requires, always be referred to the apostolic see.* Further, by the words *ut sancta synodus statuit* we judge that there are denoted, not the canons 3, 4 or 7 of the Council of Sardica, by which it is permitted to appeal to the Roman bishop, but the letter of the same synod to Julius, in which the synod declares *that it is best and exceedingly most fitting, if the priests of the Lord refer to the see of the apostle Peter from each and every province*. Nor indeed does Innocent in this place reserve to himself the judgment of the greater causes; rather he requires that a report of those causes, judged by an episcopal council, be sent according to custom to the apostolic see. Wherefore that interpretation of Hincmar, tome II, p. 774, does not please us: *that if greater causes, one of which is concerning the appeal of a bishop who has been judged, have been brought into the midst, they be referred to the apostolic see after the episcopal judgment, as the synod, namely that of Sardica, laid down, and old custom requires.* For there it is not a question of the appeal of a bishop who has been judged; but only this is commanded, that what the bishops have judged concerning graver matters be referred to the apostolic see. On this matter, recall what we have set out more fully in the general preface.] as the synod laid down and blessed custom requires, after the episcopal judgment.

## CHAP. IV.



## 7.

That a cleric do [nt: The Roman edition, following Dionysius: *ut viduam*. Quesnel, following the earlier editions of the Councils and the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections: *ut mulierem viduam*. We prefer, with Sirmond, Labbe and the other old books, *ut mulierem*. So also wrote Siricius, Epistle 5. So too in the synodal decree of Hilary, after his Epistle 14, n. 14, Tiberius, bishop of Sabina, speaks: *If in my Church I shall have caught any one of the clerics . . . . either to have married a woman.*] not take a woman to wife; because it is written [n: (Levit. XXI, 13, 14; Ezech. XLIV, 22)], *Let the priest take a virgin to wife, not a widow, [nt: One of the Colb. manuscripts, with the old Corb.: *nec relictam*.] not one put away.* Certainly he who by his own labour and uprightness of life strives towards the priesthood ought to take care lest, hindered by this impediment, he may not be able to attain it.

## CHAP. V.



## 8.

That he who [nt: So Sirmond and Labbe, with the better manuscripts. But others, with the Dionysian, Spanish and Isidorian collections: *viduam*, which word Siricius also uses here.] has taken a woman to wife, though a layman, [0474A] whether before baptism or after baptism, be not admitted to the clergy [n: (Dist. 34, c. 13)]; because he is seen to be excluded by the same fault. For in baptism crimes are remitted; the partnership of a wife taken is not dissolved.

## CHAP. VI.



## 9.

That he who has married a second wife be not made a cleric: because it is written [n: (I Tim. III, 2)], *The husband of one wife.* [nt: In the Langob. manuscript and another Colb.: *Unius uxoris virum. Ac ne ab aliquibus*, what lies between being omitted. They seem to have been suppressed deliberately by some studious person, who believed them additions the more readily because he did not come upon the testimonies cited in the sacred writings, and indeed read in Jerome, Epistle 83, to Oceanus: *As by the Law it had been granted to the people to scatter children among many wives, so to the priests themselves also the free use of this licence lay open.* Yet the testimonies which Innocent adduces here Tertullian also expressly cites as from the Law: the first in the book *On Monogamy*, ch. 7, where he says: *Meanwhile, as regards the Law . . . it forbids the same priests to marry again*; the other in the book *Exhortation to Chastity*, ch. 7, where he not only says: *It is laid down in Leviticus, Let my priests not marry more*, but also adds: *I can say that even that is more which is not once.*] And again: *Let my priests marry once.* And elsewhere [n: (Tit. I, 6)]: *My priests shall not marry more.* And lest [nt: The Roman edition: *Etsi ab aliquibus*, which Crabbe noted in the margin, and below, *satis errant*: against all the other books.] it be thought by some that if perchance anyone took a wife before baptism and, she departing from the world, has married another, this is remitted in baptism, he strays far from the rule [nt: So the old Corb. codex with one Colb.. But others: *quia in baptismo peccata remittuntur, non*. In this and the foregoing decree Innocent so copies out the words of Siricius as to have judged it necessary to define for himself, in both, what is to be thought about wives married before baptism, of whom Siricius had said nothing. A recent controversy on that matter required it. Carterius, namely, a bishop of Spain, *against whom*, says Jerome, Epistle 83, to Oceanus, near the end, *nothing else could be objected by his rivals beyond the conjugal bond, and that before baptism*, had, his first wife being dead, married another after the laver. Oceanus judged that he had been ordained against the precept of the Apostle. Jerome, on the contrary, keenly defends his ordination as lawful in the aforesaid Epistle 83, and that the whole world is full of ordinations of this kind. Before he wrote it, however, he does not keep silent that he had met at Rome a most eloquent man who opposed his own opinion. He met others too not long afterwards. Certainly Rufinus, while Siricius was still living, cast this opinion in his teeth. Jerome, answering him at the end of book I of the *Apology*, when Siricius had not yet been taken from among the living, so tempers his earlier opinion as no longer to say that the world is full of ordinations of this kind, but that there are some priests of this sort in the Church; and he testifies that he is not bound by this question, but permits each to abound in his own sense. *Questioned by the brethren*, he says, *we answered what seemed good to us, prejudging no one to follow what he wishes, nor overturning another's decree by our opinion.* The first Council of Valence, held in the year 374, can. 1, had already defined *that after this synod, by which help is brought to such unlawful things even though late, no one of the digamists or of the husbands of women married a second time can be ordained a cleric. Nor is it to be asked whether they defiled themselves by this necessity of an unhappy lot when initiated in the divine sacraments or as gentiles, since the form of the divine precept is chaste.* The same opinion is approved by Ambrose, book I *On the Duties*, ch. 50, n. 2, and Epistle 63, to the Church of Vercelli, n. 63; by Augustine, in the book *On the Good of Marriage*, ch. 18, where he also explains the mystical reason of this discipline; and by Leo, Epistle 45.] who thinks that in baptism [0475A] this is remitted: sins are remitted; the number of wives taken is not abolished: since indeed a wife is married by the precept of the law, to such a degree that even in paradise, when the parents of the human race were joined, they were blessed by the Lord himself [n: (Gen. I, 28)]; and Solomon says [n: (Prov. XIX, 14)], *By God a wife shall be prepared for a man.* That all priests also keep this form, the very practice of the Church shows. For it is quite absurd for anyone to believe that a wife taken before baptism is not counted after baptism; since the blessing which is laid by the priest upon those who marry is taught not to have given matter for sinning, but to have kept the form of the law instituted by God of old. But if [n: (Dist. 34, c. 13)] she is not thought to be counted a wife who was married before baptism, then [0475B] neither will the children who were begotten before baptism be held [nt: So the Royal exemplar of Dionysius. But the old Corb. manuscript with Fossat. and two Colb.: *haberentur*. Quesnel: *habendi sunt*. The rest, printed and written: *et habentur*.] for children.

## CHAP. VII.






## 10.

That no one presume to ordain a cleric from another's Church [n: (Dist. 71, c. 2)], unless his bishop, [nt: In the Corb. manuscript and another Colb., *precibus* is lacking. In the Council of Nicaea, can. 16, and in that of Sardica, can. 19, it is forbidden that another's cleric be ordained *without the consent and without the will of his own bishop*. In the *Codex canonum Ecclesiae Africanae*, c. 54, Bishop Epigonius, having first said that *it was laid down in many councils that no bishop snatch another's cleric for himself without the judgment of him whose cleric he was*, remonstrates with Julian because he had usurped his own cleric for himself and ordained him deacon without consulting him. To whom *Bishop Numidius says: If Julian appears to have done this without thy good pleasure being asked or consulted, we all judge it done unjustly and unworthily*; then he decrees that he be separated, unless he make satisfaction by returning the cleric.] won over by prayers, has been willing to grant it. This too the synod of Nicaea laid down [n: (Can. 17)], that one church should not receive a cleric cast off by another.

## CHAP. VIII.



## 11.

That those who come from the Novatians or the Montenses be received by the imposition of hands only; because, although by heretics, they were nevertheless baptized in the name of Christ: except those who, if perchance passing over from us to them, [nt: In Quesnel, *baptizati*; which the Corb. manuscript and another Colb. also have here. But the same manuscripts, in Epistle 5 of Siricius, to whose words Innocent keeps close, show *rebaptizati*; which the other codices also retain here, and the very context of the sentence demands. On the same passage of Siricius we have explained who are called Montenses, and at the same time proved that the practice of rebaptizing was familiar to them, as also to the Novatians.] have been rebaptized. These, if, coming to their senses and considering their ruin, they wish to return, are to be admitted under a long [nt: In two manuscripts the word *poenitentiae* is lacking. The first Council of Valence, c. 3, so lays down that the approach to satisfaction be not denied to those *who after the one and holy laver have polluted themselves with an unchaste washing*, as to wish them *to do penance up to the day of death*.] satisfaction of penance [0475C].

## CHAP. IX.



## 12.

Moreover, what is worthy and chaste and honourable, the Church ought by every means to hold, [0476A] that priests and levites do not come together with their wives [nt: The Colb. Langob. codex: *non misceantur: quia ministeriis quotidianis*. *Ministeriis quotidianis* is not displeasing; above all since in Siricius one may read *quotidianis necessitatibus*.]; because they are occupied with the necessities of their daily ministry. For it is written [n: (Levit. XI, 14)], *Be ye holy, for I also am holy, the Lord your God.* For if in former times the priests did not depart from the temple of God in the year of their course [n: (Levit. XXI, 12)], as we read of Zachary, nor touched their houses at all, to whom certainly the use of a wife had been allowed on account of the succession of offspring, because from another tribe, and apart from [nt: Crabbe added *quam* here in a smaller type; which the other editions retained thereafter, against Merlin and the manuscripts. Shortly after, with two Corb. and one Colb., we restore *nulli fuerat praeceptum*, where in other books it is *nullus fuerat praeceptus*.] the seed of Aaron, it had been commanded to none to approach the priesthood [n: (Num. XVIII, 7)]: how much more shall these priests or levites be bound to keep chastity from the day of their ordination, whose priesthood or ministry is without succession, and no day passes on which they are free either from the divine sacrifices or from [0476B] the office of baptism! For if Paul writes to the Corinthians [n: (I Cor. VII, 5)], saying, *Abstain ye for a time, that ye may give yourselves to prayer,* and this he certainly commands to laymen: much more shall priests, for whom there is a continual office of praying and of sacrificing, be bound always to abstain from a union of this kind. If such a one has been defiled by carnal concupiscence, with what shame will he presume to sacrifice? or with what conscience, or by what merit, does he believe that he is heard [nt: The Colb. Langob. manuscript, with the Roman edition and the later ones: *exaudiri posse*. *Posse* is absent from the other manuscripts, as also from the old editions of the Councils.], since it is said [n: (Tit. I, 15)]: *All things are clean to the clean, but to the defiled and to unbelievers nothing is clean?* But perhaps he believes this lawful because it is written [n: (I Tim. III, 2; Tit. I, 6)], *The husband of one wife:* [nt: Quesnel: *non de remanente*. Labbe, in the margin: *non propter manendum*. The Roman edition, after Merlin and Crabbe: *non permanenti*. The Fossat. manuscript: *non permanente*. As in the letter of Siricius, so here too the other manuscripts, which we follow, are consistent with themselves. Ambrose, Epistle 63, to the Church of Vercelli, n. 62, observes that by the words adduced the marriage is not indeed dissolved, but neither is the conjugal use permitted, and a repeated marriage is forbidden.] he did not speak of one remaining in the concupiscence of begetting, but on account of the continence to come. For he did not refuse those whole in body, who says [n: (I Cor. VII, 7)]: [0476C] *But I would that all men were even as I myself.* And he declares it more openly, saying thus [n: (Rom. VIII, 8)]: *But they that [0477A] are in the flesh cannot please God. But ye are now not [sic: nom] in the flesh, but in the spirit.*

## CHAP. X and XI.



## 13.

Of monks [n: (16, q. 1, c. 3)] who, having long dwelt in monasteries, if afterwards they have attained [nt: Quesnel, with one Colb. manuscript: *pervenire nituntur*. Shortly after, in the Colb. Langob. manuscript: *aut enim sit, ut in monasterio fuit*, a reading not to be despised. Innocent here takes care that a monk advanced to those orders of the clergy in which marrying a wife is not forbidden should not think it lawful for himself to fall away from his former purpose of celibacy. Hence Dionysius, following the Royal exemplar, rendered this chapter in summary thus in the index of titles: *Quod monachus, si clericus factus fuerit, uxorem non ducat*.] to the order of the clergy, that they ought not to turn aside from their former purpose. For either, as he was in the monastery, what he long kept he ought not to lose when placed in a better rank: or if, corrupted, afterwards baptized, [nt: In two Colb. manuscripts: *baptizatus, monasterium deserens, et ad*. Nothing else will this reading of two Corb. manuscripts signify either, *baptizatus, et monasterio sedens*, if *cedens* be read for *sedens*. The Royal exemplar of the Dionysian collection shows *et in monasterium sedens*, perhaps for *secedens*.] and sitting in the monastery, he has wished to approach the order of the clergy, he will by no means be able to have a wife; because he cannot be blessed with a bride, being already [nt: Some editions and manuscripts: *jam ante corruptus*. The practice of the rule mentioned here by Innocent is confirmed by Caesarius of Arles, in Augustine, appendix, tome V, sermon 289, in these words: *He who desires to take a wife, just as he wishes to find her a virgin, so let him himself be a virgin until the wedding: because if he has not been, he will not deserve to receive the blessing with his bride.* Hence also is had the reason of the old rule, which commanded that he who, corrupted before baptism, wished afterwards to become a cleric, should promise that he would by no means take a wife: that is, lest the disgrace for which he was judged unworthy of the nuptial blessing should return into the very order of the clergy. And if any cleric whatever is held back by this impediment from taking a wife, how much more a monk! For the same reason Siricius, Epistle 1, n. 13, willed that a cleric to be advanced to a higher rank should be content *with one only, and her whom he received a virgin with the common blessing through the priest*.] corrupted. Which form is kept in the case of clerics, above all since the old rule has this, that whoever, corrupted, being baptized, wished to be a cleric, should promise that he would by no means take a wife.

## CHAP. XII.



## 14.

Moreover, certain of our brethren [0477B] frequently strive to make clerics of curials [n: (Dist. 51, c. 3)], or of those occupied with any [0478A] public functions [nt: In two manuscripts *aut honoribus* is added here. Shortly after, in Quesnel, as in the Roman edition, *de adscitis*, where in all the manuscripts *de adscito*. Worse, in Crabbe, *de accito*. In what is subjoined, moreover, about the pleasures invented by the devil, which curials were often compelled to exhibit, is found the reason why many Christians too declined those duties: against whom Julian, the apostate emperor, *Codex Theodosianus*, book XII, tit. 1, law 50, decrees: *Let the decurions who decline their duties as Christians be recalled.*] whatever; for whom afterwards a greater sadness arises, when something is commanded by the emperor about recalling them, than favour arises from the one admitted. For it is agreed that they, in those very duties, also exhibit pleasures which without doubt were invented by the devil, and either preside over or take part in the apparatus of games or of shows. Let the solicitude and sadness of the brethren certainly serve as an example, which we have often endured with the emperor present, when we more often entreated for these, as thou, placed with us [nt: That Victricius came to Rome, Paulinus of Nola recalls, Epistle 37, n. 1, to Victricius himself, in this manner: *Hence, being saddened, we had drawn bitterness, that thou, who hadst come to the City across such great spaces of lands, dost not hold on from the City to us, as we hoped.* Then he hints that he had come there for this reason, that he might clear himself of some crime laid on him by calumny, in these words: *But thy holiness holds glory not only from the misuse of lawful things . . . but, as I have learned, also from the multitude of adversaries, from the endurance of temptations: since unjust witnesses have risen up against thee, and iniquity hath lied to itself. But a knot in a bulrush and a blemish in the light could not be found.*] thyself, learned, for whom a huge vexation was pressing, that not only inferior clerics from the curials, but also those already set in the priesthood should be surrendered [nt: So Sirmond, Labbe and Quesnel, with the old Corb. manuscript and also the Fossat. and two Colb. But the others have *instabat*, in no other sense. The wording of this whole chapter is somewhat perplexed, by which Innocent wishes to persuade that curials be not ordained. And of this matter the principal reason is that which he touches as if in passing: *For it is agreed that they, in those very duties, also exhibit pleasures*, etc. But not dwelling on this, he quickly returns to the other, which he had set first, namely that for the bishops who ordain them a greater sadness arises from it. Which he also proves by his own example, of which Victricius was witness, adding: *Si certe in exemplum sollicitudo*, etc. Whence the last words, *quibus . . . . . ingens ut redderentur molestia imminebat*, are to be understood thus: while they were pressing on me with huge vexation, that those who had been ordained from the curials, not only the inferior clerics, whom the law of Arcadius adduced in the *Monitum* commanded to be recalled, but also those set in the priesthood, should be surrendered.].

## CHAP. XIII.



## 15.

Likewise [n: (27, q. 1, c. 10; Ivo, p. 7, c. 18)] [0478B] those who have been married spiritually to Christ [nt: In the early editions of the Councils, as in the exemplars of the Dionysian, Hadrianic, Spanish and Isidorian collections: *nubunt et a sacerdote velantur*. In Epistle 10 of Siricius, n. 3, we read that that virgin is called veiled *who, having professed her integrity by public testimony, has received the veil of blessing, the priest having poured forth prayer*. Ambrose, in book III *On Virgins*, ch. 1, inserted the sermon which Pope Liberius delivered when Ambrose's own sister Marcellina, on the Saviour's birthday, at the apostle Peter's, *was marking her profession of virginity also by the change of garment, many girls of God also standing by*, and others. And thus begins this pope's sermon to Marcellina: *A good marriage, daughter, hast thou desired. Thou seest how great a people has come together for the birthday of thy bridegroom, and none goes away unfed.* The canons, moreover, wished girls to be veiled or consecrated only at a mature age. In the year 381 the Council of Saragossa, can. 8, laid down *that virgins who have vowed themselves to God are not to be veiled, except at the proven age of forty years*. The same afterwards both the Emperor Majorian and the Council of Agde, can. 19, confirmed. But in the year 397 the Third Council of Carthage, which the Second of Arles, can. 52, also seems to support, can. 4, ordained *that neither deacons be ordained, nor virgins consecrated, before twenty-five years of age*. Afterwards also it pleased the Second Council of Milevis, which was held in the year 416, can. 26, or *Codex canonum Ecclesiae Africanae*, c. 126, *that whatever bishop, through the necessity of chastity in peril . . . . , has veiled a virgin, or shall have veiled one, within twenty-five years, the council* (of Carthage) *by which it was laid down about that number of years shall not stand against him*. And the aforesaid constitution of the Council of Carthage, with the exception of Milevis, the capitularies of our kings hold ratified, that of Aachen, in the year 789, c. 45, and that of Frankfurt, in the year 794, c. 14. It is to be noted, however, that these things were provided only for the solemn profession, since before that too girls were permitted to take up the purpose of virginity.] and have deserved to be veiled by the priest, [0479A] if afterwards they have either married publicly or [nt: The old editions of the Councils: *si postea nupserint*; and then *vel publice vel occulte corruptae fuerint*, the words *vel publice* being moved from their place against the testimony of the manuscripts. In the Colb. Lang., as in others of the Spanish and Isidorian collections, is found *vel fuerint occulte corruptae*. Quesnel prefers *vel clangulo corruptae fuerint*, and thence conjectures that *clangulo* was once said as if *clam in angulo*. But in the exemplars of the Codex published by him, Thuan. or Colb., Pith., etc., as in the Dionysian and Hadrianic and in the manuscripts Corb., Fossat., etc., we read *vel se clanculo corruperint*. Whence that conjecture seems to have no authority.] have corrupted themselves secretly, they are not to be admitted to do penance, unless he to whom they [nt: Most and the better manuscripts: *se junxerat*, following which Quesnel supplied certain words of his own. Then the Dionysian and Hadrianic: *de mundo recesserit*; the Colb. Langob. codex, with others of the Spanish and Isidorian collections and also the early editions of the Councils: *de hac vita decesserit*, or *dicesserit*. We judge that this double reading was substituted for the genuine one from a marginal note. For since this *de saeculo recesserit* seemed ambiguous, and it could be interpreted either of withdrawal from the world into a monastery or of departure out of this world and mortal life, the ambiguity was removed by a marginal scholion, by which we are advised that *saeculo* is the same as *hac vita*. And indeed what is subjoined about the woman who has married another while her husband lives favours this annotation. But how those are helped by it who think that the marriages of consecrated virgins are indeed disapproved by Innocent but not dissolved, is hidden from us. Or at any rate they must prove that the same pope, and the framers of the canons older than he, judged that for a woman who has married another while her former husband lives, this latter marriage is not to be dissolved. The mind of Innocent is illustrated by the First Council of Toledo, celebrated in the year 400. For can. 16 sets down first *that a devoted woman who sins is not to be received in the Church, unless she has ceased to sin, and, ceasing, has done fitting penance for ten years*: and then adds: *But she who has taken a husband, let her not be admitted to penance, unless, while that husband is still living, she has begun to live chastely, or after he himself has died.* But how could she be proved to live chastely, unless by withdrawing from the adulterer? The fathers of the same council explain this more openly in can. 19, where, concerning the daughter of a bishop or presbyter or deacon, *if she has been devoted to God, and has sinned, and has taken a husband*, they lay down *that she be not admitted to communion, unless, her husband being dead, she has done penance; but if, while he lives, she has withdrawn, and repented . . . . . , let her receive penance when failing at the last*. Certainly the Second Council of Tours, can. 20, understood that the dissolution of a marriage of this kind is commanded by Innocent. For it first copies out his words, then professes that it is not lawful for it to depart from his decrees. And at last, having added to them the canon 52 of the Second Council of Arles and some others, it decrees concerning a consecrated virgin who has married, and concerning him whom she has married: *They are to be slain with the sword of the mouth, and deprived of communion, if they have been unwilling to observe the decrees of the elders left to them, and have disdained to hear their pastor, and have been unwilling to be separated. Those therefore who have wished to endure in this obstinacy, and to remain in the wallowing-place of evil conversation rather than to separate themselves from the forbidden marriage, let them be condemned by perpetual excommunication.* For if by *decrees of the elders* it there means, above all, the words of Innocent adduced, which cannot be denied, since it judges that they are despised by those *who have been unwilling to separate themselves from the forbidden marriage*; it remains that it judged Innocent to have commanded the dissolution of a marriage of this kind. So the Fifth Council of Orleans, can. 19, with which the First of Mâcon, can. 12, agrees, permits the same persons to be recalled to the grace of communion only on this condition, *if they have healed the fault by separation*. And indeed that marriage, which by the judgment of the Council of Mâcon *is rather a debauchery than a marriage*, is by the sentence of Siricius, Epistle 10, n. 3, *incest*, and on the *adulterer* nothing but the false name of husband can be laid. Innocent also, with the same pope, judges consecrated virgins to be truly *married*, inasmuch as *they had joined themselves to an immortal bridegroom*. Whence it follows that, since their bridegroom never ceases to live, neither can the law of the marriage entered into with him ever be dissolved. The Second Council of Tours praises the law 2 of the *Codex Theodosianus*, tit. 25, by which the Emperor Jovian decrees: *If anyone shall have dared, I will not say to carry off, but even to make an attempt on consecrated virgins for the sake of joining marriage, even unwilling ones, he shall be struck with capital sentence.*] had joined themselves has departed from the world. For if this principle is kept in the case of all, that whatever woman has married another while her husband is living is held an adulteress, and leave to do penance is not granted her unless one of them has died: how much more is it to be held in the case of her who had before joined herself to an immortal bridegroom, and afterwards has passed over [nt: Merlin and Crabbe, with the Colb. Langob. manuscript and also other codices of the Spanish and Isidorian collections: *transire elegit*.] to human nuptials!

## CHAP. XIV.



## 16.

But those [n: (27, q. 1, c. 9; Ivo, p. 7, c. 17)] who, not yet covered with the sacred veil [nt: In two manuscripts, *consecratae*: which word Leo uses, Epistle 5, to Rusticus, c. 15, where the discourse is of the same girls. Our reading is confirmed by that verse of Prudentius, book II against Symmachus: *Our virgins too have fairest rewards, both modesty and the face covered with the holy veil.*], have nevertheless [0480A] promised [nt: The old editions of the Councils, with the manuscripts of the Dionysian, Hadrianic, Spanish and Isidorian collections: *semper se simulaverunt permanere*. Quesnel, with the old Corb. manuscript and one Colb.: *se permanere simulaverant*. Fossat.: *semper manere simulaverant*. Sirmond and Labbe: *semper manere promiserant*. The Roman edition: *semper se permanere promiserant*. That we should prefer, with two Colb. manuscripts, *se promiserant permanere*, what is joined after persuaded us, where the *sponsio* of the same girls, the *pacta pollicitatio*, and the *prior promissio* are mentioned. Certainly the discourse here is of girls who had bound themselves to God to keep virginity, and who are therefore said by Siricius, Epistle 10, n. 3, *not to have kept the precept of the heavenly marriage*; but who lacked the solemn consecration.] to remain in the virginal purpose, although they have not been veiled, if perchance they have married, these must do penance for some time; because her pledge [nt: Crabbe and the later editions of the Councils: *sponsio earum*. But Merlin and all the manuscripts: *sponsio ejus*; supply *quae hujusmodi est*. Below, the same Crabbe and the Roman edition: *pepigerunt*; where Sirmond, restoring *pepigit* from the manuscripts, added *virgo* of his own, which thenceforth Labbe and Quesnel retained.] was held by God. For if among men a contract of good faith is wont to be dissolved on no account: how much more ought this promise, which she has made with God, not [nt: In the exemplars of the Dionysian, Hadrianic, Spanish and Isidorian collections, as in the early editions of the Councils: *non poterit*.] to be dissolved without punishment! For if the apostle Paul said that those who departed from the purpose of widowhood have damnation, because they have made void their first faith [n: (I Tim. V, 12)]: how much more virgins, who [nt: The Roman edition, with the Colb. Langob. manuscript: *quae pactionis suae fidem minime servarerunt*. Merlin and Crabbe, following the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections: *quae prioris promissionis fidem minime servaverunt*. The Dionysian and Hadrianic: *quae priori promissioni fidem frangere sunt conatae*. In Quesnel, as in one Colb. exemplar and another Pith.: *quae prioris promissionis fidem frangere sunt nisae*. In place of the word *nisae*, Sirmond and Labbe substituted *visae*. We follow the old Corb. manuscript and the Fossat.] will break faith with their prior promise! [0481A]

## 17.

This rule, therefore, [nt: What follows from here seems to a learned man, who prepared the latest edition of the works of St Paulinus of Nola, dissertation 4, at the end of ch. 4, *to be excerpted from some other writings, and not to cohere with what precedes*. But what he subjoins next, *unless the whole letter is understood of this rule*, shows on how light a foundation his opinion rests. For it is not ambiguous that here by the name *rule* is understood the body of decrees which the whole letter comprises; and that what at the beginning had been called the *book of rules* is now more simply called *the rule*. Wherefore it is manifest that this clause too coheres most fittingly with the opening and the rest of the letter. He who in the opening praised Victricius, *greatly asking for the norm and authority of the Roman Church*, and who promised him *a book of rules*, by which he might be able to learn *what discipline is kept in the churches of the city of Rome*, having set out that discipline most amply, fittingly concludes that by the diligent observance of the same rule or discipline peace and concord are guarded in the Church, and thus schisms and heresies, etc., are guarded against. Finally, since among all the codices, written as well as printed, there is none that presents this letter without this clause, the general consensus attributes to it a holy and undoubted authority.] dearest brother, if it has been observed with full vigilance by all the priests of God, ambition will cease, dissension will be quieted, heresies and schisms will not emerge, the devil will not find room to rage, unanimity will abide, iniquity, overcome, will be trodden underfoot, truth will blaze with spiritual fervour, peace preached with the lips will accord with the will of the soul. There will be fulfilled also the saying of the Apostle [n: (Philip. II, 2 et 3)], that, unanimous, of one mind, we may remain in Christ, claiming nothing for ourselves through contention nor through vainglory; pleasing not men, but God our Saviour, to whom is honour and glory for ages of ages. Amen. Given on the fifteenth day before the Kalends of March [n: (Februar. 15, ann. 404)], Honorius Augustus for the sixth time and [nt: Merlin and Crabbe, following the Spanish and Isidorian collections: *Aristo*. Crabbe added in the margin *Aristoneto*, which the Roman editor seized on. The old Corb. manuscript and one Colb. show *Aristineto*. Another Colb., with Pith.: *Aristhineto*. Another Colb. likewise: *Aristinio*. In Sirmond and Labbe: *Aristaeneto viro clarissimo*. These last two words, in place of which in the Roman edition there stands *viris clarissimis*, are absent from all the manuscripts, as also from the old editions of the Councils. But the whole consular or chronological note is lacking only in the collection of Dionysius, whether pure or later augmented, as we have said beforehand.] Aristenetus being consuls.

## NOTICE ON THE FOLLOWING LETTER.

[0481B]

## 1.

*This letter is shorter in some books, fuller in others. Who cut it down.* --Some books present this letter shorter, others fuller. In the shorter form it was first published by Jacques Merlin, and was then reprinted in the same manner both in the collection of pontifical letters and in various editions of the councils, until Jacques Sermondus, having found it entire in old books, judged that it should be published for the public good. In the exemplars of the Spanish collection, such as the libraries of the Churches of Laon, Noyon and Beauvais preserve, as in all the exemplars of Isidore Mercator, it is had only mutilated. Hence we suspected that it was first truncated by the one who put together the Spanish collection of canons. But that suspicion was then removed by the Colbert codex 408, *On the Institution of Clerics*, written 700 years ago, and another like it and no more recent, the Coislin, which, although they are composed of the monuments of the Spanish collection alone, [0481C] nevertheless retain those chapters of this letter which are lacking in the shorter copy. Finally, when we learned that sound and genuine exemplars of the Spanish collection still exist in the Churches of Urgel, Gerona and Ripoll, in which this letter is read entire, we were fully persuaded that it had been truncated not by him who arranged that collection, but by some other. And since in all the exemplars of Isidore Mercator without exception it is had only mutilated and cut down, who would think another author of this deed should be sought? Nor is it surprising that a man who made no scruple of forging many things, interpolating others, altering more in various ways, did not fear to suppress some things as well. Content, namely, to have copied out Innocent's general decrees, he withdrew the rest, which pertain to special persons or causes, as more foreign to his purpose. Nor do the aforesaid books of Laon, Noyon and Beauvais stand in the way of this opinion. For although [0482A] they were copied from the Spanish collection, yet it is clear, expressly from the letter of Pope Vigilius to Profuturus, that he by whom they were written or dictated also had the collection of Isidore by him, and sometimes, although rarely, preferred it to the Spanish. Further, this letter of Innocent is preserved entire not only in the genuine exemplars of the Spanish collection but also in the very old one of Corbie, and in that of Saint-Maur-des-Fossés, now Colbert's. Since these things are so, that nothing remains for the reader to desire, we now present it entire from the fidelity of the sounder codices, in such a way that we also indicate, at its end, how it was published by Mercator.



## 2.

*That it is inscribed to the bishops of the synod of Toledo, not of Toulouse.* --In both kinds of exemplars a false inscription is prefixed to it. And in the preliminary index of the Corbie codex, indeed, it is given this title: *Epistola Innocentii papae ad universos episcopos ecclesiae Tolosanae de universis herisebus* [n: (leg. haeresibus)] *vel de ordinationibus episcoporum, et caetera in epistola requirenda:* [0482B] but at the head of the letter itself this superscription is set before it: *Innocentius episcopis universis in Tolosana synodo constitutis.* The same is read also in the one of Saint-Maur-des-Fossés, except that in this one *diversis* has been substituted for *universis*. In most exemplars too, of the Spanish as well as of the Isidorian collection, it is likewise inscribed *universis episcopis in Tolosana synodo constitutis*. Nor did Merlin change this inscription. Indeed Jacques Sirmond also retained the same, and on that account gave this letter a place among the very councils of Gaul. « On which matter, he says in the preliminary *Monitum*, although I understood that it could with good reason be doubted, yet it seemed better that it should stand here entire, even in another's place, especially since it had hitherto been mutilated, than perhaps be missed in its own. » Afterwards, however, he added in the notes that it did not seem doubtful to him « that it was written to the bishops of Spain after the synod of Toledo indeed, but concerning the things done in it, or that followed afterwards. Wherefore, he says, I judge that the ancient inscription of the letter should be restored, [0482C] so that *in Toletana synodo* is read. » Further, he calls it *ancient* because, having already been restored by Crabbe, it was retained by those who reprinted either the councils or the letters of the Roman pontiffs. Before that too it prevailed at least in some manuscripts. We ourselves, among the various codices of Isidore's collection, have come upon one of Navarre written at least 600 years ago, and another of the monastery of St Martin of Champs, which show *in Toletana synodo*. Nor is the soundness of this reading to be doubted, as what we are about to subjoin will confirm. And whence the other crept in may be conjectured from various manuscripts compared with one another.

## 3.

*How it came about that it was inscribed* TO THE SYNOD OF TOULOUSE. --Although in the Noyon exemplar this letter is inscribed *universis episcopis in Tolosana civitate constitutis*, yet in the general index of the same it is headed *ad universos Tolotanos episcopos*. That *Tolotanos* was written there for *Toletanos*, not for *Tolosanos*, is proved by the codex of Saint-Germain, no. 424, [0483A] containing a collection of canons put together 800 years ago, in which, as often as any synod of Toledo is cited, it is constantly called *Tolotana*. Since, therefore, here too the scribes had written *Tolotana* for *Toletana*, the copyists judged the more readily that *Tolosana* ought to be restored, because they both saw a letter of Innocent *ad Exsuperium Tolosanum*, and in the text of this letter, n. 3, read corruptly *Galliarum* or *Galliorum*, in place of *Galliciorum* or *Gallaecorum*. Moreover, since below, n. 6, where from the testimony of the Colbert exemplar we read that Rufinus *in Toletano concilio erroris tui veniam postulasse*, the Corbie one shows *Tolosano* for *Toletano*, no one will fail to agree that the same fault could have crept into the inscription with equal ease.

## 4.

*That it was written to the fathers of the First Council of Toledo.* --What that synod is to which Innocent now writes, and when it was held, is disputed. Baronius, in the earlier edition of the *Annals*, had laid down two things as certain: first, that this letter of Innocent was written to the First Council of Toledo; the other, that this council [0483B] was celebrated in the last consulship of Stilicho, that is, in the year of Christ 405. But in the latest edition he changed his opinion, and, persuaded that the First Council of Toledo is to be dated to the first consulship of Stilicho, and thus to the year of Christ 400, he thought that this letter pertains to some later synod of Toledo. To this opinion many subscribe, and by name Antoine Pagi. And the former opinion, by which the First Council of Toledo was put off to the second consulship of Stilicho, can by no means be defended. But neither does there seem to be any reason why we should distinguish the council, to whose bishops Innocent now writes, from the First of Toledo. That this may become more evident, it will be worth the labour to weigh what has been published under the name of the First Council of Toledo.

## 5.

*The three parts [sic: *patres*] of the First Council of Toledo as published.* --In this council three parts are to be distinguished. The first consists of the preface, 20 canons, and the subscriptions of the bishops. The second contains the rule of faith and [0483C] eighteen chapters against the heresies, with this inscription: *Here begins the rule of the catholic faith against all heresies, and most especially against the Priscillians, which the bishops of Tarragona, Carthagena, Lusitania and Baetica made, and with the precept of Leo, pope of the city of Rome, transmitted to Balconius, bishop of Gallicia.* The last is marked with this title: *Here begin the exemplars of the professions in the council of Toledo against the sect of Priscillian, era* 438.
