Then follows the preface: « After the council was held on the Kalends of September, on the third day before the Nones of September, after various hearings then held, on the eighth day before the Ides of September there were excerpted from the full acts the professions of the lord Symphosius and the lord Dictinius, bishops of holy memory, and of the lord Comasius of holy memory, then a presbyter, which among the rest they made in the council of Toledo concerning the condemnation of Priscillian or of his sect, in this manner: After some things, and among some things done at the same time, Bishop Dictinius said, » etc., that is, the rest [0483D] of Dictinius's profession, after which, set down, is subjoined the profession of Comasius, with this preface or interlocution placed first: « In the era as above, on the third day before the Ides of September, the professions of the lord Simphosius and the lord Dictinius, bishops of holy memory, and of Comasius of holy memory, then a presbyter. Comasius said. » Finally this profession of Comasius is followed by « the exemplar of the definitive sentence, transferred from the acts. On the day as above the bishops said: Let the sentence be read. And he read, » etc.

## 6.

*They present a council celebrated in the year 447.* --But how those three parts cohere among themselves, or can be referred to one and the same council, no one, as far as I know, has hitherto explained. Only this is agreed among the learned, that the middle part belongs to that council which Leo, writing to Turibius in the year 447, commanded to be gathered; and thus that it is foreign to the First Council of Toledo. Hence, after Baronius, Binius notes that it was inserted among the other two *by the error of the collector*. But neither can the last [0484A] be more fittingly referred to the First Council of Toledo. For the bishops who speak in it indicate that they are holding a synod when Symphosius, Dictinius and Comasius were already dead, who, it is agreed, were still living at the time of the First Council of Toledo, and indeed, present at that very council, condemned the heresy of Priscillian. But those three parts will cohere with one another most aptly if they are referred to the general council of the Spains which was gathered by order of Pope Leo: so that in it first the 20 canons of the First Council of Toledo were read out and confirmed, then the rule of faith was composed, 18 new canons were added, and lastly some excerpts from the full acts of the First Synod of Toledo were recited. What therefore has been published as the First Council of Toledo would more aptly be inscribed: *The council of the Spains which the bishops of Tarragona, Carthagena, Lusitania and Baetica celebrated by the precept of Leo, in the year 447, on the Kalends of September: to which are prefixed the 20 canons of the First Council of Toledo, celebrated in the year 400, and certain excerpts of the same council, copied from [0484B] its full acts, are subjoined.*



## 7.

*That a council was held at Toledo in the year 400.* -- But we have so restored that council to the year 447 as not to deny that another was held at Toledo in the year 400, but rather to assert it plainly from that very fact. Of this council of Toledo Idatius speaks thus in his *Chronicle*, at the year 400: « In the province of Carthagena, in the city of Toledo, a synod of bishops is gathered, in which, as is contained in the acts, Symphosius and Dictinius and other bishops of the province of Gallaecia with them, followers of Priscillian, condemn his most blasphemous heresy, together with its very author, by the subscription of their profession. Certain things also are laid down to be observed concerning the discipline of the Church, Bishop Ortygius, who had been ordained at Celenae, taking part in the same council. » Now the council held by the order of Leo in the year 447 first reviews the canons pertaining to discipline which the synod of Toledo issued *Stilichone consule*, that is, in the year 400: and afterwards, from the [0484C] acts of the same synod, dated according to Spanish custom *era* 438, for the year 400, it presents not only the professions of Symphosius, Dictinius and others condemning the heresy of Priscillian together with its author himself, but also the sentence of the same council, by which their honour is preserved to them, and which is closed, in Ortygius's favour, with these words: « But to our brother Ortygius we pronounce that the churches from which he had been driven are to be restored. » Whence the agreement of those acts with the *Chronicle* of Idatius is manifest. Moreover, in the sentence of the synod of Toledo just mentioned Simplician is recorded as the one who then ruled the Church of Milan. But Simplician succeeded Ambrose only in the year 397, and he himself, before the sixteenth day before the Kalends of July of the year 401, had Venerius as successor, as is clear from the Council of Carthage inserted in the *Codex canonum Ecclesiae Africanae*, c. 56. And hence, therefore, the time at which the synod of Toledo was celebrated is confirmed.

## 8.

*In what sense the following [0484D] letter can be said to have been written to this synod.* --These things being laid down, since it is ascertained and beyond doubt that that synod of Toledo, whose canons and acts are reported in the aforesaid council of the Spains, was celebrated in the year 400, no controversy now seems to remain but that the following letter of Innocent was inscribed to this synod of Toledo. For first, in it, as may be read at nos. 6 and 7, the acts and decrees of this synod are praised more than once. Next, Innocent, at no. 10, notes that he is addressing the bishops « who decreed by their sentence that ordinations are to be made according to the Nicene rules. » And indeed the prelates of the First Council of Toledo, when in the very preface they had decreed « that the constitutions of the Nicene council are first of all to be kept in perpetuity, and that there must be no departing from them », proceed to lay down what ought to be observed in ordinations. Not, however, that Innocent is to be understood as writing to the synod of Toledo as though still assembled, but rather to those bishops who had come together in it. Unless one prefer [0485A] that here by the name *of the synod of Toledo* the bishops belonging to the synod of Toledo are understood. Certainly the praised pope speaks of the First Council of Toledo, no. 6, as celebrated *long since*. Nor does that *Patruinus of venerable memory*, in whose place he records, no. 9, that Gregory was substituted, seem other than the one who is read to have subscribed first to the canons of the First Council of Toledo. Finally, Innocent's zeal in this letter aims chiefly at this, that he may suppress and settle the schism which arose from Symphosius, Dictinius and others having been received with their honours at the synod of Toledo, and which was spreading more widely day by day. Wherefore it is manifest that some interval came between this letter and that synod. But how much came between cannot be defined with certainty. We may only conjecture that this letter was written before Spain was thrown into disorder by the tumults of war, and thus before the year 408, in which Constantine snatched that province from Honorius. But whether the letter is later than the preceding one or not, although we do not know, yet on account of the affinity of the matters defined in both concerning the ordinations of clerics, [0485B] it will be pleasing and convenient to the reader to have both next to one another. Nor is it now of much concern to him to have ascertained the time at which it was written, since he holds to whom, on what occasion, and with what design it was written. Here perhaps someone will wonder how the same pontiff now so earnestly praises and approves the indulgence of the synod of Toledo towards the followers of Priscillian, who afterwards so constantly refused that clerics ordained by Bonosus should keep their honours. But a wise priest ought to discern between leprosy and leprosy, and to search out when the severity of discipline is either to be retained or relaxed. For although the holy leaders of the churches are animated by the same spirit, yet at different times they have laid down different things concerning clerics returning to the Church from heresy or schism. The Nicene fathers, can. 8, willed the Novatians to remain in the clergy. On the testimony of the Africans, a council beyond the sea, in the times of Siricius and Ambrose, forbade that clerics coming from heresy [0485C] should keep their honours. But the Council of Toledo, in the year 400, judged that this was to be granted to the followers of Priscillian, and Innocent now approves its sentence *for the purpose of unity*, who in the year 414 will sharply vindicate the severity of ecclesiastical discipline in the case of the Bonosians. The Council of Carthage, however, held in the year 401, judged that this moderation should be used: that for clerics returning, where by their return some compensation of unity is made, their honours should be preserved, and where there is no compensation of this kind, they should be refused [n: (Cod. can. eccl. Afr., c. 68)].

## EPISTLE III. On [nt: We have borrowed this title and those that follow from Sirmond, not from the manuscripts.] the dissension and corrupted discipline of the Churches of Spain.



## I.

*That some bishops wrongly dissent from others on account of converts received from heresy.* --II. *On bishops [0486A] ordained by Rufinus and Minicius in other men's churches against the canons.* --III. *That the cause of Bishop John be examined, and of the others who were shunning the fellowship of communion.* --IV. *On the faulty ordinations of many, that what has been usurped hitherto be so passed over that it be forbidden for the future.* --V. *That the complaint of Gregory, bishop of Merida, be examined.* --VI. *Whom it is lawful to admit to the order of the clergy: and of what sort they are to be who are chosen. And that she too makes a man a bigamist who was his wife before baptism.*

*INNOCENT to all the bishops assembled in the synod of Toledo, most beloved brethren, greeting in the Lord.*

## 1.

*A schism in Spain. And contempt of the canons.* -- Though an excessive care has often held me anxious concerning [nt: Already before the First Council of Toledo a certain dissension had prevailed among the Spaniards concerning the ordinations of clerics. Wherefore Bishop Patruinus, in the preface of this council, speaks first thus: *Since we have each begun to do different things in our churches, and from this there are such great scandals, which reach even to schism, if it please you, let us decree by common counsel what is to be followed by all the bishops in ordaining clerics.* But after the council just mentioned, to this cause of schism was added another, when some admitted, and others contended should be rejected, those converted from the heresy of Priscillian to catholic communion whom the same synod had received in their honours. Innocent now tries to heal that twofold cause of so great an evil.] [0486B] the dissension and schism of the Churches, which report declares to be creeping more widely day by day through the Spains, and to be advancing at a quicker pace, the necessary time has come in which so great an amendment could not be put off, and a fitting medicine ought to be provided. For our brethren, our fellow bishop [nt: Among those who subscribed the First Council of Toledo, Hilarius is read in the fourth place.] Hilarius and the presbyter Elpidius, partly moved by love of unity, partly roused, as was fitting, by the ruin with which the province labours, have come to the apostolic see, and with grief and groaning have set forth that in the very bosom of the faith the peace within the province has been violated, the order of discipline confounded, and many things committed against the canons of the fathers, order being despised and the rules neglected, in the usurpation of churches, and that the concord in which the whole stability of our faith consists cannot be retained: which things [0486C] are kept in the record of acts drawn up in the session [nt: That is, of the Roman clergy, this name being taken from the more worthy rank. To the same session, above all in causes of this kind, the bishops who were staying at Rome were also accustomed to be summoned.] of the presbytery, and can be shown to you by reading.

## CHAP. I.



## 2.

*The bishops of Baetica separate themselves from the rest on account of the Gallaecians who were received.* --First, then, as regards the faith itself, a dissension has arisen because the bishops of Baetica or of Carthagena, on account of communion with the Gallicians, have withdrawn [nt: So the Corb. and Colb. manuscripts. But in Sirmond, *dissederunt*. *Discesserunt* would rather have to be restored. Earlier, in the Corb. manuscript, *Galliarum*; in the Colb., *Galliorum*; perhaps in place of *Gallicorum* or *Gallaeorum*. For, as we heard above from the *Chronicle* of Idatius, the bishops of Gallaecia were Symphosius, Dictinius and others, who at the synod of Toledo condemned the heresy of Priscillian, together with its very author, by their subscription; that the Baetici and Carthaginians took ill their being received into peace, and therefore fell away from the communion of the rest, is not at all surprising. Gregory, bishop of Elvira in the province of Baetica, had sown deep the seeds of the Luciferian error. Nor is it astonishing that they propagated an unhappy fruit down to the times of Innocent.] from the peace of all: which is not only not diminished, but is even increased day by day by the zeal of contention, since, each one purposing to obtain what he wished, they have made out of such animosity an eternal round of evil and a kind of circle: when surely for every good man it is better in matters of this kind to be overcome than by an evil habit to obtain a perverse [0487A] purpose which has once pleased him. For what other cause, in former times too, was there of that Lucifer, besides obstinacy, which drew him back from concord [nt: In the manuscripts, *cum illorum*. The sentence would be more complete in this manner: *cum illorum qui Arianorum haeresim prudenti conversione damnaverant, tum illorum qui satisfactionem eorum susceperant.* On the same matter Sulpicius speaks thus, book II: *Lucifer so condemned those who had been at Ariminum that he separated himself even from the communion of those who had received them under satisfaction or penance.* *And to whom*, says Augustine, Epistle 185, n. 47, *an indulgence of this kind was displeasing, he fell into the darkness of schism, the light of charity being lost.* Since in these places Innocent, Sulpicius and Augustine reproach Lucifer with schism only, the error by which they dared to repeat baptism seems to be attributed to his disciples alone, not to himself.] with those who had condemned the heresy of the Arians by a prudent conversion? With the same zeal, [nt: Sirmond and Labbe: *post Priscilliani detestabilem sectam omnium detestatione damnatam*. The preposition *post* is absent from the manuscripts, in which moreover *consensione* is found, more elegantly than *detestatione*. To this refers what Jerome writes to Ctesiphon, that Priscillian was *condemned by the authority of the whole world*.] the detestable sect of Priscillian having been condemned by the deserved consent of all, we have learned that some took it very ill that those were received into the catholic faith who were converted by sounder counsel: to whom a useful deed, and the very peace of the churches, is discovered to have been displeasing. For when, for the purpose of unity, [nt: The Colb. manuscript: *Symposius*. That Symposius is not to be believed the one who subscribed the synod of Saragossa held against the Priscillianists in the year 380. How Symphosius, Dictinius and their companions, having confessed their error and professed the faith, were admitted to catholic communion is explained in the council which was held under Pope Leo, from the acts of the council of Toledo, tome II of the *Concilia* of Labbe, p. 1229 and following. In the sentence, however, which the fathers of Toledo pass on them, it is to be noted that, permitting Paternus to hold the church in which he had been established, they add: *about to receive* (him) *also into our communion, when the apostolic see shall have written back*; and, decreeing the same of the remaining bishops of Gallaecia who had always persisted in the communion of Symphosius, they wish them too at the same time to await, *by a like example, what the pope who now is, what St Simplician, bishop of Milan, and the other priests of the churches write back.* Where by the way it may be observed that the Roman bishop is designated by the simple name of *pope*. The fathers of Toledo continue: *But let Symphosius, an aged religious man, who did what we have written above, remain in his church . . . thence* (that is, from the pope and from the bishop of Milan) *he will await communion, whence he had before received the hope of future peace. Which we have decreed is to be observed also for Dictinius and Anterius.* Then there follows next: *But we have laid down that, before communion is restored to them through the pope* (see again the name of pope for the Roman pontiff) *or through St Simplician, neither bishops nor presbyters nor deacons are to be ordained by them.* Why do they pass sentence on all those bishops in such a way as to wish it to be awaited until communion is restored to the same by letters of the apostolic see and of the bishop of Milan? Light is thrown on this question by similar decrees both of the Council of Carthage held in the year 401, on the 18th day of June, and of another celebrated in the same year on the 13th day of September, by which, as may be seen in the *Codex canonum Ecclesiae Africanae*, c. 65, the Africans resolve that it should be sent to Anastasius the Roman pontiff and to Venerius bishop of Milan, that it might be lawful for them through these both to promote to the clergy infants baptized by the Donatists, and to receive in their honours clerics who wished to pass over from the Donatists to catholic unity, where this seemed to profit Christian peace. For they subjoin the reason of this matter, *because this had been forbidden from these sees*. Nor do the fathers of Toledo teach obscurely that they had determined that the aforesaid bishops should await the communion of the Roman and Milanese prelates for this reason, that some kind of judgment concerning them too had already gone before from those sees, inasmuch as Ambrose and Siricius had prescribed to them by letters certain conditions of peace. Whence the sentence of the Spaniards concerning Vegetinus is more absolute, in this manner: *But Vegetinus, against whom no sentence had been specially pronounced before, having given the profession which the synod accepted, we decree is to be restored to our communion.*] Symphosius himself also and [nt: That Dictinius had not only followed the tenets of Priscillian, but had also asserted them in books. Afterwards, however, in the Council of Toledo, detesting whatever he had either thought or written amiss, he said: *All my presumption in my writings I censure and condemn.* And soon after: *Again I repeat, whatever I wrote in my former comprehension and in the beginnings of my conversion, I reject all with my whole heart. . . . Except the name of God, I anathematize all . . . All things that are found against the faith I condemn together with their very author.* Yet his writings could not be suppressed so carefully but that down to the times of Pope Leo they were read by many with veneration, to whom indeed that pope, Epistle 17, n. 19, counsels that *they ought rather to love his restoration than his fall*.] Dictinius, condemning the depraved heresy, were received, so that, such persons being cut off, the inbred dissension might be utterly extinguished; there were found some to whom the very correction rightly made [n: (Mss. facit)] was displeasing. And now [0487B] the churches are at variance, which with no small animosity dissemble from one another ( *Or* disagree and go off into enmities). But if the correction had been kept by the priests with sounder counsel, both the state of the catholic faith would [0488A] have remained entire, and no scandal would have corrupted the concord useful in all things.



## 3.

*That some bear with an unjust mind the honours kept for them.* --For I ask why they should have grieved that Symphosius and Dictinius, and the others who condemned the detestable heresy, were then received into the catholic faith? Is it [nt: Sirmond, with the Corc. and Colb. manuscripts: *Numquidnam aliquid*. The codex of Bonneval: *Num quod non aliquid*. By recalling *num* from the first two and *quod non* from the last, the reading is now had entire and clear. The very examples by which Innocent now proves that clerics may lawfully have their honours kept for them after penance, Augustine too employs for the same cause, Epistle 185, n. 45: *For holy David also*, he says, *did penance for deadly crimes, and yet he remained in his honour: and blessed Peter, when he shed most bitter tears, certainly repented of having denied the Lord, and yet he remained an apostle.*] that they did not lose anything of the honours which they held? But if this pricks or goads any, let them read that Peter the apostle was, after his tears, what he had been; let them consider that Thomas, after that doubting of his, lost nothing of his former merits; and finally that David, the eminent prophet, after his open confession, was not deprived of the merits of his prophecy. But if the amendment of conversion both cuts off the errors themselves and retains the dignities; what evil reason [0488B] is it, out of a purpose of obstinacy, to be unwilling to hold to the way of right and the road which leads to salvation?

## 4.

*The force of unity, and the ruin of schism.* --Wherefore your charity must apply itself, and good priests [0489A] must strive, that, with teaching going before [nt: By which, namely, both the perversity of schism or heresy and the rightness of the catholic faith may be proved, *so that the light of truth may drive out the darkness of error*, says Augustine, Epistle 93, n. 5, *and the Church may be seen to receive men corrected rather than perverse*.], all who have been scattered may be gathered into the unity of the catholic faith; and there may begin to be one impregnable body, which, if it be separated into parts, will lie open to every injury of laceration, and will suffer an internal plague from itself, when the frame itself fights against itself. But let all the priests receive these things, written in general concerning the restoration of unity, as though written to each. Thereafter let your charity turn its mind in detail to all the things which shall be proposed.

## CHAP. II.



## 5.

*Rufinus and Minicius usurped ordinations in other men's churches.* --For it could not lie hidden that Rufinus and Minicius, bishops, usurped the ordaining of bishops in other men's churches, against the Nicene canons. That [n: (Nicaen. can. 4, 6 et 16)] no one [0489B] may dare to claim this for himself, provision must now at least be made by us wholesomely: lest the dissimulation by improper [nt: It would be said more clearly: *ne improba usurpatio dissimulatione*.] usurpation grow stronger for the worse, and a rule be made of custom which does not come from the very discipline that has been committed to writing. In which matter let the complaint of Hilary, our brother and fellow priest, be heard first, who asserted that Rufinus had been engaged with every kind of assault against the peace of the churches, and had some while ago asked pardon for his error in the council [nt: So the Colb. manuscript, where the Corb. has *Tholosano*. Here it must be recalled that the acts of the synod of Toledo have by no means come down to us full and entire, but only fragments of them, recited in a later council as then suited the purpose; and that nothing was recited in that council except what concerned the Priscillianists. Rufinus, however, was rather one of those bishops of whom Patruinus complains in the preface of the first council of Toledo, namely those who, having undertaken illegitimate ordinations, stirred up such scandals as went as far as schism, not one of those who had been devoted to Priscillian. The same is to be thought of Minicius also. Whence also that part of the acts of the council of Toledo which concerned them was passed over in the aforesaid council.] of Toledo, and now, although the prerogative of ordaining [nt: Sirmond and Labbe: *sacerdotis*. More correctly the manuscripts: *sacerdotes*. The council of Laodicea, can. 12, the Nicene, can. 4 and 6, and that of Antioch, can. 19, forbid the bishops of each province to be ordained without the consent of the metropolitan. See also Ferrandus, *Breviatio canonum*, c. 4. And the creation of a bishop in out-of-the-way places is contrary to canon 6 of the council of Sardica.] priests was owed to the metropolitan bishop, had ordained a bishop in out-of-the-way places, against the will of the people and the reason of discipline, and had embroiled the churches in scandals. Thereafter the cause of the bishops of Tarragona must be treated, who have complained that Minicius in like manner [0489C] ordained a bishop in the church of Gerona; and sentence must be passed on such usurpation according to the Nicene [nt: That he who has been so ordained ought not to be a bishop, the Nicene synod declares in can. 6. And the council of Antioch, confirming this in can. 13, further decrees the penalty of deposition against the authors of the usurped ordination.] canons. Let there be a full examination also of those bishops who were ordained by Rufinus or by Minicius [0490A] against the rules: so that, since they were made wrongly, they may understand that they cannot any longer hold what they obtained from a faulty beginning.

## CHAP. III.



## 6.

*That John be cut off, if he does not cease to resist the decree on receiving those who return to the Church, to which he had subscribed.* --Concerning JOHN also, the bishop, whose consent was given through legates in the synod of Toledo to the reception of SYMPHOSIUS and DICTINIUS, and to whom that correction seemed approvable, let the things which followed afterwards be examined. And, above all, concerning all those whose communion has come into doubt on account of withdrawal, let a full inquiry be made, so that, according to the decree of the synod of Toledo, they may either be joined in the fellowship of communion for the abolishing of the suspicion of schism, or, [0490B] if any be found who [nt: The Corb. manuscript: *qui abnegant*.] refuse concord and the constitutions of what was agreed, they may be cut off from the communion of the catholic faith by the sentences of your charity: so that what is shunned may now be, not an internal evil, which does not cease [nt: In Labbe and Sirmond: *non desinit serpere*, where the manuscripts have *non desinit semper*, perhaps for *non se sinit semper*. For Innocent, unwilling that anyone be shunned on any suspicion of internal evil, but only where his profession of it is manifest, gives an apt reason: that internal evil does not always allow itself to be kept silent or held within, but is wont at length to break out and betray itself by words or deeds. And in this way he guards against a grave harm into which the Ithacian schismatics had fallen, or those who at their urging had been sent into the Spains by the emperor Maximus to search out the Priscillianists, of whom Sulpicius committed this to writing: *Nor was there any doubt that that storm would have laid waste the greatest part of the saints, with little distinction between the kinds of men. For then judgment was made by the eyes alone, when a man was reckoned a heretic by his pallor or his dress rather than by his faith.*] always silent, but the manifest profession and contagion of the schismatics.

## CHAP. IV.



## 7.

*Certain ordinations against the canons to be tolerated for the sake of peace. But that henceforth they be void, and their authors to be deposed.* --For concerning the ordinations which we learn that the bishops of Spain celebrate through the fault of a depraved custom, something was to have been decreed according to the tradition of the elders, had we not weighed it, lest we should stir up very many disturbances [nt: So the Corb. manuscript, with which the Colb., retaining *quam plurimas*, also adds *ecclesias*. But Sirmond substitutes *quam plurimis ecclesiis*. Augustine, animated by the same Spirit as Innocent, writes in Book III against the Letter of Parmenianus, n. 14: *When the same disease has seized very many, nothing else remains for the good but grief and groaning.* *For then*, he says a little after, *counsels of separation are both vain and pernicious and sacrilegious; because they become both impious and proud, and they disturb the weak good more than they correct the bold bad.* Wherefore both thereafter in n. 16, and in the epistle formerly 64, now 22, n. 5, he explains how circumspectly a multitude is to be rebuked. Which, although said properly of the multitude of the people, is aptly accommodated to every multitude of offenders.] in the churches. Whose deed we so reprehend that, on account of the number of those to be corrected, we do not call into doubt the things which have been done in whatever [0490C] manner, but rather leave them to the judgment of God. For how many of those who, after receiving [nt: The word *baptismi*, which is added here in Sirmond and Labbe, stands indeed in the curtailed exemplars of this letter, but in the entire ones it is passed over in silence. As in the preceding letter, n. 5, *after the remission of sins*, so now *after receiving grace* means the same as *after receiving baptism*. Leo likewise designates baptism by the name of grace, Epistle 18, n. 4, where he writes: *The very quality of the work therefore teaches that the lawful day for celebrating grace generally* (in place of *celebrating baptism*) *is that on which Christ, having been buried, rose again.* Again, below, Innocent in n. 11, as also Cyprian, Epistle 76, to Magnus, and Damasus, Epistle 9, n. 1, call baptism *the grace of God*. It is familiar to others, and indeed to Innocent himself, to call the same sacrament *the grace of Christ* and *the sacrament of Christian grace*.] grace, were engaged in the exercise of the courts [0491A], and took up an obstinacy in winning [nt: Here advocates of causes seem to be noted, who, although they sometimes take up unjust causes, strive nevertheless to win them by the force of eloquence and obstinate zeal.], we find to have been admitted to the priesthood! of whose number Rufinus and Gregory are said to be; how many from some military service, who, while they obeyed the powers, [nt: In some exemplars *severa* is added here; it is absent from the purer ones. For the same reason it is now said *praecepta sunt exsecuti*, as presently *quae sibi sunt imperata fecerunt*. In either place it is signified that those bound to military service and to the curia are wont to be compelled to do things which keep them from the clergy. In Gratian, dist. 51, c. 4, canon 8 of the first council of Toledo is exhibited, interpolated thus: *If anyone after baptism has served as a soldier, and has taken up the cloak or the belt for killing the faithful* (the last three words do not stand in the codices of the council of Toledo), *even if he has not committed graver things, if he has been admitted to the clergy, let him not receive the dignity of deacon.* And the same prejudiced opinion which gave occasion to the interpolation of this canon seems also to have been the cause of the word *severa* being added here.] of necessity carried out commands! how many from the curials, who, while they obey the powers, did what was commanded them! how many who celebrated pleasures and shows [sic: *editiotiones*] [nt: From the preceding letter, n. 15, supply *ludorum vel munerum*.] for the people, have come to the honour of the highest priesthood! of all whom it had behoved none to come even to the fellowship of the order of clerics. If we should order these to be examined one by one, we shall bring upon the provinces of Spain, which we desire to heal, no small commotions and scandals out of zeal for amendment. Therefore we think these things should rather be remitted. But lest like things be committed henceforth, [0491B] the maturity of your charity will have to provide that a necessary end be put at least now to so great a usurpation: this, namely, being established, [nt: In the exemplars of the Isidorian collection there is added here *ut quicumque tales ordinati fuerint, cum ordinatoribus suis deponantur*: which is a mere marginal scholion, or a summary heading of Innocent explaining the text. In the same books, presently, *ordinatoribus* has been substituted for *creatoribus*.] that if any after this shall attempt to come, against the forms of the canons, either to the ecclesiastical order or to the priesthood itself, they be deprived, together with their creators, of the very order and honour in which they have been found.

## CHAP. V.



## 8.

*That the complaint of Gregory of Merida be heard.* --Of GREGORY also, bishop of Merida, who was ordained in the place of PATRUINUS [nt: Those who judged that Patruinus, whose name is read in the preface of the first council of Toledo, and who subscribed first to it, was bishop of Toledo, are corrected at this place. Gregory, for his part, seems to have had as successor Antoninus, who, as Idacius relates in his Chronicle, olymp. 306, n. 21, ruled the church of Merida at the time when Turibius ruled that of Astorga, that is, in the age of Leo.] of venerable memory, let the complaint, if there be any, be heard: and if he has suffered injury against his desert, let him be avenged upon those envious of another's honour: and henceforth let not the spirit of faction rise up against any one of the good.

## CHAP. VI.



## 9.

*That the Nicene rules be kept in ordinations [0492A]. Who are to be kept from orders.* --And although the Nicene rules are known to your charity, dearest brethren, according to which you decree by sentence [nt: Namely, as we observed after Sirmond in the Notice, with Patruinus speaking first, in the preface of the council of Toledo itself.] that ordinations are to be made; yet I have thought fit to insert some part which has been provided concerning ordinations, [nt: The sequence of the discourse here requires *et secundum*. And indeed for copyists *ut* for *et*, and in turn *et* for *ut*, frequently slips out. The slip here is old; wishing to repair it, Isidore Mercator after the word *celebrandas* added *esse sciamus*. From his exemplars the same addition was carried over into the Noyon codex of the Spanish collection, not into the Laon exemplar of the same collection.] that according to this ordinations may be celebrated hereafter, lest free choice to interpret otherwise be left to anyone. And first the things which are forbidden are set out. That no one who has served as a soldier after baptism ought to be admitted to the order of the clergy; nor those who have pleaded causes after receiving baptism; or who after receiving the grace of God have held administration: nor that any of the curials can come to the ecclesiastical order, who after baptism either have been crowned [nt: Namely in those solemnities which flowed from gentile rite, and in which the magistrates were compelled to represent a priesthood dedicated to the worship of demons. To which that of Tertullian looks, in the book *On the Crown*, c. 10: *You have the crowns of all the priestly colleges in Claudius*; whose book *On Crowns*, namely, as is recalled in the same place, chap. 7, was extant, teaching both the origins and the causes and the kinds and the solemnities of them.], or have sustained the priesthood, as it is called, [0492B] and have celebrated public shows. For this also is to be guarded against in the case of curials, lest those same who have been of the curials be at some time demanded back by their curias, which we see frequently happen. All which things, reasonably forbidden, ought to be guarded in every way.

## 10.

*Who are to be chosen. That the baptism of wives is not abolished.* --But what sort of men ought to be chosen into the order of the clergy, a clear form declares: that is, those who have been baptized from their earliest age, and [nt: Labbe: *et electorum officio deputati*: it is corrected from other books. And their reading is confirmed here by Siricius, Epistle 1, n. 13: *Whoever has vowed himself to the services of the Church from his infancy... ought to be JOINED to the office of LECTORS*; and n. 14: *But he who is already advanced in age... at the time when he is baptized, let him at once be JOINED to the number of lectors or of exorcists.*] joined to the office of lectors; or, if they be older, when they have obtained the grace of God, have at once bound themselves to the ecclesiastical orders. And if they have had wives, it must be asked whether they took a virgin wife: because it is written in the old Testament, *Let the priest take a virgin wife [n: (Levit. XXI, 13)]*; [0493A] and elsewhere, *Let my priests marry once.* Nor he who has had two wives [n: (Vide epistolam superiorem. n. 9)], because Paul the apostle says, *The husband of one wife [n: (I Tim. III, 2)]*. Nor that ought to be admitted which some oppose and assert in defence of a depraved error, that a wife taken before baptism ought not to be imputed, because in baptism all things are forgiven; not understanding in this matter that in baptism only sins are forgiven, and the number of wives is not abolished. For if *by God*, as it is written, *a wife is prepared for a man [n: (Prov. XIX, 14)]*, and *what God has joined, let man not put asunder [n: (Matth. XIX, 6; Gen. I, 28)]*; and the very authors of the human race are blessed by the Lord in the beginning: how are these believed to be able to be forgiven among sins? But if what they believe who believe so is true; then [0493B] all righteousness which has been wrought by catechumens before baptism will be taken away through baptism. Let no one therefore think or admit any such thing against the Apostle: but let him faithfully understand that *the husband of one wife* was named whether before baptism or after baptism. For if a wife taken before baptism is not counted in the number, neither will the sons begotten of her be able to be numbered among sons. How absurd and foreign this is, your prudence will judge better. Whence let it be lawful for no one to interpret the divine Scriptures otherwise than as right reason permits: lest, while [nt: In the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections *quidam* is added here.] they prepare unjust remedies for themselves as an excuse, they be judged both to have corrupted the law and to have overturned the rules. But those things are to be held which both the course of the divine Scriptures contains and have been instituted by priests with useful reason. [0493C] *And in another hand.* Farewell, dearest brethren.

## THE SAME LETTER.

In the curtailed form in which it stands in the old exemplars of Isidore Mercator, as also in the Noyon, Laon [0494A] and Beauvais exemplars of the Spanish collection, and such as it is in the Roman edition of the pontifical letters and in the old common editions of the councils.



## I.

*On the repression of the Spaniards* ( *for* reprehensione) *who ordain clerics disorderly.* --II. *That pleaders, or curials, or men given to secular military service be not admitted to the clergy.* --III. *That whoever have been so ordained be deposed together with their ordainers.* -- IV. *What sort of men are to be chosen into the order of the clergy.* -- V. *That he who had a wife before baptism, and another after baptism, be not made a cleric.*

INNOCENT to all the bishops assembled in the synod of Toledo [n: ( plures mss. Tolosana)], his most beloved brethren in the Lord, greeting.

Since care has often and exceedingly held me anxious [0494B] over the schism of dissension among the churches, which evil report follows up as advancing more widely day by day through the Spains by degrees of separation; a time has arisen in which so great an amendment cannot be put off, and a fitting medicine ought to be provided. For concerning the ordinations which through the fault of a depraved custom the Spaniards, *and the rest thereafter as in the preceding exemplar, from n. 8 to the end, except that the contents of n. 9 are also passed over here: so that, only the general decrees being retained, the other things which pertain to particular causes or persons have been suppressed.*

## EPISTLE IV. [nt: In the edition of the Councils, after 27. It has been handed down to us by Palladius in the dialogue on the life of St John Chrysostom. From there it was first published at Rome in Latin among the letters of the Roman pontiffs, from the translation of Ambrose of Camaldoli; afterwards it was also printed in Greek at the head of the letters of Chrysostom, with a new Latin translation. At length in the year 1680, through the zeal of Emery Bigot, it came out more correct and more elegant together with the whole dialogue of Palladius. We have examined it afresh against the Coislin and Hamburg manuscripts. It was written immediately after the violence done to the church of Constantinople by the adversaries of Chrysostom on the vigil of Easter, and so about the month of April of the year 404.] OF JOHN, BISHOP OF THE CITY OF CONSTANTINOPLE, TO POPE INNOCENT.

[0494]

*How unjustly he was driven from his city and church by the faction of Theophilus [0494C], and how great evils were perpetrated whether then or afterwards.*

[n: (Haec epistola proprius habetur inter caeteras S. Chrysostomi, ubi videsis tom. III operum S. Doctoris.)]

## EPISTLE V. [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 34; the one which was 5 is now 68. It was inserted, without title or inscription, on page 24 of the dialogue on the life of Chrysostom by Palladius, to whom alone it is owed. It was printed in Latin at Rome among the pontifical letters from the translation of Ambrose of Camaldoli, and from there reprinted in the editions of the councils. The occasion and the time of its writing the same Palladius teaches in the same place thus: *A few days after Innocent had received letters from Theophilus by an Alexandrian lector, and had answered them, there came again to the City, in the name of Theophilus, Peter his presbyter and Martyrius, deacon of the church of Constantinople, bearing another letter of Theophilus with certain acts against John. When these had been read through, Innocent, having found that there were no grave accusations, dismissed those new legates with this other letter.* Whence it is gathered that it was written at no long interval after the preceding one.] OF POPE INNOCENT I TO THEOPHILUS, BISHOP OF THE CHURCH OF ALEXANDRIA. *That he cannot withdraw from the communion of John unless he be condemned by a lawful judgment. Theophilus is summoned to a canonical synod.*

Brother Theophilus, we hold both thee and our brother John as communicating with us, as we also made our mind manifest in our former letters [nt: These letters are not extant.]. And not even now departing from the same purpose, we write the same things to thee again, and as often as thou shalt write back to us, namely that it cannot be that, unless a fitting [0495A] judgment follow upon the things which have been done in mockery, we should withdraw without reason from the communion of John. Do thou therefore, if thou art confident of the judgment, present thyself at the synod which shall have been [nt: Bigot, preferring *cogitur*, seems to follow the reading of George of Alexandria in the life of Chrysostom, which exhibits γενομένη for γεγενημένη. Ambrose of Camaldoli translates: *hasten to the synod to be celebrated shortly in Christ*: which is a sign that he read γενουμένη. Tillemont thinks that Innocent speaks of the future synod as certain, because Honorius, who was then at Rome, had promised that he would ask it of Arcadius. But if this pope ever had that hope as certain, he did not have it long. For about the month of May of this year 404 he wrote back to John and his companions that the powerful in evil were hindering him from being able to bring them help more quickly. Wherefore we understand that Innocent means nothing else here than to summon Theophilus to a lawful synod, *saying that another irreprehensible synod of Westerners and Easterners ought to be assembled*, as Palladius speaks, p. 23.] according to Christ, and there, the accusations having been set forth under [nt: Ambrose of Camaldoli renders this thus: *and there contend according to the Nicene canons and decrees. For others*. By these words, as will be more fittingly expounded at Epistle 7, Innocent warns Theophilus beforehand that he strives in vain with the canons of Antioch.] the Nicene canons as witnesses (for the Roman church admits no other canon), thou shalt have a security free of contradiction.

Brother Theophilus, we know both thee and our brother John to be in communion, as we also made our mind manifest in our first letters; and now too, not departing from the same purpose, we write the same things to thee again, and as often as thou shalt send, that unless a due judgment follow upon the things done in mockery, it is impossible [0496A] that we should withdraw without reason from the communion of John. So then, if thou art confident in the tribunal, come to meet the synod which has been held according to Christ, and there, having set out the charges under the canons of Nicaea as witnesses, for the church of the Romans does not admit another canon, thou shalt have a security that cannot be gainsaid.

## EPISTLE VI. [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 3; the one which was 6 is now 39. It stands in all the ancient collections of every kind, for instance those of Isidore, the Spanish, that of Hadrian I, of Dionysius Exiguus, and of the Code published by Quesnel. The Corbie codex written in the sixth century exhibits the same, and the Colbertine written in Lombardic capital letters, etc. Besides, in the year 506 it is read as cited by the council of Agde, can. 9. Wherefore a learned man rightly laughs at, and judges unworthy of refutation, a certain heterodox writer who would have this letter taken away from Innocent for this one reason, that Siricius is cited in it. As though it were unworthy of a pontiff to confirm what he himself defines by the decrees of his predecessors. But the question put to Innocent was one which necessarily required that the constitution of Siricius be recalled by him, so that he might temper his sentence with fair distinction against those who had sinned either knowingly or in ignorance.]

[0495A]

## I.

*On the incontinence [nt: We have copied these titles from Dionysius Exiguus; yet so that what is first for us is XXI for him, and what is seventh for us is XXVII for him. In the old exemplar of the Colbertine library this letter is inscribed: *of Pope Innocent to Exsuperius, bishop of Toulouse, by which he set out to him in six titles the causes of his questions and the decrees of his answer: where he added a summary of the divine books of the old and new Testament*.] of priests or levites.* -- II. *On last [nt: The edition of the councils, and the Spanish and Isidorian collections, after the old Corb. codex: *De iis, qui post baptismum omni tempore incontinentiae dediti, in ultimo poenitentiam poscunt*.] penance.* --III. [nt: The Corb. manuscript with the Isidorian collection: *De iis, qui post baptismum omni tempore administraverunt, et saeva tormenta exercuerunt*. In the early editions of the councils: *De administratoribus vel judicibus saecularibus*.] *On administrators.* --IV. *That husbands do not come together with adulterous wives [0495B].* --V. *That those who dictate petitions or criminal ones be held immune.* --VI. *That those who, a repudiation intervening, have undergone divorce, if they have joined themselves in other marriages, be shown to be adulterers.* --VII. *What books are received in the canon.*

*INNOCENT [nt: So most manuscripts, not, as in the printed editions, *Exuperio*. Presently several of good note omit *salutem*; a word which seems to have been first added by Dionysius. Jerome praises the merits of Exsuperius in more than one place. Of him he writes to Ageruchia, Epistle 11: *I cannot without tears make mention of Toulouse, which the merits of the holy bishop Exsuperius have ensured has not fallen down to this day*: and at the end of Epistle 4, to Rusticus, among other things: *Nothing is richer than he who carries the body of the Lord in a wicker basket, the blood in glass.*] to EXSUPERIUS, bishop of Toulouse, greeting.*

## 1.

To thee, dearest brother, who consulted me as to what I thought on each proposed case, I have answered, according to the capacity of my understanding, what things seemed good: what either [nt: The Colb. Langob. codex and another manuscript: *vel lucidior ratio*.] docile reason would persuade to be followed, or the authority of reading would show, or the observed series of the times would demonstrate. And indeed thy charity, following the practice of the prudent, has chosen rather to refer to the apostolic see what [0495C] it ought to keep in doubtful matters, than by presumed usurpation to hold [0496A] what seemed good to itself on each point. For why should we think it more shameful to learn something than to be wholly ignorant? To me myself also docility accrues from the conference, while [nt: The older manuscripts: *praescrutatis*; and below: *qui postulatur docere*.] having searched out the reasons I am compelled to answer the things proposed: and thus it comes about that he always learns something who is asked to [0496B] teach. I shall propose, therefore, each thing, and subjoin the answer.

## CHAP. I.



## 2.

Thou hast proposed what ought to be observed concerning those whom, placed in the ministries of the diaconate or in the office of the presbyterate, sons begotten have revealed to be or to have been incontinent [n: (Dist. 82, c. 2; Ivo p. 6, c. 57; Agathense concil. c. 9)]. Concerning these, both the discipline of the divine laws is manifest, and the admonitions of Siricius, bishop, a man of blessed memory [n: (Epist. 1 c. 7, et epist. 5, n. 3)], have gone forth plainly, that the incontinent placed in such offices be deprived of every ecclesiastical honour, and not be admitted to approach the [nt: In Merlin and Crabbe, as in the manuscripts of the Dionysian, Spanish and Isidorian collections: *tale ministerium*. Better, *tale* is absent from the other books.] ministry which it behoves to be fulfilled by continence alone. For there is a very old authority of the sacred law, kept from the beginning, that [nt: Six ancient manuscripts: *in templis*. Then four: *anno vicissitudinis suae*.] the priests were commanded to dwell in the temple in the year of their [0496C] turn [n: (Luc. I)]; so that, serving the sacred oblations, pure and [0497A] cleansed from every stain, they might claim for themselves the divine [nt: Merlin, Crabbe and most manuscripts: *mysteria*. We retain *ministeria* with the Pith. manuscript and with the Roman edition and others. Siricius indeed, Epistle 1, n. 11, enacts that priests and levites who are incontinent can never handle the divine *mysteria*: but there he speaks of the priests and levites of the new law. And when Gratian, dist. 31, c. 6, cites what follows thus, *that it be lawful for those to be admitted to the sacrifices who do not exercise the carnal office even with a wife*, he is rightly arraigned for adulterating Innocent's sentence. He himself reports it more faithfully at dist. 82, c. 2.] ministries [n: (I Paralip. XXIV)]: and that it be not lawful for those to be admitted to the sacrifices who exercise carnal intercourse even with a wife, because it is written: *Be ye holy, because I also am holy, the Lord your God [n: (Levit. XI, 44, et XX, 7)]*. To whom certainly, on account of the succession of offspring, [nt: In the Roman edition, following Merlin and Crabbe, *propterea* is suppressed here, and presently, after the words *ex alia tribu*, there is added *praeter* (Crabbe, in smaller letters, *quam*, which in the Roman edition is expressed without any distinction) *ex semine Aaron*: against the manuscripts.] the use of wives had therefore been relaxed, because no one from another tribe had been commanded to approach the priesthood; how much more ought these priests or levites to keep chastity from the day of their ordination [n: (Confer. concil. Turon. I, an. 461, c. 1)], for whom the priesthood or the ministry is without succession, and no day passes on which they are free either from the divine sacrifices or from the office of baptism! For if Paul writes to the Corinthians, saying, *Abstain for a time, that you may give yourselves to prayer (I Corint. [0497B] VII, 5)*; and this certainly he commanded to lay people; much more shall priests, for whom there is a perpetual office both of praying and of sacrificing, always have to abstain from intercourse of this kind. Who, if he has been defiled by carnal concupiscence, with what shame will he usurp to sacrifice, or with what conscience or by what merit does he believe himself heard, when it has been said: *All things are clean to the clean, but to the defiled and unbelieving nothing is clean [n: (Tit. I, 15)]*?

## 3.

But perhaps he believes this lawful, because it is written, *the husband of one wife [n: (I Tim. III, 2)]*. Not [nt: Crabbe and the Roman edition: *ad permanentem*. Labbe: *de permanente*. Quesnel: *propter manendam*. One Colb. manuscript: *propter permanentem*. The Noyon and Laon manuscripts, with Merlin: *permanenti*. The others, more than twelve, and those the older: *permanentem*: and that it is so to be read the following note will confirm.] remaining in the concupiscence of begetting did he say this, but on account of the continence to come. For neither did he not admit those whole in body, who says, *But I would that all were so, as I myself am [n: (I Cor. VII, 7)]*: and he declares more openly, [0497C] saying, *But they that are in the flesh cannot please God. But you are not now in the flesh, but in the [0498A] spirit [n: (Rom. VIII, 9)]*: and [nt: Again the Roman edition, after Crabbe: *et ad habentem*, the preposition *ad* wrongly added. Ambrose, Epistle 63, to the church of Vercelli, n. 22, illustrates the same sentence with the same words of the Apostle in this manner: *for he said having sons, not making them*. Before that, the same editions, after Merlin: *Quod et apertius*, with *quod* added against the faith of the manuscripts.] *having sons [n: (I Tim. III, 4)]*, not begetting, did he say.

## 4.

But the sentence is plainly unlike and divided [nt: The Roman edition and the later editions of the councils: *diversa*: which we have found in one Pith. manuscript only. From the other books we recall *divisa*, that is, into two parts, of which one pertains to those who sinned in ignorance of the decrees of Siricius sent to the provinces, the other to those who knowing:]. For if the form of ecclesiastical life and likewise of discipline which went forth from bishop Siricius to the provinces shall not be proved to have reached some, to these the pardon of ignorance shall be remitted, so that they begin wholly to abstain for the rest. And so let them keep their grades in which they have been found, in such a way that it be not lawful for them to ascend to higher ones. To whom it ought to be a benefit that they do not lose this very place which they hold. But if any shall be discovered to have known the form of living sent by Siricius, and not to have at once cast away the desires of lust; these are in every way to be removed, [nt: The printed editions, except Quesnel: *quia post*: against the manuscripts.] who after the admonition was known judged pleasure [0498B] to be preferred.

## CHAP. II.



## 5.

And this was asked, what ought to be observed concerning those who, having been given over at every time after baptism to the pleasures of incontinence, at the extreme end of their life ask at once for penance and for the reconciliation of communion [n: (23, q. 4, c. 45)].

## 6.

Concerning these the earlier observance was harsher; the later, mercy intervening, more inclined to mildness. For the earlier custom held that penance be granted, but communion denied. For since in those times persecutions were frequent, lest the facility of communion, once granted, should not recall men, secure of reconciliation, from lapsing, communion was rightly denied; penance was granted, lest all be wholly denied: and the reason of the time made the remission harsher. But after our Lord [0498C] restored peace to his Churches, the terror being now driven off, it was decided that communion be given to those departing [nt: So Labbe, Sirmond and several and better manuscripts. Other printed editions, however: *obeuntibus*; and below, with Dionysius and the Isidorian collection, *subsequi* for *sequi*. That harsher discipline, recalled by Innocent, prevails chiefly in the council of Elvira, can. 6, 7, 8, etc. It had prevailed before in Africa also, but only among certain bishops, who indeed did not think peace should be given to adulterers in such a way, and shut the place of penance against adulteries altogether, and yet did not withdraw from the college of their fellow bishops who thought otherwise and granted them peace, as Cyprian relates, Epistle 52, to Antonianus. There is besides a certain example of this severity in the common book of Ambrose on the Fall of a consecrated virgin, to whom, c. 8, n. 58, this speech is addressed: *Cling to penance until the end of life, and do not presume that pardon is given thee by a human day; for he deceives thee who would promise thee this. For since thou hast sinned properly against the Lord, it befits thee to await the remedy from him alone in the day of judgment.* And although Cyprian, in the letter cited, proves at length that neither penance nor indulgence is to be denied to the lapsed; yet toward its end he decrees this: *Those not doing penance, nor testifying the grief of their offences with their whole heart and with a manifest profession of their lamentation, we have judged are to be wholly kept from the hope of communion and peace, if in sickness and in danger they have begun to beg: because it is not repentance for the offence but the warning of impending death that drives them to ask; nor is he worthy to receive comfort in death who did not think that he was to die.* Which sentence indeed, if with Pamelius it is to be judged somewhat harsher, the same will have to be said also of this canon 22 of the first synod of Arles: *Concerning those who apostatize and never present themselves at the church, nor even seek to do penance, and afterwards, seized by sickness, ask for communion, it has pleased that communion is not to be given them, unless they recover and bring forth worthy fruits of penance.* But this so seemed good to them, not because they greatly distrusted the clemency of God, but the truth of penance of this kind. And indeed Cyprian, toward the end of the book to Demetrianus, teaches that no penance is late for one still remaining in this world, and that no one ought to be deterred from it either by sins or by years; that pardon is given to him who confesses, and that one passes to immortality under death itself. Which, although he says it chiefly of the grace of baptism, there is yet no doubt that he thinks the same of every penance undertaken after baptism, provided it be true. Hence, as is to be seen in his Epistles 13, 14, 15, etc., he always took great care that there should not be lacking one who would impart peace and communion to the lapsed, after the exomologesis had been made, if they had begun to be pressed by some sickness and danger. What Innocent now defines, Celestine afterwards, Epistle 4, to the bishops of the province of Vienne and Narbonne, n. 3, and Leo, Epistle 100, to Theodore, n. 5, expressly confirm, and before them the Roman clergy, in Cyprian, Epistle 3, to the clergy of Carthage, had prescribed in these words: *If those who have fallen into this temptation begin to be seized by sickness, and do penance for their deed, and desire communion, they certainly ought to be helped.* Jerome, above, p. 550, notes that the example of the thief was set before us, lest anyone should at any time think conversion late. But there he asks for conversion and change.], [0499A] both on account of the mercy of the Lord, as a viaticum for those setting out, and lest we seem to follow the harshness and severity of the Novatian heretic who denies pardon. Communion therefore shall be granted, with penance, at the last: so that men of this kind, even at their very end, by the permission of our Saviour, may be delivered from perpetual destruction.

## CHAP. III.



## 7.

It was asked also concerning those who after baptism have held administration, and have either exercised tortures [nt: The Pith. manuscript: *tormenta sua*. More truly the others: *sola*, namely those by which death is not inflicted on the accused. To what end this question is set on foot may be supplied and explained from Epistle 25 of Ambrose, to Studius. For he had likewise asked whether anyone, after a capital sentence had been passed, ought to abstain from the communion of the sacraments. To whom Ambrose, n. 2, answers: *There are some, outside the Church however, who do not call into the communion of the heavenly sacraments those who have judged that a capital sentence should be passed on any.* And straightway he adds: *Many also abstain of their own accord; and they are praised indeed; nor can we ourselves not commend them, we who observe the authority of the Apostle so far as not to dare to deny them communion.* Lastly, what seemed good to the elders on this matter he discloses in n. 9 in these words: *And therefore our elders preferred to be more indulgent toward judges, so that, while their sword is feared, the fury of crimes might be checked, and not provoked: for if communion were denied, the punishment of criminals would seem to be avenged.* Our forebears therefore preferred that it should be in the will of the one abstaining rather than in the necessity of the law.] only, or have also pronounced capital sentence.



## 8.

Concerning these we read nothing defined by the elders. For they remembered that these powers had been granted by God, and that the sword had been permitted for the punishment of the guilty, and that the minister of God had been given as an avenger in matters of this kind [0499B] [n: (Rom. XIII, 1, 4)]. How then should they reprehend a deed which they saw had been granted with God as its author? Concerning these, therefore, we hold thus, as has been observed hitherto, lest we should seem either to overturn discipline or to come against the authority of the Lord. But for them, in the rendering of account, all their deeds will be kept.

## CHAP. IV.



## 9.

And this was desired to be known, why husbands who communicate do not come together with adulterous wives, when on the contrary wives are seen to remain in the fellowship of adulterous husbands.

## 10.

Concerning this [n: (32, q. 6, c. 23; Ivo p. 8, c. 214)] the Christian religion condemns adultery in both sexes with equal reason. But women do not easily accuse their husbands of adultery, and hidden [0500A] sins do not have punishment. Husbands, however, have been accustomed more freely to report adulterous wives before the priests: and therefore to the women, their crime being disclosed, communion is denied. But of the husbands, the offence lying hidden, no one is easily kept away on suspicions. Who certainly will be removed, if his shameful deed be detected. Since then the cause is equal, sometimes, when proof is lacking, the reason for punishment is at rest.

## CHAP. V.



## 11.

This also [n: (23, q. 4, c. 46; Ivo p. 10, c. 94)] thou hast wished to inquire [nt: In the margin of the Colb. Langob. manuscript this summary of the chapter is noted: *On advocates, who bring forth sentences according to the crime*. But the question is rather about those who demand capital sentences, or rather who dictate petitions for those demanding them, than about those who bring forth sentences of this kind. And this, as also what was proposed in chap. 3, makes plain how greatly the Church shrinks from blood. And so Innocent does not deny communion to those who after baptism have either held administration or dictated petitions for those demanding a capital sentence, in such a way that he nevertheless teaches, Epistle 37, n. 5, that they are to be kept from the clergy.], whether it be freely granted to those who dictate petitions, namely after the regeneration of baptism, to ask of princes the death of anyone, or blood on account of guilt. A thing which princes never grant without inquiry; but they always send back the very matters committed, or the crimes, to the judges, that, [nt: The early editions of the councils and several manuscripts: *causae cognitae*. The Royal exemplar of the Dionysian collection, with the Colb. Langob.: *causa cognita vindicetur*. According to our reading, which the other books exhibit, before *vindicentur* the word *crimina* is understood. See the Theodosian Code, book II, tit. 18, law 1, and book XIX, tit. 40, law 1.] the cause [0500B] having been examined, they may be punished. And when these have been delegated to the investigator, either acquittal or condemnation is pronounced according to the nature of the business. And while the authority of the laws is exercised against the wicked, the one who dictated will be immune.

## CHAP. VI.



## 12.

Thy charity has also inquired concerning those who, a repudiation intervening, have joined themselves to another in marriage. Whom it is manifest are adulterers on both sides. But those who, while the wife is still living, although the marriage seems to have been dissolved, have hastened to another union, cannot but seem adulterers, so much so that even these persons with whom such are joined seem themselves also to have committed adultery, according to that which we read in the Gospel: *Whoever shall put away his wife, and marry another, [nt: In the old Corb. manuscript and another Colb. *moechatur* does not stand here. The integrity of the sentence would be safe if they had afterwards added *moechantur*.] commits adultery; likewise he also who marries her who is put away commits adultery* [0501A] [n: (Matth. XIX, 9)]. And therefore [nt: In the early editions *tales* is added here: which is absent from the manuscripts. Then six manuscripts of good note subjoin *omnes a communione abstinendos*. After the word *abstinendos*, however, *esse manifestum est*, which Innocent expressed at the beginning of this answer, is understood. Wherefore there is no reason why, with Quesnel, *abstinendi* should be substituted against the faith of the manuscripts.] all are to be kept from the communion of the faithful. But concerning their parents, or their relations, nothing of the kind can be decreed, unless they be discovered to have been instigators of the unlawful union.

## CHAP. VII.



## 13.

But what books are received in the canon, [nt: Dionysius added here *scripturarum*. Before which word the author of the Spanish collection thereupon put *sanctarum*. The Isidorian collection seized on both, but the other manuscripts have neither. Further, the noun *brevis* here means a catalogue. The same sacred books, though not in the same order, are recounted among the canonical Scriptures in canon 47 of the third council of Carthage, in the year 397. And the cited canon of this council is read inserted in the Code of canons of the African church, c. 24, with this addition: *Let this also be made known to our brother and fellow priest Boniface, or to other bishops of those parts, for the confirming of this canon: because we have received from the fathers that these are to be read in church.* Whence there is a conjecture that the decree of the third council of Carthage on the sacred books was recited in another council of Carthage, which was held in the year 419 when Boniface was Roman pontiff, and confirmed with the clause just mentioned.] the list annexed shows. These are the things which thou didst wish to be told in word, as desired: the five books of Moses, that is, of Genesis, of Exodus, of Leviticus, of Numbers, of Deuteronomy, and of Jesus Nave, of Judges one, four books of Kingdoms, together also with Ruth, sixteen books of the Prophets, five books of Solomon, the Psalter. Likewise of histories, one book of Job, one book of Tobit, Esther one, Judith one, of the Maccabees two, of Esdras two, [0502A] two books of Paralipomenon. Likewise of the new Testament: four books of the Gospels, fourteen epistles of Paul the apostle, three epistles of John, two epistles of Peter, the epistle of Jude, the epistle of James, the Acts of the Apostles, [nt: The fourth council of Toledo, held in the year 633, seems to have looked to this when it said in can. 13: *The authority of many councils and the synodal decrees of the holy Roman prelates prescribe that the book of the Apocalypse is John the evangelist's, and to be received among the divine books.* Whence too it may be gathered that this letter was synodal.] the Apocalypse of John. But the rest, which were written either under the name of Matthias or of James the less, or under the name of Peter and John, which were written by a certain Leucius [nt: What is enclosed in brackets Quesnel omits, and it stands only in the exemplars of the Dionysian, Spanish and Isidorian collections. Presently the same Quesnel, with one Colb. manuscript and another Pith., prefers *et si qua sunt talia*, where in the other books *alia* is read.] [or under the name of Andrew, which were written by Nexocharis and Leonidas the philosophers], or under the name of Thomas, and if there are any others, know that they are not only to be rejected, but also to be condemned. Given on the tenth day before the Kalends of March [nt: February 20, year 405. In Merlin and Quesnel, as in the two manuscripts just mentioned, *secundo* is lacking; that it is rightly expressed in the others the name of his colleague Anthemius added confirms.], Stilicho for the second time and Anthemius, most illustrious men, being consuls.

## EPISTLE VII. [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 29; the one which was 7 is now 18. It has been copied from Sozomen, who, having found it together with the following Epistle 12 turned from the Latin tongue into Greek, took care to publish both in book VIII, c. 26. From him Nicephorus Callisti afterwards received both, and inserted them in book XIII, c. 32, of his History. By this letter Innocent answered the letters sent to him by the same clergy through the agency of Germanus and Cassian, and so received when the year 405 was already somewhat advanced. For, as Palladius relates, pp. 26 and 27, Germanus and Cassian arrived at Rome after Palladius, and Palladius a month after Cyriacus, bishop of Synnada; who indeed had begun to flee from the East to Rome after the law promulgated at Constantinople in the year 404, on the 18th day of November. In Sozomen and Nicephorus nothing is prefixed to the inscription Ἰννοκέντιος ἐπίσκοπος, etc. Wherefore the title prefixed to it in the editions of the councils, in which the name ἀρχιερέως is attributed to Innocent, being destitute of all authority, is of no weight to prove that the word ἀρχιερέως was then in use. The same is to be said also of Epistle 12, in whose title the editors of the councils of their own accord call both Innocent ἀρχιερέα and John ἀρχιεπίσκοπον.] TO THE CLERGY AND PEOPLE OF CONSTANTINOPLE. *Having consoled them out of their own very judgment, Innocent deplores the unjust substitution of another bishop in the place of John while he lives. Then he declares that the Nicene canons alone are to be received, and that others which oppose them are to be repudiated. Lastly he signifies that it is his care that an ecumenical synod be assembled.*

*[0501B]INNOCENT, bishop, to the presbyters and deacons, and to all the clergy and people of the church of Constantinople, who are under John the bishop, beloved brethren, greeting.*

## 1.

*For what they suffer there is one consolation, that of patience.* From the letters of your charity which you sent by [nt: This Germanus seems to be no other than the one whom Chrysostom mentions above, Epistle 4, n. 3. Nor is there doubt that Cassian is the author of the Conferences, in which Germanus frequently speaks in dialogue as his companion.] Germanus the presbyter and Cassian the deacon, I have learned with anxious solicitude the scene of evils which you have set before our eyes: and by reading them over again and again I have perceived with how great calamities and hardships the faith labours. Which thing indeed the consolation of patience alone can heal. For our God will shortly give an end to such great tribulations, and it will help to have endured these things. [0503A] Truly, this very consolation, necessary as it is, set in the beginning of the letter of your charity, we have recognized, praising your purpose, as confirmed by many testimonies exhorting to patience. For the consolation which we owed you through our letters, you yourselves have anticipated by your letters. For this patience our Lord is wont to supply to those who labour: that the servants of Christ, even in tribulations, may console themselves, reckoning with themselves that the things which they themselves suffer have already before befallen the saints. But we too can borrow consolation for ourselves out of your very letters. For we are not strangers to the fellowship of your grief; since we are afflicted in you.

## 2.

*How unjustly a bishop has been substituted for John while he lives.* [0503B] --For who could bear the things which are committed by those [nt: Namely by Acacius, bishop of Beroea, Theophilus of Alexandria, Antiochus of Ptolemais, and Severian of Gabala, whom the people of Constantinople by their letters signified by name to be the authors of the hardships which they were suffering.] whom it befitted above others to be zealous for tranquillity and peace and concord? But now, in a preposterous manner, innocent priests are driven out of the seats of their own churches. Which indeed our brother and fellow priest John, your bishop, first unjustly suffered, when he had by no reason been heard. No charge is brought, nor is it heard. And what is this abandoned counsel? That there may not be, or be sought, any form of judgment, others are substituted in the place of living priests: as though those who have made their beginning from a crime of this kind could be judged to have or to have done anything right. For we have never found that such things were done by our fathers, but rather [nt: Namely by the council of Sardica, can. 4, where, in favour of a bishop who has appealed to the Roman see, it is enacted: *Let another bishop by no means be ordained in his see, after the appeal of him who seems to have been deposed, unless the cause has been determined in the judgment of the Roman bishop.* But John was cast out even while appealing to a synod, as he himself testifies above, Epistle 4, n. 3. That canon, in somewhat different words, is cited by Ignatius the patriarch in his letter to the pope and the Roman council, in Baronius at the year 861. This very thing, which the synod of Sardica expressly forbids, may be seen to be prohibited also by the Nicene canon 9, by which it is provided *that two bishops be not approved to exist in one city*.] forbidden; since licence has been given [0503C] to no one to ordain another in the place of one living. For a reprobate ordination cannot take away the honour of a priest: since he who is unjustly substituted cannot be a bishop at all.

## 3.

*What canons are to be received or repudiated.* --But as to what pertains to the observance of the canons [nt: Namely, so that the judgment, however unjustly pronounced, might prevail against John, and no place of defence might be given him, Theophilus had sent to Constantinople the canons of the council of Antioch, framed long before chiefly against Athanasius, among which the enemies of John made much above all of the fourth, reported thus by Palladius, cap. 8, p. 78: *If any bishop or presbyter, deposed rightly or wrongly, return of himself without a synod, let such a one have no longer any place of defence, but let him be expelled absolutely.* The very words of the council of Antioch are these: Εἴ τις ἐπίσκοπος ὑπὸ συνόδου καθαιρεθείς, ἢ πρεσβύτερος ἢ διάκονος ὑπὸ τοῦ ἰδίου ἐπισκόπου, τολμήσειέν τι πρᾶξαι τῆς λειτουργίας, etc.; and while Gentian Hervet turns them into Latin thus, *If any bishop deposed by a synod, or presbyter or deacon by his own bishop, shall have dared to celebrate the sacred rite*, he seems not to have rendered accurately enough the words τολμήσειέν τι πρᾶξαι λειτουργίας. For it is not a deacon's part to *celebrate the sacred rite*: and it is plain that a deposed bishop, presbyter and deacon are forbidden by this canon not only to celebrate the sacred rite, but to touch anything of the ministry from which they have been cast down. Wherefore Theodoret, book V of the History, c. 34, relates that the adversaries of John exacted punishment of him no longer for false crimes, but ἀλλὰ τῆς μετὰ τὴν καθαίρεσιν λειτουργίας, *but for the ministry discharged after deposition*. Then a controversy arose about the authority of the canons objected against John, *some*, says Palladius, p. 79, *contending that they were of the orthodox, others showing that they were of the Arians*. On which occasion Elpidius, ibid., p. 80, is said to have said wittily to the emperor: *Let our brothers Acacius and Antiochus subscribe that they are of the same faith as those who put forth the canons which they propose as put forth by orthodox men, and the controversy is solved.* This same controversy, brought before him, Innocent now decides, and teaches that those canons are of heretics, not because orthodox men were altogether lacking in the council of Antioch, but because it is admitted that the Arians, the adversaries of Athanasius, held the first place in it: and a witness of this is the canon cited, which had been put forth on purpose to take away from Athanasius all hope of restoration.], we say that those alone are to be obeyed [0505A] which were defined at Nicaea; which alone [nt: Besides those, says Paschasius Quesnel, Dissertation 12 at the end of the works of Leo, c. 2, *Innocent received the Sardican ones also, but held as Nicene, and adorned and clothed with their name*. And indeed from this, that the pontiff next subjoins about the synod of Sardica condemning the Antiochene canons, it is plain that what was done at Sardica is received by him.
