But since he praises those things under the proper name of the *Sardican synod*; it is no less manifest that the Sardican canons were received by him as distinct from the Nicene, not as *held for Nicene and clothed with their name*. Wherefore we understand Innocent to proclaim that the Nicene canons alone are to be followed in such a way, not that those should be rejected or spurned which agree with them, but only those which disagree: *Since*, says Leo also, Epistle 96, to Pulcheria, n. 2, *against the statutes of the paternal canons, which were founded by spiritual decrees in the city of Nicaea many ages ago, nothing may ever be dared; so that if anyone wishes to decree something different, he rather diminishes himself than corrupts those*. Certainly the Sardican may be reckoned as it were an appendix of the Nicene synod, inasmuch as Hosius presided over it, who at Nicaea had sat either as chief or among the foremost prelates, and in which the same faith was established and the same heresy overthrown. In the time of pope Damasus, as we observed above, p. 591, n. 3, following Athanasius, lest the Arians, *who devised various names of synods*, should impose upon anyone, various councils were held at Rome and in other places, in which it was enacted *that no synod should henceforth be named in the catholic Church except the one of Nicaea*. How much it concerned Athanasius that the authority of the synod of Sardica should by no means be lightened is unknown to no one.] the catholic Church ought to follow and to acknowledge. But if others are brought forward by certain men, which disagree with the Nicene canons, and are proved to have been composed by heretics, these are rejected by the catholic bishops. For the things which have been devised by heretics, these it is not lawful to join to the catholic rules. For they, by contrary and unlawful decisions, perpetually aim at this, to undermine the judgment of the Nicene fathers. We say, therefore, that these canons are not only not to be followed, but are also to be condemned together with heretical and schismatic dogmas, as they have already been condemned in the synod of Sardica [nt: Not indeed by opposed canons or decrees, but by contrary acts, when they received Athanasius and other bishops into communion, no account being taken of the aforesaid canons, and restored them to their sees. This explanation we do not devise ourselves out of our own, but Palladius supplies it, p. 78, where, having cited canon 4 of the council of Antioch, he adds: *And this canon, as impious and put forth by the impious, was abrogated in the council of Sardica by the Romans, Italians, Illyrians, Macedonians and Greeks, when Liberius or Julius* (Julius was then over the church) *in the principate of Constans admitted into communion Athanasius and Marcellus the Galatian, on whose account this canon also had been made.*] by the bishops who went before us. For it would be better that the things which [nt: It seems that in the Greek κακῶς or οὐ καλῶς should be read: and so here there should be restored *quae male*, or *non recte acta sunt*.] have been rightly done should be condemned, [0505B] than that those things which have been done against the canons should obtain any firmness, dearest brethren.



## 4.

*That the evils can be healed by one ecumenical synod.* --But what are we to do at present against these things? There is need of the examination of a synod, which we said long since ought to be assembled. For it alone is that which can calm the motions of storms of this kind. And that we may obtain it, it will be useful meanwhile to await the medicine of these evils from the will of the great God and of Christ himself our Lord. All things which have now been thrown into confusion through the envy of the devil, for the proving of the faithful, will be pacified. There is nothing that we ought not to hope for from the Lord through firmness of faith. For we too have long and much been considering by what means an ecumenical synod may be assembled, so that the turbulent motions may at length, by the [0505C] nod of God, come to rest. Let us bear up, then, for a little while; and, fortified with the rampart of patience, let us hope that all things will be restored by the help of our God. Moreover, all the things which you have said you endure, we had already learned before, by careful questioning, from our fellow bishops who fled to Rome, although at different [nt: The different times at which those bishops fled to Rome Palladius notes, pp. 10 and 26: whence also we have assigned above the time of this letter.] times [0507A], that is, from Demetrius, Cyriacus, Eulysius and Palladius, who are with us.

*[0502B] Innocent, bishop, to the presbyters and deacons and all the clergy and the people of the church of Constantinople, who are under John the bishop, beloved brethren, greeting. *

[0502B] From the letters of your charity, which you sent by Germanus the presbyter and Cassian the deacon, I learned with anxious care the scene of evils which you set before our eyes, and by reading them over many times I perceived with how many hardships and labours the faith is afflicted. Which thing the consolation of patience alone heals. For our God will shortly give an end to such great afflictions, and it will be of profit to have borne these things. But indeed this very necessary [0504A] consolation, set at the beginning of the letter of our charity, we have recognized, praising your purpose, as containing many testimonies toward endurance. For the consolation of ours which we owed to send to you, you anticipated by your letters. For our Master is wont to supply this endurance to those who labour: that the servants of Christ, even when they are in afflictions, may comfort themselves, reckoning within themselves that the things which they suffer happened before to the saints also. And we too can bring consolation to ourselves out of your very letters. For we are not strangers to suffering with you, since we too are chastised in you.

## 2.

For who will be able to bear the offences committed by [0504B] those whom it most behoved to be zealous for calm, for peace, and for concord itself? Now, in an inverted manner, guiltless priests are thrust from the presidency of their own churches. Which indeed our brother and fellow minister John, your bishop, was the first to suffer unjustly, having obtained no hearing. No charge is brought, nor is it heard; and what is this forbidden device? That no pretext of judgment may arise, or be sought, others are installed in the places of living priests, as though those who start out from such an offence could be judged by anyone to hold or to have done anything rightly. For we have never known such things to have been dared by our fathers, but rather to have been forbidden, in that authority has been given to no one to ordain another in the place of one living. For an unapproved ordination cannot take away [0504C] the honour of the priest, since neither can he be a bishop who is unjustly put in his place.

## 3.

And concerning the observance of the canons, we write that those ought to be followed which were defined at Nicaea, [0506A] which alone the catholic Church ought to follow, and these to acknowledge. But if others are brought forward by some, which disagree with those of Nicaea, and are proved to have been composed by heretics, these are rejected by the catholic bishops. For the things found out by the heretics, these it is not lawful to attach to the catholic canons. For always through contrary and lawless things they wish to lessen the counsel of those at Nicaea. We say, therefore, not only that these ought not to be followed, but rather that they are to be condemned together with the heretical and schismatic dogmas, as also happened before in the synod of Sardica at the hands of the bishops before us. For it were more fitting that things rightly done be condemned, than that things done directly against the canons should have any firmness, most honoured brethren. [0506B]

## 4.

But what shall we now do at present against such men? There is need of a synodal examination, which we said long ago was to be assembled. For it alone is that which can calm the motions of such tempests. And that we may obtain it, it is useful meanwhile to commit the healing to the will of the great God and of the Christ of them, our Lord. All the things which have now been thrown into confusion through the envy of the devil, for the proving of the faithful, will be made gentle [Lab. παραμυθήσεται]. We ought to despair of nothing from the Lord through the firmness of the faith. For we too are considering much in what way the ecumenical synod may be assembled, so that by the will of God the [0506C] turbulent motions may cease. Let us bear up, then, for a while, and, fortified by the wall of endurance, let us hope that all things will be restored by the help of our God. And all the things which you have said you undergo, we had learned before by complete questioning, from those who ran together to us, although indeed at different times, that is, from Demetrius, [0508A] Cyriacus, Eulysius and Palladius, who are with us

## EPISTLE VIII. OR COPY [nt: In the edition of the Councils, after Epistle 31. It first came out in the Roman collection of pontifical letters from an old book of the Vatican library. Nor has it been placed without reason among the letters of Innocent, inasmuch as he was the chief author of its writing. For, as Palladius witnesses, p. 28, when holy bishops and other religious men were flocking to Rome from every side, bringing letters of various churches or synods about the wretched state of the church of Constantinople, the pious pontiff, no longer bearing it, informed the emperor Honorius of the things which those letters severally conveyed. Moved by which, he admonished Arcadius once and again, and even a third time, by letters sent, of what needed to be done. Of those three letters the first has perished; this second is had only in Latin; the third Palladius has preserved for us in Greek, from whom we shall subjoin it to this one. By Baronius this is referred to the year 404: but the order of the things which, on Palladius's testimony, preceded it requires that it belong to the year 405, and indeed when it was already drawing to its end.] OF THE SACRED LETTER OF HONORIUS AUGUSTUS SENT TO ARCADIUS, PRINCE OF THE EAST. *Honorius grieves over the things which were perpetrated at Constantinople on the day of Easter. That the burning of the august temple and of the senate is the first sign of the divine wrath. That other things are to be feared unless God be appeased. That he cannot be silent on this matter, lest silence be taken for assent. How over-hastily, while an episcopal judgment was awaited, the sacred prelates were driven into exile. That hence a disturbance of religion has been brought in, and a wide field opened to heresies and schisms.*



## 1.

[0507B]Although, concerning [nt: Namely the silver statue of the augusta Eudoxia, set up on a column of porphyry, and placed toward the southern side of the church of St Sophia, with only a square set between, not without popular games, as count Marcellinus in his Chronicle, Socrates, book VI, c. 18, and Sozomen, book VIII, c. 20, have committed to memory; Baronius thinks this deed took place in the year 404, Pagi, however, vol. II, p. 60, about the end of the year 310.] a woman's image, by a new precedent, the report of detractors, carried about [nt: While Baronius refers the word *circumlata* to the image, he so interprets this place that the statue of Eudoxia was not only exposed to veneration at Constantinople, but was also carried about through the provinces, just as of old the heathen were wont to carry about the image of Cybele, and that this is the truer cause why John was so greatly moved against Eudoxia. But against this understanding stands not only the silence of the ancient writers about so profane a rite, but also the fact that they proclaim that woman's image *set up*, *placed* and *dedicated*, not *carried about*. And the natural understanding of these words is that the report of the detractors about this matter was carried about through the provinces and spread through the whole world.] through the provinces and spread through the whole world, I admonished you by other letters, that by repentance of such a deed and by the discontinuance of the purpose the envious rumour might grow old, and the public tongue might not have what to carp at in the morals of the times: although also, concerning the destruction of perishing Illyricum, we have declared among you with pious affection that it is a grief to us, why you [nt: The sequence of the discourse requires *nos*, not *vos*. For Honorius remonstrates with Arcadius that he wished the losses of the commonwealth to be hidden from him, about which he did not deign to write him anything.] have been unwilling to acknowledge these losses of the commonwealth, and they have been announced by other informers rather than by the letters of your piety: yet it is not lawful for us to dissemble with your serenity even this, that report, ever the swift announcer of evils, has not been silent about what has very lately happened in divine matters, not without fear of public danger; and, as the nature of men bears, [0507C] which is ever roused by new chances to detraction, an occasion of carping being offered, it exercises its malice [0508A] against the times with the zeal of savage loquacity. For it has lately been published [n: (Vid. Pallad. pag. 82 et 83)], that at Constantinople, on the most sacred and venerable day of Easter, when religion had gathered almost all the peoples of the neighbouring cities into the same place, the rite being to be celebrated more strictly under the presence of the princes, the catholic churches were suddenly closed, the priests thrust into custody, so that, namely, at the very time at which by princely indulgence [nt: In Sirmond, appendix to the Theodosian Code, pp. 17 and 19, there is a double constitution of Theodosius, by which *for the Paschal festival, except those whom the enormity of the five crimes by no means allows to be released*, he frees the rest from the custody of prison and restores them to their former state. And this clemency he proclaims in the same place, p. 19, *as enjoined and handed down by our forebears*.] the gloomy prisons of the guilty are unbarred, a savage prison should shut in the ministers of the pious law and of peace, and all the mysteries were thrown into confusion in the manner of war, some slain in the very sanctuaries of the church, and such violence raged about the altars that both [0508B] venerable bishops were thrust into exile, and human blood, what is impious to say, drenched the heavenly sacraments. On suddenly learning these things, I confess that we were disturbed. For who, in so bloody a crime, would not fear the offence of almighty God? or how [ *Supply,* quis] would think it to lie outside the highest peril of the Roman world and of all mortals? when God almighty himself, the author of our Empire and the governor of the commonwealth which he has entrusted to us, was believed to be angered by the exceedingly deadly and execrable deeds committed, holy lord brother and nephew, venerable augusti; when, if anything were being treated between the prelates concerning the cause of religion, the judgment ought [nt: Ambrose, Epistle 21, to Valentinian the younger, n. 2, praises the edict of Valentinian I, by which he enacted that *in a cause of faith or of any ecclesiastical order he ought to judge who is neither unequal in office nor unlike in right*; *that is*, says Ambrose, *he willed that priests should judge concerning priests. Indeed, if a bishop were accused on other grounds also, and a cause of morals were to be examined, he willed that this too should pertain to the episcopal judgment.*] to have been episcopal. For to them belongs the interpretation of divine things, to us awaits [ *By the ancient usage for* spectat] the obedience of religion. [0508C] But let it be granted that the princely care presumed something further to itself concerning mystical and catholic questions: [0509A] ought indignation, once roused, to have gone so far as to the exiles of priests, so far as to the slaughter of men; that where chaste prayers, where sincere vows, where unblemished sacrifices are offered, there the sword, not easily to be drawn even against the throat of the guilty, should thrust itself forth?



## 2.

By the very events, in short, it is taught what the divine majesty has thought about these things. The first judgment [nt: Baronius would prefer *indicium*. Yet *judicium* is to be retained, that is, the punishment and vengeance of God the judge.] of the present commotion indeed was this, and would that it were the only one! for human trepidation, conscious of so great a thing perpetrated, makes us fear, after the proofs of a terrible vengeance, something graver, which may almighty God avert. I hear [n: (Pallad. pag. 91 et 93)] that the most holy church, set out with the wealth of so many emperors [ *Perhaps* expolitam], noble in its precious adornments, most august through the prayer [0509B] of so many supplicating princes, has burned, and that that unique light of the city of Constantinople [ *Baronius adds* ecclesiae] has smoked, fallen into ashes, God not forbidding (for he seems to execrate the defiled mysteries, and to have turned away his eyes from that place which blood had now stained, lest anyone should be able to entreat the heavenly mercy beneath bloody altars); that the sacred buildings also [nt: That is, the most ample house of the senate, which, says Palladius, *although many paces distant, opposite the church to the south, that fire, passing over the multitude of people between as though over a bridge, consumed*. Zosimus bewails its loss. That fire happened on the twelfth day before the Kalends of July, Honorius for the sixth time and Aristaenetus being consuls, as Socrates relates, book VI, chap. 18, and, as the Alexandrian Chronicle adds, on the second weekday, at the sixth hour.], noble with no less splendour, were consumed by the violence of the raging fire as the flame poured itself out more widely, and that the ornaments wrought by our forebears, which adorned the public face, were burned up as though at a kind of funeral of the city.

## 3.

Although I, provoked by frequent injuries, ought to have kept silent about these things, and not so faithfully to admonish a most closely joined brother and the partners of the realm *[Baron. consortem]*, [0509C] yet, putting the tie of blood before the goad of silent grief, I exhort and urge that these things, if it can be done, be corrected by amended morals for the future, and that the divine wrath, so far as by the event it is disclosed to have been roused, be appeased by the diligence of vows. Receive from me the highest proof [nt: In the Roman edition, *judicium*. Better here, with Baronius, *indicium*.] of simplicity. For this reason I thought that this should be intimated to your clemency, lest silence itself should make me suspect with anyone as of a hidden rejoicing, or lest anyone should believe that I give [0510A] consent to such deeds, and that I, who often admonished that they should not happen, did not grieve after they had been committed.

## 4.

For who could be without grief, who remembers that he is a Christian, that so great a disturbance of religion has suddenly been brought in, that the whole state of the catholic faith must needs waver? There was among the bishops a cause which ought to have been resolved by counsel brought together [nt: Baronius: *contractoque concilio*. The preceding word *collato* suggests, and in the Roman edition there rightly follows, *tractatoque consilio*.] and treated. Legates [nt: Here too Baronius substitutes *missis,.... legatis*. Legates were sent to Rome, as Palladius witnesses, pp. 9, 23, etc., from both parties: first, from the party of Theophilus, an Alexandrian lector bearing his letter, then a presbyter of the same Theophilus, Peter by name, with Martyrius, deacon of the church of Constantinople, who delivered other letters of Theophilus together with certain acts to pope Innocent; and lastly Paternus, who called himself a presbyter of the church of Constantinople, bearing letters of the bishops Acacius, Paul, Antiochus, Severian and certain others, in which they slandered John, as though he had set fire to the church of St Sophia. From the party of John, however, to pass over others who afterwards came in great number, there first betook themselves to Rome four bishops, Pansophius, Pappus, Demetrius and Eugenius, and they delivered three letters, one of John, another of 40 bishops concerning communion with him, the third of his clergy. And by name, concerning the letter of John to Innocent, Palladius testifies, p. 22, that it was written also to Venerius of Milan and Chromatius of Aquileia, bishops. Hence Honorius could say that those legates had been sent *to the priests of the eternal City and of Italy*.] were sent from both parties to the priests of the eternal city and of Italy: a sentence was awaited from the authority of all, which was to shape the rule of discipline (for all ought to have been kept intact, and nothing innovated, while a deliberate definition proceeded), when meanwhile a certain wonderful headlong haste blazed up, so that, the letters of the priests who had been consulted by the mutual embassy of the parties not being awaited, the matters not [0510B] examined, the prelates were thrust into exile, delivered over to punishment before they had experienced the sentence of an episcopal judgment. In short, how premature that condemnation was the event has proved. For these [nt: What the emperor here attributes to several, Palladius ascribes to Innocent alone, and says, p. 22: *Upon this the blessed pope Innocent sent letters of communion to both parties, disapproving the judgment which had been made by Theophilus.* And indeed his Epistles 4 and 5 are witnesses of the communion granted by Innocent to both parties. But who would persuade himself that he accomplished a thing of such moment without a synod? especially since we read that Innocent, tenacious of the custom of the elders, prescribed, Epistle 3, n. 1, that the Spanish bishops, who laid something before him, should be heard *in the session of the presbytery*.] whose authority was awaited, having permitted to John the bishop a peaceful communion *[Baron. praemissa]*, judged that concord should be ratified, and thought that no one should be repelled from fellowship before judgment.

## 5.

What now remains but that schisms, dissociated in divers directions, should tear the catholic faith apart, that heresies, ever hostile to communion, should be born from so great a variety of things done, so that it can now scarcely be imputed to the people if perhaps they depart into dissonant parties by the diversity of sects; since from public authority the matter of discords has been sent before, [0510C] and a certain tinder of nourishing sedition has been kindled. That this may not break out afresh into some great ruin of the human race, vows must be made, that God, patient toward human lapses, may prosper and further a thing ill done. For so far as it rests with us, we can fear what has been done; so far as it rests with the mercy of God, ever placable, impunity will be granted not to merit, but to pardon.

## EPISTLE [nt: When Honorius, provoked by the letters of Innocent, had already written twice to Arcadius about the cause of John, and had achieved nothing, he asked Innocent that five bishops, two presbyters and one deacon be sent to him, to carry a third letter to his brother. There were sent Aemilius, bishop of Benevento, Cythegius and Gaudentius (perhaps the bishop of Brescia), and also the presbyters Valentinian and Boniface, the same, as it seems, who succeeded pope Zosimus. The names of the rest Palladius passes over in silence, but he teaches that Cyriacus, Demetrius, Palladius and Eulysius set out with them to Constantinople. For this letter we are indebted to Palladius alone, who inserted it in the dialogue, p. 29. Its Latin translation, taken from Ambrose of Camaldoli, Baronius caused to be reprinted at the year 405. It seems to have been written about the spring of the year 406.] IX.

*OF THE EMPEROR HONORIUS TO ARCADIUS AUGUSTUS.*

*That he order a synod to be assembled at Thessalonica, and Theophilus to betake himself thither.* [0511]

[0511A]I write now for the third time to thy mildness, asking that the things which have been done by compact against John, bishop of Constantinople, be amended: and, as it seems, it has not yet been accomplished. Wherefore I have written again by these bishops and presbyters, being very solicitous for the peace of the Church, through which our empire also obtains peace; that thou wouldst deign to order the bishops of the East to come together at Thessalonica. For our bishops of the West, having chosen men who know not how to turn aside to evil and falsehood, have sent five bishops, two presbyters, and one deacon of the great Roman church, whom I would have thee deem worthy of every honour; so that, if they are persuaded that John was expelled by right, they may teach me to withdraw from his communion; or, if they convict the bishops of the East [nt: The Greek word ἐθελοχακοῦντας, which is said properly of soldiers who on purpose do not put forth their strength, so that the adversary may win, is rightly applied to those bishops who, when they could have opposed the canons to Theophilus, yielded of their own accord, so that he might be able to oppress John unjustly.] [0511B] of being driven by a depraved will of mind, they may turn thee away from their communion. For what the judgment of the Westerners about John the bishop is [that thou mayest have it ascertained], from all the letters which have been written [nt: What is enclosed in brackets I have judged must be supplied, lest the sentence hang in doubt. It is not more complete in Ambrose of Camaldoli, who here subjoins: *From almost all the letters which they have frequently given me, of these I have subjoined two containing the same thing as the rest.* Palladius, p. 28, relates that Innocent, when he had received various letters, wrote to Honorius, ὑποτάξαντα κατὰ μέρος τῶν γραμμάτων δύναμιν, *and subjoined one by one what those letters contained*, where he intimates that only the force, or the sum, of the several letters was expressed. But from this place it is gathered that not only the sum of the several letters, but also the very letters themselves, or copies of them, were sent to him at the same time.] to me, I have subjoined [nt: Palladius writes, p. 31, that not only the letters of the emperor Honorius, of Innocent, Roman bishop, and of Chromatius of Aquileia, but also those of Venerius of Milan and of others, were delivered to the same legates at the same time. But Honorius was content to mention the writings of Innocent and Chromatius sent to him some while before, not those which they were sending afresh to Arcadius, or which he himself was dispatching in his own name, not those which others had joined to them as occasion offered.] two, containing that which the rest contain, one of the Roman, the other of the bishop of Aquileia. Further, before all things I beseech thy clemency that thou order Theophilus, bishop of Alexandria, who is said to be the author of all the evils, to present himself even against his will: so that nothing may hinder the synod of the bishops who shall have come from ratifying a peace befitting our times.

* OF HONORIUS THE EMPEROR TO ARCADIUS THE KING. * [0512A]

For the third time I write to thy gentleness, asking that the things done in the conspiracy against John, bishop of Constantinople, may obtain correction; and, as it seems, it has not been accomplished. Wherefore I have sent again by the bishops and presbyters, being very careful for the peace of the Church, through which our kingdom also is at peace, that thou deign to command the bishops of the East to come together at Thessalonica. For the bishops of our West too, having chosen men not inclined to wickedness and falsehood, have sent five bishops, two presbyters, and one deacon of the greatest church of the Romans, whom deign to care for with every kind of honour, so that, if they are persuaded that John the bishop has been justly cast out, they may teach me to withdraw from their communion; or, having convicted the bishops of the East of wilful baseness, [0512B] they may turn thee away from their communion. For what the Westerners think about John the bishop, out of all the bishoprics which wrote to me I have subjoined two equivalent to them all, the letter of the bishop of Rome and of Aquileia. And this I beseech thy gentleness before all things: deem it right that Theophilus of Alexandria present himself even against his will, through whom above all all the evils are said to have come about; so that the synod of the bishops who have come, hindered in nothing, may award the peace befitting our times.

## EPISTLE [nt: It stands in Dionysius under title XXXVIII of the decrees of Innocent, as also in the Spanish and Isidorian collections. It was carried by Germanus, who with Cassian had delivered at Rome, about the beginning of the year 405, the letters of the clergy and people of Constantinople, to which Innocent answered by Epistle 7. It is probable that Innocent, either in this very year 405 or rather in the following one, used the services of the same Germanus to greet Aurelius and Augustine, and that on that occasion Germanus recounted to the Africans how great and how unworthy were the things John had suffered, and what discord had for this reason arisen between Innocent and Theophilus. Certainly the council of Carthage held in the year 407, on the 13th day of June, and reported in the Code of canons of the African church, c. 101, decrees that it should write to Innocent, so that peace might be composed between the Roman and the Alexandrian church. Nor is it less certain that Aurelius, about the same time, made plain his zeal toward John; for which matter he gives him thanks in a letter written to him.] X. OF POPE INNOCENT I TO AURELIUS OF THE CARTHAGINIANS AND AUGUSTINE OF THE HIPPONIANS, BISHOPS, *Of greeting and full of charity.*

[0511C]

*[0512C]INNOCENT to AURELIUS and AUGUSTINE, bishops.*

The return of Germanus, our fellow presbyter, most welcome to me, ought not to be empty of our duty. For to greet those most dear through those dear seems to us in a manner natural and fitting. We desire, therefore, that your brotherhood, most loving ones, rejoice in the Lord, [0513A] and we pray that you repay [nt: Reg.: *respondere*.] equal vows to God for us: because, as you well know, we accomplish more by common and mutual prayers than by single or private ones.



## EPISTLE [nt: From the edition of the works of Chrysostom, where it is set before his letters; repeated by Baronius at the year 406, as also by the editors of the pontifical letters or of the councils, but examined by us against the old Hamburg exemplar. Chrysostom testifies, n. 4, that it was written by him in the third year of his exile, and so in the year 407. From this it also follows that it was written before the month of July, if Chrysostom reckons the first year of his exile from the day on which he was brought to Cucusus to the same day of the following year come round, and does not call it the third by the common way of reckoning from the threefold change of consuls.] XI. OF JOHN CHRYSOSTOM, BISHOP, TO POPE INNOCENT. *He praises his fatherly affection toward himself and his utmost vigilance in afflicted affairs, and entreats him again and again not to slacken this zeal.* [n: (Hanc epistolam, ut aliam Chrysostomi supra memoratam, reperire potes tomo III operum ejusdem Doctoris.)]

## EPISTLE XII. [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 28. The one which was 12 is Isidore Mercator's. This letter, like the preceding VII, we owe to Sozomen alone. Of both he speaks thus by way of preface, book VIII, c. 24: *When Innocent was striving to assemble an ecumenical synod, he wrote to John, and separately to the clerics of the city of Constantinople.* By which words he seems to signify either that both letters were sent at the same time, or at least that this one is not later than the seventh. But it is not unusual for this writer to put together and confound things done at distant times. Certainly, since there is deep silence in this letter about assembling the synod, on which Innocent had placed all his hope, this is no light argument that it was written when he had fallen from this hope. It is not far from the truth that Innocent, moved by the last letter of John, wrote this one to console the holy prelate in the evils by which he had learned that he was afflicted. And if so, it could scarcely have been delivered to John while he survived.] OF POPE INNOCENT TO JOHN CHRYSOSTOM, BISHOP OF CONSTANTINOPLE. *He consoles John by setting before his eyes the commendation of patience.*

*To his beloved brother JOHN, INNOCENT.*

Although the innocent man ought to expect all prosperous things and to ask mercy of God, yet from us also, who counsel patience, fitting letters have been sent by [nt: The same Cyriacus, with Paul, likewise a deacon, had already in the year 404 been sent to Rome by John to deliver his earlier Epistle 4 to Innocent, as is declared in the same letter, n. 1.] Cyriacus the deacon, lest [0513B] insult avail more to crush than a good conscience to raise up to hope. For neither art thou to be taught, the teacher and pastor of so many peoples, that all the best men are always and constantly tried, whether they abide in the vigour of patience, and do not give way to any labour and trouble. And truly conscience is a firm defence against all things that happen unjustly: which unless one has overcome by patience, he offers an argument of evil suspicion against himself. For he ought to bear all things who relies on trust first in God, and then in his conscience. For a good and upright man can indeed be exercised toward patience, but cannot be overcome; since the divine Scriptures fortify and guard his mind. For the sacred readings which we hand on to the peoples abound in examples, which testify that almost all the [0513C] saints were vexed in various ways, and proved as by a kind of examination, and so came to the crown of patience. Let conscience itself, then, console thy charity, brother greatly to be revered, [nt: Valesius rendered this into Latin thus: *which in calamities is wont to be the consolation of virtue*. We cleave to the Greek wording, in which it is not conscience that is proclaimed the consolation of virtue, but rather virtue the consolation of conscience. Innocent asserts more surely what the Orator had declared somewhat more timidly: *Punishments which are undergone through virtue have some consolation in themselves.* And by the name of virtue Innocent chiefly understands patience, of which in Epistle 7, to the clergy and people of Constantinople, having set out their calamity, he writes in n. 1: *Which thing indeed the consolation of patience alone can heal.*] which in calamities has the consolation of virtue. For under the eyes of the Lord Christ a pure conscience stands in the haven of peace.

* To the beloved brother John, Innocent. * [0514A]

Although the blameless man ought to expect all good things, and to ask mercy from God, nevertheless from us also, who counsel forbearance, the fitting letters have been sent by Cyriacus the deacon; so that insult may not be able to do more [0514B] in crushing, [Add. ἢ] the good conscience in hoping. For thou oughtest not to be taught, thou the teacher and shepherd of so many peoples, that the best men are always and often tried, whether they abide in the vigour of endurance, and fall under no labour of suffering. And conscience is in truth a sure thing against all that befall unjustly; which unless one overcome by enduring, he brings forth a proof of a base suspicion. For he ought to endure all things who trusts first in God, and then also in his own conscience. Whereas the good and noble man can above all be exercised toward endurance, but not be conquered; since the divine writings guard his understanding. And the divine readings which we hand on to the peoples abound in examples, which have assured that almost all the saints were worn down in divers ways [0514C] and continually, and tried as in a kind of examination, and so came to the crown of endurance. Let conscience itself console thy charity, most honoured brother, which in afflictions has the consolation of virtue. For while the Master Christ looks on, the conscience, having been cleansed, will stand in the haven of peace.

## EPISTLE [nt: Formerly recited in the Roman synod under Boniface II, thereafter also cited by Nicholas I, Epistle 2, it was afterwards brought to light by Lucas Holstenius together with the other monuments of the aforesaid Roman synod. The time at which it was written is indicated by the consular note. But the most illustrious man Tillemont, vol. X, p. 829, thinks that there is a fault in this note, and that for *Honorius IX and Theodosius V* there should be restored *Honorius VII and Theodosius II*. From which it would follow that this letter was written in the year 407. And he strives to prove this opinion by this induction. First, indeed, he states that of the letters which Innocent wrote to Rufus this is the first, and was given by him as soon as he was informed of Rufus's ordination; which is not to be denied. Then he adds that that Rufus to whom Innocent now writes seems to be no other than he to whom the same pope, Epistle 17, to Marcian, mentions that he had already given letters about the clerics of Naissus. Which being likewise granted, he subjoins: *But Innocent wrote this letter to Marcian while staying at Ravenna, whither he came at the beginning of the year 409, and where he was still living in the month of August of the year 410; and there is no trace in history from which we might conjecture that he returned besides to the same city.* From which he thinks it follows that neither Rufus's ordination nor this letter is to be put later than the year 408. But since by no means all things that happened have been written, and he who went to Ravenna once could also go there a second and a third time; and since, as the most illustrious man himself admits, a cause and occasion of this was not lacking in the year 413, in which Heraclianus was preventing grain from being carried to Rome: it seems by no means safe to change a certain epoch by an uncertain conjecture. Surely that slip of the copyists would be exceedingly unusual, if, when they had erred in expressing the years of two consuls who had borne the same dignity several times, their error nevertheless so aptly held together in designating their consulships, that with the ninth consulship of Honorius they joined the fifth of Theodosius, not the fourth or the third, and in turn with the fifth of Theodosius the ninth of Honorius, not the seventh or any other whatever.] XIII. TO RUFUS, BISHOP OF THESSALONICA. *The care of various provinces is committed to him, in which he is constituted Innocent's vicar and the first among the primates.*

*To his most beloved brother RUFUS, INNOCENT.*

## 1.

To the most choice and most glorious Moses, in the freeing and ruling of Israel, the Lord so committed all things that he commanded him, in a manifest report, that the counsel of his father-in-law Jothor was to be followed [n: (Exod. XVIII)]: namely that he should constitute men nearly equal in the rank of judicial dignity, who should settle the multitudes of causes among the peoples according to the place entrusted to them, he himself being free chiefly, or even, for divine [nt: Having found by experience that in old books, where the sequence of the discourse requires the word *consultationis*, the word *consolationis* is not rarely, indeed for the most part, wont to be read, we suspect the same fault here also, and think there should be restored *ipso maximis vel etiam divinis consultationibus vacaturo*. For Innocent looks to these words of Exodus XVIII, 19 and 22: *Be thou to the people in those things which pertain to God, to refer what is said to him... But whatever is greater, let them refer to thee, and let them judge the least things themselves.*] consolations. Nor was the form of the Apostles promulgated otherwise [n: (Act. VI, 2 et 3)] than [0515B] that they themselves, constituted princes of the Gospel, should commit to their disciples the causes and needs of other matters, to be cared for and attended to. So, in short, Paul, wonderful in all compassion, committed to Titus what he should care for in Crete [n: (Tit. I, 5)], to Timothy what he should dispose throughout Asia [n: (I Tim. I)], as we learn from the reading of the sacred epistles.

## 2.

Divinely, therefore, this grace running forward, let it learn so to take counsel for the churches separated from me by long intervals, that they may judge that to thy prudence and gravity [nt: That those at whose suggestion Justinian wrote in Novel 11 that only in the times of Attila, that is after the year 440, was the dignity of vicar through Illyricum attributed to the bishop of Thessalonica, imposed upon this emperor, is manifest from this.] are to be committed the care and the causes, if any arise throughout the churches of Achaia, Thessaly, Old Epirus, New Epirus, and Crete, Dacia Mediterranea, Dacia Ripensis, Moesia, Dardania and Praevalis, with Christ [0516A] the Lord assenting [n: (Ad hunc locum respicit Nicolaus I epist. 2)]. For truly, by his most sacred admonitions, we enjoin this solicitude upon the providence and virtue of thy most choice sincerity; not establishing these things so for the first time, but imitating our apostolic predecessors, who willed that these things be enjoined upon the most blessed [nt: That this charge was enjoined upon Acholius at least from the year 380, against the opinion of Haller, we observed in the Notice on the 8th letter of Damasus.] Acholius and Anysius according to their merits. For it is most just that those who have deserved well be so adorned with honours, as it seems necessary that the arrogant be beaten down. It pleases, therefore, that there be reward for the good, and for the less good [nt: *Censio* commonly signifies a mark of censure from the censor. Presently by the name *primatum* are understood the metropolitans of the provinces named before.] censure: for so both the one is corrected, and the other is ennobled.

## 3.

Take up, therefore, most beloved brother, in our stead, the care throughout the churches written above, saving their primacy: and first among the primates themselves, whatever [0516B] it shall be necessary for them to send to us, let them not ask without thy judgment. For so either through thy experience whatever it is will be finished; or we direct that by thy counsel it is to be brought as far as us. Know, moreover, that it is lawful and permitted to thy fraternity by the favour of the apostolic see that, when any ecclesiastical question is to be treated and examined either in thy province or in the provinces mentioned, thou mayest take with thee whom thou wilt of the bishops as associates from whatever Churches, by whose faith and moderation thou mayest direct, as the best arbiter, whatever necessity or cause shall have demanded, and mayest settle it as the chief mediator, as one chosen by us. The whole preparation of the documents in the cause [nt: So Holstenius, not, as Tillemont reads, *Achivorum*. That the discourse here is of archives, as of documents, both what precedes and what follows persuade. For it appears that the truth of the documents by which the office of vicar was delegated to the prelate of Thessalonica was then called into doubt by some. Wherefore Innocent, although he had said to Rufus just before, *as one chosen by us*, which sounds as though the power was conferred on him not so much by the prerogative of his see as by the gift of the one choosing him, yet does not deny that it was supported by custom: and therefore he urges the same man to review both his own former letter and others of his predecessors given on that account.] of the archives we have ordered to be made with the presbyter [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 11. The one which was 14 is now 25. By that very man, perhaps, who by Siricius, Epistle 9, n. 5, is said to have been given to Bassus, bishop, *into partnership in ruling the church*.] Senecio, a man very [0517A] mature. And so, from our former [nt: Written not, as it seems to me at least, to Rufus himself, but to his predecessor Anysius, and which holds the first place above. And hence again it is confirmed that the word *archivorum* was to be retained, and not changed.] letter, and from these documents, reviewing well what thou oughtest to do, take cognizance of it. For we have made this our will sufficiently manifest by letters, as was fitting, throughout each province. Given on the fifteenth day before the Kalends of July [n: (Junii 17 ann. 412)], Honorius for the ninth time and Theodosius for the fifth time, augusti, being consuls.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the Roman edition of the pontifical letters it is noted as copied *from Cresconius*. It would perhaps have been more fittingly noted *from Dionysius*. With him it makes up among the decrees of Innocent title or chapter 50. From there both the author of the Spanish collection and Isidore borrowed it. That this letter treats of the Paschal celebration of the year 414 all agree; and therefore most judge that it was written in the year 413. But Tillemont, weighing everything subtly as is his wont, and observing that the Easter to be kept is not yet prescribed by Innocent, but that this pope, before he could prescribe it with certainty, wished his opinion about it to be reconsidered in an African synod, and a reply sent back to him from there, thinks that it should be assigned to the year 412: *chiefly*, he says, *since Aurelius was accustomed to hold both a provincial council about the end of June, and a plenary council of Africa about the end of August, in which, by the prescription of canon 41 of the third council of Carthage, the Paschal day of the following year was to be made public.* Unless therefore we suppose some synod held in Africa beyond those customary times, the opinion of the learned man seems to be subscribed to. Otherwise it does not appear how Innocent could conveniently have learned the opinion of an African synod on this question, and from there have indicated the day of Easter to the bishops of the whole West. Besides this, pope Leo, likewise solicitous about the Paschal day of the year 455, took pains by many letters to be informed about that matter, not in the year 454, in which that day was to be prescribed and announced to the churches, but from the year 453.] XIV. *On [nt: In the manuscripts of the Dionysian and Hadrianic collections either simply *de Pascha*, or *de Pascha in qua re fallitur*, that is, whether Innocent is mistaken in prescribing its day, or not. In the Spanish: *de insinuatione vel indictis temporibus Paschae*.] the Paschal reckoning of the year* 414.

*INNOCENT TO AURELIUS.*

The duty of our charity [nt: The genuine Royal exemplar of the Dionysian collection: *Charitatis tuae*, perhaps in place of *charitatis mutuae*. The Coislin manuscript of the Spanish collection: *Charitatis vestrae*.] is broken off by no interval, even if the paper carries no letters. For the spiritual grace lives in hearts turn by turn, and the priestly fellowship itself cherishes our love. [nt: So we emend by the help of the aforesaid Royal exemplar, in which there stands *Curatio ut dignum est*. With which agrees this of the Coislin: *Cura ergo ut dignum est*. But in other books there is had *Cur ergo indignum est*; and presently, in the printed editions only, *custodiri*. Then in the Coislin: *idque omnes... fratres carissimi*.] Take care, as is fitting, to guard the unity of the Church; [0517B] and let us all alike both think and pronounce the same thing, dearest brother. These letters, therefore, concerning the Paschal reckoning of the other [nt: By the word *alterius* one must understand the year either other than that in which the day of Easter is to be celebrated, or other than that in which it is to be made public. If this latter is admitted, this letter will have to be assigned to the year 412; but if the former is preferred, it will have to be put off to the year 413. Although he himself signifies below not obscurely that he writes in the year other than that in which the day of Easter was to be made public.] (I mean, the coming) year I have written beforehand. For since on the eleventh day before the Kalends of April the moon is reckoned almost at the 16th (for it is somewhat less), [nt: In the manuscripts of the Hadrianic collection *itemque*, without the added *cum*, which is likewise omitted in the Royal exemplar of the Dionysian collection, the sense unharmed.] and likewise since on the fourth day before the same Kalends the twenty-third comes: I have judged that the Paschal feast is to be celebrated on the eleventh day before the said Kalends; since on the twenty-third moon [0518A] we know that no Easter has ever [nt: So the Royal exemplar. But the manuscripts of the Hadrianic collection: *ante Paschu*. The printed editions, however: *ante hoc Paschu*.] been kept before this. I have set out and declared the tenor of my opinion. Now it will be for thy prudence, [nt: In the Coislin manuscript: *concors mihi*.] my partner and brother, to reconsider [nt: In the common editions after Crabbe, *tractare*. But in all the manuscripts, as in Merlin, *retractare*, which is to review.] this very matter with our fellow priests of one mind in a most religious synod: so that, if nothing conflicts with our disposition, thou mayest write back to us most fully and openly, whereby we may prescribe in its own time the Paschal day, deliberated upon, to be kept, by letters beforehand, as [nt: That it was the custom that the Roman pontiff should direct letters *to all* about the day of Easter, the first synod of Arles, held in the year 514, is witness, and Leo, Epistle 88, to Ravennius, confirms its practice. And this whole passage of Innocent breathes this, that the time at which that custom had to be satisfied was not yet imminent.] is the custom. Although I know that thou wilt receive our fellow presbyter Archidamus most gladly and in thy accustomed manner, yet [nt: All the manuscripts: *ex abundantibus*.] out of abundance I ask that thou deign to hold him among thine own.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 13; the one which was 15 is now 20. Dionysius Exiguus thought this letter not unworthy to be reported among the decrees of Innocent. He placed it under title or chapter 39, immediately before the preceding one. It holds the same place in the Spanish and Isidorian collections. Perhaps it was sent with the same letter. Baronius thinks it was written in the year 413, so that the book of Pelagius to Demetrias, the daughter of Juliana, might be tacitly refuted. To the same Juliana there are extant letters of John Chrysostom, 169, of Augustine, 150, and again of Augustine and Alypius, 188. To her Augustine in the year 414 dedicated his work *On the Good of Widowhood*. Jerome also mentions her in Epistle 8, to Demetrias, her daughter.] XV. OF POPE INNOCENT, TO JULIANA, A NOBLE LADY, EXHORTATORY.

[0518B]That the amplitude of thy religion stands forth a singular member of the Church, and is most reverently honoured by us, is sufficiently manifest to all. For on the very summit of nobility thou bestowest a far nobler devotion upon the Church, and, rejoicing the more in the knowledge of Christ, thou obeyest his precepts, and exultest in faith rather than boastest of the flower of so great a lineage. It is of the highest virtue to have overcome the glory of the flesh: and it is of the great grace of Christ [0519A] [nt: Baronius observes that this was said not without cause, but in order that by it, as by an antidote, the poison lurking in these words of Pelagius to Demetrias might be shaken out, words which so greatly displeased Alypius and Augustine: *Spiritual riches no one besides thyself will be able to confer on thee: in these thou art rightly to be praised, in these deservedly to be preferred to others, which cannot be except from thee and in thee.* Nor is the close of the letter less opposed to them, in which Innocent does not say *that thou mayest render thyself more illustrious through the ages to him to whom thou hast shown thyself distinguished*, but refers the whole of this to the gift of God. Certainly, if this letter preceded the aforesaid little work of Pelagius, it is not to be denied that Innocent, not without the nod of God, so aptly forestalled the mind of Juliana against his pestilent doctrine.], to have surpassed nobility by morals, lady daughter, deservedly illustrious. Being, then, secure, most beloved, strive to make illustrious with eternal and divine offices whatever spans of this life there are: that he who made thee distinguished may render thee more illustrious to himself through the ages.



## EPISTLE [nt: In the collection of Dionysius Exiguus this letter makes up Innocent's decree L. The title which the same Dionysius Exiguus prefixed to it is cited by Cresconius, cap. 58. The Spanish collection and the Isidorian also present it. Innocent testifies that he was stationed at Ravenna when he wrote it. And that he betook himself to that city at the beginning of about the year 409, and was stationed there in 410, when Rome was taken by Alaric, is related by Zosimus, *Hist.* book v, Sozomen, book ix, c. 7, and Orosius, book vii, c. 39. Hence some think it was sent within that interval. Others judge it much later, of 414, inasmuch as they reckon that the following letter, to Rufus, Marcianus and other bishops, is mentioned in its opening. And perhaps for that reason it was thrown back in the Roman edition to stand after the following one, although Dionysius places it before. But in the following letter the question is not of receiving the clerics of Naissus who had been ordained by Bonosus before his condemnation, but of repelling from the clergy those whom the same heretic had ordained after he was condemned. Wherefore nothing certain can be determined about the time at which this letter was written. Here, however, it is not ill placed, in imitation of Dionysius: not only because it is believed, with some probability, to be earlier than the one subjoined, but also because the order of the matter demands that we first set out what Innocent thinks of those ordained by Bonosus before his condemnation, and then of those ordained by the same man after his condemnation.] XVI. *On receiving the clerics whom Bonosus is known to have ordained before he was condemned.*

*INNOCENT TO MARCIANUS, BISHOP* [nt: So most manuscripts. But some, as in Merlin: *Naisitano*. The Roman edition, after Crabbe: *Naxitano*. Crabbe likewise wrote in the margin *Naresitano*, which was preferred in the later editions of the councils. In the pure Royal exemplar of the Dionysian collection this whole inscription is wanting.] *OF NAISSUS.*

At an earlier time, if indeed I rightly [nt: The manuscripts of the collections of Dionysius and Hadrian: *reor*.] remember, I recall that I sent letters of this kind, both to thy love and to our brethren and fellow bishops Rufus and the rest, concerning the clerics [nt: Merlin: *Naisensibus*. Crabbe and the Roman edition: *Naxensibus*. The later editions of the councils, from Crabbe's margin: *Naresiensibus*. The Navarre manuscript: *Naisitensibus*. The rest: *Naissensibus*. Those who will have it that Bonosus was bishop of Naissus rest on this passage, reckoning that clerics of Naissus could not have been ordained by him unless he had presided over the Church of Naissus. Their opinion would certainly not be one to reject, if it were established that this heretic had kept himself within the rules of ecclesiastical discipline. But, as is shown in the following letter, n. 12, *he made unlawful ordinations everywhere and without any examination.* Nothing therefore prevents us from holding to the authority of Marius Mercator, who calls Bonosus bishop of Sardica.] of Naissus, namely those who asserted that before the condemnation of Bonosus they had been ordained by him, as presbyters as well as deacons: that if, having abandoned and condemned his error, they wished [0519B] to be joined to the Church, they should be gladly received, lest perhaps those who were worthy of recovering salvation should perish in the same [nt: In the printed editions *errore* is added here, which is absent from the manuscripts and is rightly understood.]. But now, while I am stationed in the city of Ravenna because of the very frequent necessities of the Roman people, [nt: The Roman edition alone: *Germanus*. Merlin and Crabbe: *Germanum*, and next *Lupentinum*; where the more recent editions of the councils preferred *Lupentinus*, setting aside in the margin *Lupentius*, which is the reading of the manuscripts.] Germanio, who asserts that he is a presbyter, [0520A] and Lupentius, who says that he is a deacon, having taken up a kind of embassy on behalf of many such men, pouring out prayer, were eager to express their own griefs, [nt: The word *asserentes*, omitted in Crabbe and thereafter in the printed editions, is recalled from Merlin and the manuscripts. What the clerics ordained by Bonosus were then asserting, namely that Marcianus had allowed them to keep their churches on such terms that he denied them his communion, is illustrated by the acts of the first council of Toledo, Labbe vol. ii, p. 1251, where certain men departing from the sect of the Priscillianists are permitted their churches, and communion is not granted until it should have been restored to them by the Roman prelate. Moreover, these clerics who were permitted to keep their churches had not received a repeated ordination: and so the bishops of Macedonia were not persuaded that the Bonosians, if they were to be permitted to remain in the clergy, could be granted this only by a repeated ordination. Let this be observed in the meantime against the opinion of Paschasius Quesnel, of which we shall have to treat in the Monitum on the following letter.] asserting that they did indeed keep churches set in the parishes of thy love, but had not been able to merit thy communion, for this reason, namely, that a certain man, Rusticius by name, had received the presbyterate by a repeated ordination. And it becomes no light hindrance, while either they grieve that a man of this kind is retained in the Church, or he judges that others ought to be sinned against in the same way [nt: In our manuscripts, as in the printed editions, *peccare* stands: but what is joined after, *esse peccatum*, necessarily demands *peccari* here. Nor are those unaware who have any practice in wearing through old books how frequent is the change of i into e and back again. Next, the editions after Crabbe, as in the Royal manuscript, have *debere in alio*; where the other books have better *debere in alios*, supply: those ordained by Bonosus. Then the copies of the collections of Dionysius and Hadrian subjoin *arbitretur quem in se*, not *quemadmodum in se*: which is held more correctly in the manuscripts of the Spanish collection and of the Isidorian.] as he acknowledges that a sin was committed in his own case. And although my letters which I mentioned above contain more fully on the same matter, yet even now we have thought that thy fraternity should be admonished that, if it is truly established that some who were ordained by the same man before the condemnation of Bonosus [0520B] afterwards wished, or now wish, to return, we judge that they are to be received; especially since the men mentioned assert that they were ordained at so much earlier a time that the man of holy memory Cornelius, bishop of the city of Sirmium, and also our brother [nt: This Nicetas is thought to be the one called by Gennadius, *On Illustrious Men*, bishop of *Romatiana*; a city which the geographers place in inland Dacia, or in Upper Moesia, between Naissus and Sardica.] Nicetas, and some others were present, [0521A] when they were increased with the honours which they record that they hold [nt: The editions after Crabbe: *faterentur*; in the Royal manuscript *nihil agerentur*: corruptly. In the rest: *augerentur*. Innocent speaks of the ordained as *honoribus auctos*, "increased with honours," by that manner of speaking by which Hilary, *On the Trinity*, book x, n. 11, says that those who have received Christ are *in filios Dei aucti*, "increased into sons of God": because, clearly, as for those who come to be sons of God, so for those who are ordained there is no light accession of dignity.]. Wherefore, dearest brother, if their assertion obtains the credit of truth, thou oughtest not to doubt that those are to be received whom thou hast suffered so long to keep the churches entrusted to them.

## MONITUM ON THE FOLLOWING EPISTLE.



## 1.

In the Corbie exemplar, written about the middle of the sixth century, in the Colbert one written out in Lombardic letters, and also in the old copies of the Code published by Quesnel, and of the collections of Dionysius, Hadrian, the Spanish, Isidore, etc., this letter is presented. Dionysius divided it into seven chapters, which he placed among the decrees of Innocent in such a way that the first of these chapters is called by him Innocent's decree LI, and the last LVIII. In the Corbie codex, however, it is divided into twelve titles, which we have thought fit to set out at the head of the letter itself. A shorter title is prefixed to the same letter in the ancient copies of Quesnel's Code [0521B] in this manner: « *Rescript of Pope Innocent to the Macedonian bishops upon the inquiry concerning three titles.* I. That he who takes a widow to wife be not admitted to the clergy. II. That he who had a wife before baptism, if, she having died, he has married another after baptism, be held a digamist, and not to be admitted to the clergy. III. That those who, departing from the Catholic Church, receive ordination to the priesthood from heretics, be not reckoned until they wish to return. » And indeed it is chiefly of that threefold question that this letter treats. Looking to the first two, Innocent says, num. I: « Of which things I remember having written long since; now the redoubled inquiry of your report compels me to repeat the form with more evident arguments: » because, clearly, of the first he had written both in epist. 2, to Victricius, c. 5, and in epist. 3, to the synod of Toledo, c. 6, and in epist. 6, to Exsuperius, c. 1, and of the second in the cited letters to Victricius and to the synod of Toledo. Nor is it an obstacle that those letters are by no means general. For when [0521C] the Roman pontiffs wrote back to the questions of particular bishops or churches something that could be of use to other churches, they had commonly been accustomed to communicate it to them. But when Innocent comes to the third question, he so confesses that it demands a longer disputation for its difficulty as to indicate plainly enough that it was new and not yet handled by him.

## 2.

That new question arose on the occasion of those whom Bonosus had ordained after his condemnation. For concerning those who had been ordained by the same man before his condemnation there was no difficulty. These, indeed, the synod of Capua had judged were to be received in their honours. Approving that judgment, Innocent had written to Rufus himself and the other Macedonians by name concerning the clerics of Naissus, « who asserted that before the condemnation of Bonosus they had been ordained by him, as presbyters as well as deacons, » « that if, having abandoned and condemned his error, they wished to be joined to the Church, they should be gladly received » [n: (Epist. 17, 1)]. So the dispute was only about those [0521D] clerics whom Bonosus, already condemned, had ordained. When that question had been carried to Innocent, he observes that a distinction must be drawn between those who, baptized in heresy, come to the Church, and those who go from the Catholic Church to heresy, and thence return to the very Catholic Church from which they had gone out. For the former were at once joined to the faithful by the imposition of the hand alone. But the others were admitted to communion only after a long penance. « Whence, he says [n: (Num. 11)], it is established that those who passed over from the Catholic Church to the condemned Bonosus, and suffered or desired to be ordained by him, ought not to have received the dignity of ecclesiastical ordination: » since indeed [0522A] « not even after penance do the canons themselves by their authority permit one to become a cleric. » Wherefore he remonstrates with the Macedonians [n: (Num. 8)] that « those who passed over from the Catholic Church to heresy not only do not do penance among them, but are even heaped with honour ( *of the clerical state* ). » But concerning the others Innocent notes that « there were ancient rules, handed down by the Apostles or apostolic men, which the Roman church guards and commands to be guarded by those who have heard her, » by which it is prescribed [n: (Ibid.)] « to grant to those coming from the heretics, who were baptized there, only lay communion through the imposition of the hand, and not to put any of them in the place of even the least honour of the clerical state. »

## 3.

But he readily confesses that the necessity of the Church sometimes counselled a departure from this last law. For this cause the Nicene synod had judged that the Novatians were to be received in their honours. Such afterwards was the judgment of Anysius and his associates concerning those who had been ordained by Bonosus before his condemnation. To those also who had received ordination from the followers of Priscillian, [0522B] in the year 400, the first council of Toledo granted the same kind of favour. And Innocent indeed not only approves the statutes of the Nicene synod towards the Navatians, or of Anisius and his associates towards those who had adhered to Bonosius, but also vehemently rebukes and refutes those who were displeased by the indulgence of the council of Toledo towards the followers of Priscillian [n: (Epist. 3, c. 1)]. Yet the pontiff, most loving of equity, so admits and approves those exceptions as to contend sharply that, where a necessity of this kind is absent, one must hold to the general rules of the Church. While he strives to persuade of this, he brings forward the Nicene canon 8 in this manner: « Concerning those who name themselves Cathari, that is, the pure, and at any time come to the catholic Church, it pleased the holy and great synod that, having received the imposition of the hand, they remain so in the clergy. » Whence a new question arises about the genuine understanding of this canon, which it will be helpful to explain here.



## 4.

In no other words does the ancient Code of the canons, [0522C] which Paschasius Quesnel will have to have been in the use of the Roman church, report that canon, in cap. 22, in this letter of Innocent, although in chapter 1 of the same Code, where the Nicene canons are presented together, it is expressed thus: « Concerning those who call themselves Cathari, that is, the pure, if any come to the catholic Church, it pleased the holy council that they be ordained, and so remain in the clergy. » Now since this translation is proper to that code, it ought to have been common to those who used that Code, and so to Innocent himself, if, as is alleged, the aforesaid Code was in the use of the Roman Church in his age. Yet in the translation which Innocent follows, while the order of the words is different, the greatest difference certainly lies in this, that where the Code mentioned presents *that they be ordained,* he gives *that, having received the imposition of the hand.* Whence it follows that this pope used a translator of the Nicene council who had read in can. 8 ὅστε χειροθετουμένους, not Quesnel's Code, in which is inserted the translation of a man who, in place of χειροθετουμένους, had come upon χειροτονουμένους. Feeling that this goes no little against his opinion [0522D] of that Code, the man cited, dissert. 12, cap. 7, devised this cause of the aforesaid difference: « that Pope Innocent, since he was answering the objection of the Macedonians, wished to write out or translate word for word the canon which had been objected, from their letters, » not from his own Code. But in answering thus he is convicted of not having weighed Innocent's words sufficiently. For that pope, having first said by way of preface: « First that canon instituted by the fathers must be set down, so that we may be able to observe, etc., » declares plainly enough that he is setting forth this canon first, not copying or translating it from the letters of others. On the other hand [0523A] it is known that in his own copy of the Nicene council he read in can. 8 χειροθετουμένους, not χειροτονουμένους, and that in the same copy the word χειροθετουμένους had been turned into Latin not by the word *of ordination,* but by that *of the imposition of the hand.* For, expressing this Nicene canon summarily in epist. 2, n. 11, after Siricius, epist. 4, c. 8, he prescribes « that those coming from the Novatians or Montenses be received through the imposition of the hand. » Nor does Siricius confirm this only in the place just cited, but also in epist. 1, n. 2, where he answers Himerius, who was consulting whether those coming from the Arian heresy were to be baptized anew: « Whom we, with the *Novatians* and other heretics, as has been established *in the synod,* join to the assembly of catholics by the invocation of the sevenfold Spirit alone, by the episcopal *imposition of the hand.* » For no prudent man will deny that there by the name of synod the Nicene is understood, and its canon 8 is recalled. It is manifest, therefore, that in this canon Siricius and Innocent read *that, having received the imposition of the hand,* not *that they be ordained.* [0523B] Leo also, treading in their footsteps, in epist. 2, answers the inquiry of Rusticus, 18, in like manner: « Through the *imposition of the hand,* the power of the Holy Spirit having been invoked, which they could not receive from the heretics, they are to be joined to the Catholics. » With these agrees the Corbie codex also, written out in the middle of the sixth century, in which the Nicene canon is reported thus: « Concerning those who surname themselves Cathari, that is, the pure, if at any time they come to the catholic Church, it pleased the holy and great council that, *receiving the imposition of the hand,* they remain so in the clergy. » But in what way the imposition of the hand there required is to be understood, it is of no light moment to explain.

## 5.

Here the man cited, in the aforesaid Dissertation 12, c. 7, lays down two things as certain which it would have been worth the trouble to prove. For first he says: « The Macedonians freely confessed that ordination was signified there [n: (Nicaen. can. 8, manus impositione)]. » And then he adds that on this the Macedonians and Innocent were agreed, [0523C] that if the Bonosians were to be permitted to remain in the clergy, this was to be granted them only *by a repeated ordination.* As regards the Macedonians, if from what one man did, although his deed is disapproved by others, one may conjecture what a whole province or region thinks, it must be granted that they were not far from the opinion which the learned man attributes to them. For in the preceding letter we saw that a certain Rusticius, returning from the fellowship of Bonosus to the catholic Church, « had received the presbyterate by a repeated ordination; » and that this was the greatest hindrance to others who desired to return, « while either they grieve that a man of this kind is retained in the Church, or he judges that others ought to be sinned against in the same way as he acknowledges that a sin was committed in his own case. » But who would wish it to be judged, from the singular sin of one man, which is a horror to the rest, what has been received in the usage of a whole province? On the contrary, from the fact that the Macedonians had permitted Germanio, Lupentius and many others to keep their churches without a repeated ordination, [0523D] it is clear that they were not of that opinion, that those ordained by Bonosus might not remain in the clergy except by a repeated ordination. Innocent also, in the words adduced concerning Rusticius, who suffered himself to be ordained again, signifies that he thinks together with the Macedonian clerics on this, namely that he had committed a sin, and indeed a sin of such a kind as does not permit the same man to be retained in the Church or reckoned among the ecclesiastics. Far, therefore, both the same pope and the Macedonian clerics and the bishops of the same province stood from that opinion, that if the Bonosians were permitted to remain in the clergy, this was to be furnished them by a repeated ordination. But let us take the matter up from further back.

## 6.

In the Church that law has always obtained, that those coming from heresy to the Church, whether clerics or laymen, were received through the imposition of the hand. That this custom had prevailed before the Nicene synod, nay, before [0524A] the times of Pope Stephen and of Cyprian, we have proved above, p. 240 and following, by the testimonies both of Stephen himself and of the councils of Africa under Cyprian, and also of Eusebius and others. But it is plain from the same testimonies that the hand was then imposed on them *unto penance,* not that they might be ordained; and anyone who contended that laymen were ordained by this imposition would offer himself to be laughed at by all. Who, therefore, would assent that the Nicene fathers spoke of the imposition of the hand in another sense, where they decree concerning the Novatians: « It pleased the holy and great synod that, having received the imposition of the hand, they remain so in the clergy? » For us, lest we should think they spoke in another sense, the authority is Siricius, who indeed, in the place already cited, epist. 1, to Himer., c. 1, testifies that the Arians and other heretics are joined at Rome to the assembly of catholics by that very imposition of the hand by which the Nicene synod prescribed that the Novatians were to be received, and that this is guarded by the whole East and West together. Whence it follows that what is prescribed in the Nicene canon concerning receiving [0524B] the imposition of the hand, the whole East and West, tradition persuading them, understood to have been commanded concerning all heretics; but that what is added there, *and so let them remain in the clergy,* was granted to the Novatians alone, Innocent himself being the interpreter. And if towards the Arians or other heretics that imposition of the hand was not a repeated ordination, then neither was it towards the Novatians. Besides this, Innocent below [n: (Num. 8)], having professed that he adheres to the law of his church, which willed that the hand be imposed on those coming from heresy, openly teaches that by this imposition ordination is by no means conferred, since he affirms at the same time that only lay communion is granted through it [n: (Num. 9)]. When, therefore, he says, « This was the chief deliberation of Anysius, our brother of former days, and of other fellow priests, that those whom Bonosus had ordained, lest they should remain with him and no mean scandal should arise, should be received *as ordained,* » he does not understand that *ordained* as though they were to be ordained again before they were received, but [0524C] because those whom Bonosus had ordained were to be held as ordained, and received in the grade and order which they had received.



## 7.

But, as the same pope warns beforehand [n: (Ibid.)], one thing is to be distinguished as the *lawful order,* another as *usurpation.* Nor should we undeservedly call, with him, *guilty of a usurped dignity* those whom Bonosus, when condemned, had ordained [n: (Num. 12)]: because *they thought themselves to be that* which *had been granted them by no regular reckoning.* And although what had been given remained in them, yet because it remained, to speak with Augustine [n: (Lib. II, cont. Epist. Parmen. n. 29)], *to the punishment of the usurper,* both *of him who unlawfully gave* and *of him who unlawfully received;* they are truly to be said to have received nothing, inasmuch as they could not lawfully use the power made for them against the rules. « For the rest, says the same Augustine [n: (Num. 30)], concerning those who are separated from the unity of the Church, there is now no question but that they both have and can give, and that they have perniciously and hand on perniciously (baptism and ordination) [0524D] outside the bond of peace. For each, » he says a little above [n: (Num. 28)], « is a sacrament, and each is given to a man by a certain consecration; the one when he is baptized, the other when he is ordained: and therefore *in the Catholic Church neither may be repeated.* » And a few things being interposed. « But just as they have in baptism what can be given through them, so in ordination the right of giving; each indeed to their own destruction, as long as they have not the charity of unity: but it is one thing not to have, another to have perniciously, another to have salutarily. » And these things are of so much the greater weight for illustrating and proving Innocent's opinion, because, when Augustine set them out about the year 400, and so before Innocent wrote to the Macedonians, he asserts that he is defending not what he himself privately thought, but « what has been discussed and considered, perfected and defined in the very unity of the whole world » [n: (Ibid.)]. Certainly he would not have been going to say « this was defined in [0525A] the very unity of the whole world » if the agreement of the Roman Church in the same doctrine had been lacking. For how scrupulously the Africans, especially at that time and concerning questions of this kind, took care not to dissent from the decrees of this Church, their embassies and letters directed to Siricius, to Anastasius and to Innocent himself are witnesses. But if we weigh where that definition which Augustine cites was published, there will occur first of all what the Africans write, Cod. can. Eccl. Afr., c. 48: « But we suggest this which was commanded us, which is also seen to have been established in the plenary synod of Capua, that rebaptizings and reordinations may not take place. » Nor can anyone deny that in so celebrated a council nothing was done except according to the mind of Siricius, certainly nothing that he himself did not approve.
