Although, therefore, the learned man already mentioned more than once most recently asserts [n: (Dissert. 12 in Leon. c. 7)]: « Nor was it a question (between the Macedonians [sic: *Macedodonas*] and Innocent) whether the Bonosians ought to be ordained again, so that they might remain in that order of the clergy [0525B] which they had obtained under Bonosus, on that both were agreed; but whether they were to be permitted to remain in the clergy even *by a repeated ordination;* » one may rightly appeal from his opinion to that of Augustine [n: (Lib. II, cont. Epist. Parmen. n. 28)]: « Concerning those who are separated from the unity of the Church, there is now no question but that they both have and can give (baptism and ordination). And therefore in the Catholic Church neither may be repeated. » Whence, he says, when « it seemed necessary that (those coming from the party of Donatus) should perform the same offices which they were performing, they were not ordained again: but as baptism in them, so ordination remained whole. »

## 8.

If we go even to the very source, that is, to the Greek text of the Nicene council, it will become manifest to us that the fathers of this council were unwilling that ordination be repeated except for those whose baptism they judged null, and therefore to be repeated. Certainly, since in can. 8 they grant that the Novatians, whose baptism they admitted, χειροθετουμένους, [0525C] that is, *having received the imposition of the hand,* remain in the clergy; in can. 19 they are unwilling that the clerics of the Paulianists, whose baptism they rejected, be admitted into the clergy, unless « *they had first been baptized and ordained* ( ἀναβαπτισθέντες χειροτονείσθωσαν ) *by a bishop of the catholic Church.* » For not without cause do they not use the same word in those two places: but they admit to the clergy the first χειροθετουμένους, which sounds simply *to receive the imposition of the hand,* the others χειροτονουμένους, which properly sounds *to receive ordination.* Whence it is clear also that the translation of the Nicene canon which Innocent uses in this letter is more apt and more congruous than that which obtains in Quesnel's Code. Perhaps too the craftsman of the latter, since in can. 19, on the Paulianists, he came upon χειροτονείσθωσαν said, was led thence to judge that in can. 8, on the Novatians, one should read in Greek [sic: χειρονουμένους] and in Latin *that they be ordained.* And from a faulty translation of this kind chiefly that opinion seems to have been born which holds that clerics coming from heresy to the Church were received into the clergy only by a repeated ordination. [0525D] But even if there have from time to time been some who doubted whether sacraments were efficaciously conferred by condemned bishops, yet never was the mind of the Church wavering or doubtful in that matter. At the close of the fifth century, when it was the opinion of certain men that, « the judgment having been pronounced by Pope Felix [n: (II adversus Acacium)], he had afterwards acted inefficaciously in the sacraments which Acacius usurped, » Pope Anastasius answered, epist. I, c. 8: [0526A] « Since the condemned man claimed for himself the name of priest, the swelling of pride was inflicted upon his own head; because the people, who in the mysteries thirsted for his gift, was not shut out, but that soul alone which had sinned was, by just judgment, liable for its own fault. » Which is truly said of him who seeks the sacraments with a simple soul, not of him who abuses the occasion of schism to serve his own ambition. In the ninth century a like controversy arose concerning the clerics ordained by Ebbo after he had been cast down from the episcopate. But the third council of Soissons, in the year 866, decreed concerning them that « they should be received in their grades, according to the indulgence of the Nicene synod concerning those whom Meletius, when condemned, ordained, and according to the tradition of the African council » towards the Donatists. See also the second council of Nicaea, Labbe vol. VII, from p. 66 to 78. Thus the Nicene fathers judged that the Meletians and Novatians were to be received in their honours, the Africans certain of the Donatists, the synod of Capua and Innocent those ordained by Bonosus before his condemnation, Anastasius II those [0526B] whom Acacius, when condemned, ordained. Besides, Gregory I, in the epistle formerly 61, in the month of July, Indiction IV, to Quiricus, judged that even the Nestorians likewise were to be received in their honours. Other like judgments and decrees we pass over in silence.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 22. And what was 17 is now 19.] XVII. From Dionysius Exiguus.



## I.

*That if a priest or anyone of the clergy has married a widow, or a woman put away, he lose his office.* -- II. *That if anyone as a catechumen had a wife, and, she having died, has after baptism obtained another, he be not a cleric.* --III. *That those ordained by heretics be not received in the clergy.* --IV. *That in ordinations crimes or vices are not rightly believed to be taken away.* [0526C] --V. *That those who were ordained by the heretic Bonosus were received for this reason, that scandal might not remain for the Church.* --VI. *That in the Church the sin of a people is wont to be passed over unpunished.* --VII. *That the apostolic see had been taken by surprise, and has changed its sentence for the better.*

*From the ancient Corbie codex.*

## I.

*Of those who are alleged to have taken two wives.* --II. *That he who as a catechumen had and lost a wife ought not to be called a digamist.* --III. *In what way crimes are remitted in baptism.* --IV. *Of sons who were born before baptism.* --V. *Of catechumens who have applied themselves to holy virtues.* --VI. *Of heretical clerics, in what way they ought to be reconciled.* --VII. *That the sacrilegious [0526D] and adulterers ought not to be admitted to the clergy.* --VIII. *And in what way heretics are received.* --IX. *Of those who call themselves cathari and pure.* --X. *Of those returning from the Novatians and heretics.* --XI. *Of catholic clerics who pass over to heresy.* --XII. *Of the reception of Fontinus and Eustasius.*

INNOCENT [nt: This inscription is offered thus in the old Corbie manuscript: *Innocentius Eustasio Eugenio, Policronio, Hilario, Zosimo, Hermogeni, Thorentiano, Rufo, Claudio, Gerontio, Suffronio, Macedonio, Profuturo, Vincentio, Herodiano, Eusebio, Maximiano, Joanni, Flaviano, Calecrati, Nicitae, Aciologo, Martiano, episcopis Macedonibus et diaconibus in Deo salutem.* The other Colbert manuscript agrees with the same codex. Whence it is clear that from the order which the bishops hold in inscriptions of this kind no firm argument can be made by which their dignity or seniority may be proved. Dionysius Exiguus, content to name two of those bishops, abridges the whole inscription in this manner: *Innocentius Rufo, Eusebio et caeteris episcopis Macedonibus et diaconibus in Domino salutem:* and afterwards the author of the Spanish collection and Isidore imitated him.] TO RUFUS, EUSEBIUS, EUSTATHIUS, CLAUDIUS, MAXIMIANUS, EUGENIUS, GERONTIUS, JOHN; POLYCHRONIUS, SOPHRONIUS, FLAVIANUS, HILARY, MACEDONIUS, CALICRATIUS, ZOSIMUS, PROFUTURUS, NICETAS, [ *Quidam mss.* NICETIO], HERMOGENES, VINCENT, ASIOLOGUS, TERENTIANUS, HERODIANUS and MARCIANUS, [nt: In the Roman edition and in the Councils after Crabbe, *et caeteris* is added here: which is absent from all the manuscripts in which the names of the bishops are called one by one, and is without doubt taken over from those in which, as we observed just now, only two bishops being named, the rest are passed over in silence. And of those named above, Chrysostom, writing from exile in epistle 165 to the Macedonian bishops, likewise names *Eustathium, Eusebium, Maximianum, Eugenium et Gerontium* in that inscription. Here the question arises whether there were in Macedonia as many bishops as are now enumerated. For the name of Marcianus, who is established to have been a bishop, set in the last place, does not permit us to think that some deacons were named above together with the bishops. Perhaps Innocent, when he had mentioned indiscriminately the bishops whom he knew from Macedonia and other provinces of Illyricum, added *episcopis Macedonibus et diaconis*, to signify that his letter, although inscribed also to the bishops of the neighbouring provinces, concerned the Macedonians first of all: just as earlier the synod of Capua, as Siricius witnesses, ep. 9, n. 1, had decreed that the bordering and neighbouring bishops of Bonosus, *chiefly the Macedonians*, should take cognizance of his case.] to the Macedonian bishops and deacons, greeting in the Lord. [0527A]



## 1.

Great rejoicing took hold of me when, after so many perils of the whole world, so to speak, Vitalis the archdeacon, the bearer of your letters, sent from those parts, arrived all the way to us. When we had seen him, we at once, as was fitting, inquired about your state. But when [nt: The Corbie manuscript with one Colbert manuscript: *reperiremus*.] we found that you were living as we wished, we returned abundant thanks to our God, who protects his servants and those who serve his altars both in [0527B] adversity, and deigns to govern them in prosperity. When he had delivered the letters, I ordered them to be gone through at once: in which I perceived many things set down which might bring [nt: The same manuscripts: *nostris ut inducerent*. The others, with Merlin: *nostri inducerent*. *Injicerent* would not displease.] astonishment upon our minds, and make us doubt not a little whether we were thinking otherwise, or those things were set down just as they sounded. When I had had them read over again several times, I noticed that [nt: From the better manuscripts the word *aliquam* is recalled here, which was formerly wanting. Innocent had already forbidden, in epist. 2, n. 7 and 8, and also in epist. 3, n. 11, that anyone be admitted to the clergy who had married either a widow or a second wife, even if he had taken the first before baptism. It is probable, moreover, that after the example of Siricius he took care that letters and decrees of this kind should travel through the provinces, as he himself speaks in epist. 1, n. 2, lest any of them should plead ignorance. Certainly that about which he testifies that he had written before falls under the two aforesaid heads, not under the other two, which are treated next.] some injury was being done to the apostolic see, to which the report sent was running as to the head of the churches, whose sentence was still being drawn into doubt. Hence, concerning matters on which I remember having written long since, the redoubled inquiry [nt: In the manuscripts of the collections of Dionysius, Hadrian and the Spanish, *relationis vestrae* is wanting. In the Colbert Lombardic codex *relationis* stands, and in the other Colbert and the old Corbie *relegationis*, without *vestrae* added. Then all the other manuscripts subjoin *geminata percontatio vestra*. Merlin printed *gemina percontatio vestra*. The old copies of Quesnel's Code preserve the common reading.] of your report compels me now to repeat the form with more evident arguments.

## CAP. I.



## 2.

Those [n: (Ivo p. 8, c. 503)] who are alleged to have taken widows [0528A] as wives I have learned not only became clerics, but even reached the fillets of the highest priesthood: which no one is unaware is against the precepts of the law. For since Moses the lawgiver cries out: *Let the priest take a virgin to wife [n: (Lev. XXII, 13, 14)]*; and, lest anything in this precept be thought ambiguous, he added, *not a widow, nor one put away.* Against this precept, set under [nt: So the better manuscripts. But other books: *subnixum*. Pithou: *subnexum*.] divine authority, no defence of any other command is opposed except your custom: which, as you yourselves confess, was established from ignorance, to speak more modestly, not from apostolic tradition and sound reason. But let your love know that we, and all the churches through East and West, by no means admit these things; and do not take up such men into even the last place of the ecclesiastical order [nt: Agreeing with the first part of this decree, the first council of Valence, in the year 374, can. 1, decreed that henceforth no one *of the digamists or of the husbands of women married twice could be ordained a cleric*, whether they had fallen into digamy before baptism or after. Yet it does not dare to remove those who had been ordained up to that time. From that severity of discipline the Gallican councils, the first of Orange, can. 25, and the second of Arles, can. 45, later relaxed somewhat, thinking it enough to forbid that men of this kind be promoted beyond the subdiaconate. Nor were the Spaniards, the Moors or the Italians more observant of the apostolic rule in this matter, as are witness the first epistle of Siricius, to Himerius of Tarragona, n. 12, Leo's epistle 1, to the Moors, n. 3 and 5, and his epistle 4, to the bishops of Campania, Picenum and Tuscia, n. 3.] [0528B], and, if they are found, remove them.

## CAP. II.



## 3.

Next it is set down that one ought not [n: (Dist. 26, c. 3; Ivo, p. 1, c. 292, et p. 8, c. 303)] to be called a digamist who as a catechumen had and lost a wife, if after baptism he has obtained another; and that she is to be regarded as the first who was joined to the new man: because that marriage was washed away through the sacrament of baptism together with the other crimes. And since this is said of one, certainly if, while placed in the old man, he had three wives, she who is fourth after baptism will be, for those who interpret thus, the first: [nt: Quesnel: *primae virginis nomen*, against the credit of the manuscripts and the mind of Innocent. In the other editions: *prima virginis aeque nomen*. In several manuscripts: *virgini aeque nomen*.] and she who was married in the fourth place will receive the name of a virgin. Who, I pray, would not see that this is against the precept of the Apostle, who says *(I Tim. III, 2; [0529A] Tit. I, 6) that a man of one wife* ought to be made priest?

## 4.

But it is objected that in baptism everything whatever that was done in the old man is loosed. Tell me, I speak with your leave: are only crimes remitted in baptism, or also those things which are fulfilled according to [nt: So the manuscripts of better note. But Merlin, Crabbe and the Roman edition, with the manuscripts of the collections of Dionysius and the Isidorian: *Domini praecepta*. Our reading is confirmed by that with which number 5 closes: *quod Lex praecepit, et Deus junxit*.] the precepts of the Law and the institutions of God? To take a wife is a crime, or is not a crime. If it is a crime, then, begging pardon beforehand, I would say, the author will be at fault who, when he himself joined them thus in paradise, blessed them to commit crimes. But if it is not a crime, because what God joined [n: (Gen. I, 28)] it is impious to call a crime; and Solomon added: *For a wife is prepared for a man by God [n: (Prov. XIX, 14)]* [var: Vulg. *a Domino autem proprie uxor prudens*, "but a prudent wife is properly from the Lord"]: how is that believed to have been remitted among the crimes which is read to have been accomplished with God as its author? [0529B]

## 5.

What is reckoned of the sons of such men [n: (28, q. I, c. 3)]? Will those not be admitted to the fellowship of the inheritance who were begotten of her who was wife before baptism? And are they to be called either natural or spurious, because there is no lawful marriage, as it seems to you, except that which is taken up [nt: The manuscripts of Dionysius and some others: *assumetur*.] after baptism? The Lord himself, when he was asked by the Jews whether it was lawful to put away a wife, and was explaining that it ought not to be done, added: *What therefore God has joined, let no man put asunder [n: (Matth. XIX, 6)]*. And lest he be believed to have spoken of those who [nt: So the manuscripts, with great agreement. But Crabbe and the Roman edition: *qui*. Further, the verb *sortiuntur* is to be understood passively, just as if *accipiuntur* stood there.] are obtained after baptism, let them remember that this was both asked by Jews and answered to Jews. I ask, and I ask anxiously, if the wife of him who before was a catechumen and afterwards is one of the faithful is one and the same, and he begot sons of her when he was a catechumen, [0529C] and afterwards others when one of the faithful: whether they are to be called brothers, or whether they are afterwards, the father having died, not to have the fellowship of dividing the inheritance, from whom spiritual regeneration is believed to have taken away the name of sons? Since it is absurd to think and judge so, what reason is there (a plague on it) for this to be defended, and to be tossed about by an empty opinion rather than strengthened by any authority, [0530A] since what the Law commanded, and God [nt: Several manuscripts: *jungit*. One of the Colbert: *indulget*; Pithou and Quesnel: *injungit*. It is plain that the words of Christ, *Quod Deus junxit*, etc., are looked to here. But what is subjoined after the words *Deus junxit*, in Merlin *et lex junxit*, in Crabbe *et lex jungit*, rests on no authority.] joined, cannot be reckoned among sins?

## 6.

If a catechumen has applied himself to the virtues, has followed humility, has held to patience, has done almsgiving, has by whatever means rescued those appointed to death, has shuddered at adulteries, has held to chastity; I ask whether, when he has been made one of the faithful, he loses these things, because through baptism all that the old man did is thought to be taken away? Let us look at the gentile man Cornelius, who in his prayers [nt: One of the manuscripts, a Colbert: *orationibus deditum atque eleemosynis per revelationem Petrum evocasse, ipsumque vidisse*. From it Quesnel snatched *orationibus deditum*, and then from Crabbe and the other editions after him subjoined *et eleemosynis vacantem per revelationem Angelum Petrumque*. Merlin had earlier printed *orationibus et eleemosynis per revelationem Angelum Petrumque*. Our reading is that of all the manuscripts, with the one Colbert named above excepted.] and almsgivings saw a revelation, and Peter himself: were these things taken away [nt: Six manuscripts of good note: *abluta*: which can be understood thus, that as stains are wont to vanish when washed away, so the merits of prayers and almsgivings, washed away, are judged to have perished. In which sense we heard above, n. 3, *conjugium per baptismi sacramentum cum caeteris criminibus ABLUTUM*. But if *abluta* is not foreign to Innocent's mind, what we read just before, *quia per baptismum, totum quod vetus homo gesserit putatur AUFERRI*, persuades that the word *ablata* is no more alien to it.] from him through baptism, on account of which baptism is seen to have been granted to him? If it is so believed, believe me, there is no small error: because whatever has been well done, and kept according to the precepts of the law, cannot [nt: The editions after Dionysius and the Isidorian: *non potest facientibus deperire. Nuptiarum ergo copula quia Dei mandato perficitur, non potest*: they are corrected by the authority of the manuscripts, which are wont to be more sincere.] perish for the doer; and that which, [0530B] because it is brought about by God's command, cannot be called sin: and what is not sin ought by no means to be loosed among sins [nt: Crabbe added *credi* here: a word the following editors seized on, although it is absent from the manuscripts.]. And it will be sound to judge that the name of the former wife cannot be abolished, since it was not remitted as a sin, because it was accomplished by the will of God.

## CAP. III.



## 7.

[n: (I, q. I, c. 17; Ivo p. 6, c. 59.)] We have come to the third question, which for its difficulty demands a longer disputation: since we say that those ordained by heretics have a head wounded through that imposition of the hand. [And where a wound has been inflicted, medicine must be applied, that it may be able to receive health. Which health, following after a wound, cannot be without a scar:] and where the remedy of penance is necessary, there the honour of ordination [0530C] cannot have place [nt: The word *decernimus*, which was added here in the Roman edition and the later editions of the councils, we have not met in even one written codex, nor in Merlin, Crabbe, etc. At the opening of this disputation it will be helpful to have observed the difference which lies between ordination and the honour of ordination. And indeed Innocent does not deny that ordination can be conferred by heretics, but contends that *the honour of the ordination* conferred by them *cannot have place*. Nay, he himself asserts at length in the preceding epistle 5 that for an ordination of this kind, where the utility of the Church demands it, its honour can be preserved.] [n: (Num. XIX, 22)]. For if, as is read, what the unclean man has touched shall be unclean: how shall that be granted to him which cleanness and purity have been wont to receive? But on the other hand it is asserted that he who has lost honour cannot give honour: nor has that man received anything, because there was nothing in the giver [nt: Two manuscripts: *in dante remanserat*. In imitation of this place, those who in the Capitulary of the year 803, c. 4, in Baluze p. 381, made definitions against the episcopal functions of the chorepiscopi, expressed their own words: *that no one had anywhere received anything of the episcopal ministry from the chorepiscopi, because there was nothing in the giver which any of them could have received; since what none of them had, he could not give*. But we should not wish Innocent's words to be explained to the sense of this Capitulary. By his argument only this is made out: that by one who lacked the lawful power of ordaining, a lawful ordination could not have been conferred.] which he might receive. We assent, and it is [0531A] true. Certainly, because what he did not have he could not give, he gave at any rate the condemnation which he had, through the depraved imposition of the hand: and [nt: So the old Corbie codex and the other Colbert, with two others favouring, in which *comparticeps factus est* is likewise read, not to mention Pithou, in which *factus est* remains. But other books: *qui particeps effectus est damnato*. Long before, according to Innocent's mind, the council of Laodicea, can. 32, had decreed: *One ought not to receive the blessings of heretics, since these are curses rather than blessings.* Further, he who is made a partaker of condemnation is not without share in reproach; and therefore he is judged by the apostolic sentence unworthy of the honour of ordination.] how he who has been made a fellow partaker of condemnation ought to receive honour, I cannot find.

## CAP. IV.



## 8.

But it is said that the true and just blessing of a lawful priest takes away every fault which had been put in by the faulty one. Then, if it is so, let the sacrilegious, adulterers and those guilty of all crimes be brought forward to ordination, since through the blessing of ordination crimes or faults [nt: In two manuscripts: *possunt auferri*.] are thought to be taken away. Let there be no place for penance, since ordination can furnish what a long satisfaction has been wont to furnish. But [nt: A council of Italy, recalled in the Code of the canons of the African church, cap. 56, had decreed that law, or rather had confirmed it as handed down by the Apostles or apostolic men. There the Africans, although they acknowledge the equity of this law, yet decree an embassy to Anastasius, Innocent's predecessor, to ask that something of its severity be relaxed for them, because of the shortage of clerics with which their churches were labouring. Complying with the same law, Augustine, in the epistle formerly 73, now 245, n. 2, wrote to Possidius: *As to ordaining one who was baptized in the party of Donatus, I cannot be your authority:* although he at once adds: *For it is one thing to do it if you are compelled, another to counsel that you do it.* By all means to be read is the letter of the Africans to John II, epistle viii, in which they ask in what way the priests of the Arians ought to be received into the catholic faith, whether in their honours or in lay communion. And presently they add: *From the silent movements of all our colleagues we have perceived that it pleases no one that they be received in their honours.* And they add also below: *We consult not only about the priests, but also about the very little ones baptized among them, whether they are wont or ought to be brought forward to the clerical state, if they have asked.* The opinion of Cyprian on that matter, in epist. 72, to Stephen, is this: *It is enough that pardon be given to such men returning; but faithlessness ought not to be promoted in the house of faith.*] IT IS THE LAW OF OUR CHURCH, for those coming from the heretics who were nevertheless baptized there, to grant through the imposition of the hand [0531B] only lay communion, and not to put any of them in the place of even the least honour of the clerical state. But those who [nt: The editions, with only the manuscripts of the Spanish collection and the Isidorian: *a catholica fide*. More truly other manuscripts: *a Catholica*; supply *Ecclesia*. So also below, n. 11: *De Catholica ad haeresim transiens*; likewise, *De Catholica ad Bonosum transierunt*. And this manner of speaking is familiar to other writers too, and by name to Augustine.] passed over from the Catholic Church to heresy, whom it is fitting to receive in no other way than through penance, among you not only do not do penance, but are even heaped with honour.

## CAP. V.



## 9.

But [nt: One of the Colbert manuscripts with the old Corbie: *Sed ante duodecim fratres quondam nostri*: corruptly. From the epistle of Siricius, 9, n. 1, we had already learned that the synod of Capua delegated the hearing of the case of Bonosus to Anysius and other bishops of Illyricum, and especially to the Macedonians; now we are taught what their judgment on this case was; and while Innocent approves it, he shows that he is far from thinking the ordination of heretics void and altogether null.] this was the chief deliberation of Anysius, our brother of former days, and of other fellow priests: that those whom Bonosus had ordained, lest they should remain with him and no mean scandal should arise, should be received as ordained. [nt: The same manuscripts: *Vicensimus*: faultily. Nor better another Colbert: *Vicibus, ut opinor, ambiguis*. Further, it was in doubt whether the honour of ordination was always to be kept for those ordained by a heretic, or always to be denied. Innocent resolves and overcomes this doubt, when he answers that it is to be denied them in such a way that, when necessity demands or grave scandal is feared, he judges it is to be kept for them; and he praises the judgment of Anysius and his associates, by which, Bonosus being set apart, they allowed those ordained by him their honours, lest they should remain with him.] We have overcome, as I think, the doubtful points. [0532A] Now therefore [n: (I, q. 7, c. 7; Ivo in prologo)] it is established that what was decreed as a remedy and for the necessity of the time did not exist originally, and that there were ancient rules, handed down by the Apostles or apostolic men, which the Roman church guards and commands to be guarded by those who have been accustomed to hear her. But the necessity of the time greatly demanded that this be done. Therefore [n: (I, q. I, c. 41; Ivo in prologo)] what necessity found as a remedy, when the necessity ceases, what was pressing ought certainly to cease likewise: because the lawful order is one thing, usurpation another, which time drives to be done for the present.



## 10.

But the canons established at Nicaea permitted this to be done concerning the Novatians. First that canon instituted by the fathers must be set down, so that we may be able to observe either what or in what manner it was meant or commanded by them. [0532B] Concerning those, it says, who name themselves Cathari, that is, the pure, and at any time come to the catholic Church, it pleased the holy and great synod that, having received the imposition of the hand, they remain so in the clergy [n: (Nic. syn. c. 8)]. But I can say that this was commanded of the Novatians alone, and does not pertain to the clerics of other heresies. For if indeed they were defining so concerning all, they would have added that those returning from the Novatians and other heretics ought to be received [nt: The editions: *in suum ordinem*. Next Quesnel: *suscipi*; *quod ita non esse*, the negative particle being added against the credit of the manuscripts and the mind of Innocent: who indeed, having first said by way of preface, *Possum dicere de solis hoc Novatianis esse praeceptum*, then, being about to prove that he can say this in fact, rightly adds, *Quod ita esse*. In place of these words, in the manuscripts of Dionysius or Hadrian, *Quod si ita esset* has been very badly substituted; and from there the author of the Spanish collection, Isidore and Merlin took that reading. Opposite this place, in the Colbert Lombardic manuscript, this marginal note is written: *He speaks of the Novatians: When they have returned to the catholic Church, and been received through the imposition of the hand, let these remain in the clergy. Let the clerics of other heresies, when they have returned, receive only lay communion.*] in their own order. That this is so, that also above all which was said of the Paulianists will be able to confirm, those coming from whom they command even [0533A] to be baptized. When they give orders concerning the Paulianists, will all who return from heretics be to be baptized after this example [n: (Nic. syn. c. 19)]? Since no one would dare to do this, reason itself shows that it was commanded of them only. Finally, Peter and John complete by the imposition of the hand alone those duly baptized by Philip the evangelist. But those whom the apostle Paul found baptized with the baptism of John, and asked whether they had received the Holy Spirit, and who confessed that they had not even heard that name, he ordered to be baptized [n: (Act. VIII, 17; XIX, 2 seqq.)]. You see, therefore, that those duly baptized cannot be repeated with that gift: and that those otherwise washed with water alone are perceived to need to be baptized in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit. So too it is read over again, by lucid [0533B] manifestation, that the command was concerning the Novatians only. Manifest reason declares that the distinction was drawn between those two heresies for this cause: because the Paulianists by no means baptize in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit [n: (I; q. I, c. 53)]; and the Novatians baptize in the same tremendous and venerable names, nor was any question ever stirred among them concerning the unity of the divine power, that is, of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit; and therefore [nt: Several manuscripts, with Dionysius: *de omnibus*. It pleases more, with the printed editions and also the Corbie manuscript and two Colbert: *omnibus*; supply *haeresibus*. By the examples of the Novatians, whose baptism is accepted because they sin in nothing in conferring this sacrament, and of the Paulianists, whose baptism is rejected because they sin in the same, the Nicene synod gave us a pattern of those whose baptism is either to be admitted or to be refused.], all being set apart, this one alone was chosen, to which they believed this should be granted, because they sinned in nothing in the sacrament of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit.

## 11.

But if anyone, passing over from the Catholic Church to heresy, or one of the faithful [nt: The Isidorian, after the Spanish collection, and from there the Roman edition and the Councils: *ab apostasia revertens et resipiscens*.] turned back to apostasy, coming to his senses, [0534A] wishes to return, will he be able by the same reckoning to be permitted [nt: The Roman edition: *admitti*. Then Dionysius with the older manuscripts: *quorum commissum*. What Innocent says next, that by the canons it is not lawful to become a cleric after penance, rests on rules established before the Nicene synod, which that very synod marks in can. 10 in these words: *Whoever of the lapsed have been promoted to the order of the clergy through ignorance or through the connivance of those ordaining, this does not prejudice the rule of the Church; for once known they are deposed.* Augustine, epist. 185, n. 41, teaches that this rule, which Siricius also confirms, epist. 1, n. 18, was prudently established, both so that, through despair of temporal eminence, the medicine of humility might be greater and truer, and so that the conversion of those returning might be more sincere; since perhaps the fathers had experienced the feigned penances of some through affected powers of honours. A third reason for the same decree, no less firm, Siricius adds in the place cited: namely that *although cleansed, those who were formerly vessels of vices ought not to take up any instruments for administering the sacraments.* A fourth Innocent sets out below. A fifth Simplicius touches on, epist. 18: *lest one who is convicted of having long been perverse should, under the appearance of one coming back, seek not the remedy of salvation, but find the means of propagating depravity.*] to the clergy, whose offence can be abolished only by a long penance? Not even after penance do the canons themselves by their authority permit one to become a cleric. Hence it is established that those who passed over from the Catholic Church to the condemned [nt: That is, after he was condemned in the council of the bishops of Illyricum, to which his case had been carried by the synod of Capua. Hence it is clear that in that whole third question the dispute was about the clerics ordained by Bonosus alone. Nor did the question turn on this, whether those whom that heretic had ordained before the council of Illyricum were to be received. For in this council Anysius with his colleagues, condemning Bonosus, had decreed that those ordained by him, lest they should remain with him, were to be received in their grades; and Innocent approves this decree above, n. 9. But the matter concerned those who had been ordained by him after Bonosus had been condemned by the judgment of Anysius and the Macedonians.] Bonosus, and suffered or desired to be ordained by him, ought not to have taken up the dignity of ecclesiastical ordination, since, deserting the common judgment of all the churches, they judged that their own vanity was to be followed peculiarly in Bonosus.

## 12.

But [n: (1, q. I, c. III)] it is established that many suffered violence, and were dragged unwilling, and were ordained by him while resisting. Let whoever believes this tell me, I wish it: if after the ordination [nt: Quesnel alone: *Bonoso*. Yet the other books, not badly: *Bonosi*, that is, conferred by Bonosus.] of Bonosus he was not present when he performed [0534B] the sacraments; if he was not made a partaker of his communion; if, departing at once from his most wicked conventicle, he returned to the Church: such a man can have some colour of excuse. But those who returned to the Church after either a year [nt: In the printed editions, *aut mensem .... intelligimus eos qui in* (Quesnel: *qui a*) *Catholica*. Quesnel below: *reperire locum*; Merlin and Crabbe: *recipere locum*; where other books keep *invenire locum*. Here Innocent sets out a new reason why he shows himself difficult towards those who come ordained by heretics, so that they be not admitted to the clergy; and he observes that it was the custom of most heretics that any Catholics, however corrupt and depraved in morals, sometimes even expelled or degraded from the Church for crimes, who passed over to them, were not only taken in by them without the satisfaction of penance, but were sometimes even heaped with new honours. For the devil, the parent of the heretical sects, is not at all anxious about purity of morals. The Church, on the other hand, never rejoices in a multitude of hypocrites or of the wicked. Certainly he betrays himself a lover of himself, not of God, who honours those given over to him and does not care that they be good.] or months, it is understood that, because they knew that in the Catholic Church they could not receive ordination on account of their vices, they therefore went to him who made unlawful ordinations everywhere and without any examination, believing that through that appointment they could find in the Catholic Church a place of which they had before despaired. Now I ask what remains: he who returned after a month or more, when he trusted that he had been ordained a presbyter by Bonosus, [0535A] if he did not perform [nt: The copies of the collection of Hadrian: *si non sacra confecit*. Then Crabbe in the margin, and from there the Roman edition and the Councils: *si non populis distribuit*: which is also read in the Pithou exemplar of Quesnel's Code.] the sacraments, if he did not give them to the peoples, if he did not complete Masses according to custom, what you judge of these things, I ask you, set forth more openly. In sum, certainly those who received nothing from Bonosus are guilty of a usurped dignity, who claimed for themselves [nt: In the printed editions, as in the Noyon manuscript, the words *aut non vindicaverunt* are wanting, which are presented in the other manuscripts with great agreement. Hence the sentence of Innocent is had fuller and more whole, pronouncing generally of those who were ordained by Bonosus against right, that whether they claimed for themselves, or did not claim, the authority of performing the sacraments, they are guilty of a usurped dignity, inasmuch as they received nothing of regular and lawful power: and so in vain *they thought themselves to be that which* (badly in the Roman edition and the Councils, *quod de eis*) *had been granted them by no regular reckoning*. Here indeed Quesnel omits the word *regulari*, but it stands in all the books, both printed and written, except that in its place one Colbert faultily has *relaxandi*, and Pithou *relaxari*.] or did not claim the authority of performing the sacraments, and thought themselves to be that which had been granted them by no regular reckoning.

## CAP. VI.



## 13.

Let your love therefore perceive that such things have passed up to now, and take note that what, as you say, necessity certainly commanded, churches already set in peace [nt: Quesnel, with two manuscripts: *non posse praesumere*. If anything were to be added, *debere* would be more apt than *posse*. For Innocent teaches that what necessity commanded to churches when disturbed is by no means to be presumed by churches set in peace. And here, therefore, he not obscurely confesses that he would grant the Bonosians the honours which he now denies, if there were an equal necessity.] do not presume. But [n: (I, q. 7, c. 14)], as often happens, whenever a sin is committed by peoples or by a crowd, because vengeance cannot be taken on all on account of the multitude, [0535B] it is wont [nt: Four manuscripts, with Quesnel: *solere*. Several others, with Merlin and Crabbe: *soleat*. These two editors, as also Quesnel, then subjoin *transire. Priora ergo*. Our reading is that of the more correct manuscripts. Innocent, ever consistent with himself, here confirms what he had already taught in epist. 3, c. 4, concerning offences which are either to be handled more gently or tolerated, or severely restrained and punished. Akin to this is what Augustine writes, book III, against the Epistle of Parmenian, n. 11: *For rebuke by the many cannot be salutary unless he is rebuked who has not a multitude as his associate. In truth,* he says, some things being interposed, *if the contagion of sinning has invaded a multitude, the severe mercy of divine discipline is necessary: for counsels of separation are both empty and pernicious and sacrilegious.* But, as he premises in the same place, n. 13, *when the crime of anyone is so known that he has either no defenders at all, or not such that through them a schism could come about, let not the severity of discipline sleep, in which the correction of depravity is the more efficacious the more diligent the preservation of charity.* So the prudent pastor, moderating discipline according to the different condition of the times, is more indulgent towards some of those ordained by Bonosus, whom he thinks it enough to leave to the judgment of God, and more severe towards others, whom he judges are to be punished according to the prescription of the stricter discipline.] to pass unpunished; I say that the former things are to be left to the judgment of God, and that for the rest one must take precaution with the greatest solicitude.

## CAP. VII.



## 14.

I shall come now to Photinus, as it were a certain very great theme, and, what is to me anxious and most difficult, I shall turn over again the sentences of my elders [n: (36, q. 9, c. 5)]. Concerning him too, by some arrangement, [0536A] as you yourselves also remember, something certainly rather grave had been decreed. But since you show that this was obtained from this see by stealth through a false rumour, as you assert, and drawn out by snares, since the matter has returned to safety; [nt: The particles *et vel*, first suppressed by Dionysius and then passed over in the Spanish collection and the Isidorian, are recalled from six manuscripts of sincerer note. By their help Innocent softens and removes the odium of the word *veniam*, as though he said: *Photinus indeed, whom you testify was not guilty, needs no pardon: but if he had been guilty, I judge it harsher not to ratify even the pardon which you granted him.*] even pardon too, so far as this, at your striving, we substitute after the condemnation [nt: The editions, with several manuscripts: *more*: from a repetition of the last letter of the preceding word, familiar enough to copyists. We prefer, with the old Corbie manuscript and one Colbert, *ore*, that is, pronounced by the apostolic see. And this sentence against Photinus had been pronounced not by Innocent himself, but either by Anastasius or by Siricius. Wherefore he had said beforehand, *Majorum meorum revolvam sententias*: which, although he felt it *anxious* for him, the love of equity and justice nevertheless overcame the feeling of anxiety.] by apostolic mouth; and not to give consent to such great assertions of yours, you being so good, so dear, we judge harder than all hard things. For your approval therefore, dearest brethren, and your sentence and demand, hold it lawful to appoint Photinus bishop, as you pray, and receive our sentence, changed for the better by your labour or testimony, having gained [nt: Crabbe and several manuscripts: *votis*: the letter s wrongly doubled, with which the following word takes its beginning. Next, where some manuscripts present *Eustasium*, others *Eustachium* or *Eustatium* or *Eustathium*, and all *comprobatum*, Quesnel substituted *Eustathius... comprobatus*, I think in order to fit the sentence to the laws of grammar. Here Innocent begins to ask the Macedonians to pardon and remit the sentence which they had pronounced against Eustathius, just as at their request he himself had pardoned and remitted that which the apostolic see had brought forth against Photinus.] your wish.

## 15.

And do not expect that Eustathius, very often approved by me, [0536B] be stripped of the grace of the diaconate. For I gladly hear you when you are anxious for salvation [n: (II Cor. II, 10)]: against the head, even if it must be done, I do not gladly admit it. To whom you stretch out the hand, I stretch it out with you: to whom I stretch it out, stretch it out with me. For this is the apostolic kindness declared to the Corinthians, that the good should always follow without distinction a sentence pronounced [nt: In the printed editions and several manuscripts: *ductam*. In the old Corbie and one Colbert: *ducta ac reducta sententia*. What stands in the other Colbert pleases more: *dictam*. Innocent looks to the words of Paul, both in I Cor. v, 4, where he pronounces sentence against the Corinthian guilty of incest, and in II Cor. II, 6, where he draws back and revokes the sentence pronounced.] and drawn back in one spirit. For, I confess, [0537A] this matter first sits well with me concerning Eustathius the deacon: that he neither spoke anything against the faith, nor admitted one who spoke, nor have I recognized that he committed any crime unto death, either ever bandied about or feigned. And he who is sound in these things, if in some way he does not have amiable manners, is to be held as less loved for the time, not as an enemy [nt: The word *aestimatur* Quesnel passed over. In its place in the other editions stands *aestimatus* or *existimatus*. Hence it is gathered that Eustathius had been not only stripped of the grace of the diaconate but also struck with anathema: which was very harsh against a man of whom no one ever either bandied about or feigned that anything had been said by him against the faith, or any crime unto death committed.] is he judged to be delivered over to the devil for ever. I recognize that amid those rivalries and whirlwinds he thought differently against the zeal of many, I do not say of what kind, and that hence, although the affairs and calamities themselves have been amended, up to [nt: So the manuscripts. But the editions: *adhuc*, *usque* being omitted. Then the Pithou manuscript: *Dyzoniani*. Some others: *Dezoniani* or *Diozeniani*. Next most: *et Quiriaci*.] this point he has not been able, on the commendation of Dizonianus and Cyriacus the subdeacons, to [nt: The Roman edition and the Councils, following the collections of Dionysius, the Spanish and the Isidorian: *emendationem reviviscere. Compescite, quaeso*: they are corrected by the authority of six manuscripts of better note.] revive among you. Extinguish, I ask, from him and from those aforesaid, the [nt: So one Colbert codex and the other, Pithou's, with Quesnel. But others: *animos ac*.] spirited and unjust [0537B] rivalries of whomsoever you see, so that he himself, whole among us, and restored among you together with his own [nt: The editions after Dionysius and the Isidorian: *reparatus mercatur pacem, non fictam pervidens charitatem. Omnibus igitur vobis ac singillatim occurrat charitas, et vinculis quae ..... connexa laetetur*: they are corrected by the help of the better manuscripts, in three of which *charitati* stands in place of *charitatis*. The sentence would be fuller if *charitatisque vinculis* were read.], perceiving a deep peace and an unfeigned charity, may meet you all, and each one singly, and, joined together with you equally for ever by the bonds of charity which are in no way loosed from Christ, may rejoice in the Lord. Given on the Ides of December [nt: In the printed editions *Flavio* is wanting. Next, in three Colbert manuscripts and the Pithou, *Constantino* stands. From the Corbie and Fossés manuscripts, in which *Flaviis Constantio* remains, we conjecture that *et Constante* is to be added. For in this year 414 Constans had been created consul together with Constantius.] [n: (13 Decemb. ann. 414)], Flavius Constantius, a most illustrious man, being consul.

## EPISTLE XVIII [nt: In the edition of the councils, 7. And what was 18 is now 21. It is found not in Mercator's collection only, but also in the Spanish and in that of Dionysius Exiguus. With him it holds the 55th place among the decrees of Innocent. We put it back here, both lest the things which pertain to the affairs of Macedonia be disjoined further, and above all because there is no lack of a conjecture from which we may judge that it was written while the peace of the Antiochene church was being negotiated, and so about the year 414. For Innocent relates below, epist. 22, that this Maximianus, one of the legates from Macedonia, took part in that great business. And this perhaps was the chief cause why this Maximianus and his associate made a longer stay in the City than was hoped.]. *On Bubalius and Taurianus, condemned by the bishops of the province, whose sentence the apostolic see took care to reconsider.*

*[0538A]INNOCENT TO RUFUS, GERONTIUS, SOPHRONIUS, FLAVIANUS, MACEDONIUS, PROSDOCIUS AND [nt: So the better manuscripts. But the Navarre: *Aristeo*; and another, with the printed editions: *Oristeo*. The names of this Aristeas and of Prosdocius do not appear in the inscription of the preceding letter, in which we read the rest named above. From which one may conjecture that in the preceding letter neither the bishops of Macedonia alone nor all of them are named, but in this one both all and only the bishops of this province, their legates being joined in the body of the letter.] ARISTEAS, bishops established throughout Macedonia.*

## 1.

The delay of our fellow bishops Maximianus and [nt: In the letter immediately preceding, as also in Chrysostom's epistle 165, likewise written to the Macedonians, *Eugenius* is joined to *Maximiano*. Hence there is a suspicion that here too *Eumenii* has crept in in place of *Eugenii*.] Eumenius, or rather the importunity of the times, has brought it about that you have repeated your complaint about Bulbalius and [nt: The Royal codex of the pure collection of Dionysius shows *Thariano* here, and *Tariano* below. Others of the collection of Hadrian have *Tariano* here, but *Tauriano* below: which the manuscripts of the Spanish collection and the Isidorian also have in both places.] Taurianus, and that we have risen up again against most abandoned men. But [n: (35, quaest. 9, c. 7)], as I can, I shall measure out [nt: The editions: *imitabor*: they are corrected from the Royal codex cited above.] in fewer words a summary of such great evils, and shall go over again briefly the things I have seen in the volume of your letters. It ought not [nt: In the copies of the collection of Hadrian: *non potuit*. It is to be kept, with the rest, *non oportuit*: as Nicholas I also reads it. Hadrian II imitates the same place in the letter to King Charles given in Indiction v.] [n: (Nicolaus I, epist. 32, n. 12)] to seem grievous to your most pious minds that anyone's judgment be reconsidered: because truth, when more often stirred up, shines out the more in the light: and ruin, recalled into judgment, is condemned more heavily and [0538B] without repentance [nt: That is, of him who judged before, as is also noted in Labbe's margin. From this it is gathered that Bubalius and Taurianus, judged by the Macedonians, appealed to the apostolic see, and that the Macedonians took it ill that their own judgment was being reviewed.]. For it is a divine fruit, dearest brethren, that justice be more often reviewed.

## 2.

But I see that this has moved your minds: that Bubalius, often caught out in many deceits, put forward copies of letters feigned as if from us, although, from the man's habit, nothing that he brought forward seemed by now most worthy of credit. But let all murmuring about such a business now be silenced, and let the very breathings of the convicted devil be suppressed. We have, moreover, subjoined to these the earlier [nt: These letters are not extant.] letters, which we had sent by the bishops mentioned, in which the full [0539A] sentence of our mind was so set out that, when you have read them again, you will seek again nothing doubtful, nothing to be inquired into, in this case for the rest. And send this little page of ours more carefully [nt: In the manuscripts of the collection of Hadrian: *credentibus*: to which word the author of the Spanish collection added *sibi*: and that reading prevailed both in the Isidorian and thereafter in the printed editions. The Royal exemplar of the pure collection of Dionysius supplies the true one. Further, among the churches in which Innocent, epist. 13, n. 2, commits his office to Rufus, Crete also is reckoned.] to the Cretan bishops to be read again: that they may know most fully what has been pronounced concerning Bubalius and Taurianus and the rest; that [nt: It would please more: and *observent*. Next, in place of *administratione*, which we recall from the excellent Royal codex, there stands in the others *admonitione*. Presently the Noyon manuscript: *ne talibus sortiantur*: from which the common reading *ne talibus socientur* was made up. To be kept, with the better manuscripts: *ne talia sortiantur*, that is, that knowing what was judged concerning Bubalius and Taurianus, they may beware lest they do such things, and thence incur an equal penalty. And what that penalty was is understood from the words *qui digni administratione sunt*: for this is said by antiphrasis, since Bubalius and Taurianus had been cast down from the administration of the churches.] those who are worthy of administration may keep to beware of such men, lest they obtain such a lot.

## MONITUM ON THE FOLLOWING EPISTLES.



## 1.

All those letters Dionysius Exiguus has preserved for us. From his collection they flowed into the Spanish and the Isidorian. It is admitted that they were written on the occasion of the peace of the Antiochene church. Of which peace Theodoret, book V of the History, c. 35, to touch on his words summarily, committed these things to memory. [0539B] Alexander, excelling in the institute of a stricter life, in the pursuit of wisdom, in contempt of riches, in eloquence and in countless other gifts, when he had succeeded Porphyrius, joined to the body of the Church, by the gentle persuasion of his words and frequent exhortations, the Eustathians, whom formerly Paulinus, and after him Evagrius, had by no means permitted to be united with the rest; and for that cause he instituted a festival of such a kind as no mortal had ever seen. Having set these things first, the same writer adds concerning Alexander: « He first of all inscribed the name of the most excellent John in the ecclesiastical tablets. » And the dissension of the Antiochene church, as the same author recounts, book III, c. 5, « lasted eighty-five years, » up to Alexander. Whence the time also at which that peace was entered into, and the following letters were written, could be discerned and determined with certainty, if there were agreement among the learned about the beginning of the aforesaid dissension. And Baronius indeed, at the year 308, while he thinks it is to be reckoned either from the day on which Meletius professed the faith of the consubstantial, [0539C] or from that on which Lucifer ordained Paulinus, that is, from the year 361 or 362, is compelled to reject Theodoret's reckoning as false. But Tillemont, counting the beginning of the Antiochene dissension from the deposition of Eustathius, which he judges to have happened in the year 430 or 431, observes that those eighty-five years end in the year 413 or 414. Eutychius, although not accurate enough elsewhere, supports this computation, when he relates that Porphyrius, whom he calls Barchus, presided over the church of Antioch for ten years. For Palladius, in the dialogue on the Life of Chrysostom, p. 141, is the authority that he was put in place of Flavian about the beginning of the year 403. Besides this, Theodoret, book V, cap. 35, teaches that the reconciliation of the Antiochene church came about when Cyril, the successor of Theophilus, was bishop of the Alexandrian church. But Theophilus, as Socrates witnesses, book VII, c. 7, ceased to live in the year 412, on the 15th day of the month of October. From which it follows that this reconciliation cannot be assigned earlier than the year 413. But there is no inconvenience [0539D] if it is put off to the year 414 or even 415.

## 2.

The reconciliation of the dissenting parties in the Antiochene church was followed at no long interval by the embassy by which its bishop Alexander made haste to ask for the communion of the Roman [0540A] see. The father of the Annals judged that he did not obtain it at once; and therefore he devised a double embassy: one in which Alexander asked for Innocent's communion, and to which Innocent prescribed the conditions for granting peace; the other in which Alexander testified that the prescribed conditions had been satisfied by him. But no cause appears for this double embassy. For already from the death of John Chrysostom, that is, from the year 407, Innocent had made these conditions well enough known, when he denied his communion to the bishops of the East until they should have inscribed John's name in the sacred diptychs. On which matter Theodoret, book V, c. 34, speaks thus: « After the death of the great Doctor of the world, the Western bishops (without doubt by the consent and prescription of Innocent) did not admit the communion of the bishops of Egypt and of the East and of the Bosphorus and of Thrace before they had recorded the name of that divine man together with the rest of the bishops who had died. » Nor was Alexander so ignorant of affairs as not to know this; nor [0540B] so inconsiderate as knowingly to send a useless embassy. And indeed Innocent, in epist. 22, recalling that the conditions of peace had been fulfilled by Alexander, and demanding a like « profession of the fulfilment of all the conditions » from Atticus, does not say, which we prescribed when Alexander's embassy was sent back, but « which we declared beforehand at different times. »

## 3.

Nor can we assent either to the learned Tillemont, who judges that epistle 20 was sent first, and 19 given somewhat later. For we judge that both, with 21 to Acacius joined to them, were given together. And the note added at the foot shows that the former, that is 19, was synodal and common to Innocent with the twenty bishops who subscribed it. The other, that is 20, we judge private and familiar. Mention of the synodal acts, which were subjoined to the former, is made in both.

## EPISTLE XIX [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 17. And what was 19 is now 21. In the Roman edition it is noted as copied *ex Cresconio*. It would more truly have been ascribed *ex Dionysio*, in whom it is placed under title XLIV of the decrees of Innocent.]. OF POPE INNOCENT I TO ALEXANDER, BISHOP OF ANTIOCH. On Peace. *Recalling what was done at Antioch for the sake of peace, having attained the height of his wishes, he gladly grants communion, and offers it to Acacius, provided he too performs the same things.*



## 1.

By the grace of apostolic favour the great beauty of peace has shone out even to us: and it has shed so much light and joy upon the faithful that, speaking the greatest praises to God, we confessed that we owed greater. And we were the more gladdened by God because, all the sides [0540D] of the actions of thy fraternity having been examined, we learned that the whole had been so done with piety and patience that in all things we praised the Lord. And we see that success itself was granted [0541A] to thy labours for this reason: that, being a lover of peace with all thy strength, thou didst find it when sought, and didst keep it when found with the highest charity, both towards all, and especially concerning those who had once been counted under the name of the bishops Paulinus and Evagrius. [nt: (I have the sum of my wishes, in that the cleansing of the ancient blemish is bestowed in thy times and by thy merits.)] Of whom I judged that those also of the same name who received the merits of the clerical state in Italy should, for the good of quiet, retain the grace they had received. And because our fellow presbyter Cassianus said [nt: Merlin and Crabbe, with the Royal, Noyon and one Colbert manuscript: *duxit*. This Cassian is thought to be the writer of the *Conferences*, who, as Palladius relates in the Life of Chrysostom, p. 27, came to Rome while still a deacon as the year 404 was passing: but afterwards was honoured with the grade of the presbyterate. This whole passage, inasmuch as it is that of a mind speaking with the highest feeling, and caring not so much how it should order the words as how it should express its feeling, will perhaps seem intricate to some. To understand it, it must be recalled that the Antiochene church had been split into two parties, namely that of Paulinus, to whom Evagrius succeeded, and that of Flavian, in whose place Alexander was put after Porphyrius, Evagrius having already died. And because the West favoured Paulinus and then Evagrius, some who were attached to them betook themselves to Italy, and there had earned the dignity of the clerical state. Now the custom of ecclesiastical discipline required that, if the dignity conferred were judged to be kept for these men, they should be counted clerics of that city by whose bishops they had been ordained or promoted. But Innocent first judged that the grace of the clerical state was to be kept for the clerics of the parties of Paulinus or Evagrius ordained or promoted in Italy, and then, at Cassian's instance, decreed that the same *should be numbered among the other priests and ministers who are in the city* of Antioch. The same things are confirmed below too, in epist. 23, and stated a little more openly. Here it is to be observed in passing that by the word *sacerdotes*, contrary to the usual custom of the ancients, presbyters, not bishops, are signified. In the same sense too Jerome, using this word on chapter 48 of Ezekiel, rebukes the arrogance of the deacons, *who, ignorant of the humility of their order, swell beyond the priests, that is, the presbyters.* And in the epistle to Evangelus: *If a presbyter is ordained from a deacon, let him know that he is less in gains, greater in priesthood.* Innocent, in epist. 25, n. 6, writes that presbyters are *second priests*.] it would be pleasing to thy worthiness if, by my counsel, they were judged to hold the order of the clerical state in your city, I have decreed, on account of thy benevolence and the promises of the man mentioned, that they be numbered among the other priests and ministers who are in the city, dearest brother. Gladly [0541B] besides I learned concerning the bishops [nt: That those two bishops were shut up in their houses for three years for the cause of Chrysostom, Palladius relates, p. 195, in these words: *Elpidius, that great bishop of Laodicea in Syria, and Pappus, for the whole three years did not come down the stairs of their houses, giving themselves to prayers.*] Elpidius and Pappus that they have recovered their churches without dispute: and, as the acts subjoined [nt: In the printed editions, as in the Isidorian: *subscripta*. Better in the manuscripts of the collections of Dionysius, Hadrian and the Spanish: *subjecta*. Namely, Innocent did not confirm the peace before he had heard Alexander's legates in a public assembly, and had diligently questioned them about the things which had been done at Antioch. And since he had taken care that acts should be drawn up on that matter, he saw to it that they were subjoined to this letter.] testify, I inquired anxiously and much whether all the conditions had been satisfied in the cause of the blessed and truly God-worthy priest [0542A] John. And when the assertion of the legates acknowledged point by point that they had been fulfilled as wished, giving thanks to the Lord, I received the communion of your Church in such a way that I openly declare that the fellow disciples of the apostolic see were the first to give the others the way of peace, in which the kindness of the Lord Christ will so embrace and fortify you and us, established in it, that for the rest it may be struck by no [nt: The Colbert exemplar: *titulamento*: which would not unfitly be said for a title, or pretext, or colour. A little before, the Antiochenes are called *fellow disciples of the apostolic see*: because the Antiochene church was founded by the same Apostle as teacher as the Roman.] titillation, or by the slight shame of anyone's contention.

## 2.

And the writings [nt: The edition of the councils, with one Colbert manuscript and the Noyon: *Attici*. In place of this word Labbe wrote in the margin *Acacii*; which word the manuscripts of the collections of Dionysius and Hadrian and the Roman edition present, and which epistles 21 and 22 plainly persuade us is to be kept. In the latter, further, Innocent testifies that he has received no writings from Atticus. And he who substituted the name *Attici* here manifestly betrays his error at the head of epistle 21, which in the same manuscripts is read inscribed *ad Atticum Beroeae episcopum*. For not Atticus but Acacius presided over Beroea. This Acacius Palladius is wont to count first among the enemies of John Chrysostom and the chief actors of the tragedy stirred up against him. For which cause he had deserved to be suspended by Innocent: which is to be understood thus, that Innocent's communion with him was suspended, not that he interdicted him from his office. The same pope opens his mind more plainly in epist. 22.] of Acacius the bishop, since they were handed in together with yours, we have received, lest through wrong done to you he who had formerly been suspended by us should be repudiated: and yet we have decreed amply enough in the acts [nt: The editions: *quae in actis*: they are corrected from the manuscripts.], as thou wilt deign to take care to read again, what ought to be observed in his person: so that if he shall have asked to be in communion with your counsels and so holy acts in all things, [0542B] the grace of communion and of letters from us, bestowed beforehand by your favour and our judgment, may be furnished to the old man [nt: Rightly is Acacius now called *old man*; *whose very nostrils had very long grey hairs when he came to Rome bearing the decree of the ordination of John the bishop* (that is, before the year 404), as Theodore the deacon speaks in Palladius, dial. p. 39. The same man is also read to have taken part in the Roman synod under Pope Damasus, in Baluze, *nov. coll. concil.* p. 703. Which we proved above, p. 491, n. 3 and 4, happened in the year 378.]. Subscribed [nt: So the manuscripts with great agreement. But Merlin: *Subscripserunt episcopi xx, Italiae xxiv*. Then Crabbe, removing the numeral mark xx, kept xxiv, and that reading prevailed thereafter. The cause of the error flowed from the manuscripts of the Spanish collection, in which after the words *subscripserunt episcopi 20 Italiae*, xxiv is added, not to indicate the number of the subscribing bishops, but the order which the following letter holds among the others in that collection.] twenty bishops of Italy.

## EPISTLE XX [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 15. And what was 20 is now 41. This letter too is marked in the Roman edition *ex Cresconio*. It was rather excerpted *ex Dionysio*, by whom it is called Innocent's decree XLII.] OF POPE INNOCENT I TO ALEXANDER, BISHOP OF ANTIOCH. On Peace. *Innocent testifies, the acts being sent, that the embassy sent to him by Alexander, bishop of Antioch, was most welcome to him.*

*INNOCENT TO ALEXANDER, BISHOP.*

How welcome to me, how pious, how necessary was the embassy sent to us by thy holiness, dearest brother, thou wilt learn from the unfolding of the acts themselves. For our fellow presbyter Cassianus wished that this little page of our friendship should be written by our fellow presbyter Paul, Nicholas the deacon, [0543B] and Peter the subdeacon, our sons, as it were the first fruits of our peace. I therefore greet both thy brotherhood to me in Christ and all that church which thinks so well with thee. And I ask that, as you do, you both address us more often by letters and gladden us more frequently about your welfare. For the Lord, as I trust, will grant us to make up the losses of all the past time by a most loving converse [nt: The editions: *recompensare*: the manuscripts opposing.] of letters.

## EPISTLE XXI [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 19. And what was 21 is now 17. From Dionysius, from whom, and not, as is noted in the Roman edition, *ex Cresconio*, we received this letter, it is placed under title XLVIII of the decrees of Innocent. That it was sent together with the two above to Alexander is manifest from epistle 19. Nor is it less clear that in Merlin and Crabbe, as in the manuscripts of the Spanish collection, it is faultily inscribed *ad Atticum Beroeae episcopum*. For Atticus was bishop of Constantinople, not of Beroea. The summary which was set before it by Dionysius, and thence in other collections and in the old editions of the councils, *De sancto Joanne Constantinopolitano episcopo*, is not accurate enough. That Acacius, to whom it is inscribed, played the chief parts in the deposition of Chrysostom is known from Palladius everywhere, and also from Socrates, book vi, c. 18, and from Sozomen, book viii, c. 20. When he thought himself slighted by John, as Palladius witnesses, p. 49, overcome by anger he said: *I am seasoning a pot for him.* Next the same Palladius, p. 82, records that Acacius together with Antiochus said: *Emperor, on our head be the deposition of John.* Hence, as Theodore the deacon speaks in the same place, p. 39, *he was held both the author of the sedition and the leader of the offence of those who stirred up revolution at Constantinople.* Wherefore he needed a mediator and go-between whose favour with Innocent should be no less than Alexander's. And not without cause did Innocent send the letters that were to signify communion not to Acacius himself, but to Alexander, to be delivered to him only on certain conditions.] OF POPE INNOCENT I TO ACACIUS, BISHOP OF BEROEA. *On what condition Innocent grants communion to Acacius.*

[0543C]We have gathered from thy letters that thy fraternity rejoices over the reception of the clerics and peoples of the bishops Paulinus or Evagrius, and over the restoration of the fellow bishops Elpidius and Pappus, [0544A] and we have perceived that thou dost cherish the grace of a peace received, even if late, all contention being put aside. Wherefore we have sent these to our most excellent brother and fellow bishop Alexander, to be delivered to thy unanimity: namely that, if all enmities, all rivalry, both concerning the name of saint John the wonderful bishop and concerning all the partakers of his communion, have departed from thy animosity, thou mayest receive these letters of our fellowship, dearest brother; provided that all the things which [nt: In the edition of the councils, as in one Colbert manuscript: *quae his actis*. The things done in the ro... synod (a word worn on the plate), at which at least twenty bishops of Italy were present, are noted here.] have been confirmed here by the acts thou confess with thine own mouth before our amiable mediator Alexander, as befits one [nt: The Royal exemplar: *communicantes*.] in communion. For as this cause of unanimity and charity is venerable to us, so it clings anxious lest anything oblique or residual be known [nt: The editions, except Merlin: *cognoscat*: they are emended from the written copies. The sincerity of Acacius in this whole business becomes very suspect from what is written about him in Nicephorus, book xiv, c. 26 and 27, both by Atticus to Cyril and by Cyril to Atticus.] to remain in anyone.



## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 16. What was 22 is now 16. In Dionysius, from whom, and not *from Cresconius*, we first have this letter, it stands under title XLIII of Innocent's decrees. There seems to be no doubt that the Maximianus to whom it is written is the very man whom, as also the other bishops of Macedonia, Chrysostom, when he had been made an exile, thanks in Epistle 163 for having stood constantly on his side, and who is counted above, in Epistle 17, among the bishops of Macedonia. Nor does he seem to be other than that Maximianus, legate of the Macedonians, who in the preceding Epistle 18 is recorded to have made a longer stay at Rome than was hoped. The cause of this stay was probably that he might be present at the Roman synod, and be a witness of all that had been done in the reconciliation of the Church of Antioch. Then, moved and encouraged by the recital of what he had heard in this synod, as soon as he could reach Constantinople he did not cease to urge Atticus to follow the example of the bishop of Antioch. When therefore he had somehow drawn out his assent, he could not bear delay, but wrote to Innocent on his behalf. Hence also the occasion of writing this letter was given to this pope. At what time it was written may be gathered even from this simple account. For it appears to have been composed neither together with the three preceding letters, nor long after them: but only so much interval is to be supposed between this one and those as was needed for Maximianus to reach Constantinople from the Roman synod, and for his letters on behalf of Atticus to be delivered thence to Innocent. By what means Alexander, on coming to the imperial city, obtained from Atticus what Innocent desired, Atticus himself relates in Nicephorus, book xiv, c. 26.] XXII. ON ATTICUS, BISHOP OF CONSTANTINOPLE. *That communion is not to be restored to him unless he has first asked for it by a solemn embassy, and has himself accomplished what was done at Antioch, that is, has restored the name of John in the sacred diptychs.*

*INNOCENT TO MAXIMIANUS, BISHOP.*

We wonder that thy prudence, both by thine own pleading and by the petition that is annexed below, asks of us letters to Atticus, bishop of the city of Constantinople, from whom thou hast brought not so much as any [0544C] letters sent to us or to your synod; and that thou reckonest that to be bestowed on one who does not ask, which thou seest granted, after the case was examined, to those who entreat. [0545A] For suspended communion [nt: Innocent's communion with Atticus had been suspended for this reason: that he, as Palladius testifies, p. 95, *the contriver of all the machinations against John*, had forced his way into John's place; so that the bishops of the East and the people of the city of Constantinople shrank from his communion, and he could scarcely obtain, by an imperial rescript, that they should communicate with him even against their will. And the bishops of the West expressly, as Theodoret testifies, book v, c. 34, had determined after the death of Chrysostom to admit none of the bishops of the East to communion until they had inscribed the name of this great Doctor in the sacred diptychs. And it is this cause of the suspended communion that Innocent wishes above all to have removed, so that he may be able to communicate with Atticus.] is restored to one who shows that the causes through which it had happened have now been wiped away, and who professes that the conditions of peace have been fulfilled. This Atticus, as I said before, has been willing neither to state nor to show as accomplished, either among you or among us, by sending any of his own people, in the way that our brother and fellow bishop Alexander of the church of Antioch both pursued it and proved it by a worthy embassy. Having deigned indeed to be present at all these things, thou hast learned how I examined [nt: One of the Colb. manuscripts: *discusserint*; which once found favour, because it seemed to belong to those whose part it was to fulfil what was demanded, to examine what was to be fulfilled. But afterwards, when Epistle 19, n. 1, was read again, it became clear that Innocent had examined in his synod whether the conditions which he had prescribed at various times, and in writing, for the sake of obtaining peace had been satisfied. Next, in the printed editions *ut quae*, where *utque* should be read, that is, *and how*. Soon after, for *monstrarint*, the Royal exemplar has *nuntiaverint*, and some editions *ostenderint*.] one by one all the points of our earlier writings on the cause of the most blessed John, formerly our bishop, and how they showed quite evidently in every respect that all things which ought to have been done at Antioch had been accomplished. Embracing their peace, we have indeed given [0545B] assurance, and have shown [nt: The printed editions: *magno tramite*; they are corrected from the manuscripts.] a great path to all who shall ask for the same [nt: Merlin: *eamdem quam praestaturis*, corruptly. Although Innocent, not content with the writings of those who asked for peace, seems also to demand a solemn embassy, he did not, however, require that each should send legates to him in his own name: but, as is clear from the example of Maximianus, he held it enough if anyone offered him his writings through legates sent by anyone at all. But as regards Atticus, who had been put in John's place after Arsacius, the approval of his ordination was also then in question. For now, as we observed above, p. 598, n. 21, the custom had begun to be introduced that the Roman pontiffs should not hold the ordination of the prelates of the imperial city ratified until they had been informed of it by a solemn embassy.]; provided only that they too, for their part, show at some time that they have done or accomplished what has been examined and accomplished, and, as these did, by an embassy solemnly dispatched, ask that communion be restored to them. We await, therefore, both the profession of the aforesaid concerning the fulfilment of all the conditions which we laid down beforehand at various times, and his petition for communion, so that we may rightly restore it to one who both asks and proves that he merits it, dearest brother. For concerning all these things thou hast long since received most full letters addressed to the holy synod of [0546A] our brethren [nt: The letters which he mentions here are not extant.].

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 14. What was 23 is now 3. It was arranged by Dionysius under title XLI of Innocent's decrees, and in the Roman edition, just like the preceding ones, it is marked as copied *from Cresconius*. The Boniface to whom it is written may not unfittingly be taken to be that presbyter of the Roman church who is read in the dialogue of Palladius, p. 51, to have been sent by Honorius to Arcadius with another presbyter and five bishops, and who succeeded Innocent after Zosimus. Perhaps he was then discharging an embassy at the court of Theodosius on behalf of the affairs of the apostolic see. From the last words, by which he is bidden *to inform those who are accustomed to intervene on the side of Atticus*, it is clear that this letter was not written earlier than the one next to it, to Maximianus. We may rightly suppose that both were sent together. A simple summary is prefixed to it in Dionysius and the Isidorian collection: *On the church of Antioch*.] XXIII. *On the peace granted to the church of Antioch.*

*INNOCENT TO BONIFACE, PRESBYTER.*
