The church of Antioch, which the blessed apostle Peter made illustrious by his presence before he came to the city of Rome, did not, as a sister of the Roman church, suffer itself to be long estranged from [nt: Crabbe and the other editions after him: *ab eodem*. More fittingly Merlin, as in the manuscripts: *ab eadem*, that is, from the Roman church.] her. For, having sent legates, it so asked for peace, and merited it, that it received the Evagrians in their ranks and places, the ordination which they had received from the aforesaid [nt: Supply *Evagrio*. For Innocent carries on his sentence as if he had said before *Evagrii clericos*, not *Evagrianos*. Then in the manuscripts *susciperent*, although in the same manuscripts *colligeret atque congregaret* follows. Both can be understood thus: that *susciperent* refers to the church, and *colligeret* to Alexander. Next, instead of the word *promittens*, which is used in the epistolary style then accepted, a grammarian would prefer *Promittit praeterea*.] being left unviolated; and that the bishop of that city himself, my brother [0546B] Alexander [n: (Vide epist. 19, n. 1)], gathered and assembled into one both the clerics and the laymen of John of holy memory, promising that even if anyone of later ordination should perhaps come to them, either from us or from elsewhere, he would receive him without dispute, and would recite the name of the aforesaid bishop *(John)* among the bishops at rest. Know that we have given our hands to this matter, dearest brother, and have received them into our bowels: lest members which had sought their health again should long be held estranged from the unity of the body. All that was done, in order, my son the deacon Paul, the bearer namely of this letter, will be able to relate to thy love, so that thou mayest both [0547A] have a common joy with us, and inform those who have been accustomed to intervene on the side of Atticus.

## EPISTLE XXIV [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 18. What was 24 is now 29. In the Roman edition this letter too is given the note common to the preceding ones, *From Cresconius*. Cresconius himself, *Breviarium* c. 53, cites it according to the division introduced by Dionysius. For Dionysius divided it into three titles or decrees, namely XLV, XLVI and XLVII, with a summary prefixed to each decree, which the compiler of the Spanish collection and Isidore copied out after their custom. We present here the same division, with the summary of each title or chapter prefixed by Dionysius. Although this letter bears no mark of its date, it does not seem that it should be separated from the preceding ones; above all since it is probable that Alexander, invited by Innocent's Epistle 20 to refresh him *more often* with his letters, did not delay to cultivate the friendship offered, and, by putting forward as soon as possible the scruples by which he was troubled, to make it useful to himself.], TO ALEXANDER, BISHOP OF ANTIOCH.



## I.

*That the first see of blessed Peter is recorded to be at Antioch.* --II. *That it is not right, according to the constitutions of the Emperors, for there to be two metropolitan bishops.* --III. *That the clerics of the Arians are not to be received in their offices, although the Church confirms the baptism* [nt: So the exemplars of Dionysius in the general index. But within the text of the letter the title is repeated with a change of this kind: *although the Church confirms their baptism, which is agreed to be catholic*; and Isidore, and from him the editions of the Councils, preferred this.] *which is given through them.*

*INNOCENT TO ALEXANDER, BISHOP.*

## CAP. I.



## 1.

Both the burden and the honour laid upon us by thy fraternity have brought in the grounds of a necessary treatment, by which we may answer your letter or memorandum, as the grace [0547B] of the Holy Spirit grants. Turning over, therefore, the authority of the synod of Nicaea, which [n: (Can. 6)] alone sets forth the mind of all the priests throughout the world, which decreed concerning the church of Antioch what all the faithful, not to say the priests, must needs keep: whereby, over its own diocese and not over some province [nt: That is, which it established ought to preside not over one province but over the whole Eastern diocese, which consisted of fifteen provinces. This right of the see of Antioch over the East, asserted by the synod of Nicaea, Jerome, Epistle 61, to Pammachius, against John of Jerusalem, vindicates and explains in these words: *Thou who seekest the ecclesiastical rules, and makest use of the canons of the council of Nicaea, answer me: what hast thou in Palestine to do with the bishop of Alexandria? Unless I am mistaken, it is decreed there that Caesarea be the metropolis of Palestine, and Antioch of the whole East. Either, then, thou oughtest to have referred it to the bishop of Caesarea; or, if judgment was to be sought from afar, letters should rather have been directed to Antioch.* Where Jerome has in view the Nicene canons 6 and 7. Moreover, the Nicene synod did not so much establish Antioch over its diocese as recognize it as established, and it wills that this prerogative be kept unimpaired for it.], [0548A] we recognize the aforesaid church to have been established. Whence we observe that this was granted to it not so much on account of the magnificence of the city as because it is shown to be the [nt: In the Reg. manuscript *prima* is lacking. In one Colb. there stands *prima sedes apostolica esse*. From this passage it is plain why Innocent, in Epistle 19, n. 1, calls the Antiochenes *fellow disciples of the apostolic see*.] first see of the first apostle, where the Christian religion also received its name, and which merited that a most famous assembly [nt: The assembly of the Apostles held at Antioch is not only praised by Gregory also, bishop of the Pisinuntians, in the second council of Nicaea, vol. vii of the Councils of Labbe, p. 63, but he also produces from it a canon in favour of images. The learned, however, judge that no assembly of the Apostles was held there. On which matter see Tillemont, vol. I of the Ecclesiastical History, p. 525. But this passage of Innocent is at least an argument that the document which the aforesaid bishop cited in favour of a council of Antioch celebrated by the Apostles had not been written recently; and so that the authority which he produced from it in favour of images is not to be spurned: not that that canon may truly be assigned to the Apostles, but that it may with likelihood be believed to be not more recent than the fourth century.] of the Apostles should take place in it [n: (Act. XI)], and which would not yield to the see of the city of Rome, except that the former merited him in passing, while the latter rejoiced that he was received and brought to his consummation in her [n: (Nicol. I, epist. 8)]. Therefore we judge, [nt: The Corb. manuscript: *fratres charissimi, ut sicut metropolitanus ordinat*: corruptly.] dearest brother, that just as thou ordainest the metropolitans by a singular authority, so thou shouldst not allow the other bishops either to be created without thy permission and knowledge. In which thou wilt rightly keep this measure: that those placed far off thou decree, by giving letters, to be ordained by those who now ordain them by their own judgment alone; but those nearby, if thou thinkest fit, thou appoint to come to the imposition of the hand of thy grace [0548B]. For those whose care in the greatest measure awaits [nt: Nicholas I, epistle otherwise 42, n. 9, accommodating this passage to his own see, speaks thus: *We are indeed bound by the care of all who make up the Church, and all* (perhaps *omnes*) *whose care in the greatest measure awaits us ought especially to merit our judgment, as Innocent to Alexander*, etc. And in the epistle otherwise 1, appendix 4, where he reported Innocent's very words, he infers: *Therefore, because the care of the whole Church awaits us*. From which it follows that Nicholas understood the word *exspectat* here to have been said in its common sense, that is, for *praestolatur*. We, however, are persuaded that it is written here in the ancient manner for *spectat*, and that this *te exspectat* means nothing else than *pertains to thee*. Which interpretation would have suited Nicholas's own purpose far better.] thee ought especially to merit thy judgment.

## CAP. II.



## 2.

For [n: (Nicolaus I, haec laudat, epist. 32, n. 6)] as to thy inquiry whether, when provinces have been divided by imperial judgment so that two metropoles come to be, two metropolitan bishops ought likewise to be named: it has truly seemed that the Church of God is not [nt: The printed editions, and the manuscripts of the Spanish collection and of the Isidorian collection: *non vere visum*; Nicholas I and the codices of Hadrian's collection: *non ergo visum*; they are corrected with the help of the Royal exemplar of the Dionysian collection, in which *non esse visum* stands. At variance with this opinion of Innocent is what the synod of Chalcedon laid down in can. 17: that *if any city has been renewed by imperial authority, or shall hereafter be renewed, the order of the ecclesiastical parishes too should follow the civil and public forms*. Only against those *who, doing things beyond the ecclesiastical statutes*, by the authority of imperial letters *divide one province into two, so that as a result of this act there seem to be two metropolitans in one province*, did the same synod decree, in can. 12, that whoever should attempt this hereafter should be punished by the loss of his own rank.] to be [0549A] changed according to the fickleness of worldly necessities, or to undergo the honours or divisions which the Emperor has thought fit to make for his own reasons. Therefore it is fitting that metropolitan bishops be [nt: So the manuscripts. But in the printed editions, *nominari*.] numbered according to the former custom of the provinces.

## 3.

Thou assertest indeed that the Cypriots, once worn down by the power of the Arian impiety, did not keep the Nicene canons (5 *et* 6) in ordaining bishops for themselves, and that up to now they hold it as a presumption that they ordain at their own discretion, consulting no one. Wherefore we urge them to take care to be of catholic mind according to the faith [nt: Although Crabbe, rashly and against the authority of the manuscripts, repeated the word *fidem* here, that repetition was nevertheless retained in the following editions. Nor indeed is it a question here of the faith, but of the discipline instituted by the rules of the canons and received by the usage of the universal Church. Innocent exhorts the Cypriots to take care to be minded according to the catholic understanding of the canons. Not only Alexander but his successors too claimed for themselves the ordinations of the bishops of Cyprus: but the Cypriots constantly protested. On this controversy there is extant the seventh session of the synod of Ephesus, in which, when Zeno, bishop of Carium in Cyprus, had said: *From the holy Apostles they can never show that the bishop of Antioch was present and ordained, or ever communicated to the island the grace of ordination*, *the holy synod said: Let the holy synod be mindful of the canon* (namely, 6) *of the holy fathers gathered at Nicaea, which preserves to each its ancient dignity*. See both what precedes and what follows. In this business Innocent, the Cypriots and the synod of Ephesus agree in this, that all wish the Nicene canons to be kept. Nor did Innocent advise the Cypriots to allow themselves to be ordained by the prelate of Antioch, except in so far as, on Alexander's word, he took it as certain that they had usurped this right for themselves against the ancient custom.] of the canons, and to think as one with the other provinces, so that it may appear that they too, like all the Churches, are governed by the grace of the Holy Spirit.

## 4. CAP. III.

The Arians, moreover [n: (I, q. I, c, 73)], [0550A] and [nt: In the manuscripts, *caeterosque*.] the other pests of this kind: because we receive their laity, converted to the Lord, under the image [nt: What that phrase *sub imagine poenitentiae*, repeated again at the close of the letter, may mean is a question. The word *imago* will recur also in the following letter, n. 7, where it is declared that the image of the Lord's day and of the sixth day (on which Christ suffered and rose again) is to be renewed every week. But another passage, of Epistle 29, n. 2, contributes more to illustrating this place, where Pelagius and Coelestius are brought in as men furnished, or rather destroyed, by perverse subtleties of words, *disputing under the image of the catholic faith*. For as there it is called an image of the catholic faith because it bears a certain appearance of the catholic faith, so here too what bears a certain image of penance is named an image of penance. And not without reason did Innocent call the imposition of the hand, by which heretics once corrected were formerly joined at once to the faithful, an *image of penance*: because, of course, that imposition carried a certain profession of penance. Wherefore Augustine, in the book on Baptism against the Donatists, c. 2, says aptly to the mind of this pope: *If the imposition of the hand were not applied to those coming from heresy, they would be judged as though they were outside all fault. But on account of the bond of charity, which is the greatest gift of the Holy Spirit, without which whatever other holy things there may be in a man avail nothing for salvation, the hand is laid on heretics who have been corrected.* But this imposition of the hand did not have the reality of penance, that is, squalor of garments, frequent fasts, and the other labours and apparatus of mournful penance to which those were subjected who, having been separated from the assembly of the faithful, were admitted to it again only after long satisfaction. Of this penance, then, it was merely a shadowing and an image. Further, Siricius, Innocent and Pope Vigilius distinguish two kinds of heretics returning to the Church: one of those who, having been baptized among heretics, say Novatians, Donatists or Arians, betake themselves to the Church; the other of those who, having received baptism in the catholic Church, allowed themselves to be rebaptized by those heretics. And to both they prescribe the imposition of the hand, but one which in the first bears only the image of penance, in the last shows the reality. Of the first Siricius speaks thus, Epistle 1, n. 2: *Whom we, with the Novatians and other heretics, as was established in the synod* (of Nicaea), *join to the assembly of Catholics only by the invocation of the sevenfold Spirit, through the imposition of the bishop's hand*. Of the same he says again, Epistle 4, c. 8: *That those coming from the Novatians or the Montenses be received through the imposition of the hand*. But he notes that others are to be distinguished from these by the exception subjoined: *except those whom they rebaptize*. Our Innocent explains the mind of Siricius in Epistle 2, n. 11, where he likewise prescribes, *That those coming from the Novatians or the Montenses be received through the imposition of the hand*: but he subjoins a more explicit exception in this manner: *except those who, if perhaps passing over from us to them, have been rebaptized: these, if they repent and consider their ruin and prefer to return, are to be admitted under a long satisfaction of penance*. The first, therefore, were received *under the image of penance*; because the hand was laid on them for receiving the Holy Spirit, in such a way that no satisfaction of penance followed. But the others were admitted no longer under the image, but under the very reality of penance; because communion was granted them only *under a long satisfaction of penance*, which followed upon the imposition of the hand. Pope Vigilius, plainly distinguishing that twofold imposition of the hand in Epistle 2, to Profuturus (or, as it is corruptly circulated in the printed editions, to Eutherius), n. 4, illustrates and confirms our explanation, where he likewise decrees concerning those who have been rebaptized: *Whose reconciliation, however, is accomplished not through that imposition of the hand which works through the invocation of the Holy Spirit, but through that by which the fruit of penance is acquired and the restoration of holy communion is completed*.] of penance and with the sanctification of the Holy Spirit through the imposition of the hand, it does not seem that their clerics ought to be received with the dignity of the priesthood or of any ministry: since those for whom we allow [nt: The copies of Hadrian's collection: *promittimus*. More correctly Dionysius, with other codices both manuscript and printed: *permittimus*. What Innocent does not now permit, because no necessity underlies it, he acknowledges above, Epistle 16, n. 9 and 10, can be permitted *for necessity and as a remedy*.] baptism alone to be valid, which is indeed performed in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, we do not judge to have the Holy Spirit from that baptism and those mysteries: since, when their authors fell away from the catholic faith, they lost the perfection of the Spirit which they had received. Nor can they give its fullness, which works above all in ordinations, which they lost through what I should call the perfidy of their impiety rather than their [nt: In the printed editions, *quam per fidem*. More fittingly the particle *per* is absent from the manuscripts. It is noted here that the faith of heretics is rightly called perfidy, because, as Hilary observes in the book against Constantius, n. 24, *whatever is beyond the one faith is perfidy, not faith*.] faith. How can it be that we should judge their profane priests worthy of the honours of Christ [nt: To this points what the Donatists, to whom it was promised that the honour of their clerical rank would be kept, object in Augustine, Epistle 185, n. 44: *If then it is right that we should repent of having been outside the Church and against the Church, how after this penance do we remain among them clerics, or even bishops?* To whom Augustine answers: *This would not be done, since in truth, as must be admitted, it ought not to be done, unless it were healed by the compensation of peace itself.* But it ought not to be done, because those are not worthy of the honours of Christ who are not free from reproach.], [0551A] when we receive their laity, imperfect as I said, for receiving the grace of the Holy Spirit, with the image of penance? Let thy gravity therefore bring these things to the knowledge of our fellow bishops, either through a synod, if it can, or through the reading of this letter: so that what thou hast so necessarily inquired, and we have so carefully answered, may be kept by the common consent and zeal of all.

## EPISTLE XXV [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 1. What was 25 is now 50. This letter is presented by the Corbie exemplar written in the sixth century, and by the Codex published by Quesnel, and also by the collections of Dionysius Exiguus, of Hadrian, the Spanish and that of Isidore. By Dionysius it is called the *regular letter*, and is set before all of Innocent's decrees under eight titles, that is I, II, etc., up to VIII. It is cited, with Innocent's name tacitly passed over, by the second council of Seville, held in the year 619, c. 7, and the sixth of Paris, in the year 829, book 2, c. 27; with his name given, by Isidore of Seville, book II of the Ecclesiastical Offices, c. 26, the council of Meaux of the year 845, c. 44, and Nicholas I, epistle otherwise 2, appendix 2.]. From Dionysius Exiguus.



## I.

[nt: Instead of the subjoined titles, which are copied from the exemplars of Dionysius and of the codex published by Quesnel, the old Corbie codex presents these: I. *How one ought to give the peace in church.* II. *On the names to be recited.* III. *On signing infants.* IV. *On the leaven.* V. *On the baptized who are afterwards vexed by a demon.* VI. *On anointing the sick.*] *On giving the kiss of peace after the mysteries have been completed.* -- II. *On not reciting the names before the priest's prayer.* --III. *That the baptized ought not to be signed except by the bishop.* --IV. *That fasting is rightly kept every sabbath.* --V. *On the leaven, that it is to be sent only to the [0551B] presbyters of the city.* --VI. *On baptized energumens.* --VII. *On penitents.* --VIII. *On the epistle of St. James the apostle, in which prayer for the sick is enjoined.*

*From the Colbertine manuscript 932.*

## I.

*That those about to communicate should give one another the peace in church when the mysteries have been completed.* --II. *That the names of those who offer be recited not beforehand, but within the mysteries.* --III. *On signing infants; that it is lawful for bishops alone to give of the consecrated oil.* --IV. *On the leaven on the holy day of Easter, that it ought to be delivered only to those priests who are within the city.* --V. *On the fast of the sabbath.* --VI. *On those who after baptism are seized by a demon.* --VII. *On penitents, what measure ought to be kept about the holy day of Easter.* --VIII. *That [0551C] it is lawful not only for priests but also for all Christians to anoint with the holy oil, in their own need and in that of their own people.* --IX. *That the holy oil, like the other sacraments also, is not to be granted to penitents in the time of penance.*

*INNOCENT TO DECENTIUS, bishop of Eugubium* [nt: One Colb. codex: *Capuano*. Others of good repute: *Egubino*. In the copies of Dionysius, where *Eugubino* is read, *salutem* is added, which was expressed from them in the printed editions too.]*, greeting.*

## 1.

If [n: (Ivo p. 4, c. 67)] the priests of the Lord wished to keep the ecclesiastical institutions whole, as [0552A] they were handed down by the blessed Apostles, there would be no diversity, no variety in the orders and consecrations themselves. But while each one reckons that what is to be held is not what has been handed down, but what has seemed good to him, thence different things are seen to be held or celebrated in different places or churches; and a scandal arises for the peoples, who, not knowing that the ancient traditions have been corrupted by human presumption, think either that the churches do not agree, or that contradiction was introduced [nt: The Corb. manuscript with one Colb.: *aut Apostolis*. Then, with the same, three others: *vel apostolicis ipsis*, not *viris*. Next, one Colb.: *contraria et non utenda inducta*. Already above, in Epistle 17, n. 9, we heard Innocent commending *the ancient rules which, handed down by the Apostles or by apostolic men, the Roman Church keeps*.] by the Apostles or by apostolic men [n: (Dist. 5, c. 11)].



## 2.

For who does not know, or does not observe, that what was handed down to the Roman Church by Peter, the prince of the Apostles, and is kept until now, ought to be kept by all; and that nothing ought to be brought in over it or introduced [0552B] which has no authority, or which seems to take its pattern from elsewhere? especially since it is manifest that throughout all Italy, the Gauls, the Spains, Africa and Sicily, and the islands [nt: Supply *terrasque*. Hilary, writing the book on the Synods to those who were joined with the Gauls in the one communion and fellowship of faith, lists together with them the bishops of the first and second Germany and of the Britains. If to these are added the various provinces of Illyricum, which Innocent lists in Epistle 13, n. 2, when committing his vicariate over them to Rufus, a fairly accurate enumeration will be had of the dioceses which belong to the Roman patriarchate. Of the churches named by this pope, however, there are some who think that the church of Lyons should be excepted, which they judge received its first priest neither from Peter nor from his successors. But the author of this opinion confesses elsewhere that it is unknown to him by whom Pothinus, the first prelate of that same church, was either ordained or sent.] lying between, no one founded churches except those whom the venerable apostle Peter or his successors appointed priests. Or let them read whether in these provinces any other of the Apostles is found, or is read to have taught. And if they do not read it, because they find it nowhere, they must follow what the Roman Church keeps, from which it is not doubtful that they received their beginning; lest, while they are eager for foreign assertions, they be seen to abandon the head of their institutions.

## 3.

It is not doubtful that thy love has often come to the City and [0552C] assembled with us in church, and has come to know what custom it keeps, whether in consecrating the mysteries or in performing the other secret rites. That this would suffice [nt: The word *arbitrarer* is added here in the old editions of the Councils, as in the manuscripts.] for the instruction of thy church, or for its reformation if thy predecessors held anything less or otherwise, we should hold as quite certain, hadst thou not thought that we should be consulted about some matters. [0553A] To these we reply, therefore, not because we believe thou art ignorant of anything, but so that with greater authority thou mayest either instruct thine own, or, if any stray from the institutions of the Roman Church, either admonish them or not delay to report them, so that we may be able to know who they are that either bring in novelties or think that the custom of another church than the Roman is to be kept.

## CAP. I.



## 4.

Thou assertest, then, that some enjoin the peace upon the peoples before the mysteries have been completed [n: (De consec. dist. 2, c. 9)], or that the priests give it to one another among themselves, whereas the peace must [nt: Merlin and several manuscripts: *necessaria*. The usage of the churches was not one in this matter. In the Constitutions of the Apostles published under the name of Clement, book II, c. 61, the kiss of peace follows the *oblation*. Indeed the kiss of peace, as Justin Martyr testifies, Apology 2, to Antoninus Pius, preceded the oblation itself. *When the prayers are finished*, he says, *we greet one another with a kiss. Then bread and a cup of water and wine are offered to him who presides over the brethren.* The Laodicene synod, can. 19, states that rite thus: *After the bishop has given the kiss of peace to the presbyters, then the laity shall give the peace to one another, and so the holy oblation shall be celebrated.* In the Mozarabic Missal, for the Sunday of Advent, the peace is prescribed to be proclaimed and offered to the peoples before the Illation, or, as we say, the preface, *that they may be fit to be present at the most holy mysteries of God*. For the same reason, in certain others, for instance of the church of Salisbury, the priest is bidden to offer the kiss of peace to the deacon and subdeacon, *that they may be fit for the ministry of the most holy altar of the Lord*, and so before the sacred mysteries are celebrated. With these agrees the old Gallican Order, in which a *collect for the peace*, or, after the peace has been given, is everywhere set before the Illation. Noteworthy is that caution and reverence of Innocent, by which, even when writing to a bishop, he says of the mysteries, *which I ought not to disclose*. This certainly leaves no room for the novel opinion of the heterodox about those mysteries.] necessarily be enjoined after all the things which I ought not to disclose, so that by it it may be established that the people has given its consent to all that is done in the mysteries and celebrated in the church, and that these are shown to be finished by the seal of the concluding peace.

## CAP. II.



## 5.

As for reciting the names *(De consecr. [0553B]dist. I, c. ult.)* before the priest makes [0554A] his prayer and by his prayer commends the oblations of those whose names are to be recited, how superfluous this is thou thyself recognizest in thy prudence: that thou shouldst put forward beforehand the name of one whose victim thou dost not yet offer to God, although nothing is unknown to him. First [nt: Merlin: *Prius enim oblationes sunt commendandae, ac tunc nomina edicenda*. But in one Colb. manuscript, after the word *commendandae*, there is subjoined: *Et nomina offerentium non ante nisi infra mysteria recitentur. Ac tunc eorum oblationes sunt edicendae, ut inter sacra*, etc., doubtless from the marginal note of a scholiast, who, it is clear, read in his exemplar, as is found in the other Colb., *quorum sunt oblationes edicendae*, for *quorum sunt edicenda*. That reading would favour the custom which Jerome, on chapter 18 of Ezekiel, thought should be censured in these words: *We see many who oppress by power, or commit thefts, so that out of much they may give a little to the poor, and glory in their crimes, and the deacon publicly recites the names of those who offer:* SO MUCH SHE OFFERS, SO MUCH HE HAS PROMISED, *and they are pleased with themselves at the applause of the people*. But it is plain that Innocent does not approve boasting of this kind, since he wishes the oblations of the faithful to be commended to *God*, not to the people, and not by the public recitation of the deacon but by the silent *prayer* of the priest. His precept was renewed by the council of Frankfurt in the year 794, by a decree issued *on not reciting the names before the oblation is offered*. For what this synod there calls the *oblation*, Innocent above calls the *prayer*. And by each of those names is understood what we call the *Canon*. Hence Gregory the Great, in the same epistle to John, otherwise 64, Indiction 2, where he first mentioned that it had been complained of by some *that he had decreed that the Lord's Prayer be said soon after the* CANON, then adds: *We say the Lord's Prayer soon after the* PRAYER *for this reason*. Socrates, book vii, c. 25, also names the same part of the sacred mysteries εὐχάς. Wherefore Innocent and the synod of Frankfurt do not approve those who, with the Eastern church, *recited the names* before the canon, or, as Cyprian says in Epistle 10, to his clergy, *offered the names*. That usage, however, which they disapprove, was received afterwards too in the Gauls and the Spains, as the ancient Gallican order and the Mozarabic Missal bear witness.], therefore, the oblations are to be commended, and then the names of those whose they are are to be proclaimed; so that they may be named among the sacred mysteries, not among the other things which we set before them, so that by the mysteries themselves we may open the way for the prayers to come.

## CAP. III.



## 6.

As for signing infants, it is manifest that it is lawful for this to be done by no other than the bishop [n: (De consecr. dist. 4, c. 119)]. For presbyters, although they are priests in the [nt: In the printed editions *secundi* is lacking: which we restore on the authority of the older Corb. manuscript and of two others. To this points that saying of Optatus, book I: *Why should I mention deacons in the third, presbyters in the second priesthood? The bishops themselves are the summits and princes of all.*] second place, nevertheless do not have the summit of the pontificate [n: (Ivo p. 1, c. 265)]. But that this [0554B] [nt: So the manuscripts just mentioned. But other books: *Haec autem pontificibus solis deberi, ut vel consignent*. Theologians are accustomed to interpret this saying of Innocent of ordinary law. And Jerome indeed, in the dialogue against the Luciferians, teaches that the custom by which *in the Church one who is baptized does not receive the Holy Spirit except through the hands of the bishop was instituted rather for the honour of the priesthood than for the necessity of law*. And not only does the same Doctor seem to claim the right of signing for presbyters, but he also hints, in the letter to Evangelus, that its exercise is sometimes permitted them, where he says: *For what does a bishop do, ordination excepted, which a presbyter does not do?* With which agrees what Chrysostom writes of bishops, homily II on the first epistle to Timothy: *For they are superior by ordination alone, and in this only do they seem to have more than presbyters.* Gregory indeed writes, book IV, epist. 9, to Januarius, bishop of Cagliari in Sardinia: *Let presbyters anoint those to be baptized on the breast, so that bishops may afterwards anoint them on the forehead.* But, ibid., epistle otherwise 24, he writes back to the same: *It has also reached us that some were scandalized because we forbade presbyters to touch with chrism those who are to be baptized.* To whom he first answers, *And we indeed did so according to the ancient usage of our church*; then he adds, *But if some are altogether saddened about this matter, we grant that, where bishops are lacking, presbyters may touch with chrism those to be baptized even on their foreheads.* Nor is Gelasius opposed to these, when in epist. 12, c. 6, he enjoins presbyters *not to seize for themselves the faculty of making chrism, nor of applying the pontifical signing*. For it is one thing to seize such a faculty for oneself, and another to use a power that has been granted. In the commentaries published under the name of Ambrose, on Ephesians c. IV, one reads: *In Egypt the presbyters sign, if the bishop is not present.* With which agrees also what the ancient author of the questions on both Testaments, mixed together, in Augustine, appendix to vol. III, cap. 101, relates: *In Alexandria and through the whole of Egypt, if the bishop is lacking, the presbyter consecrates* (more correctly the Colb. manuscript, *consignat*). And this usage still obtains among the Greeks. Nor does the first council of Toledo, can. 20, seem at variance with it, by which *it was established that the deacon should not chrismate, but the presbyter, in the absence of the bishop; when he is present, however, only if it has been enjoined by him*; nor the first of Orange, can. 11, and that of Epaon, can. 16, when they wish *heretics placed in danger of death, if they desire to be catholics, and if a bishop is lacking, to be signed by presbyters with blessing and chrism*. As for Innocent, both from this decree and from the preceding decrees and from the proem of the whole letter it appears that what he wished above all to guard against was that the churches which received the faith from the Roman should decline to rites alien to the custom of that same church. Deferring to him, the second council of Seville, held in the year 619, c. 7, defines that it is not lawful for presbyters *either to make chrism, or to sign the forehead of the baptized with chrism*. In imitation of him also Theodulf, bishop of Orléans, in the book on Baptism, c. 17, concludes: *That it is lawful for presbyters to anoint the baptized with chrism, but not lawful to hand on the Holy Spirit through the imposition of the hand, this ancient custom was handed down by the Apostles* (that is, by the example of the Apostles recorded in the Acts). And this power is denied not to presbyters alone but also to chorepiscopi, both by the sixth council of Paris, in the year 827, part II, c. 27, and by that of Meaux, in the year 843; c. 44. Wherefore Isaac, bishop of Langres, title XI, can. 30, decrees: *If anyone has been confirmed not by a bishop but by a chorepiscopus, he ought to be confirmed over again with other blessings.* The Capitulary of the year 803 too, in Baluze, p. 381, declares the same thing, as resting on the authority of Leo III, who had been consulted beforehand.] pontifical power is due to bishops alone, namely either to sign or to hand on the Paraclete Spirit, is demonstrated not only [0555A] by ecclesiastical custom, but also by that reading of the Acts of the Apostles which asserts that Peter and John were sent to hand on the Holy Spirit to those already baptized. For it is lawful for presbyters, when they baptize, whether apart from the bishop or with the bishop present, to anoint the baptized with chrism, but chrism which has been consecrated by the bishop; yet not to sign the forehead with the same oil, which is due to bishops alone, when they hand on the Paraclete Spirit. But to say [nt: Hence it is concluded that the sacrament of Confirmation was accomplished not by the anointing of the forehead alone but also by prescribed formulas of certain words. But while Innocent deliberately keeps silent about these words, he makes plain how great was the scruple of the ancients against revealing the mysteries of the sacraments. Examples of this are more frequent when they speak of the Eucharist.] the words I cannot, lest I should seem rather to betray than to answer the consultation.

## CAP. IV.



## 7.

That on the sabbath [n: (De consecr. dist. 3, c. 13)] one must fast, a most evident reason demonstrates. For [n: (Ivo p. 4, c. 28)] if we celebrate the Lord's day on account of the venerable resurrection of our Lord Jesus [0555B] Christ not only at Easter, but also through the several cycles of the weeks repeat the image of that very day, and fast on the sixth day on account of the Lord's passion, we ought not to pass over the sabbath, [nt: In the manuscripts, *qui* (Quesnel here adds *dies*) *inclusus*. Consult, if you please, the special Dissertation of the same Quesnel, at the end of the works of Leo, *on the fast of the sabbath in the Roman church*.] which is seen to be enclosed between the sorrow and the joy of that time. For it is certainly agreed that the Apostles on those two days were both in grief and hid themselves for fear of the Jews. And it is certainly not doubtful that they fasted on the two days mentioned [n: (Joan. XX)] to such a degree that the tradition of the Church holds that [nt: In the Capitularies, book vii, c. 430, where it is read, *For since the canons command it, the sacraments ought not to be celebrated on these two days*, we may conjecture, not rashly, that this passage is in view. The council of Rouen of the year 1072, c. 22, records that this very custom was prescribed in the pontifical book.] on these two days [0556A] the sacraments are not celebrated at all. Which form [nt: Supply *jejunii ab Apostolis servati*. For the nearby mention of the sacraments not celebrated on the two days before the resurrection of the Lord is made only in passing, as added solely to assert the fast of the sabbath, and the very sequence of the sentence confirms this. Socrates indeed says, book v, c. 22: *Although all the churches throughout the world celebrate the sacred mysteries on the sabbath day every week, the Alexandrians and the Romans, following an ancient tradition, refuse to do this.* But the liturgies of Gregory and of Gelasius, which prescribe Masses even on the sabbaths of Lent, suggest otherwise. Nor would Jerome, Epistle 28, to Lucinius, have written: *As to what thou askest about the sabbath, whether one must fast on it; and about the Eucharist, whether it is to be received daily, which the Roman churches are said to observe*, unless the sacred mysteries, from which the Eucharist is received, had been celebrated daily. Augustine, Epistle 54, to Januarius, n. 4, reports the same usage of receiving the Eucharist daily. From which it follows that the divine mysteries too were celebrated daily. For the Eucharist was not commonly received outside their celebration, except by the sick. What we have just cited Socrates perhaps said because the Romans and the Alexandrians, having transferred the solemnity of the sabbath to the Lord's day, refused to celebrate both days with equal reverence.] is certainly to be kept through each of the weeks, because the commemoration of that day is always to be celebrated. But if they think that one must fast once, and on a single sabbath; then both the [nt: In the manuscripts, *Dominicum... celebrandum*. But very badly in Merlin and Crabbe, *Dominica... celebrandum*.] Lord's day and the sixth day will certainly have to be celebrated once, at Easter. But if the image of the Lord's day and of the sixth day is to be renewed each week, it is the part of a madman to keep the custom of the two days with the sabbath passed over; since it has no unlike cause, namely from the sixth day, on which the Lord suffered, when he was also in the underworld, so that rising on the third day he might restore joy after the preceding two days' sorrow. We do not, then, deny that one must fast on the sixth day; but we say that this is to be done on the sabbath too, because both days imposed sorrow on the Apostles, or on [0556B] those who followed Christ. And they, made glad on the Lord's day, not only wished it to be most festive, but also judged that it should be kept again and again through all the weeks.

## CAP. V.



## 8.

As for the leaven which we send through the titles on the Lord's day, thou hast wished to consult us superfluously, since all our churches are established within the city. Because their presbyters cannot assemble with us on that day on account of the people entrusted to them, they therefore receive the leaven [nt: In one Corb. manuscript *a nobis confectum* is lacking. By the name of leaven some understand here blessed bread which serves in place of the Eucharist, but others, far more numerous, the Eucharist itself. And indeed our Mabillon observes that it is quite often designated in the Roman Order by the word leaven. Nor need we fear that by this latter understanding the opinion of those is helped who think that the Eucharist was formerly made from leavened bread in the Latin Church. For the argument drawn from here in favour of this opinion is very light. For that venerable sacrament, according to the custom of those times, by which they were careful to hide the secret of the sacred thing from the profane, was aptly called leaven when it was sent to the churches, so that it might signify that, as leaven put into flour and mixed with it binds the whole together, so it was for them a certain bond of union and of mutual communion. For that reason Christ himself too, even apart from the sacrament of the Eucharist, is called *leaven* in the encyclical codex of the letters sent to the emperor Leo, epist. 37, vol. IV of the Councils of Labbe, p. 920, and on that account the council of Chalcedon is praised by the bishops of maritime Phoenicia, because it destroyed *those who monstrously believe that our* LEAVEN *is not from the virgin Mother of God*. But that we should not think that bare eulogia are understood by Innocent under the word leaven, his reason subjoined forbids, by which he teaches that this rite of sending the leaven suits neither the church of Eugubium nor the others which did not have several titles within one city, *because, of course, the sacraments are not to be carried far*. For if anyone were to deny that the eulogia, or blessed bread, could be carried farther without any distinction, he would be refuted by the letters both of Paulinus of Nola in Augustine, 24 n. 6, 25 n. 5, and 32 n. 5, and of Augustine himself, epistle formerly 34, now 31, n. 9, in which we read that those holy men sent one another blessed loaves from Italy into Africa, and in turn, as a symbol of unity or of mutual communion. The usage of sending the leaven to the churches, which was perhaps peculiar to the Roman city, is attributed to Melchiades, as was observed above, p. 339, where we also discussed this matter more fully.] made by us [0557A] through acolytes, so that they may not judge themselves separated from our communion, especially on that day. That this ought to be done through the [nt: The printed editions: *parochias*. In the copies of Dionysius and Hadrian, *paroecias* or *parroecias*. In the very old Corbie codex, *proicias*, doubtless for *paroicias*. From which one may gather that it was not at first, but in the course of time, that copyists came to write *parochias* or *parrochias*. By this name Innocent here understands the churches situated outside the episcopal city. That among the Romans the cemeteries too stood outside the cities, since it was against their religion to bury bodies within the walls, no one is unaware.] parishes I do not think; because the sacraments are not to be carried far (nor do we send them to the presbyters stationed throughout the various cemeteries) [nt: Quesnel, after the old editions of the Councils and also the manuscripts of the Spanish collection and of the Isidorian collection: *ut et presbyteri eorum*. Two manuscripts: *ut precibus eorum*. Pith.: *et precibus eorum*. The reading afterwards published is both the sounder and that of the better manuscripts. To the first reason, by which he had taught that it was not fitting for the sacraments to be carried into the parishes, Innocent now adds another, by which he shows that this is not even necessary.] and the presbyters have the right and the licence of making them.

## CAP. VI.



## 9.

As for those baptized who afterwards [0558A] are seized by a demon, through some vice or sin intervening, thy love [nt: So some manuscripts of good repute. But Pith., Dionysius and the Isidorian collection: *quaesivit dilectio*.] is anxious whether they can or ought to be [nt: So the old Corbie codex, with the ancient exemplars of the Codex published by Quesnel, and also another Colb. manuscript of good repute. So too Dionysius seems to have read, whose Royal copy still has *assignari*. In the other books, however, *consignari* stands. Then the Roman edition: *quod hoc nisi episcopis praecipere*. Quesnel: *quod nisi*, suppressing *hoc* against the agreement of all the codices. The sentence will be whole if before that *quod* the word *censeo* is supplied, or if, with the Royal exemplar of Dionysius and some others, *Quod hoc nisi episcopum praecipere non liceat* is preferred. Joh. Morin, in a certain posthumous work, supposes that Innocent in this place gave deacons, when necessity presses, the power of administering Confirmation: but his editor Moret rightly observes that there is no talk here of that sacrament. The word *consignari*, by which the sacrament of Confirmation is expressed above, deceived that learned man. The question raised was plainly about *the baptized*, that is, the faithful, who were seized by a demon not in the very receiving of baptism but *afterwards*. That in this age the sacrament of Confirmation was customarily conferred on those coming out of the sacred font of baptism, neither the learned Morin nor anyone skilled in antiquity is unaware: and this is taught by Tertullian, book on Baptism, c. 7 and 8, Cyprian, epist. 73, to Jubaianus, Ambrose, book on the Mysteries, c. 7, Jerome, Dialogue against the Luciferians, Augustine, Serm. 324, Pope Leo, Serm. 4 on the Nativity of the Lord, and long afterwards Magnus, bishop of Sens, in the booklet which he composed on the mystery of baptism at the command of Charles the Great, and which our Edmond Martène published in the Ancient Rites of the Church, vol. I, p. 160. In which work we read: *When all the sacraments of baptism have been completed, last of all, through the imposition of the hand by the high priest, they receive the Holy Spirit of sevenfold grace*. Besides, there is in this whole chapter not a word about the Holy Spirit, though Innocent is not accustomed to be silent about him where he speaks of the sacrament of Confirmation. Finally, he who in the preceding chapter 3, n. 6, asserted that *this is due to pontiffs alone, either to sign or to hand on the Holy Spirit*, and denied this power to presbyters, was far from granting the same to deacons. It is evident, therefore, that the rites by which one who is seized by a demon is succoured are explained here. These rites our Edmond Martène explains at length, Ancient Rites of the Church, book III, c. 9. Expressly, among those which he describes from an old Royal codex 3866, and another of Vendôme, ibid. p. 366, it is prescribed that the priest to whom the energumen has been brought should receive the confession of all his sins, *lay his hand on the top of the sick man's head, and make the cross three times*, and after many prayers *sign him on the forehead, on the eyes, on the ears, on the nostrils, on the breast, on the mouth, on the feet, on both hands*, a blessing proper to each member being applied. Wherefore there is scarcely any room for doubting that Innocent marks this last rite by the word *designari*. Yet I make no objection to *consignari* being kept: nor do I deny that this favours that reading, that in the codices cited, copied out by our Martène, p. 520, a twofold formula of signing is given, and the latter is headed: *Another signing of the head, and of the eyes, or of all the members*. Care must be taken, however, lest the word *consignandi* be thought to be used here in the sense in which it is used above, cap. 3. Perhaps it was to distinguish the two things that Innocent used the verb *consignandi* above and *designandi* now. But whether thou preferest *designari* or *consignari* here, neither seems to have to mean anything other than simple *signari*.] marked by a presbyter or deacon. This, unless the bishop has enjoined it, is not lawful. For the hand is not to be laid on them at all, unless the bishop has given authority to do it. But that it be done, it is the bishop's part to command that the hand be laid [nt: The Corb. manuscript: *imponantur*. That imposition of the hand was a certain kind of penance. And whatever belongs to public penance has always been subject in the Church to the power of bishops. Where the bishop was present, the hand was laid on penitents both by him and by his clergy. Hence Cyprian, in Epistle 10, to his clergy, complains that *whereas in lesser sins sinners do penance for a long time, and according to the order of discipline come to confession, and through the imposition of the hand of the bishop and clergy receive the right of communion; now at an unripe time... before penance has been done, before confession has been made, before the hand has been laid on them by the bishop and clergy, the Eucharist is given them*. But the bishop, when he was absent, and not even necessity pressed, permitted this very thing to presbyters or even to deacons. Wherefore the same Cyprian writes to his clergy, Epistle 13: *If they are overtaken by any misfortune and the danger of sickness, without waiting for our presence, before any presbyter who is at hand, or, if a presbyter is not found and death begins to press, before a deacon too, they may make confession of their sin, so that, the hand being laid on them unto penance, they may come to the Lord with peace*. To this points what Regino, book I, c. 295 and 296, and Burchard, book X, c. 155 and 156, bring forward as from the Roman Penitential: *For as none but bishops and presbyters ought to offer the sacrifice, so neither ought others to usurp the judgments of penitents. But if necessity arises, and a presbyter is not present, let a deacon receive the penitent to holy communion*. And these things indeed were said of reconciling a penitent where danger of death threatens: but Innocent defines that a certain power regarding energumens can be granted by the bishop which seems to belong not to the presbyter or the deacon alone, but also *to the other clerics*, among whom the lowest is the order of exorcists. Which is to be understood thus, that the inferior clerics omitted those rites which it is granted to priests alone to perform.] on them either by a presbyter or by the other clerics. For how [0559A] can it be done without great labour that an energumen situated far away be brought to the bishop, since, if such a mishap befalls him on the journey, he can neither easily be carried on to the bishop nor carried back to his own?

## CAP. VII.



## 10.

As for penitents, who do penance whether for graver offences or for lighter ones, if no sickness intervenes, the custom of the Roman Church shows that remission is to be given them on the fifth day of the week before Easter [n: (De consecr. dist. 3, c. 17; Ivo p. 15, c. 40; Isaac Lingon. c. 15)]. But as to estimating the weight of the offences, it is for the priest to judge, so that he may pay heed to the confession of the penitent, and to the weeping and tears of one amending himself, and then order him to be released when he has seen his fitting [nt: So two Colb. manuscripts. But other books: *satisfactionem. Sane si quis*. How anxious the ancients were that penitents should neither be absolved before fitting satisfaction, nor depart from the world without reconciliation and communion, Cyprian bears witness in the passages just cited, to pass over others. There too that holy martyr mentions penance done *for lesser sins*; but we may think, not rashly, that these were called lesser and lighter in comparison with graver ones, although they too were deadly.] satisfaction. Or if anyone has fallen into sickness, and [0559B] has come to the point of despair, he is to be released before the time of Easter, lest he depart from the world without communion.

## CAP. VIII.



## 11.

Indeed, since thy love wished to consult about this, as about the rest, my son [0560A] the deacon Coelestinus also added in his letter that thy love had put forward that which is written in the epistle of the blessed Apostle James: *If anyone among you is sick* [nt: So six of the better manuscripts, which in expressing this passage agree almost entirely with the ancient exemplar of the Italic version. But the printed editions: *Infirmatur quis in vobis? Inducat presbyteros Ecclesiae*: except that the word *Ecclesiae* is not found in Merlin, as in the remaining manuscripts. Then two Colb.: *ut orent pro eo*: Pith.: *et orent pro eo*.]*; let him call the presbyters, and let them pray over him, anointing him with oil in the name of the Lord: and the prayer* [nt: One of the Colb. manuscripts, with Pith. and the cited exemplar of the Italic version: *in fide*: and then, with the older ones, *salvabit laborantem*: where in the printed editions, *salvabit infirmum*.] *of faith shall save him who labours, and the Lord shall raise him up,* [nt: The printed editions: *si in peccatis fuerit, remittentur ei*: they are corrected by the authority of the better manuscripts, in three of which *si peccata fecit* stands. Then in Dionysius *remittetur ei*, as it is read also in the ancient exemplar of the Italic version.] *and if he has committed sin, he shall forgive him* [n: (Jacob. V, 24)]. There is no doubt that this ought to be taken or understood of the faithful who are sick, who can be anointed with the holy oil of chrism, [nt: Crabbe and the other editions after him: *quo ab episcopo confecto*, Merlin and the manuscripts resisting. Our Edmond Martène, vol. II of the Ancient Rites of the Church, p. 114, proves that among the Greeks that oil is customarily made by presbyters as well.] which, made by the bishop, it is lawful not only for priests but also for all Christians to use, in their own need or in that of their own, for [nt: Quesnel: *ungendo*. The Roman edition: *ad ungendum*. The rest: *inungendo*. More truly the manuscripts: *ungendum*, or, as it is written in most, *unguendum*. Nor indeed is it unusual for grammarians to join a fourth case to the verb *uti*, as may be seen in these words of Plautus: *Caetera quae utimur*. Moreover, those stray from Innocent's mind who think that in this place he attributes to all Christians the right of anointing both themselves and their own with the holy oil, and declare all the faithful to be ministers of this sacrament. He only refutes the opinion of those who had perhaps then persuaded themselves that the use of the holy oil was so proper to the priests by whom it is made that no others were to be anointed with it; and he teaches that it is made by them not so that they alone may be anointed with it, but all Christians too. For here there is not yet any talk of the minister of the anointing, but only of him on whom it can be conferred. Then, proceeding to the other question, namely whether it is so proper to presbyters to administer this sacrament that it is not lawful for bishops to confer it, he refutes the false opinion of some on that matter, about which Decentius had thought he should consult him. And indeed this question of Decentius plainly shows how far it was from the belief of those times that any and every Christian could anoint the sick with the sacred oil.] anointing. But [n: (Dist. 95, c. 9; Ivo p. 2, c. 75)] we see that this was added superfluously, that doubt should be raised about the bishop concerning a thing which, it is not doubtful, [0560B] is lawful for presbyters. For it was said to [nt: The Roman edition and that of the Councils, after Crabbe, with one Colb. manuscript: *de presbyteris*. Merlin with the other manuscripts: *presbyteris*, which we so understand that *licere* is understood with it. Nor is what stands in Quesnel displeasing, *presbyteros*, with this meaning, namely: *for this reason it was said* (by James) *presbyters*, supplying *vocet*.] presbyters for this reason, that bishops, hindered by other occupations, cannot go to all the sick. But if the bishop either is able, or deems someone worthy to be visited by him, he may both bless and [nt: Quesnel: *et ungere*. Although above, n. 6, we read *chrismate baptizatos ungere licet*: yet here in no old codex have we found *ungere*. In two manuscripts *tangere* is lacking; and in one of them, for *chrismate*, there stands *chrismare*: with which reading agrees this of the first council of Toledo, can. 2, where it is laid down that *a deacon may not chrismate*. Gregory, book 4, epist. 26, to Januarius, uses the expression *to touch with chrism* once and a second time.] touch with chrism; without hesitation [0561A] he can, he whose office it is to make the chrism. For this cannot be poured on penitents, because it is a kind of [nt: In one of the Colb. manuscripts, *sacramentorum*. In favour of which is the fact that *sacratorum* is read in the old Corbie. That this reading is faulty, however, and that the anointing of the sick is reckoned by Innocent not only among sacred things but also among the sacraments, is plain from what is subjoined. And where he denies this sacrament to those penitents to whom the rest of the sacraments are denied, he teaches plainly enough that it was then received usage that it be granted to other faithful who were sick without danger of death. For he prescribes above, n. 10, that the rest of the sacraments be conferred without delay on penitents too, if they have fallen into a desperate sickness. But the Greeks, as Peter Arcudius testifies, book v, c. 4, went so far as to anoint, applying the form of the sacrament too, not only the sick who labour under no dangerous illness, but also *penitents in good health, no differently from the sick*.] sacrament. For to those to whom the rest of the sacraments are denied, how is it thought that one kind can be granted?



## 12.

To all these things, then, dearest brother, which thy love wished us to set forth, we have taken care to answer as far as we were able, so that thy Church may be able to preserve and guard the Roman custom, from which it draws its origin. The rest, which it was not right to write, we shall be able to declare when asked, once thou art present. And it will be for the power of the Lord to bring this about too: that thou instruct well both thy Church and our clerics who serve the divine offices under thy pontificate, and give to others a pattern which they ought to imitate. [0561B] Given on the fourteenth day before the Kalends of April [n: (Martii XIX, ann. 416)], Theodosius Augustus for the seventh time and Palladius [nt: The printed editions: *viris clarissimis*. More correctly the manuscripts: *v. c.*, that is, *viro clarissimo*: which applies to Palladius alone. But where the notaries wish to express *viris clarissimis*, they double the same abbreviation in this manner: *vv. cc.*, which is then understood to be predicated of two consuls.], a most illustrious man, being consuls.

## MONITUM ON THE FOLLOWING EPISTLES.



## 1.

The six following letters are presented by a great many old codices examined in the newest edition of Augustine. The same, together with the following 32, are presented by the exemplars not only of the collection of Isidore, but also of the Spanish collection, and also of the Codex published by Quesnel. Augustine himself, epistle formerly 106, now 186, num. 2, mentions them one by one in this manner: « So reports were sent on this matter from two councils, that of Carthage and that of Milevis, to the apostolic see . . . We also wrote to Pope Innocent of blessed memory, besides the reports of the councils, a familiar letter, in which we dealt with the cause itself at somewhat greater length. To all of it he wrote back to us in the manner which was right and fitting for the apostolic [0561C] see. » And expressly concerning the letters of Innocent he says this, book I against Julian, c. 4: « For what could that holy man answer to the African councils, except what the apostolic see and the Roman Church, with the others, has held perseveringly from of old? » Then, having copied out certain sentences which Innocent sets forth in those letters, he presses Julian thus: « Answer him, or rather the Lord himself, whose testimony that prelate used. » Likewise, book II against the Two Letters of the Pelagians, c. 4, n. 6, where he has brought forward some words of Epistle 29, he exclaims: « What is clearer than this judgment of the apostolic see? » And again, num. 7, having set out other words from Epistle 30, he adds: « What does the ungrateful man say to this? » So he calls Coelestius, whom there too, n. 5, he convicts of deceit, since he afterwards answered falsely before Zosimus that he agreed with these letters of Innocent. With so many and such evident testimonies Henry Noris, Hist. Pelag. p. 74, refutes the censure of Vossius, who after Erasmus calls into doubt « whether these letters are worthy of so great a prelate, » [0561D] and at the same time confutes the opinion of a certain more recent writer, who seems likewise to hesitate about their genuineness. We pass over in silence Prosper, Pope Celestine and the other [0562A] writers who ascribe those letters to Innocent in express words. Nor is there need to explain at greater length why he by no means discourses more fully on so abundant and so ample a matter, since he himself has given the reason for this.

## 2.

What the cause was for the Africans to write to Innocent, they themselves did not keep silent. They testify that they desire, in so grave a storm as Pelagius and Coelestius had stirred up, to be made more certain about the truth of the faith; and that their own sentence be approved by the judgment of Innocent; and that in this way the firm authority of the apostolic see be added to their statutes. Then at last they indicate that they hope that those who hold such perverse and pernicious opinions will more easily yield to the authority of the same see, as being « drawn from the authority of the holy Scriptures [n: (epist. 27, n. 3)] ».

## 3.

How much the Africans judge should be deferred to the apostolic see, they show clearly in their letters. From this Innocent too seized the occasion of extolling it. If it should seem to anyone that in this he exceeded measure, Augustine will refute him, [0562B] in whose judgment, as we saw above, « he wrote back to all of it in the manner which was right and fitting for a prelate of the apostolic see. » Or are Augustine and the other Africans to be thought to have approved only what Innocent judged concerning Pelagius, Coelestius and their associates, and not likewise what he says by way of preface, in Epistles 29 and 30, about the prerogative of his see? That these things too were approved by them is gathered from what their successors write in the opening of the letter which, sent by three councils of Africa to Pope Theodore, was afterwards read out in the Lateran council under Martin I. For there whatever Innocent, epist 29, maintains about the dignity of the Roman Church, they repeat in their own name in the same sentences, and all but entirely in the same words [n: (Lab. t. 6, p. 118)]. But nothing more illustrious can be wished for to commend the pre-eminence of the apostolic see than what Augustine, on receiving the letters of Innocent, openly proclaimed, serm. 131, n. 10: « For already two councils have been sent on this cause [0562C] to the apostolic see; rescripts too have come from there: the cause is finished; would that the error may at some time be finished! » That is, the Pelagian cause was diligently examined and judged in two African councils. And since the judgment of the apostolic see also agrees with them, the cause, decided by a threefold judgment of so great authority, is rightly judged to be finished.

## 4.

So Augustine preached, when he was still uncertain whether the Pelagians would abide by those rescripts or not; and so when they had recently arrived in Africa. Now it has been observed in the new edition of Augustine that that Sermon is headed in an old codex « preached on the ninth day before the Kalends of October, on the Lord's day », and that in the year 417 the ninth day before the Kalends of October fell on a Sunday. From which it also follows that those rescripts are not more recent than the month of September of the year 417. But their date remains certain, preserved in manuscripts of the best repute, in which they are dated « on the sixth day before the Kalends of February, after the consulship of Theodosius for the seventh time and Junius for the fourth, V. C., » that is, on the 27th day of the month of January of the year 417. Nor does this other date, which some codices present, make that date [0562D] doubtful, but rather confirms it: « on the sixth day before the Kalends of February, Honorius and Constantius, V. C., being consuls. » For [0563A] in fact Honorius and Constantius held the consulship in the year 417. And we shall judge, not rashly, that this change came about partly through the work of a copyist, who noted in the margin, in place of the consuls of the past year, the consuls of that very year, whom Innocent perhaps did not then know, and partly through the simplicity of a scribe, who, removing the original reading, carried that annotation into the text. Further, of the seven following letters, the first was written a little before 27 and 28, but was sent together with them by the same Julius, an African bishop, as the year 416 was running out. The four following, however, were dated on one day and were delivered by the same bishop Julius, who carried them. That these too were delivered to the Africans before the month of October of the year 417 is concluded from the note prefixed to Augustine's Sermon 131, as we pointed out before.

## 5.

Peter Marca, book VII On the Concord, c. 12, n. 5, and John Garnier, in the Dissertation on the synods held in the Pelagian cause, assert that Innocent wrote back to the Africans from some Roman synod: but Ant. Pagi, at the year 416, n. 19, denies this, and confirms his opinion [0563B] with the words of Augustine, book IV to Boniface. But that twofold opinion may be reconciled without much trouble. For if, when the Pelagians boasted that the dogmas of Pelagius and Coelestius had been condemned without the gathering of a synod, Augustine, book IV against the Two Letters of the Pelagians, c. 12, n. 31, does not deny this to them; the same Doctor, in the same work, book II, c. 3, answers the same heretics, who were accusing the Roman clerics of prevarication: « If, which God forbid, judgment had then been given in the Roman church concerning Coelestius or Pelagius in such a way that those dogmas of theirs, which Pope Innocent had condemned in them and with them, were pronounced worthy to be approved and held; from this rather would the brand of prevarication have to be burned upon the Roman clerics. » But there would be nothing for which these clerics could be blamed, if Innocent had written against Pelagius and Coelestius without their knowledge. That holy pope, therefore, composed these letters without the gathering of a synod of bishops summoned from different provinces, but not without a gathering of the Roman [0563C] clerics: and in this way opinions that seem to conflict are reconciled; and the perpetual custom [0564A] is confirmed by which, as we have seen hitherto, the Roman pontiffs, when consulted by the Churches, were accustomed to answer only when at least the assembly of their clerics had been gathered.



## EPISTLE XXVI. OF THE COUNCIL OF CARTHAGE TO INNOCENT. *That the authority of the apostolic see may be added to their sentence, by which they condemned the impieties of Pelagius and Coelestius. And finally, if those men deny that these are theirs, anathema is decreed against any who assert them.*

To the most blessed and most honourable lord, the holy brother, Pope INNOCENT, AURELIUS, NUMIDIUS, RUSTICIANUS, [nt: So the manuscripts, one excepted, in which *Fidentianus*. But the printed editions: *Fidentius*, with the Conference of Carthage favouring it, in which three bishops are read who bore the name of Fidentius.] FIDENTINUS, EVAGRIUS, [nt: In Crabbe, in the margin: *Antonius*: which the manuscripts also have here. But the letter by which Innocent wrote back to him is addressed, in the Corb. and Germ. manuscripts, *Antonio*, not *Antonino*.] ANTONIUS, PALATINUS, ADEODATUS, VINCENTIUS, PUBLIANUS, THEASIUS, TUTUS, [nt: The Roman edition: *Pantonius*, the manuscripts resisting. In the Conference of Carthage, cap. 126, *Pannonius, bishop of the people of Puppita* is read.] PANNONIUS, VICTOR, RESTITUTUS, another RESTITUTUS, RUSTICUS, FORTUNATIANUS, AMPELIUS, [nt: In the Corb. and Germ. manuscripts the name of this bishop is lacking, who in the same manuscripts is called *Ambivius* in Innocent's rescript, and is noted in the Conference of Carthage, c. 133, as *bishop of Pisita*. But in Quesnel, as in the Pith. and Colb. manuscripts and those of the Isidorian collection, he is called *Avivius*, and in Merlin and Crabbe, as in the Roman edition, *Avinius*.] [0564B] AMBIVIUS, FELIX, DONATIANUS, ADEODATUS, OCTAVIUS, SEROTINUS, [nt: In one manuscript, *Majorianus*. Then the Roman edition, after Merlin, *Porturianus*; next, the same edition, *Trispulus*; Merlin and Crabbe, with the manuscripts of the Isidorian collection, *Crispotis*. In the Conference of Carthage, c. 123, *Posthumianus, bishop of the people of Tagora*; and, c. 128, *Crispulus, bishop of the church of Volita* are mentioned.] MAJORINUS, POSTHUMIANUS, CRISPULUS, VICTOR, another VICTOR, [nt: The Navar. manuscript of the Isidorian collection: *Leucianus*. Two Colb. and the Corb., as in Merlin, *Leutius*. Another Colb., *Lucius*, with the Conference of Carthage favouring it; the Vatic., *Leontius*; the Germ., *Eleutius*. Next, the Roman edition *Macinus*, Merlin *Marinus*, Crabbe *Marinianus*. In the Conference of Carthage two bishops are read who were called by the name of *Mariani*.] LEUCIUS, MACIANUS, FRUCTUOSUS, FAUSTINIANUS, QUODVULTDEUS, CANDORIUS, MAXIMUS, [nt: Quesnel and two Colb. manuscripts: *Magerius*; another Colb., *Magarus*; Pith., *Megarius*; the Capuchin codex of the Isidorian collection, *Magorus*; Merlin, Crabbe and the Roman edition, *Magarius*. From the Corb. and Germ. manuscripts, and also from the edition of Augustine, we restore *Megasius*.] MEGASIUS, RUSTICUS, [nt: So the manuscripts, except that in one Colbert, as in Quesnel, *Rufianus*. But Crabbe and the Roman edition: *Sufinianus*.] RUFINIANUS, [nt: None of our manuscripts supports the conjecture of Garnier, by which he thinks *Proculianus* should be read. Next, from the Corb. and Germ. manuscripts and the new edition of Augustine, the name of *Severus* is supplied, which is lacking in the other manuscripts and printed books.] PROCULUS, SEVERUS, THOMAS, JANUARIUS, OCTAVIANUS, PRAETEXTATUS, SIXTUS, QUODVULTDEUS, PENTADIUS, [nt: In one manuscript, as in Quesnel, *item* is set first here: which is absent in five others, as in the edition of Augustine. In the Roman edition, Crabbe and Merlin, *Quodvultdeus* is lacking here. Indeed the same name is omitted before too in the manuscripts of the Isidorian collection.] QUODVULTDEUS, CYPRIANUS, SERVILIUS, [nt: So in Augustine, as in the Corb. and Germ. manuscripts. But in the other books, *Pelagius*.] PELAGIANUS, MARCELLUS, VENANTIUS, DIDYMUS, SATURNINUS, [nt: One Colb. manuscript: *Bazathinus*; another, from the Vatican: *Bizatenus*; two other Colb. and Pith., with the Roman edition and Quesnel: *Bazacenus*; Merlin and Crabbe: *Bazecenus*; the Isidorian collection: *Batecenus*. We follow the edition of Augustine, which agrees with the Corb. and Germ. manuscripts.] BIZACENUS, GERMANUS, GERMANIANUS, [nt: In Augustine, *Inventius*. In the Roman edition, Quesnel, Crabbe and Merlin, as in the Pith. manuscript, *Juventius*. The Isidorian collection, *Viventius*. In other manuscripts, *Invenitus*.] INVENITUS, MAJORINUS, [nt: The printed editions and the manuscripts just mentioned, with which the Isidorian collection now agrees, keep *Inventius* or *Juventius* here too, to which word Quesnel prefixed *item*. Here indeed the Corb. codex shows *Juventinitus*, but in the rescript it keeps the name *Inveniti*, which two Colb. and the Germ. also show here.] INVENITUS, CANDIDUS, CYPRIANUS, AEMILIANUS, ROMANUS, AFRICANUS, and MARCELLINUS, [nt: *et caeteri* was first added here in the edition of Augustine, on the authority of the Corb. and Germ. codices.] and the rest of us who were present at the council of the Church of Carthage.

## 1.

[0564C]When, according to custom [nt: It was their custom to assemble at Carthage about the month of June or about autumn. But they did not constantly assemble in the same months. Therefore that *ex more* refers rather to the place than to the time.], we had solemnly come to the Church of Carthage, and a synod assembled [0565A] from among us was being held for various causes, our fellow presbyter Orosius gave us letters of our holy brethren and fellow priests [nt: In the manuscripts of the Isidorian collection, *Nerotis*, or *Anerotis*. In the editions of the Councils, *Averothis*. Heros was bishop of Arles, and Lazarus of Aix. Both, driven from their sees, when they had come into Palestine, presented a libellus against Pelagius and Coelestius to Eulogius of Caesarea; by whose authority the synod of Diospolis was afterwards assembled in the year 415. But the letters of those bishops mentioned here have perished.] Heros and Lazarus, whose text we have decided should be subjoined to these. These having been read, then, we perceived that Pelagius and Coelestius were [nt: The word *argui*, which was formerly lacking, we supply with the edition of Augustine from the Corb. and Germ. manuscripts. From there too, as also from the Pith. manuscript, we have restored *anathemandi* in place of *anathematizandi*.] charged as the authors of an error altogether wicked and to be anathematized by all of us. Whence it came about that we asked that there be reviewed what had been debated about the name of Coelestius here, at the Church of Carthage, almost five years before. When this had been read out, as thy holiness will be able to observe from the [nt: The Roman edition, *ex subsequentibus*. More fittingly the other books, *ex subditis*. Those episcopal acts, which had been subjoined to this letter, have perished, except the extracts which M. Mercator takes from them at the beginning of the Commonitorium against the Pelagians, and those which Augustine copies out of them, both in book II On Original Sin, c. 3 and 4, and in the book On the Proceedings of Pelagius, cap. 11. Moreover, they are touched upon by the same Doctor in Epistle 157, to Hilary, n. 22.] subjoined documents, although the judgment was manifestly established by which at that time this so great wound seemed to have been cut out of the Church by episcopal judgment; nevertheless we decided this by common deliberation: that the authors of such a persuasion, although [0565B] the same Coelestius is said to have afterwards come to the presbyterate too, unless they most openly anathematize these things, must themselves be anathematized; so that, if their own health cannot be procured, at least that of those who have been deceived by them, or can be deceived, may be procured, once the sentence passed against them is known. This proceeding, therefore, lord brother, we have thought should be made known to thy holy charity, so that to the statutes of our mediocrity the authority of the apostolic see may also be applied, for protecting the salvation of many, and for correcting also the perversity of some.

## 2.

For these men aim at this by their damnable disputations: that, not by defending free will, but rather by exalting it into sacrilegious pride, they leave no room for the grace of God by which we are Christians, by which too the very choice of our will is made truly free, [0565C] while it is freed from the dominion of carnal concupiscences, as the Lord says: *If the Son shall have made you free, then you shall be truly free [n: (Joan. VIII, 36)]*. [nt: This sentence, *Quod auxilium fides impetrat quae est in Christo J. D. N.*, is omitted in the Corb. and Germ. manuscripts.] Which help faith obtains, which is in Christ Jesus our Lord. But these men assert, as we have learned from brethren who have also read their books, that the grace of God is to be reckoned in this: that he established and created such a nature of man as could fulfil the law of God by its own will, whether [0566A] the law written naturally in the heart, or that given in letters. That this same law also belongs to grace, because God gave it to men for their help. But that grace by which, as has been said, we are Christians, of which the Apostle is the preacher when he says, *For I am delighted with the law of God according to the inward man; but I see another law in my members fighting against the law of my mind, and making me captive* [nt: The Roman edition and that of the Councils, with the Isidorian collection, and also two Colb. manuscripts and Pith.: *in lege*.] *under the law of sin, which is in my members. Unhappy man that I am, who shall deliver me from the body of this death? the grace of God through Jesus Christ our Lord [n: (Rom. VII, 22 seqq.)]*, they are altogether unwilling to acknowledge, nor indeed do they dare to attack it openly [n: (I Cor. II, 14)].

## 3.

But what else are they doing, when they do not cease to persuade [0566B] animal men, who do not perceive the things that are of the spirit of God, that for working and perfecting justice, and for fulfilling the commandments of God, human nature alone can suffice them? not attending to what is written, *The Spirit helps our infirmity [n: (Rom. VIII, 26)]*; and, *It is not of him that wills, nor of him that runs, but of God that shows mercy [n: (Rom. IX, 16)]*; and that *we are one body in Christ Jesus, and each of us members one of another, having different gifts according to the grace which is given to us [n: (Rom. XII, V)]*; and, *By the grace of God I am what I am, and his grace in me was not void, but I laboured more than all of them: yet not I, but the grace of God with me [n: (I Cor.
