XV, 10)]*; and, *Thanks be to God, who has given us the victory through our Lord Jesus Christ [n: (Ibid., 57)]*; and, *Not that we are sufficient to think anything as of ourselves, but our sufficiency [0566C] is from God [n: (II Cor. III, 5)]*; and, *We have this treasure in earthen vessels, that the excellence of the power may be of God, and not of us [n: (Ibid. IV, 7)]*; and innumerable things, for which, if we wished to collect them from all the Scriptures, [nt: In Augustine, as in the Corb. and Germ. manuscripts, *tempus*. With the other books we keep *tomus*; by which word a synodical letter is usually signified among the ancients. And indeed in that sense Athanasius entitled the letter of the synod of Alexandria *the tome to the Antiochenes*: and the council of Constantinople, in epist. 13, to Damasus, n. 4, called the letter of the council of Antioch τόμον. The synodical letter 4 of Damasus himself is designated by the name of *tome* by the same council of Constantinople, can. 5. The use of this word in the same sense is more frequent in the council of Chalcedon, for instance in Labbe's edition, vol. IV, p. 187 d, 264 d, 491 e. Commonly too the celebrated letter of Leo to Flavian is called the *tome*. Sometimes also *tome* signifies the Compendium of a longer discourse, and in that sense it is taken in the preface of the eighth council of Toledo: literally, however, it is the same as *sectio*. As regards the Africans, they seem to mean nothing else here than that they would exceed the measure of an ordinary letter if they were to collect all the testimonies that serve their purpose.] a tome does not suffice. And we fear lest, by recalling before thee these very things [nt: The Roman edition and that of the Councils, after Crabbe: *qui majore*. More fittingly the other books: *quae majore*.] which thou preachest with greater grace from the apostolic see, we should seem to act unfittingly. But we do so because, in proportion as, being weaker, each of us everywhere in preaching the word of God [0567A] is thought the more attentive, the more often and the more boldly we suffer them rising up against us.

## 4.

If, then, Pelagius should have seemed to thy veneration also to have been justly acquitted by the episcopal proceedings ( *of the synod of Diospolis* ) which are said to have been drawn up in the East; yet the error itself and the impiety, which already has many assertors scattered through various places, must be anathematized by the authority of the apostolic see as well. For let thy holiness consider, and suffer with us with pastoral bowels, how pestilent and deadly it is to the sheep of Christ, what follows necessarily from the sacrilegious disputations of these men: that we ought not even to pray that we enter not into temptation, which the Lord both admonished his disciples and placed in the prayer which he taught [n: (Matth. VI, 13)]: or that our faith fail not, which he himself testified that he had asked for the apostle [0567B] Peter [n: (Luc. XXII, 32)]. For if these things are placed in our power by the possibility of nature and the choice of the will, who does not see that they are asked of the Lord in vain, and prayed for deceitfully, when by praying things are sought which are obtained by the sufficient powers of a nature already so created: and that the Lord Jesus ought not to have said, *Watch and pray [n: (Matth. XXIV, 41)]*; but only, Watch, *that you enter not into temptation:* nor to the most blessed Peter, first of the Apostles: *I have prayed for thee,* but, I admonish thee, or I command and enjoin, *that thy faith fail not (Lucae XXII, 32)*? Our blessings too are contradicted by the contention of these men, so that we seem to say over the people in vain whatever we pray the Lord for on their behalf, that by living rightly and piously they may please him; or those things which the Apostle prays for on behalf of the faithful, [0567C] saying: *I bow my knees to the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, from whom all paternity in heaven and on earth is named, that he may grant you, according to the riches of his glory, to be strengthened with power through his Spirit [n: (Ephes. III, 14)]*. If then, in blessing, we wish to say over the people: *Grant it, O Lord, to be strengthened with power through thy Spirit:* the disputation of these men contradicts us, affirming that free will is denied if that is asked of God which is in our power. For if we wish, they say, to be strengthened with power, we can be, by that possibility of nature which we do not receive now, but received when we were created.

## 5.

They deny too that little ones are to be baptized for the salvation which is given through Christ the Saviour; and so by this deadly doctrine they slay them for eternity, promising [0568A] that even if they are not baptized they will have eternal life, and that they do not belong to those of whom the Lord says, *For the son of man came to seek and to save that which had perished [n: (Luc. XIX, 10)]*: because these, they say, had not perished, nor is there anything in them to be saved, or to be redeemed at so great a price; because nothing in them is corrupted, nothing is held captive under the power of the devil, nor was [nt: The Pith. manuscript: *fusus esset*; the Isidorian collection and also the Roman edition and that of the Councils: *fusus esse*, omitting next *qui fusus*: they are restored from the manuscripts of better repute. And the blood of Christ, to be shed *for the remission of sins*, is read in Matt. xxvi, 28.] the blood shed for them, which is read to have been shed for the remission of sins. Although Coelestius has already confessed in his libellus, in the church of Carthage, that through the baptism of Christ the redemption of little ones too [nt: In the Roman edition, as in Merlin, *dicunt* is wrongly added here. Augustine, epist. 157, n. 22, speaks of this very thing concerning Coelestius thus: *He was compelled to confess, on account of the little ones to be baptized, that redemption is necessary for them also. Where*, he says, *although he was unwilling to say anything more expressly about original sin, yet by the very name of redemption he committed himself not a little.*] takes place. But many who are said to be or to have been their disciples do not cease to affirm these evils, by which they try to overturn the foundations of the Christian faith, in whatever way they can [nt: The Roman edition and that of the Councils: *possunt astutia*. We remove the word *astutia* on the authority of all the manuscripts.]. [0568B]



## 6.

Wherefore, even if Pelagius and Coelestius have been corrected, or say that they never held these things, and deny that whatever writings have been brought forward against them are theirs, and there is no way by which they may be convicted of lying: yet in general, whoever dogmatizes and affirms that human nature can suffice for itself for [nt: In the Corb. manuscript, by the first hand, *ad nitenda*, and by a second correction, *ad vitanda*: which also stands in the Germ. and was preferred in the edition of Augustine. We keep, however, *vincenda*, both on the authority of more manuscripts, and because below, epist. 28, n. 13, the five bishops write again that Pelagius defends *the possibility of nature for conquering sins and fulfilling the commandments*. Wherefore in the synod of Diospolis too he had been compelled to confess that *when we fight against temptations and unlawful concupiscences, the* VICTORY *comes not from our own will but from the help of God*.] conquering sins and doing the commandments of God, and in that way is found an adversary of the grace of God, which is declared more evidently by the prayers of the saints: and whoever denies that little ones are freed from perdition through the baptism of Christ and receive everlasting salvation, let him be anathema. As for whatever else is objected to them, we do not doubt that thy veneration, when it has looked through the episcopal proceedings ( *of the synod of Palestine* ) which are said to have been drawn up in the East in the same cause, will so judge [0568C] that we may all rejoice in the mercy of God. Pray for us, most blessed lord pope.

## EPISTLE XXVII. OF THE COUNCIL OF MILEVIS TO POPE INNOCENT.

*That he may restrain as soon as possible by his authority the pernicious heresy of the enemies of the grace of Christ.*

*To the most blessed lord, rightly to be venerated and to be honoured in Christ, Pope INNOCENT, SILVANUS* [nt: In the Roman edition and that of Augustine *senex* is lacking: which word is expressed in all our manuscripts. The same Silvanus signs in Augustine, both at the end of epistle 128 as *senex of the church of Summa*, and at the end of epistle 129 as *of the first see of the province of Numidia*. Moreover, among the Africans *senex* is the same as *primas*: because among them the primacy was accorded to him who was senior by ordination.] *the senior, VALENTINUS, AURELIUS, DONATUS, RESTITUTUS, LUCIANUS, ALYPIUS, AUGUSTINE, PLACENTIUS, SEVERUS, FORTUNATUS, POSSIDIUS, NOVATUS, SECUNDUS, MAURENTIUS, LEO, FAUSTINIANUS, CRESCONIUS, MALCHUS,* [nt: Merlin and Crabbe, with one manuscript: *Lictorius*. The other editions of the Councils, after the Roman: *Victorius*. The other books: *Litorius* or *Littorius*. In the Conference of Carthage, c. 133, *Litorius, bishop of Suava* is named.] *LITTORIUS, FORTUNATUS, DONATUS, [0569A]* [nt: The Pith. manuscript and one Colb.: *Ponticianus*. Two others, with Merlin, Crabbe and Quesnel: *Pontianus*.] *PONTICANUS, SATURNINUS,* [nt: In the Roman edition and that of Augustine: *Christonius*. In the other editions, as in all our manuscripts: *Cresconius*. That the name of Cresconius, or Crisconius, was familiar to the Africans is witnessed by the Conference of Carthage, at which several bishops of different sees are read to have been present who bore that same name.] *CRESCONIUS, HONORIUS, LUCIUS, ADEODATUS, PROCESSUS, CRESCONIUS, SECUNDUS, FELIX, ASIATICUS,* [nt: Five of our manuscripts, with Merlin, Crabbe and Quesnel: *Rufinus*.] *RUFINIANUS, FAUSTINUS, SERVUS, TERENTIUS, CRESCONIUS, SPERANTIUS, QUADRATUS, LUCILLUS, SABINUS,* [nt: *Sabinus* again, in place of *Faustinus*, is repeated in Quesnel, Labbe, Crabbe and Merlin, as in the exemplars of the Isidorian collection, the better manuscripts resisting.] *FAUSTINUS, CRESCONIUS, VICTOR,* [nt: In Augustine, *Gignantius*. But the names of this bishop and of the nine following are passed over in the editions of the Councils, because of the repetition of the word *Victor*, by a common slip of copyists.] *GIGANTIUS, POSSIDONIUS, ANTONINUS,* [nt: The manuscripts of the Isidorian collection, and also two other Colb. with Pith.: *Innocentius, Felix*, the names of *Praesidius* and *Crescentius* being omitted.] *INNOCENTIUS, PRAESIDIUS, CRESCENTIUS, FELIX, ANTONINUS, VICTOR, HONORATUS, DONATUS, PETRUS, PRAESIDIUS, CRESCONIUS, LAMPADIUS, DELPHINUS, from the council of Milevis, greeting in the Lord.*

## 1.

Since the Lord, by a special gift of his grace, has placed thee in the apostolic see, and has granted thee to our times as such a one that it would rather count against us as a fault of negligence if we kept silent before thy veneration about what must be suggested on behalf of [0569B] the Church, than that thou couldst receive it either disdainfully or negligently; we beseech thee to deign to apply pastoral diligence to the great dangers of the weak members of Christ.

## 2.

For a new and exceedingly pernicious heresy [nt: Garnier recalled from the old editions of Augustine: *perniciosa tempestas inimicorum gratiae coepit*, the other printed editions and all the manuscripts resisting.] is trying to rise up, that of the enemies of the grace of Christ, who by impious disputations are trying to take away from us even the Lord's Prayer. For whereas the Lord taught us to say, *Forgive us our debts, as we also forgive our debtors [n: (Matth. VI, 12)]*: these men say that a man can in this life, once the precepts of God are known, attain to so great a perfection of justice without the help of the grace of the Saviour, through the sole choice of free will, that it is no longer necessary for him to say, *Forgive us our debts:* and that what [0569C] follows, *Do not bring us into temptation [n: (Ibid., 13)]*, is not to be understood as though we ought to ask for divine help, lest, being tempted, we fall into sin; but that this is placed in our power, and that for fulfilling it the will of man alone suffices: as though the Apostle had said in vain, [0570A] *It is not of him that wills, nor of him that runs, but of God that shows mercy [n: (Rom. IX, 16)]*; and, *God is faithful, who does not permit you to be tempted beyond what you are able, but will make with the temptation also a way out, that you may be able to bear it [n: (I Cor. X, 13)]*. In vain too would the Lord have said to the apostle Peter, *I have prayed for thee, that thy faith fail not (Lucae XXII, 32)*; and to all his own, *Watch and pray, that you enter not into temptation [n: (Matth. XXIV, 41)]*: if this whole thing lies in human power. Little children too, [nt: The Roman edition and that of the Councils: *si nullis*, which the manuscripts support. Next, in Garnier and Augustine, *innoventur*. The reading *etsi nullis* is favoured by that of the preceding letter, n. 5: *promising that* EVEN IF *they are not baptized, they will have eternal life*. From this too it is plain that baptism is here called the *sacraments of Christian grace*.] even if they are imbued with no sacraments of Christian grace, will have eternal life: so they contend, with a presumption [nt: In place of *nequaquam*, Garnier substituted *nequam*: which passed from there into the edition of Augustine. The ambiguity will be removed if you read *praesumptione nequaquam Christiana*.] by no means Christian, making void what the Apostle says, *By one man sin entered into the world, and by sin death, and so it passed into all men, in whom all have sinned [n: (Rom. V, 12)]*; and in another place, *as in [0570B] Adam all die, so in Christ all shall be made alive [n: (I Cor. XV, 22)]*. To omit, then, the other things, very many, which they argue against the holy Scriptures, there are meanwhile these two, by which they try to overturn entirely all that we are as Christians, and which faithful hearts [nt: Garnier: *non sustinent*, adding a negative particle against the faith of the old codices and without necessity.] might endure: that God is not to be asked to be our helper against the evil of sin and for working justice, and that the sacrament of Christian grace does not aid little ones to attain eternal life.

## 3.

Suggesting these things to thy apostolic breast, we have no need to say much, and to magnify so great an impiety with words; since beyond doubt these things move thee [nt: In Quesnel and Augustine: *te ita permoveant*.] so that, as to correcting them, lest they spread more widely and defile many, or rather destroy them, while under the name of Christ they estrange them entirely from the grace of Christ, [0570C] thou canst in no way hold back. The authors of this most pernicious error are said to be Pelagius and Coelestius, whom indeed we would rather have healed in the Church than cut off from the Church with their salvation despaired of, if no necessity compels. Of whom one, that is Coelestius, has come even to the presbyterate in Asia [nt: Mar. Mercator, Commonitorium c. 1, Baluze's edition p. 133, relates the matter thus: Coelestius, *accused on six chapters in the council of Carthage and ordered to condemn them, but in no way acquiescing, was deprived of ecclesiastical communion. From which sentence he thought an appeal should be made to the examination of the bishop of Rome: but soon, neglecting that same appeal, he made for Ephesus, a city of Asia, and there dared by stealth to seek the place of the presbyterate*. Earlier, about the end of the year 411, as Augustine testifies, epist. 157, n. 22, when he was settled at Carthage, *he had already begun to creep up to the honour of the presbyterate in the church of that same city: but through the most faithful freedom of the brethren, on account of these very disputations against the grace of Christ, he was brought as far as episcopal judgment*. There is extant a letter of Nestorius to him in Mar. Mercator, p. 131, inscribed *to the honourable and most religious presbyter Coelestius*.], [0571A] it is said. What was done about him a few years ago, thy holiness is [nt: Namely because the Church, or synod, of Carthage had subjoined to the preceding Epistle 26 the acts of another council held at Carthage about the name of Coelestius almost five years before. Since both this letter of the council of Milevis and that of Carthage, carried by the same bishop Julius, were delivered to Innocent together, there was no need for Garnier to substitute of his own *melius instruetur* for *melius instruitur*.] better instructed from the church of Carthage. But Pelagius, as letters sent by certain of our brethren say, being settled at Jerusalem, is asserted to be deceiving some. Yet many more, who have been able to investigate his meaning more diligently, fight against him for the grace of Christ and the truth of the catholic faith, but especially thy holy son, our brother and fellow presbyter Jerome. But we judge that, with the help of the mercy of the Lord our God, who deigns [nt: One manuscript: *dignabitur*. Into the new edition of Augustine *dignetur* crept, through the sheer carelessness of a copyist.] both to guide thee when thou consultest and to hear thee when thou prayest, those who hold such perverse and pernicious opinions will more easily yield to the authority of thy holiness, [nt: The manuscripts of the Isidorian collection: *de clarum* (through the unskilfulness of a copyist, who had read in his exemplar *de scarum*, that is, *de sanctarum*). The Capuchin codex, by a second correction: *declaratione sacrarum*) *Scripturarum depromptae*. From this, in the Roman edition and that of the Councils, it had become *de claro Scripturarum lumine depromptae*. The better manuscripts supply the sounder reading. Here the fathers of the council of Milevis declare that the authority of the Roman pontiff is supported by the very authority of the Scriptures.] drawn from the authority of the holy Scriptures; so that we may rather rejoice together over their [0571B] correction than be saddened by their destruction, most blessed lord. But whatever they themselves choose, thy venerableness certainly sees that provision must be made urgently and speedily at least for others, very many of whom they can entangle in their snares if they are left unregarded. We have sent these writings to thy holiness from the council of Numidia, imitating [nt: The Roman edition and Garnier omit *Carthaginensem ecclesiam, et*: which words are restored from the other books.] the church of Carthage and our fellow bishops of the province of Carthage, who, we have learned, have written on this cause to the apostolic see, which thou, blessed one, makest illustrious. ( *And in another hand:* ) Mindful of us, mayest thou increase in the grace of God, most blessed lord, rightly venerable and to be honoured in Christ, holy pope.

## EPISTLE XXVIII [nt: This letter is mentioned by Augustine, book On Original Sin, c. 9, and epist. formerly 47, now 215, to Valentinus, n. 12, as written *by five bishops*. Wherefore it was not ill inscribed *of five bishops* in the editions of the Councils. By the same Doctor, epist. formerly 106, now 186, n. 2, it is called *familiar*, to distinguish it from the preceding ones, which are synodical.]. OF FIVE BISHOPS TO POPE INNOCENT. *They uncover and refute the heresy of Pelagius, by which he contended that human nature alone, through free will, suffices for itself for taming desires and overcoming temptations, and also for fulfilling the commandments of God: and they show that wherever he preached grace, he understood by it either free will, or the remission of sins, or the precept of the law, and that something else is understood by the faithful under the proper name of grace. They also explain in what sense his saying that man can be without sin if he wills is said truly, and in what sense, though not without error, it is yet said tolerably.*

*To the most blessed lord, and brother rightly most to be honoured, Pope INNOCENT, AURELIUS, ALYPIUS, AUGUSTINE, EVODIUS, and POSSIDIUS, greeting in the Lord.*

## 1.

From the two councils, of the province of Carthage and of Numidia, we have sent to thy holiness letters subscribed by no small number of bishops, against the enemies of the grace of Christ, who trust in their own strength, and in a certain manner say to our creator: Thou hast made us men, but we have made ourselves just; who call human nature free for this reason, that they may not seek a liberator; and sound for this reason, that they may judge [0572B] a saviour superfluous. For they say it is so strong that, with its powers received once for all in the origin of its creation, it can through free will, with the grace of him who created it giving no further help, tame and extinguish all desires, and overcome temptations. And many of them rise up against us and say to our soul: *There is no salvation for him in his God [n: (Psal. III, 3)]*. The household of Christ, which says, *When I am weak, then* [nt: One Colb. codex: *tunc fortior sum*: which it also seems to have had at first.] *am I strong [n: (II Cor. XII, 10)]*; and to which its Lord says, *I am thy salvation [n: (Psal. XXXIV, 3)]*, awaits with anxious heart, in fear and trembling, the help of the Lord, through the charity of thy veneration also.



## 2.

For we have heard that there are in the city of Rome, where he *(Pelagius)* lived a long time, some who for various reasons [0572C] favour him: some, namely, because [nt: So Quesnel, from the Oxford manuscript, and from there the new edition of Augustine: with which agrees what Innocent writes back. But Merlin and Crabbe: *qui nobis talia persuasisse perhibentur*: where Crabbe added in the margin, *qui vos* and *quia vos*. From there the Roman edition and the other editions of the Councils: *quia vos talia persuasisse perhibent*. The manuscripts show similar readings, except that for *perhibent* most have *perhibentur*, and one Colb. and another from the Vatican *perhibetur*. In the latter there is read besides *quia vobis*. From which this reading could conveniently be put together: *quia vobis talia persuasisse perhibetur*. The Pelagians, namely, as Possidius testifies, c. 18, *tried through their own ambition to persuade the holy see of their perfidy*. And what they could not accomplish, they boasted falsely that they had accomplished. Among those, moreover, whom they boasted they had persuaded of their depraved dogmas at Rome, the chief was Xystus, then a presbyter and afterwards supreme pontiff, whom, after the calumny had been exposed, Augustine, epist. otherwise 101, now 191, n. 1, congratulates thus: *For what can be read or heard more welcome than so pure a defence of grace against its enemies, from the mouth of him who was boasted to be a patron of great weight to those same enemies?* See also his epistle 194, n. 1.] he is said to have persuaded them of such things; but more, who do not believe that he holds such things; especially because ecclesiastical proceedings are boasted to have been held in the East, where he lives, by which he is thought to have been cleared; where indeed, if the bishops pronounced him catholic, it must be believed to have been done for no other reason than that he said he confessed the grace of God, [0573A] and that man can live justly by his own labour and will in such a way that he did not deny that he is helped to this by the grace of God. For when these words were heard, the catholic prelates could understand no other grace of God than that which they were accustomed to read in the books of God and to preach to the peoples of God, that namely of which the Apostle says, *I do not make void the grace of God; for if justice is through the Law, then Christ died in vain [n: (Galat. II, 21)]*: without doubt the grace by which we are justified from iniquity, and by which we are saved from infirmity; not that by which we were created with our own will. For if those bishops had understood that he meant that grace which we have even in common with the impious, with whom we are men, but denied that by which we are Christians and sons of God: which of the catholic priests, we do not [0573B] say, would hear him patiently, but [nt: Two Colb. manuscripts: *tantum ante oculos*: with a certain greater emphasis. How Pelagius confessed the grace of God in the synod mentioned, Augustine sets out in the book On the Proceedings of Pelagius himself, c. 6 and 7, and presently, cap. 10, explains how he detected that he had confessed the grace of God there deceitfully.] would bear him before his eyes? Wherefore the judges are not to be blamed, because they heard the name of grace according to ecclesiastical custom, not knowing what men of this kind are accustomed to scatter either in the books of their teaching or in the ears of their own followers.

## 3.

It is not a question of Pelagius alone, who perhaps has already been corrected, and would that it were so! but of so many, by whom, as they contend talkatively, and drag weak and untaught souls as though bound together [nt: Merlin: *conjunctas*. The Roman edition and the more recent editions of the Councils, and Garnier: *convictas*. The new edition of Augustine: *vinctas*. We prefer, with Crabbe and the better manuscripts, *convinctas*, as though *bound together*. *devinctas* would not displease, because of what Innocent writes back in epist. 31, n. 2: *If then there are any whom so great a perversity has bound fast in its defence*. And indeed nearer to this word comes what we read in the copies of the Isidorian collection, *pervinctas*.], and weary the firm and those stable in the faith by the very contention, all things are now full everywhere. Either, then, he must be summoned to Rome by thy veneration, and diligently questioned what grace he means, by which he confesses, if indeed he now confesses it, that men are helped not to sin and to live justly: [0573C] or this very thing must be dealt with him by letter. And when he has been found to mean that grace which the ecclesiastical and apostolic truth teaches, then he is to be absolved without any scruple of the Church, without the hiding place of any ambiguity; then in truth we must rejoice over his clearing.

## 4.

For whether he has said that grace is free will, or that grace is the remission of sins, or that grace is the precept of the law; he says none of those things which belong, through the supply of the Holy Spirit, to conquering concupiscence and temptations, [0574A] the Spirit whom he who ascended into heaven, and, leading captivity captive, gave gifts to men, poured out most richly upon us [n: (Tit. III, 6)]. For hence we pray that we may be able to overcome the temptation of sins [n: (Ephes. IV, 8)], that the Spirit of God, of whom we have received the pledge, may help our infirmity [n: (Rom. VIII, 26)]. But he who prays and says, *Do not bring us into temptation [n: (Matth. VI, 13)]*, certainly does not pray for this, that he may be a man, which is nature; nor does he pray for this, that he may have free will, which he already received when nature itself was created; nor does he pray for the remission of sins, because this is said above, *Forgive us our debts [n: (Matth. VI, 12)]*; nor does he pray that he may receive the commandment; but plainly he prays that he may do the commandment. For if he has been brought into temptation, that is, has failed in temptation, he certainly commits a sin [0574B] which is against the commandment. He prays, therefore, that he may not sin, that is, that he may do nothing evil: which the Apostle prays for on behalf of the Corinthians, saying: *And we pray to the Lord that you do nothing evil [n: (II Cor. XIII, 7)]*. Whence it appears clearly enough that for not sinning, that is, for not doing evil, although it is not doubted that there is a choice of the will, [nt: In the Roman edition and that of the Councils *liberum* is added here, superfluously and against the faith of the manuscripts. What the bishops a little above call *liberum arbitrium*, without adding the word *voluntatis*, they now call *arbitrium voluntatis*, without prefixing the epithet *liberum*. Hence also at the beginning of num. 5 they say: *Nor would the law command, unless there were will; nor would grace help, if will were enough*, now making mention of will alone.] yet its power does not suffice unless infirmity is helped. Prayer itself, therefore, is the clearest testimony of grace. Let him confess this, and we shall rejoice in him whether he is upright or corrected.

## 5.

Law and grace are to be distinguished. The law knows how to command, grace how to help. Nor would the law command, unless there were will: nor would grace help, if will were enough. We are commanded to have understanding, where it is said, *Be not like the horse and the mule, which have no understanding (Psal. [0574C] XXXI, 9)*; and yet we pray that we may have understanding, where it is said, *Give me understanding, that I may learn thy commandments [n: (Psal. CXVIII, 125)]*. We are commanded to have wisdom, where it is said, *You fools, be wise at last [n: (Psal. XCIII, 8)]*; and yet it is prayed that we may have wisdom, where it is said, *But if any of you lacks wisdom, let him ask of God, who gives to all abundantly and does not upbraid; and it shall be given him [n: (Jacob. I, 5)]*. We are commanded to have continence, where it is said, [nt: So Merlin, Crabbe, Garnier and our manuscripts. Yet it is manifest that something is lacking here. Wherefore the Roman editor added this within brackets, in another type: ［*Something of this kind is lacking here: Let your loins be girded: and yet we pray that it be given us, where it is said.*］ Which was afterwards retained in certain other editions as well. In place of this addition Quesnel substituted: *Turn away from thy pleasures, and yet it is prayed that we may have continence, where it is said*. Neither supplement satisfies. But how this place is to be mended may be fished out from another like passage of Augustine. That Doctor, epist. 147, to Hilary, n. 9, treating the same argument, writes thus: *When the Law says, Thou shalt not covet, what else does it seem to command but continence from unlawful desires?... and yet it is prayed that we may have continence, where it is said, When I knew that no one can be continent*, etc. And presently he concludes: *God therefore commands continence, and gives continence*. According to the mind of Augustine, then, our passage will be made whole if we restore: *We are commanded to have continence, where it is said, Thou shalt not covet: and yet it is prayed that we may have continence, where it is said, When I knew*, etc. The close repetition of the same words, a fruitful crop of lacunae, gave occasion to this.] *When I knew that no one can be continent unless God grant it, and [0575A] this very thing was a part of wisdom, to know whose gift this was: I went to the Lord, and besought him [n: (Sap. VIII, 21)]*. Finally, lest it be too long to run through everything, we are commanded not to do evil, where it is said, *Turn away from evil [n: (Psal. XXXVI, 27)]*; and yet it is prayed that we may not do evil where it is said: *And we pray to the Lord that you do nothing evil [n: (II Cor. XIII, 7)]*. We are commanded to do good, where it is said, *Turn away from evil and do good [n: (Psal. XXXVI, 27)]*; and yet it is prayed that we may do good, where it is said, *We do not cease praying and asking for you [n: (Coloss. I, 9)]*, and among the other things which he prays for them he says: *That you may walk worthily of God, unto all pleasing, in every good work and word [n: (Ibid., 10)]*. As, then, we acknowledge the will when these things are commanded; so let him too acknowledge grace [0575B] when they are asked for.

## 6.

We have sent to thy reverence a book which certain religious and honourable young men, servants of God, gave us, whose names too we do not keep silent; for they are called Timasius and Jacobus. Who, as we have heard, and as thou too deignest to know, at the exhortation of Pelagius himself abandoned the hope which they had in the world, and now serve God in continence. When at length, through our efforts such as they were, by the Lord's inspiration, they had been freed from that same error, they brought forward the same book, saying that it was Pelagius's, and earnestly asked that it be answered. It was done: the answer itself was written back to them ( *Augustine's book On Nature and Grace* ); they wrote back giving thanks [n: (Epist. apud Augustin. nunc 168)]. We have sent both, [0575C] both that to which the answer was made and the answer that was made. And lest we should be too burdensome, we have made marks at those places which we ask thee not to be loath to look at, how, when the question was put to him that he denied the grace of God, he answered in such a way as to say that it was nothing but the nature in which God created us.

## 7.

But if he denies that this book is his, or those same places in the book, we do not contend: let him anathematize them, and confess most openly that grace which Christian doctrine demonstrates and preaches to be proper to Christians, which is not nature, but that by which nature is saved: [nt: So this passage was restored in the newest edition of Augustine from a Vatican manuscript, which two Colb. also favour. But in the other books, Merlin's edition excepted, *quae naturam... fovet* is lacking. Of those three words Quesnel kept only the first. Then in the same books, and also in Merlin's edition, *sicut plantatur quodammodo et irrigatur*, except that Quesnel struck out the particle *sicut*.] which fosters nature not by teaching sounding in the ears, or by some visible aid, as a planter, in a certain manner, and a waterer from without, but by the supply of the Spirit and by hidden mercy, [0575D] as he does who gives the increase, God [n: (I Cor. III, 7)]. For although, by a reckoning not to be disapproved, that is called the grace of God by which we were created, so that [0576A] we should not be nothing, nor be something in such a way as a corpse which does not live, or a tree which does not feel, or a beast which does not understand; but men, who should both be, and live, and feel, and understand; and might be able [nt: The Roman edition: *agere debeamus*: it is corrected from the other books.] to give thanks to our creator for so great a benefit; whence this too can rightly be called grace, because it was given not for the merits of any preceding works, but by the gratuitous goodness of God: yet there is another, by which, predestined, we are called, justified, glorified, so that we can say: *If God is for us, who is against us? He who spared not his own son, but delivered him up for us all [n: (Rom. VIII, 3)]*.



## 8.

It was about this grace that the question turned, when it was said to Pelagius by those whom he was gravely offending and troubling, that he was attacking it in his disputations, [0576B] in which he asserted that human nature is sufficient to itself through free choice, not only for doing but even for perfecting the divine commandments. It is this grace that the apostolic doctrine [nt: In Merlin, Crabbe and the edition of Augustine, *non immerito* is added here.] calls by that name, by which we are saved and justified by the faith of Christ. Of this it is written: *I do not make void the grace of God. For if justice is through the Law, then Christ died in vain [n: (Galat. II, 21)]*. Of this it is written, *You are made void of Christ, you who are justified in the Law; you are fallen from grace [n: (Galat. V, 4)]*. Of this it is written, *But if by grace, it is not now of works; otherwise grace is no more grace [n: (Rom. XI, 6)]*. Of this it is written, *Now to him that works, the reward is not reckoned according to grace, but according to debt: but to him that works not, yet believes in him who justifies the ungodly, [0576C] his faith is reckoned unto justice [n: (Rom. IV, 4)]*. And many other things, which thou thyself canst better remember, and understand more wisely, and preach more illustriously. But that grace by which we were created men, even though we understand, not undeservedly, that it may be so called, it is nevertheless a wonder if we read it so called in any writings of the Law, the Prophets, the Gospels or the Apostles.

## 9.

When therefore the question was put to him concerning this grace, best known to faithful and catholic Christians, that he should cease to attack it, what is it that, when he himself in his own book had put this objection to himself as from an opposing person, so that he might clear himself by answering, he answered nothing else than that the nature [nt: Quesnel alone: *naturam creati referri ad gratiam creatoris*. This *referre* means to display and represent in itself. Augustine, in the book *On Nature and Grace*, c. 51, copies out and reports Pelagius's very words in this manner: *The very possibility of not sinning lies not so much in the power of choice as in the necessity of nature. Whatever is placed in the necessity of nature is without doubt held to belong to the author of nature, namely God. How then is that thought to be said to be without the grace of God which is shown to belong properly to God?* There, says Augustine at once, *the opinion that lay hidden was expressed. He attributes the possibility of not sinning to the grace of God for this reason, that God is the author of that nature in which he says the possibility of not sinning is inseparably implanted.*] of created man displays the grace of the creator; and that he thus says that justice can be fulfilled without sin through free choice [0576D] with the help of divine grace, because God gave this to man by the very possibility of nature?

## 10.

To whom it is deservedly answered: *Then is made void [0577A] the stumbling-block of the cross [n: (Galat. V, 11)]*. *Then Christ died in vain [n: (Rom. IV, 25)]*. [nt: The Roman edition and the Councils: *Non enim*. Next, Merlin and Crabbe: *nec ascendisset in altum et captivans*; the Roman edition and Labbe: *nec ascendens in altum, captivans*.] For if he had not died for our sins and risen again for our justification, nor ascended on high and, leading captivity captive, given gifts to men, would that possibility of nature which he defends not be in men [n: (Eph. IV, 8; Psal. LXVII, 19)]? Or was the commandment of God perhaps lacking, and was it for that reason that Christ died? Nay, this too already existed, holy and just and good [n: (Rom. VII, 12)]. It had already been said, *Thou shalt not covet [n: (Exod. XX, 17)]*; it had already been said, *Thou shalt love thy neighbour as thyself [n: (Levit. XIX, 18)]*. In which saying the Apostle says that the whole law is fulfilled [n: (Rom. XIII, 9)]. And since no one loves himself unless he loves God, therefore the Lord says that on these two commandments [0577B] hang the whole law and the Prophets [n: (Matth. XXII, 40)]. These two commandments had already been divinely given to men. Or had the eternal reward of justice not yet been promised? He himself does not say this, who has set down in his writings that the kingdom of heaven was promised even in the Old Testament. If then there already existed, for doing and perfecting justice, the possibility of nature through free choice, there already existed the holy and just and good commandment of the law of God, there already existed the everlasting reward promised: then Christ died in vain.

## 11.

Therefore justice is neither through the law, nor through the possibility of nature; but from faith and the gift of God through Jesus Christ our Lord, the one mediator of God and men. Who, had he not in the fullness of time [0577C] died for our sins, and risen again for our justification [n: (Rom. IV, 25)], assuredly both the faith of the ancients would be made void, and ours. And faith being made void, what justice would remain to man, since the just man lives by faith [n: (Heb. X, 38; Rom. I, 17)]? For since by one man sin entered into the world, and by sin death, and so passed upon all men, in whom all have sinned [n: (Rom. V, 12)]: beyond doubt, from the body of this death, where another law fights against the law of the mind [n: (Rom. VII, 21 et 24)], no one has been delivered or is delivered by [nt: In some manuscripts, faultily: *suae possibilitas*. Hence occasion was given to a copyist, in a second revision, in the Navarre manuscript and perhaps in others, to add *naturae*. Whence in the printed editions too, except the Augustine edition, *suae possibilitas naturae* prevailed. Below, in the 29th epistle of Innocent, n. 3, we shall read *quasi ex nostra in totum simus possibilitate* (the word *naturae* not being added) *perfecti*. See below, epist. 30, n. 4.] his own possibility, which, being lost, needs a redeemer; being wounded, a saviour: but by the grace of God through faith in the one mediator of God and men, the man Christ Jesus [n: (I Tim. II, 5)]; who, since he was also God, made man, and remaining [0577D] God, having been made man, himself remade what he made.

## 12.

But I think it escapes him that the faith of Christ, which afterwards came into revelation, was hidden in the times of our fathers; through which nevertheless they too were delivered by the grace of God, whoever in all the times of the human race could be delivered, by the hidden judgment of God, yet not a blameworthy one. Whence the Apostle says: *But having the same spirit [0578A] of faith* (the same, that is, as they also had) *according as it is written [n: (II Cor. IV, 13)]*, *I believed, for which cause I have spoken; we also believe, for which cause we speak also [n: (Psal. CXV, 1)]*. Thence it is that the mediator himself says: *Abraham desired to see my day; and he saw it, and was glad [var: Vulg. "exsultavit ut videret," rejoiced that he might see] [n: (Joan. VIII, 56)]*. Thence Melchisedech, bringing forth the sacrament of the Lord's table, knew how to figure his eternal priesthood [n: (Gen. XIV, 18)]. But now, when the Law had been given in writing, which the Apostle says entered in that sin might abound [n: (Rom. V, 20)], and of which he says: *For if the inheritance be of the Law, it is no more of promise. But God gave it to Abraham by promise. Why then the Law? It was set for the sake of [nt: In Quesnel and the Augustine edition, as in the Pithou manuscript: *proposita est*.] transgression, until the seed should come to whom the promise was made, being ordained by Angels in the hand of a mediator. Now a mediator [0578B] is not of one, but God is one. Was the Law then against the promises of God? God forbid. For if there had been a Law given which could give life, justice would altogether be of the Law. But the Scripture has shut up all things under sin, that the promise by the faith of Jesus Christ might be given to them that believe [n: (Galat. III, 18, seqq.)]*. Is it not sufficiently shown that this was done through the Law, that sin might be recognized, and be increased by transgression? *For where there is no law, neither is there transgression [n: (Rom. IV, 15)]*; and that so, against the victory of sin, refuge might be taken in the divine grace which is in the promises: and thus the Law would not be against the promises of God, since the knowledge of sin comes through it for this purpose, and from the transgression of the Law the abundance of sin, that for deliverance the promises of God may be sought, which is the grace of God; and that there may begin to be in man a justice [0578C] not his own, but God's, that is, given by the gift of God.

## 13.

Which even now certain men, *not knowing the justice of God,* as was then said of the Jews also, *and seeking to establish their own, have not submitted themselves to the justice of God [n: (Rom. X, 3)]*. For they too think themselves justified through the Law, free choice sufficing them for keeping it, that is, by their own justice brought forth from human nature, not given from divine grace, on account of which it is called the justice of God. Whence likewise it is written: *For by the Law is the knowledge of sin. But now without the Law the justice of God is made manifest, being witnessed by the Law and the Prophets [n: (Rom. III, 21)]*. When he says *is made manifest,* he shows that it existed then too, but like that rain which Gedeon obtained, then hidden as in the fleece, but now manifest as on the threshing-floor *(Judic. [0578D] VI, 37)*. Since therefore the Law without grace could have been, not the death of sin, but its strength; for thus it is said, *The sting of death is sin, and the strength of sin is the Law [n: (I Cor. XV, 56)]*: as many now take refuge from the face of reigning sin in grace, lying open as on the threshing-floor; so few took refuge in it when it then lay hidden as in the fleece. But this distribution of the times is referred to the depth of the riches of the wisdom and knowledge of God, of [0579A] which it is said, *How incomprehensible are his judgments, and his ways unsearchable [n: (Rom. XI, 33)]*!



## 14.

Wherefore, if [nt: Quesnel expunged altogether the particle *si*, for which Garnier substituted *sic*.] both before the time of the Law and in the very time of the Law it was not the possibility of a nature weak and [nt: The Roman edition and the Councils and several manuscripts: *indignae*: which arose from *indiguae*; for so the ancients liked to write the word *indigae*.] needy and vitiated and sold under sin, but the grace of God through faith, that justified the just fathers who lived by faith, and now the same grace, coming at last into the open, revealed, justifies: let Pelagius then anathematize his writings, where he disputes against it, if not through obstinacy, yet through ignorance, by defending the possibility of nature for overcoming sins and fulfilling the commandments. Or if he denies that they are his, or says that the writings which he denies are his were foisted among his writings by his enemies, let him nevertheless anathematize and condemn them, at the fatherly exhortation and by the authority of thy holiness. If then he is willing, let him learn to remove the scandal, burdensome [0579B] to himself and pernicious to the [nt: The Pithou manuscript: *Ecclesia*: faultily. Quesnel published this whole passage thus: *si non vult; onerosum sibi et perniciosum esse discat Ecclesiae scandalum afferre, quod auditores*; against the credit of all the codices and the mind of Innocent. But the reading which he judged less correct coheres excellently with what goes before and what follows. Namely, the five bishops counsel Pelagius to learn by what means he may remove the scandal both burdensome to himself and pernicious to the Church. Nor do they doubt that it will be removed as soon as his disciples learn that the book published under his name has been condemned by himself.] Church: a scandal which his hearers, and those who love him in a perverse way, do not cease to spread everywhere. For if they learn that the same book, which they either think or know to be his, has been anathematized and condemned by him himself, on the authority of catholic bishops, and above all of thy holiness, which we by no means doubt carries greater weight with him; we do not think they will dare any longer, by speaking against the grace of God which has been revealed through the passion and resurrection of Christ, to trouble faithful and simply Christian hearts: but rather, with the help of the Lord's mercy, and thy prayers, burning with charity and piety, contending together with us, that they will trust, not only for being blessed for ever, but also for being just and holy [0579C], [nt: In Quesnel the word *conari* is added here: which is more rightly absent from the other books.] not in their own strength, but in that same grace. Wherefore we have thought that the letter written to him himself by one of us, to whom he sent certain writings, as it were of his [nt: Nor was there need here for Quesnel to add the word *causa*. This passage is illustrated by that other one of Augustine's book *On the Proceedings of Pelagius*, c. 32: *In the little paper of his defence, which he sent me by a certain Charus, our fellow citizen of Hippo but a deacon of the East, he did something which stands otherwise than is contained in the episcopal proceedings. But what the proceedings contain is far better and firmer and clearer for the catholic truth against the plague of that heresy.* From which it is manifest that Pelagius called *writings of his clearing*, or proceedings (or, as Augustine says, epist. formerly 252, now 179, n. 7, *a certain defence, as it were, composed by himself, in which he said he had answered the objections of the Gauls*), the adulterated proceedings of the synod of Diospolis which he had sent to Augustine. Of these again Augustine, in the same book *On the Proceedings of Pelagius*, c. 29, speaks thus: *Whatever proceedings of his clearing he may have brought forward.* Nor is it hence less plain that by the five bishops Augustine is to be understood under the name *one of us*; whose letter indeed, sent at that time, has perished and has not come down to us.] clearing, by a certain deacon of the East but a citizen of Hippo, should rather be directed to thy blessedness [sic: *beattiudini*], judging it better and [0580A] asking that thou thyself deign to [nt: Quesnel, with one Colbert manuscript and the Pithou: *ut eam emittere*. The reading of the other books is sounder.] send it to him. For thus he will not disdain rather to read it, attending in it more to him who sent it than to him who wrote it.

## 15.

But as for what they say, that man can be without sin and easily keep the commandments of God if he will: although, when this is said to be through the help of grace, which nevertheless was revealed and bestowed through the incarnation of his Only-begotten, it may seem more tolerable to say; yet, since it can, not undeservedly, [nt: Merlin and Crabbe: *quoniam merito quaeri potest*. Quesnel kept *quaeri potest*: which we have found in no manuscript. For *quaeri*, Crabbe noted *debere* in the margin, which the Pithou manuscript also has in the text.] move [us], where and when it is brought about in us through that same grace that we be altogether without any sin, whether in this life, when the flesh lusts against the spirit; or rather in that one, when the saying shall come to pass which is written [n: (Galat. V, 17)]: *O death, where is thy victory? O death, where is thy sting? for the sting of death is sin* [0580B] [n: (I Cor. XV, 55)]; it must be handled more diligently on account of certain others, who have held, and have committed to memory in their writings, that even in this life man can be without sin, not from the beginning of his birth, but by conversion from sins to justice, and from a [nt: In Garnier and the Augustine edition: *a vita*. Pelagius at one time came over to the opinion of those of whom there is question here. For Augustine, epist. formerly 252, now 179, n. 7, relates that he wrote this to him: *We did not say that anyone is found who from infancy to old age has never sinned; but that, having been converted from sin by his own labour and helped by the grace of God, he can be without sin.* But that he did not always stand by this opinion the same Doctor proves in the same epistle, n. 8.] reprobate way to a good life [n: (S. Ambros. in I Lucae 6)]. For so they understood what is written of Zachary and Elizabeth, that they walked *in all the justifications of the Lord without blame (Lucae I, 6)*. This that is said, *without blame,* they took as said for "without sin," not indeed denying, nay rather, as is found in other places in their writings, piously confessing the help of the grace of our Lord, not through the natural spirit of man, but through the principal Spirit of God. Who seem to have considered too little that Zachary himself [0580C] was certainly a priest. And all priests at that time were bound by the law of God to offer sacrifice first for their own sins, then for those of the people [n: (Levit. IX, 7; Hebr. VII, 27)]. As then now [nt: In one of the Colbert manuscripts: *per orationes sacrificiorum*.] we are convicted, through the sacrifice of prayer, of not being without sin, since we are commanded to say, *Forgive [0581A] us our debts [n: (Matth. VI, 12)]*: so then too the priests were convicted, through the sacrifices of animal victims, of not being without sin, since they were commanded to offer for their own sins. But if the matter stands thus, that through the grace of the Saviour we make progress indeed in this life, as desire decreases and charity grows; but are made perfect in that life, when desire is extinguished and charity completed: assuredly that which is written, [nt: The same manuscript: *Qui ex Deo est*. The other Colbert, with the Pithou: *Ex Deo*, the words *Qui natus est* being omitted.] *He who is born of God sins not [n: (I Joan. III, 9)]*, is said according to charity itself, which alone does not sin. For to the birth which is of God belongs charity, which is to be increased and perfected, not desire, which is to be diminished and consumed: which nevertheless, so long as it is in our members, by a certain law of its own fights against the law of the mind [n: (Rom. VII, 20)]. But he who is born of [0581B] God, neither obeying its desires, nor yielding his members as weapons of iniquity to sin [n: (Rom. VI, 13)], can say, *Now it is no more I that do it, but the sin that dwells in me [n: (Rom. VII, 20)]*.

## 16.

But however that question stands, since, even if a man is not found without sin in this life, it is nevertheless said that this can come about through the help of grace and of the Spirit of God, and that in order that it come about it must be striven for and prayed for: anyone is tolerably mistaken in this; nor is it diabolical impiety, but human error, to affirm what is to be laboured for and wished for, even if he cannot show what he affirms. For he believes that that can come about which it is certainly praiseworthy to will. But it suffices us that no one of the faithful is found in the Church of God, in any [nt: The Roman edition and the Councils: *profectu excellenti atque justitia*. Garnier and the Isidorian manuscripts: *profectu, excellentia atque justitia*: they are corrected from Quesnel, the Augustine edition and the better manuscripts.] advancement and excellence of justice, who would dare to say to himself [0581C] that the petition of the Lord's prayer, *Forgive us our debts [n: (Matth. VI, 12)]*, is not necessary; and to say that he has no sin, lest he deceive himself, and the truth be not in him, although he already lives without blame [n: (I Joan. I)]. For it is not [nt: Merlin and Quesnel, with one Colbert manuscript and the Pithou: *qualescumque etiam tentationes*. Truer is the reading of the other codices, by which we are taught that those who are proclaimed to live without blame are to be understood as immune from grave sins indeed, but not likewise from light ones, which we contract through temptation or human frailty.] any sin whatever of human temptation, but grave sin, that comes to blame. [0582A]

## 17.

The other things which were objected to him, thy blessedness will without doubt judge of them, just as thou shalt have seen them defended in the proceedings. The most gentle sweetness of thy heart will surely grant us pardon for having sent to thy holiness a letter perhaps longer than thou wouldst wish. For we do not pour back our rivulet into thy large fountain to increase it; but in this [nt: In two Colbert manuscripts: *non cum parva*. In the rest: *non comparva*. In the Augustine edition: *non tamen parva*. But Merlin and Crabbe, after the word *refundimus* above, immediately subjoin *verum etiam no ter licet exiguus*, etc., the intermediate words being omitted.] not small temptation of the time, from which may he deliver us to whom we say, *Bring us not into temptation,* we wish this to be approved by thee, whether ours too, though small, flows from the same head of streams as thine abundant one also, and to be consoled by thy rescript concerning the common participation of the one grace.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 24. And what *orat* 29 is now 7.] XXIX. *Innocent, writing back to the bishops of the council of Carthage, praises and confirms their doctrine and judgment against the Pelagian heretics.*

*INNOCENT [nt: The printed editions, except the Augustine edition, following the old copies of the Code published by Quesnel and of Isidore: *Aurelio et omnibus sanctis episcopis vel caeteris qui in concilio Carthaginensis ecclesiae adfuerunt*, omitting the names of the individual bishops, which are recorded in the sounder manuscripts.] to AURELIUS, NUMIDIUS, RUSTICIANUS, FIDENTIANUS, EVAGRIUS, ANTONIUS, PALATINUS, ADEODATUS, VINCENTIUS, PUBLIANUS, THEASIUS, TUTUS, PANNONIUS, VICTOR, RESTITUTUS, [nt: Hence it is clear that this second Restitutus too is missing in the inscription of epistle 26.] another RESTITUTUS, RUSTICUS, FORTUNATIANUS, AMPELIUS, AMBIVIUS, FELIX, DONATIANUS, ADEODATUS, OCTAVIUS, SEROTINUS, MAJORINUS, POSTHUMIANUS, CRISPULUS, VICTOR, another VICTOR, LEUCIUS, MARIANUS, FRUCTUOSUS, PROCULUS, FAUSTINIANUS, QUODVULTDEUS, CANDORIUS, MAXIMUS, MEGASIUS, RUSTICUS, RUFINIANUS, PROCULUS, SEVERUS, THOMAS, JANUARIUS, OCTAVIANUS, PRAETEXTATUS, SIXTUS, QUODVULTDEUS, PENTADIUS, QUODVULTDEUS, CYPRIAN, SERVILIUS, PELAGIANUS, MARCELLUS, VENANTIUS, DIDYMUS, [0582C] SATURNINUS, BIZACENUS, GERMANUS, GERMANIANUS, INVENITUS, MAJORINUS, INVENITUS, CANDIDUS, CYPRIAN, ROMANIANUS, AEMILIANUS, AFRICANUS, MARCELLINUS, and the rest who were present at the council of Carthage, most beloved brethren, greeting in the Lord.*

## 1.

In [nt: The edition of the Councils: *Inquirendo de his rebus*. In Garnier, Quesnel and the Augustine edition: *In requirendo de his rebus*. The Roman edition and some manuscripts: *In requirendis de his rebus*. More truly the manuscripts of Saint-Remi, Corbie and Saint-Germain: *In requirendis Dei rebus*; and that reading is confirmed by the letter of the Africans to Pope Theodore, inserted in the Lateran council under Martin I, act. 2, tom. VI of Labbe's Councils, p. 128, whose opening, as we noted before, being modelled on the words and sentences of Innocent, it will be of use to reproduce here. Thus then they begin: *That a great and unfailing fountain, overflowing with streams for all Christians, stands at the Apostolic see, no one could doubt; from which rivulets come forth abundantly, most largely watering the whole world of Christians; to which also, in honour of the most blessed Peter, the decrees of the fathers have decreed all special reverence IN SEEKING OUT THE THINGS OF GOD, which ought altogether and carefully, but most of all and justly, to be examined by the very apostolic summit of prelates, whose ancient solicitude it is as much to condemn evil things as to approve things worthy of praise. For it was ordained by ancient rules that whatever was done, even in provinces set in remote or distant places, should not be treated or received before it had been brought to the notice of your kindly see, so that by its authority whatever pronouncement had been just might be confirmed, and thence the other churches might take the beginning of their preaching as from their native fountain, and throughout the diverse regions of the whole world the sacraments of the faith of salvation might remain of uncorrupted purity.*] seeking out the things of God, which it befits to be handled by priests with all solicitude, above all by a true and just [0583A] and catholic council, you, keeping the examples of ancient tradition and mindful of ecclesiastical discipline, have confirmed with true reason the vigour of your religion, no less now in consulting than before when you pronounced; you who have approved that the matter should be referred to our judgment, knowing what is owed to the apostolic see, since all of us set in this place desire to follow the Apostle himself, from whom the episcopate itself and the whole authority of this name arose. Following him, we know how as much to condemn evil things as to approve things worthy of praise. Or this indeed, that, guarding the institutions of the fathers by your priestly office, you judge that they are not to be trampled upon, which they decreed by a sentence not human but divine: that whatever was done, even from separated and remote provinces, they should not consider as to be concluded before it had come to the notice of this see, [0583B] so that by its whole authority whatever pronouncement had been just might be confirmed; and that thence the other churches might take (as all waters proceeded from their native fountain, and throughout the diverse regions of the whole world the pure [nt: The Roman edition and the Councils, and also Quesnel: *puri capitis*; then Merlin: *incorrupti*; Crabbe: *incorrupte*; the others: *incorruptae*: they are restored from the Augustine edition and the Corbie and Saint-Germain manuscripts. Innocent took the matter of this opening chiefly from what the five bishops had written to him at the end of the preceding letter: *For we do not pour back our rivulet into thy large fountain... whether ours too, though small, flows from the same head of streams as thine abundant one also, this we wish to be approved by thee.*] streams of an uncorrupted head flowed forth) what to command [nt: So the Saint-Germain and Corbie manuscripts. But many others, with the Roman edition and the Councils: *quid praecipere*. Then in the Saint-Germain codex *quos absolvere*, where in the others *quos abluere*. In Quesnel and the Augustine edition *quid praeciperent, quos abluerent* has been put back. But Garnier, indulging his own ingenuity more than was fair, edited this whole passage thus: *unde aquas sumerent caeterae ecclesiae, velut de natali suo fonte a quo cuncta procedunt, et per diversas totius mundi regiones puri capitis incorruptae manant; unde perciperet quos veluti coeno*, etc. Our reading, in which there is nothing not supported by the authority of the old codices, will be complete if after the words *quos abluere* one understands *deberent*. Nor let us think it rash that a threefold class of men presenting themselves to the judgment of the Church is distinguished here, and a threefold manner of that same Church's judgments concerning them, drawn from a threefold property of water, is set forth. For she, like clean water, of her own accord receives into herself the harmless and undefiled as pure rivulets; the soiled but penitent she washes; and the uncorrected, who could stain her, she prudently avoids and rejects.] *[Forte leg. praeciperent]* whom to wash, whom, soiled as with mire that cannot be cleansed, the water worthy of clean bodies should avoid.



## 2.

I rejoice therefore, dearest brethren, that you have sent a letter to us by our brother and fellow bishop Julius: and that, while you take care of the churches over which you preside, you show your solicitude for the benefit of all: and that you ask that something be decreed which may profit all alike throughout all the churches of the whole world: so that the Church, established by her own rules [0583C], [nt: The Roman edition and the more recent Councils have *ex hoc*, and then, with the older ones, *quod illos caveat pronuntiationis justae firmata decretalibus* (Merlin: *petere*; Crabbe: *patere*; the Roman edition and those following: *pavere*) *non possit*. Garnier, cutting out *hoc quod illos caveat*, kept *et pronuntiationis justae firmata decretalibus pavere non possit*. Our reading is that of Quesnel, the Augustine edition and the better manuscripts, except that in Quesnel *hoc* has been struck out, and in the Augustine edition, as in the Corbie and Saint-Germain manuscripts, *parete* stands in place of *patere*. It is then to be understood thus, as if Innocent said: You ask that it be decreed by me that the Church, already established by her ancient rules, being now also strengthened by this new decree justly pronounced, by which she is bidden to beware of those heretics mentioned, may not henceforth lie open to such men, but may remain shut off from them for ever.] strengthened also by this decree of just pronouncement, by which she may beware of them, may not lie open to such men as, instructed, nay rather destroyed, by perverse subtleties [0584A] of words, disputing under the appearance of the catholic faith, as if breathing out a pestilential poison, so as to corrupt towards the worse part the hearts of men who think rightly, seek to overturn the whole discipline of true doctrine. It must therefore be healed the more speedily, lest an accursed [nt: One of the Colbert manuscripts: *exsecrandis animorum moribus inserpat*. The other: *exsecrandos animis morbos inserpat*. The Roman edition and the Councils, with some other manuscripts, keep the word *inserpat*. In its place the Saint-Germain and Corbie manuscripts better show *inrepat*.] disease creep further into souls: just as a physician, when he sees some sickness in this earthly body, reckons it a great proof of his art if someone despaired of quickly escapes through his intervention; or when he has looked upon a putrid wound, applies poultices, or the other things by which the wound that had arisen may be closed over; and if, remaining, it cannot be healed, lest it corrupt the rest of the body with its decay, he cuts off with the knife what was doing harm, so as to keep the rest whole and untouched. That must therefore be cut off [0584B] which has crept upon the body, all too pure and sound, like a wound; lest, when it is cleansed too slowly, a bilge of this evil that cannot afterwards be drained should settle almost in the very vitals.

## 3.

For what are we henceforth to reckon right in their minds concerning these men, who think that they owe it to themselves that they are good, and do not consider him whose grace they obtain daily? But now these men, who are such, obtain no grace of God; [nt: Crabbe, and after him the editions of the Councils and the Roman: *quia sine illo*.] who trust that they can attain without him as much as those who ask of him scarcely [nt: The Roman edition and the Councils, with the Pithou and Isidorian manuscripts: *et accipiunt*. One Colbert codex: *ut accipiunt*; whence Quesnel preferred *ut accipiant*. In the Augustine edition, as in the Corbie manuscript: *accipere*, the particle *et* not being put before it, which is kept with the Saint-Germain and others. The sentence will be clear if arranged in this way: *quantum vix illi etiam, qui postulant, accipere promerentur*.] also merit to receive. For what can be so unjust, so barbarous, so ignorant of all religion, so hostile to Christian minds, as to deny that thou owest whatever thou obtainest in daily grace to him to whom thou thyself confessest that thou [0584C] owest that thou wast born? Wilt thou then be more excellent for thyself in providing, than [nt: So the manuscripts. But the Roman edition and the Councils: *quam potest is esse*.] can be in him who brought it about that thou shouldst be? [nt: Garnier: *Si huic putes debere*. Other printed editions: *Et cum te putes ei debere*. Pelagius is here refuted without being named: who, distinguishing to be able, to will and to be, placed being able in nature, willing in choice, and being in effect. Then he so granted that being able belongs properly to God, the author of nature, as to contend that willing and being, or acting, are to be referred to man. He taught that it is of God alone that man is able to will and to accomplish the good; but that he wills to accomplish it, and actually accomplishes it, and so too that he wills to be good and perfect, or even is so, he proclaimed to be of man himself. He did not deny that man is always helped by the aid of grace: but he placed that aid in this, that God never fails to give the possibility of willing and of working. See his very words in Augustine, book *On the Grace of Christ*, cap. 4.] And to whom thou thinkest thou owest that thou livest, [0585A] how dost thou not think thou owest to him that, by obtaining his daily grace, thou livest thus? And thou who deniest that we need the divine help, as if we [nt: In the Roman edition and the Councils *simus* is missing.] were entirely perfect from our own possibility, how do we not call forth his help [nt: The word *ejus* is recalled from the Saint-Germain manuscript and the Augustine edition. Garnier alone presents this passage thus: *Dic quomodo vere adjutorium ejus in nos, cum tales a nobis etiam esse possimus, provocemus. Eum qui adjutorium Dei negat, rursus vellem interrogare cur dicat nos id non promereri?*] upon us, when we can be such even of ourselves?

## 4.

For I should like to ask him who denies the help of God what he says: that we do not deserve it, or that he cannot bestow it, or that there is nothing on account of which each one ought [nt: Quesnel, with one Colbert manuscript: *praestolari*.] to ask for it? That God is able to do this, his works themselves testify. And that we need daily help, we cannot deny. For whether we live well, [nt: So the manuscripts, and also the old editions of the Councils. But the Roman edition and the more recent Councils, with Garnier: *precamur*.] we call it forth, that we may live better and more holily; or whether, thinking wrongly, we are turned away from good things, we need his aid the more, that we may return to the right way. [0585B] For what should seem so deadly, so headlong towards a fall, so exposed to all dangers, as if, thinking that this alone can suffice us, that we received free choice when we were born, we should seek nothing further from the Lord, that is, forgetful of our author, should abjure his power in order to show ourselves free; as though he who made thee free at thy birth had nothing more that he could give: not knowing that unless grace, implored with great prayers, descends [nt: Garnier: *nequidquam*; and below, *David inclamat*, for *Nam si ille clamat*.] upon us, we should by no means strive to overcome the errors of earthly defilement and of the worldly body, since not free choice, but the aid of God alone, can make us equal to resisting?

## 5.

For if he cries out that he has need of the divine help, who would not worthily have sought it, if free [0585C] choice were of more profit to anyone; since, although a blessed man and already chosen by the Lord, he lacked nothing, yet he so beseeches God, asking: *Be thou my helper, forsake me not, neither despise me, O God my saviour [n: (Psal. XXVI, 9)]*: we ask free choice for ourselves, he [0586A] asks God for himself as helper; we say that what we were born with can suffice us, he prays God that he be not forsaken. [nt: The Roman edition: *Nos ergo dicimus manifeste*, etc. Garnier: *Nos dicimus manifeste, ut quid oremus Deum, ille*: and below, *David ergo*, for *David enim*.] Do we not, I ask, learn plainly what we should pray for, when that man, so greatly blessed, as we said above, longs not to be despised? For those who confirm the one must needs refute the other. For let David be accused of being ignorant of prayer and unaware of his own nature, who, though he knows that so much is present in [nt: The Roman edition and the Councils: *in sua natura esse*. Next, Garnier: *adjutorem invocat, nec illi sufficit assiduum invocare*: against the other books, from which it is clear that the word *adjutorem* is governed by the verb *exoptat*.] his nature, longs for God as his helper, and a constant helper, nor does constant suffice him, but, bowed down in prayers, longs that he may never despise him; and throughout the whole body of the psalter he both proclaims and cries this. If then he, so great [nt: Some manuscripts with Quesnel, and also the Roman edition and the Councils: *ita magnum scit, ut assidue diceret*. In the Augustine edition: *ita magnus sicut assidue dicere*. Garnier: *hoc illi tam magnum visum sit, ut assidue diceret, tam necessarium confessum sit*. We keep what the more numerous and older manuscripts present. We should prefer, however, *ita magnus scivit assidue dicere, et ita*.], knows so to say this continually, so confessed it necessary as to teach it: how do Pelagius and Coelestius, setting aside every answer [0586B] of the psalms and renouncing such teaching, trust that they will persuade some that we neither ought to seek the help of God nor need it; when all the saints testify that they can do nothing without it?

## 6.

For that man once, having undergone free [nt: Merlin, and from him Quesnel: *Libero enim arbitrio olim ille perversus*; against all the manuscripts and the other printed editions, not only here, but also in book II of Augustine to Boniface, cap. 4, n. 6, in Prosper's book against the Collator, c. 5, n. 15, and in the epistle of Celestine 21, n. 5, where these same words are cited. Certainly the sense of Innocent calls for *permissus* rather than *perversus*.] choice, while he used his goods too unadvisedly, falling, was sunk into the depths of transgression, and found no way by which he could rise from there; and, deceived for ever by his own liberty, he would have lain under the crushing of this ruin [nt: In the manuscripts, as in the editions of Merlin and the Roman: *ruinae latuisset oppressus* or *oppressum*, except that in the Pithou *oppressu* stands, and in the Corbie *jacuisset* has been restored by an ancient hand: an emendation proved right both by Augustine's book II to Boniface against the two letters of the Pelagians, n. 6, and by the epistle 21 of Pope Celestine, cap. 4, where these words are cited. Although in those two places too manuscripts are not lacking which present *latuisset oppressu*. In Crabbe, and thence in the editions of the Councils, one may read here *huic ruinae suae jacuisset oppressus*; in Garnier and Quesnel, *hujus ruina subjacuisset oppressus*.], had not the coming of Christ afterwards [nt: So the manuscripts with great agreement, together with the Augustine edition. But in the printed editions: *per suam gratiam*.] raised him up for his grace: who through the purification of the new regeneration cleansed all past fault by the laver of his baptism; and, strengthening his state, so that he might proceed more rightly and more steadily, yet did not deny his grace for the time to come. For although he had redeemed [0586C] man from past sins, yet knowing that he could sin again, he kept in reserve for himself many things for his restoration, by which he could correct him even after these things: he supplies daily remedies, and unless we strive relying and trusting on them, we shall by no means be able to overcome [0587A] human errors. For it is necessary that, as we conquer when he helps, so, when he does not help, we be conquered again.

## 7.

But I could say more, were it not evident that you have said everything. Whoever therefore is seen to assent to this opinion, by which he would say that we have no need of the divine help, professes himself an enemy of the catholic faith and ungrateful for the benefits of God. [nt: The conjunction *Nam* is recalled from the editions of Merlin, Crabbe, the Augustine edition and most manuscripts. Then in the Isidorian manuscripts *nostra* is missing. Next the Roman edition and the Councils, with some manuscripts: *Sic enim*, where with the other books we prefer *Ipsi enim*. But Garnier, judging this passage mutilated, advises by a note written in the margin that it could be repaired thus: *Pelagium igitur et Coelestium, quos auctores nefarii prorsus et omnibus anathematizandi erroris advertistis, justa sententia damnastis: nec illi nostra*, etc. But these words of theirs at the end of the preceding epistle 26 do not sound like a sentence pronounced by name against Pelagius and Coelestius by the fathers of the council of Carthage: *If Pelagius and Coelestius have been corrected, or say that they never held these things, and deny that whatever writings have been brought forward against them are theirs, and there is no way in which they may be convicted of lying, yet let whoever shall have been found a champion of the doctrines whose authors they are thought to be be anathema in general terms.* Nothing appears in this passage of Innocent either, from which it should be judged *matilus*.] For neither are they worthy of our communion, which they have polluted by preaching in such a way. For they themselves of their own accord, while they follow those things which they say, have fled further from the true religion. For since the whole of this consists in our profession, and in our daily prayers we do nothing but seek how we may obtain the mercy of God; how can we bear these men boasting? What error, [0587C] I ask, so great blinds their hearts, that if they themselves feel no grace of God, because they are neither worthy nor deserve it, they do not even consider concerning others what divine grace bestows daily on each? They are indeed [nt: So in Garnier and the Augustine edition, as in the better manuscripts. But in other books: *omnes*; and below, *Negantes enim Dei adjutorium*.] most worthy of all blindness, who have not left even this to themselves, that they should believe that they can be recalled from their errors by the divine aid. For denying help, they have taken this away utterly not from others but from themselves: they are to be torn further away and removed far from the vitals of the Church; lest [nt: The printed editions, except the Augustine edition: *ne diu multa*. More fittingly in the Augustine edition, as in the Corbie and Saint-Germain manuscripts: *ne diu inultus multa*. Hence there is aptly joined on: *Si enim diu fuerint sub hac IMPUNITATE*.] the error, long unpunished, seizing on many things, should afterwards grow incurable. For if they have long lived under this impunity, they must needs lead many into this depravity of their mind, and deceive the innocent, or rather the unwary, who follow the catholic [nt: This reading of Garnier, which is also that of the Corbie and Saint-Germain manuscripts, Quesnel approves, although he himself keeps the common one in the Roman edition and the Councils, namely *non sequuntur*. But here in the Augustine edition the particle *nunc* has dropped out by the copyist's drowsiness.] faith now. For they will think that those think rightly [0587C] whom they see still remaining in the Church. Let the unsound wound then be separated from the sound body, and, the breath of the raging disease being removed, let the things that are sincere endure more safely, and let the purer flock be cleansed from this contagion of the evil beast. Let there be an unimpaired perfection of the whole body, which we have learned that you follow and hold by this pronouncement against them, and which we preserve together with you with equal assent. [0588A]

## 8.
