Yet if they call forth in themselves some [nt: In the Roman edition and the Councils: *aliquando in se*.] help of God, which up to now they have denied, and recognize that they need his aid, so that they may be freed from this stain into which they had fallen by the crookedness of their heart; and, as if drawn into the light out of a foul darkness, all things being removed and renounced by which their whole sight, lest it should behold [nt: In Quesnel and the Augustine edition: *aspicerent*.] the truth, was befouled and darkened, let them condemn these things which up to now they have held; and at length, applying their mind to right disputations, corrected in some small measure from this stain, they give and submit themselves to be healed [nt: In Garnier: *conciliis*. The preceding word, *sanandos*, which Quesnel removed, all our manuscripts preserve.] by true counsels: if they do this, it will be in the power of the bishops to come to their aid in some degree, and to afford such wounds some care, which the Church is not wont to deny to the lapsed when they have come to their senses: so that, [nt: The word *revocati* is missing in Merlin. In its place the Roman edition and the later Councils have substituted *remoti*.] recalled [0588C] from their precipices, they may be brought back within the sheepfold of the Lord: lest, set outside, and shut out from so great a protection [nt: Quesnel: *praesidio aede munitionis*. Garnier: *praesidio fidae munitionis*. This last reading displeases less.] of the defence of faith, they be exposed to all dangers, to be devoured and harried by the teeth of wolves, whom they could not resist with this perversity of doctrine by which they had provoked them against themselves.




## 9.

But it is proved that sufficient answer has been made by your admonitions, so abounding in examples of our law. Nor have we thought that anything remains for us to say, since it is evident that nothing has been passed over by you, nothing suppressed, by which they may be known to be refuted and utterly convicted. And therefore no testimonies are set down by us, because in these things your report is full, and it is sufficiently evident that so many most learned priests have said everything: nor is it fitting to believe that you have passed over anything which could [0588C] profit the cause. *And in another hand:* Farewell, brethren. *And at the side:* Given on the sixth day before the Kalends of February [n: (Jan. 27 ann. 417)] after the consulship [nt: In the Corbie manuscript: *Luni Quarti*. In the Saint-Germain: *p. c. Theodosio augusto VII et Lucio Quarto v. consulibus*: from the ignorance of a copyist, who did not know that by the former note *p. c.* is signified *post consulatum*, and that therefore *Theodosii augusti*, etc., was to be kept, not *Theodosio augusto* substituted; and who did not know that the latter note, *v. c.*, was to be rendered by the words *viri clarissimi*, not by *v consulibus*.] of Theodosius Augustus for the seventh time and of Junius Quartus, V. C.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 25. And what was 30 is now in the appendix.] XXX. OF POPE INNOCENT TO THE COUNCIL OF MILEVIS. *He praises the fathers of the council of Milevis, both because they judged that obstinate heretics were to be separated and the corrected to be received, and because, following the canons received everywhere, they sought the judgment of the apostolic see on anxious matters. Then he proves that the heresy which will have it that man can suffice to himself without the grace of God, and denies that baptism is necessary for little ones for eternal life, fights against the Scriptures. He orders its authors and their followers to be cut off from the Church, but to be received if they condemn their errors.*

*INNOCENT to SILVANUS the elder, VALENTINUS, [nt: It is the more probable that in this inscription Innocent addressed by name the individual bishops who had been named at the head of the letter of the council itself, because the only copies of this rescript that survive are those which, likewise in the preceding epistle, after naming Aurelius, indicated the other bishops under a general formula.] and the rest who were present at the synod of Milevis, most beloved brethren, greeting in the Lord.*

## I.

Among the other cares of the Roman church, and the occupations of the apostolic see, in which we treat the consultations of various persons with faithful and [nt: In the Roman edition and the Councils, from the Isidorian manuscripts: *modica*: a fault which Garnier, trying to correct it on his own, adds *non* before *modica*.] healing deliberation, our brother and fellow bishop Julius unexpectedly thrust upon me the letter of your charity [0589B] which you sent, with a more inclined care for the faith, from the council of Milevis, [nt: Only in copies of the Isidorian collection, and thence in Merlin and Crabbe: *ex Milevitano ex nostra auctoritate concilio*: a gloss worthy of Mercator, and deservedly removed in the Roman edition. As if indeed Innocent would have said that the letter of a council which had been assembled by his own authority had been thrust upon him unexpectedly.] adding also the writings of the synod of Carthage with a like complaint. Truly the Church exults that the shepherds show such solicitude for the flocks committed to them, that not only do they suffer none of them to go astray; but if the grass of a perverse [nt: Merlin: *sive*; Crabbe: *saevae*; the other editions of the Councils, after the Roman, and also Garnier: *obscaenae*; more rightly the other books: *scaerae*: which Labbe too noted in the margin. Next, the Isidorian manuscripts: *dilectionis*.] delight has rather led astray any of the sheep, they are willing either, if these remain in error, to have them wholly separated, or, if they avoid what they had long unlawfully sought, to protect them with the circumspection of their former keeping: taking counsel, that is, on both sides, lest either by receiving such, the others [nt: Quesnel: *seducantur*.] be led by a like example; or, by spurning those who return, these should seem to be thrown to the bites of wolves. Very prudent, and full of the catholic faith, is [nt: Garnier: *consolatio*. The Roman edition and the Councils, with the Isidorian manuscripts: *consultationis voluntas*. In one Colbert manuscript *consultationis* indeed stands, but *voluntas* is absent. More apt, as Quesnel has already observed, though a less Latin word, is *consultio*, which the other Colbert codex presents. By this name, then, is called that by which they take counsel for both sides, or use this counsel, neither to admit the obstinate nor to spurn those returning.] this counsel. For who could either tolerate [0589C] one who errs, or not receive [nt: In the Augustine edition: *se corrigentem*.] one who corrects himself? For as [0590A] I think it hard to show connivance to those who sin; so I judge it impious to refuse the hand to the converted.

## 2.

Diligently therefore and fittingly [nt: Garnier, with the Roman edition: *apostolico consulitis honori, honori*. Quesnel, following one Colbert manuscript: *apostolici consulitis honoris arcanis, honoris*. Nor does the other Colbert codex differ, except that for *arcanis* it has *arcani*. The Isidorian manuscripts: *apostolici consulitis honores arcani, honoris*. One of the Vatican manuscripts: *apostolici consulitis oneris arcana, oneris*. Sounder is the reading which we present here after the edition of the Councils and the Augustine edition. For this is what it means: you who consult the apostolic see, whose it is to disclose secret things. In the same manner of speaking Boniface I, epist. 5, n. 4, says *quae hujus consulis sedis arcanum*. Hence it is clear that *arcam* was wrongly written here in Crabbe's margin in place of the word *arcana*.] do you consult the secret counsels of the apostolic honour (of that honour, I say [sic: *in quam*], upon which, besides the things that are without, the care of all the churches waits) as to what judgment is to be held on anxious matters [n: (II Cor. XI, 28)]: following, that is, the form of the ancient rule, which you know, as I do, has always been kept by the whole world. But these things I let pass: for I do not believe that this is hidden from your prudence. [nt: So our manuscripts. The same sense is also given by what is in Merlin: *Qui id etiam actione firmastis, nisi scientes*. But the Roman edition and the Councils, after Crabbe: *quid id etiam*, with *nisi* then suppressed: which Quesnel likewise omits, preferring before it *quod etiam*. In the Augustine edition: *Qui id enim actione*, etc. In Garnier: *Cur id enim actione*, etc. If to anyone this, that *responses always flow from the apostolic fountain through all the provinces to those who ask*, should seem exaggerated, let him recall what Jerome wrote, epist. 110, now 91: *When I was helping Damasus, bishop of the city of Rome, with ecclesiastical papers, and answering the synodical consultations of the East and of the West.*] Why have you also confirmed this by action, unless knowing that through all the provinces responses always flow from the apostolic fountain to those who ask? Especially [n: (24, q. 1, c. 12)] whenever the reason of the faith is discussed, I think that all our brethren and fellow bishops ought to refer to none but [0590B] Peter, that is, to the author of their name and honour, [nt: Inasmuch as from him the episcopate itself and the whole authority of this name arose, as he has it in the preceding epistle, n. 1; or, as Siricius says, epist. 5, n. 5, *through whom both the apostolate and the episcopate took their beginning in Christ*: which our Innocent too copies out, epist. 2, n. 2.] as your charity has now referred, what may profit all the churches in common throughout the whole world. For they must needs become more cautious, when they see the inventors of evils, on the report of a double synod, by the statutes of our judgment [nt: Quesnel alone: *se ab ecclesiastica communione sejungi*. Next Garnier: *fruetur*, for *fungetur*.] cut off from ecclesiastical communion. Your charity will therefore enjoy a twofold good. For you will both possess the grace of the canons observed, and the whole world will [nt: In the Roman edition and the Councils: *tutabitur*.] make use of your benefit. For who among catholic men would wish any longer to mingle speech with the adversaries of Christ? Who would share even the very light of life in common with them? [nt: The particle *cum* is put before it here in one manuscript.] Let the authors of the new heresy surely be shunned.

## 3.

For what more bitter could they feign against the Lord, [0590C] than when they made void the divine helps, and took away the cause [0591A] of daily prayer? This is to say, What need have I of God? Deservedly might the hymn-singer say against them: *Behold the men who did not make God their helper [var: Vulg. "Ecce homo qui non posuit," in the singular: Behold the man who did not make] [n: (Psalm. LI, 9)]*. Denying therefore the aid of God, they say that man can suffice to himself, and does not need divine grace: deprived of which, he must needs fall, entangled in the snares of the devil, while he strives for fulfilling all the commandments of life by his liberty alone. O perverse doctrine of most depraved minds! [nt: In the old editions of the Councils, as in the Augustine edition: *Advertat id tandem*. In the other printed editions and two manuscripts: *Adverte tandem*: which the epistle 21 of Celestine also favours, c. 7, where this passage is quoted thus: *Adverte tandem o pravissimarum mentium doctrina*. Wherefore, although the order of Innocent's words is changed there, yet on account of so ancient an authority we should incline to keep *adverte*, did not the train of the sentence resist it, in which one reads not *audi*, as Garnier and Quesnel edited against the manuscripts, but *audiat*. It pleases more, therefore, with some manuscripts, *advertens*, or *attendens*.] Noticing at last that liberty itself so deceived the first man that, while he used its reins too indulgently, he fell by presumption into transgression, [nt: Quesnel, with one Colbert manuscript: *praesumptionis*. Merlin and Crabbe, with the Isidorian manuscripts: *in praevaricationis praesumptione*.] and could not be rescued from it unless the coming of Christ the Lord had, by the providence of regeneration, restored [nt: Quesnel alone: *adventu*.] to him the state of his former liberty: let it hear David saying: [0591B] *Our help is in the name of the Lord [n: (Psalm. CXXIII, 8)]*; and, *Be thou my helper, forsake me not, neither despise me, O God my saviour [n: (Psalm. XXVI, 9)]*. Which he would have said in vain, if [nt: Garnier: *si totum*.] what he asked of the Lord with tearful speech had lain so fully in his own will.

## 4.

Since these things are so, and since in all the divine pages we read that nothing but the help of God is to be joined to the free will, and that it can do nothing when destitute of heavenly aids; in what manner do Pelagius and Coelestius, obstinately defending, as you assert, this possibility for it alone, persuade themselves, nay, what is [nt: In Quesnel: *gravius et dignius*. Then for *dolore communi* Garnier substitutes *dolore, communiter*. Then for the particle *jam*, which is suppressed in the Roman edition, *nam* wrongly stands in Merlin and Crabbe.] more worthy of common grief, already very many? We could indeed use manifold examples [nt: Two Colbert manuscripts: *ad instruendum*: which reading Merlin and Crabbe followed.] for destroying such a teaching, did we not know [0591C] that your holiness is fully versed in all the divine scriptures; especially since your report is so filled with so many and such testimonies, that by these alone the present doctrine could be cut down; and that there is no need of recondite ones, since they neither dare nor are able to oppose even those which you set down as readily occurring to you. So [nt: Garnier: *Ergone Dei*.] they try to take away the grace of God, which we must needs seek even when the liberty of our former state has been restored to us; since [nt: Quesnel: *quippe qui nec aliquas*; Garnier: *quippe nefarias*. Our manuscripts support neither.] we cannot avoid the other machinations of the devil either, except [0592A] with the same helping [n: (Augustin. lib. II, ad Bonifac., c. 4, n. 7)].

## 5.

But that which your fraternity asserts that they preach, that little ones can be rewarded with the prizes of eternal life even without the grace of baptism, is utterly foolish. For unless they eat the flesh of the son of man, and drink his blood, they shall not have life in themselves [n: (Joan. VI, 54)]. But those who defend this for them without regeneration seem to me to wish to make void baptism itself, since they preach that these have what is believed to be conferred on them only by baptism. If then they will have it that not to be reborn does no harm: they must needs confess that the sacred streams of regeneration are of no profit either. But, that the depraved doctrine of superfluous men may be ungirded by the swift reason [nt: So Quesnel and two Colbert manuscripts. But Merlin, with another Colbert manuscript: *distingui*: faultily. Nor better the other books: *discindi*. Silius too said *dolos ratione discingere*.] of truth, the Lord proclaims [0592B] this in the Gospel, saying: *Suffer the little ones, and forbid them [nt: Two Colbert manuscripts: *prohibere a me. Pro qua re Pelagium*.] not to come to me: for of such is the kingdom of heaven [var: Vulg. "pueros … regnum Dei," children … the kingdom of God] (Lucae XVIII, 16)*.

## 6.

Wherefore we decree, by the authority of apostolic vigour, that Pelagius and Coelestius, that is, the inventors of new words, which, as the Apostle said, are wont to bring forth nothing of edification, but rather the vainest questions [n: (II Tim. II, 23)], be deprived of ecclesiastical communion, until they come to their senses out of the snares of the devil, by whom [nt: In one manuscript: *capti*.] they are held captive at his will; and that meanwhile they be not received into the Lord's sheepfold, [nt: The Roman edition and the Councils: *quo ad ipsi perversae viae* (Merlin: *secuti tramitem*; others: *quem secuti sunt tramitem*) *deserere voluerint*: they are corrected from the manuscripts.] which they themselves, following the path of a perverse way, have chosen to desert. For they are to be cut off who [nt: In two manuscripts: *nos*.] trouble you, and would pervert the Gospel of Christ [n: (Galat. V, 12)]. And at the same time we command that whoever [0592C] strive with like obstinacy to defend it, a like punishment should bind them; for *not only they that do, but they also that consent to them that do [n: (Rom. I, 32)]* [nt: Supply: *constringere par vindicta debet*. In Quesnel there is added here *digni sunt poena*. I scarcely know whether on any authority. In place of these, in Merlin and the Augustine edition, from two Vatican manuscripts, one finds *digni sunt morte*. Garnier, however, presents these as words of Scripture: *Non solum enim digni sunt morte qui faciunt, sed etiam qui consentiunt facientibus*. Those three readings smack of an interpolator's work all the more because in our manuscripts, as in the Roman edition, Crabbe and others, the pure words of the Apostle stand. And it was enough indeed to have indicated this to bishops versed in the Scriptures.]: because I think there is not much difference between the mind of him who commits and the favour of him who consents. I add yet more: he generally unlearns erring with whom no one agrees. Let this sentence therefore, dearest brethren, remain fixed against the aforesaid: let them be absent from the courts of the Lord, let them be deprived [nt: The particle *duntaxat* is missing in the Augustine edition. Garnier not only passed it over but also changed Innocent's sentence thus: *Caveat custodia pastoralis*. Just as that pope, at the opening of this epistle, praises the shepherds who do not disdain to protect *with the circumspection of their former keeping* the sheep that *avoid what they had long unlawfully sought*, that is, that have turned to a better life; so now he wishes those sheep which obstinately remain in error to lack pastoral keeping, or to be separated from the flock which has been committed to the keeping of the shepherds.] only of pastoral keeping, lest the dire contagion of two sheep [0593A] should perhaps creep through the unwary crowd, and the wolf with ravening heart rejoice within the Lord's sheepfold over such scattered troops of sheep, while the wound of two is overlooked by the keepers. We must take care therefore lest, by admitting wolves, we seem to be hirelings rather than shepherds. We do indeed command (since Christ our Lord has signified by his own [nt: Garnier: *propria voluntate significavit*. The word *significavit* was thence transferred into the Augustine edition.] voice that he does not will the death of the dying [n: (Ezech. XXXIII, 11; II Pet. III, 9)], but only that he turn and live), that if ever they come back to [nt: In one Colbert manuscript *ad sanum* is lacking, in place of which the other presents *sano*, Quesnel *sanum*, Garnier *sani*.] a sound mind, having laid aside the error of their depraved doctrine, and condemn those things by the transgression of which they have condemned themselves, the customary medicine, that is, her own shelter, be not denied them by the Church: lest, while we perhaps forbid them as they return, [nt: In Quesnel, as in one Colbert manuscript: *velut*, for *vere*. In place of this word Garnier substituted *ores*. Then in the Augustine edition, from Vatican manuscripts, as in Labbe's margin: *remanentes et exspectantes*. Then Merlin: *hostis rapidi*. The other editions of the Councils, with the Roman: *hostis rapidis*. But Garnier omits the word *rabidis*, and substitutes none for it.] remaining truly outside the sheepfold, they be swallowed by the raging jaws of the waiting enemy, [0593B] [nt: Garnier: *quas in semetipsas*. Quesnel, with an old Colbert exemplar: *quo auctore semetipsos quibusdam* (the Colbert manuscript: *quosdam*) *spiritalis*.] which they have armed against themselves with the darts of evil disputation. Farewell, brethren. Given on the sixth day before the Kalends of February [n: (27 Jan. ann. 417)], Honorius and [nt: In the Isidorian manuscripts: *Constantino*. In Merlin: *Constantio consulibus*. In the other editions of the Councils and the Roman: *Constantio viris clarissimis* (one Colbert codex: *vv. cc.*) *consulibus*. In Garnier: *Honorio XI et Constantio II coss*. For that consular note to be accurate, it ought to be expressed thus: *Honorio Augusto XI et Constantio v. c.* (that is, *viro clarissimo*, not *vv. cc.*, which is *viris clarissimis*) *consulibus*. But this note is to be reformed from epistles 29 and 31, which have come down to us from manuscripts of better note than this one. For it seems not to be doubted that Innocent signed the three letters, given together, in the same manner, and so *post consulatum Theodosii VII et Junii Quarti*. But because after the consulship of Theodosius VII and Junius Quartus Honorius and Constantius were distinguished with the same dignity, the conjecture is that *Honorio et Constantio cons.* was added in the margin by the work of some copyist, and that this scholion was carried over from the margin into the text.] Constantius, most illustrious men, being consuls.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 26. And what was 59 is now 5.] XXXI. *Innocent writes back to the five bishops that he has already made sufficiently known what he thought of their judgment and what of the perfidy of Pelagius: that the abettors of this heretic, if they are at Rome, lie hidden; but that wherever they live they are to be condemned, and their salvation provided for: that Pelagius's clearing of himself in Palestine is very suspect to him; but that he neither blames nor approves his judges; that there is no need for him to summon him to Rome; that his book is certainly full of blasphemies: that if he himself anathematizes his errors, those whom he has led astray will more easily be recalled; but that these at least must be cared for.*

*INNOCENT [nt: In the Corbie manuscript: *Dilectissimis fratribus Aurelio, Alypio, Augustino, Evodio, Possidonio Innocentius*.], to the bishops AURELIUS, ALYPIUS, AUGUSTINE, EVODIUS, POSSIDIUS, greeting.* [0594A]



## 1.

The letters of your fraternity, full of faith and strengthened with all the vigour of the catholic religion, sent from two councils through our brother and fellow bishop Julius, we have received with a most grateful mind, because their tenor and whole contexture [nt: Garnier here has *contertio est*, and below *integra relatio ita consistit* for *integra ratione consistit*. Before that, for *correctione*, the Corbie manuscript has *correptione*.] rests with sound reason upon the consideration of the daily grace of God and upon the correction of those who think the contrary: so that it may both take away [nt: In Quesnel, as in the Pithou manuscript, *pos int*. The other books more correctly have *possit*, that is, the contexture of the letters. Next, the Roman edition and that of the Councils have *idoneo dato cuiris*, except that in the older editions of the Councils *quovis* remains. In Garnier: *idoneum cuivis, dato legis*.] all error from them, and, given any example whatever of our law, may offer them a worthy and fit teacher whom they ought to follow. But of these things, as I think, we have already said enough above, when, answering your reports, we wrote back what we thought either of their faithlessness or of your judgment. But [0594B] thereupon there comes to hand and is supplied against them what may be said: nor can there ever [nt: In the editions of Merlin, Crabbe, Garnier and Augustine, as in the Vatican manuscripts, *deesse*. It seems better to prefer *esse* with the other books, as if it were said: Nor can it ever come about that that doctrine should prevail which is so wretched and so impious that it is more fully overcome by the strength of our faith and by the truth itself.] be what may prevail, since that is so wretched and impious which is more fully overcome by the strength of our faith and by the truth itself. For he who has spurned and despised every hope of life, confounding his heart with a hostile and damnable disputation, since he believes that there is nothing which he may receive from God, nor anything left which he may ask for his healing; he who has taken this from himself, what has he left [nt: The Vatican manuscripts: *quid requirit*.] further?

## 2.

If therefore there are some whom so great a perversity [nt: Merlin: *defixit*; Garnier: *devinxerit*. Lower down Crabbe and Labbe have *oberrans*; and the Roman edition *aberrans* for *abhorrens*.] has bound to its defence, who give themselves up to and join themselves with this doctrine, hoping that that belongs to catholic teaching which, shrinking far from it, is proved to be thoroughly contrary, [nt: The editions: *infecti*; the Vatican manuscript: *infectis*; they are corrected from an old Colbert exemplar. These answer to the words of the five *e, i-covorum* no. 14: *which scandal its hearers and, perversely, ITS LOVERS do not cease to spread everywhere. For if they come to know*, etc.] led on by their affection and by their admonitions and words to slip, they will make haste to return to [0594C] the right track of the way, lest error, besieging the mind any longer [nt: Garnier: *nec diutius.... invadet*.] and as it were fed upon their senses, [0595A] should invade it. For if Pelagius, in whatever place he has stayed, has deceived by this assertion the minds of those who would easily or simply believe him as he disputed, [nt: One Colbert codex and the Pithou, with the Corbie in the first hand: *seu hic illi*, etc. The other, as in Quesnel: *seu hic aliqui decepti sint, quos nescientes*.] whether those are in this city, which, not knowing it, we can neither make manifest nor deny, since even if they are, they lie hidden, nor would they ever dare either to defend him when he preaches these things, or to throw out such things when any one [nt: In one manuscript: *quolibet nostrorum*.] of ours is present, and in so great a multitude of people someone could easily be detected, [nt: In Garnier: *nec ubi lateat possit*. Manuscripts of lower note: *nec deprehendi aliquis facile vel alicubi*.] nor be recognized anywhere; or whether they dwell in any place of the earth whatever, we believe by the mercy and grace of our God that they will easily be corrected on hearing the condemnation of him who has been found the obstinate and resisting author of this doctrine. Nor does it matter where these have been, so long as, wherever they can be found, they are to be healed. [0595B]

## 3.

We, however, [nt: In place of the particle *nec*, Garnier substituted *vix*.] cannot be persuaded that he has been cleared, although acts have been brought to us [nt: Garnier recalled here from the early editions of Augustine: *a nostris quibusdam laicis*. The acts of the synod of Diospolis are meant here.] by I know not what laymen, in which he would believe himself both heard and acquitted. Whether these are true we doubt, because they came with no proceeding of that council, [nt: Merlin and Crabbe: *executione*; then Garnier: *innotuerint*: the other books opposing.] nor have we received any letters on this matter from those before whom this man pleaded the causes of this affair. But if he had been able to be confident of his clearing, we believe that he would rather have done this, which could have been much more truly done, namely to compel them by his letters to indicate [nt: In Quesnel: *quid judicaverint*, which the Navarre codex also has from a second correction; which indeed, with the other exemplars of Isidore, showed *quid judicaverunt*. The later editions of the Councils: *qui judicarerunt*. In Augustine: *qui dijudicaverunt*. In the Roman edition, as in the Colbert, Pithou and Corbie manuscripts: *quid judicaverant*. Nearer to these comes what we recall from Merlin, Crabbe, etc.: *qui dijudicaverant*. But Garnier's *quod judicaverant more ecclesiastico indicare* smacks of a gloss.] what they had judged. But since there are some things set down in the acts themselves which, when objected, he partly suppressed by avoiding them, partly confounded in total obscurity by twisting many words back upon himself, some things he cleared by false arguments rather than by true reason, [nt: One of the Colbert manuscripts: *quod ad tempus*. Garnier presents this whole passage thus: *quam vera ratione purgavit negando, alia falsa interpretando, ne ad tempus quidem videri potuerit absolutus*, words taken away or added or moved from their place against the credit of the other books. From this account of Innocent one may suspect that the acts of the synod of Diospolis sent to him were not genuine, but those which Pelagius had adulterated and which he called the paper of his defence, such as the same heretic had also sent to Augustine. Certainly Augustine too suspects, in the book On the Proceedings of Pelagius, no. 57, that he had taken pains that those adulterated acts should run about everywhere, where he says: *Who would not believe that this was contrived, so that that paper might run about everywhere as though it were an abridgement of those acts?* Nor should we think without reason that those unknown men who brought the writing mentioned were devoted to Pelagius, and delivered to Innocent what, out of favour to their master, they held as established. Rightly therefore does Innocent say above that he doubts of those acts, whether they are true, and repeats it below.] as he could seem for the time, [0595C] denying some things, turning others by a false interpretation.

## 4.

[0596A]But would that, what is rather to be wished, he may now turn himself from that error of his own track to the true way of the catholic faith, so that he may desire and wish to be cleared, considering the daily grace of God and acknowledging his help, so that he may be seen [nt: In Augustine, as in the manuscripts: *verum*. Shortly after, Labbe, following the Roman edition and Merlin: *gestorum judicio*: wrongly.] truly and be approved by all as corrected by manifest reason, not by the showing of acts, but with his heart turned to the catholic faith! Wherefore we can neither approve nor blame the judgment of those men, since we do not know whether the acts are true; or, if they are true, whether it is certain that he rather slipped out than cleared himself with the whole truth. If he is confident, and knows that what he says is not worthy of our condemnation, [nt: Quesnel, having put before this *Qui si se confidit*, here adds *ut confirmet verum esse quod dicat, aut etiam hoc totum*, etc., a mere gloss, which we have not found even in the old exemplars of the Codex he himself published. In the Roman edition the conjunction *aut* is lacking before *jam hoc totum*. By Garnier, and thence in the new edition of Augustine, this passage was published thus: *Quod si confidit novitque nostra dignum se esse damnatione, quod dicat jam totum hoc refutare* (in Augustine, *refutasse*) *quod dixerat*. Our reading is the better in that it is not only drawn out according to the credit of the old codices, but is also fuller and more complete than the others. For Innocent means this: that if Pelagius is confident that what he even now says and teaches is not worthy of condemnation, or that he has already refuted, that is, retracted and repudiated, what he had formerly said and taught, he himself, sure of his acquittal, ought to hasten to Rome.] or that he has already refuted all this which he had said, he ought not to be summoned by us, but rather himself to hasten, so that he can be acquitted. For if he still thinks in such a way, when, summoned by any letters whatever, [nt: The editions: *acceptis*; the Pithou manuscript: *acceptus*. More fittingly the Corbie: *accitus*. A little before, for *accersiri*, the Vatican manuscript has *acciri*.] [0596B] will he commit himself to our judgment, since he knows that he is to be condemned? But if he were to be summoned, it would better be done by those who are nearer and are seen not to be separated by a long space of lands. But care will not be lacking if he offers matter for the medicine. For he can condemn what he had thought, and by letters sent, as befits a return to us, ask pardon for his error, dearest brethren.

## 5.

The book indeed which was said to be his, sent to us by your charity, we have unrolled: in which we read that many things were written against the grace of God, many blasphemous things, nothing that pleased, nothing almost [nt: This *pene*, and the words above, *multa contra Dei gratiam legimus esse conscripta*, have been restored from Augustine's edition and the Vatican and Corbie manuscripts, since they were lacking in the others.] that did not wholly displease, to be condemned and trodden under foot by anyone, whose like no other would take into his mind and think except him who had written these things. [0596C] For we did not [0597A] think it necessary in this place to dispute more broadly about the law, as though Pelagius were placed before us and resisting; since we are conversing with you who know the whole [nt: The Roman edition and that of the Councils: *totam rem*. The Corbie manuscript: *totum*. More truly the other printed and written books: *totam*, namely the law. For, as Quesnel noted, Innocent alludes to that saying of the Apostle, Rom. VII, 1: *For I speak to them that know the law*. So too afterwards Celestine, addressing the synod of Ephesus in Epistle 17, no. 4, says: *Encouraged briefly by all these things, since, as the Apostle says, I speak to them that know the law*.] of it, and who rejoice with us in equal assent. For these examples are better set down when we deal with those whom it is plain to be ignorant of these matters. [nt: In three manuscripts, as in the edition of the Councils: *prudentes*. The other books more correctly: *imprudentes*, that is, unskilled and rude men who are less versed in the law. Therefore the nearest *melius* is the same as *more opportunely* and *more fittingly*.] For concerning the possibility of nature, concerning free will, and concerning all the grace of God, and daily grace, to whom that thinks rightly would it not be most fruitful to dispute? Let him therefore anathematize what he has thought: so that those who had fallen through his discourses and precepts may know at length what the true faith holds. For they will be able to be recalled more easily when they perceive that these things are condemned by their own author. But if he wishes obstinately to persist in this impiety, it must be done [nt: Garnier: *qualiter vel iis possit subveniri, quos non scelus huc, sed magis error induxit*: corruptly. Here the author of the error is distinguished from those whom he led astray.] so that help may at least be given to those whom not their own error but rather his has led on; lest this medicine perish for them too, whose cure this man neither admits nor asks for. ( *And [0597B] in another hand:* ) May God keep you safe, dearest brethren. Given on the sixth day before the Kalends of February [nt: In Quesnel, as in the Roman edition and that of the Councils and most manuscripts, what follows is lacking. In its place Garnier substituted *Honorio XI et Constantio II coss.* He who wrote out the Corbie codex, unless the fault is older, having the genuine reading before his eyes, set it out thus corrupted through unskilfulness: *Pridie kalendas Februarias gloriosissimi Theodosii qui et Junii quarti Palladii v. c. conss.* That is, as was observed in the newest edition of Augustine, moving the sign *p. c.* from its place and joining it with *VI kalendas*, he made *pridie kalendas* out of it; and in place of *Theodosii VII aug.* he read *Theodosii qui*. By comparison with Epistle 29, as each slip becomes manifest, so it is also repaired. The same subscription is given thus in the Vatican manuscript: *Data kal. Februarii post. cons. Theodosii aug. et Junii Quarti Palladii*, the two numeral signs, namely, being omitted.] [n: (Jan. 27, ann. 417)] after the consulship of the most glorious Theodosius Augustus for the seventh time and of Junius Quartus Palladius, V. C.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 27; the one which was 32 is now 35. It is found in the old exemplars of the Codex published by Quesnel and of the collection of Isidore, and with great agreement it is entitled a familiar letter. It was written at the same time as the three above and sent by the same bishop Julius.] XXXII. *Innocent greets Aurelius again in familiar terms.*

*INNOCENT TO AURELIUS, bishop of Carthage.*

In familiar writings true love consists. For the offices of charity buy a firmer right better [nt: The Roman edition and that of the Councils: *melius se orsa mercantur*; they are corrected from the manuscripts. Just after, *extrinsecus missae*, that is, written separately.] apart. Wherefore, through our brother Julius, I was eager to answer [nt: An old exemplar of Quesnel's Codex: *respondere maluimus, ne apud nos forsitan*, and just after *frater charissime* omitted.] a letter sent separately, lest perchance there should remain with me the offence of a particular greeting [0597C] denied, dearest brother. I therefore restore the aforesaid brother of ours to thy love, with the judgments of the apostolic see [nt: In the early editions of the Councils and the Roman, the word *dignum* is wrongly added here, against the credit of the manuscripts. Further, Innocent calls *judicata* the answers which show the judgment of the apostolic see on matters referred to it. We shall see in their place that these two words, *judicatum* and *constitutum*, are synonyms for Pope Vigilius and for Gregory.] upon the report of the twofold synod. It remains that the Lord, entreated by us, may deign to grant that every stain of his Church may be wiped away by our continual labours. May God keep thee [0598A] safe, [sic: *fratrer*] dearest brother. Given on the sixth day before the Kalends of February [nt: The consular note, omitted here, is to be supplied from the preceding letters.].

## NOTICE ON THE THREE FOLLOWING LETTERS.



## 1.

The three following letters were first published at Rome among the pontifical letters from an old book of the Vatican library, and afterwards reprinted by Baronius too, at the year 416, after a collation of them had been made with another codex. Whence their first editor thought occasion was given for writing them he explains thus: « When John, bishop of Jerusalem, followed the errors of Origen, Jerome, living at Bethlehem, and very many others abstained from his communion. For which reason, through the work of John, they were afflicted with many evils. » But we have learned the truer cause of them from what Augustine relates at the close of the book On the Proceedings of Pelagius, in this manner: « But concerning those things which after this judgment (held at Diospolis in Palestine) are said to have been perpetrated there with incredible audacity by I know not what band of abandoned men, who are said to favour Pelagius in a very [0598B] perverse way, so that the servants and handmaids of God belonging to the care of holy Jerome were afflicted with most wicked slaughter, a deacon was killed, the buildings of the monasteries were burned, and he himself was scarcely protected from this onset and incursion of the impious, in the mercy of God, by a more fortified tower, I see that we must rather be silent, and wait to see what our brethren the bishops there think should be done about these great evils, which who would believe they could pass over? » For it is clear that the evils described in the letters subjoined were suffered by Jerome not at all from the Origenists but from the Pelagians.



## 2.

From the same account we learn that the irruption of these heretics into the monastery committed to Jerome's care happened after the synod of Diospolis. Whence it follows that it by no means took place at the time when Jerome was abstaining from John's communion. For in the year 415, as the month of December was running out, the synod of Palestine, or of Diospolis, [0598C] was held. But from the year 397 the learned prove that John had been reconciled with Jerome through the work of Theophilus of Alexandria. [n: (V. Tillem. tom. XII, p. 199.)] From the same place it is also manifest that the following letters were by no means written before the year 416. But neither does the death of John, recorded at the year 417, allow them to be much later than this year. By Baronius they are referred to the end [0599A] of the year 416. But he ought to have gathered from Epistle 33 itself that they are to be put off to the year 417. For Aurelius, to whom this letter is inscribed, and through whom Jerome wished his letters to be delivered to Innocent, and in turn Innocent wished those in which he wrote back to Jerome to be delivered to him, he judges to be none other than the bishop of [sic: *Cathaginensi*] Carthage; nor does anyone oppose him in this matter. But in truth, when this Aurelius with his colleagues wrote at the beginning of the year 417 to Innocent against Pelagius and Coelestius, he did not know that Jerome had suffered anything from the satellites of these heretics. At that time it was only noised abroad in Africa that Pelagius, established at Jerusalem, was deceiving many there, but that many more, of whom in the letter of the synod of Milevis Jerome alone is named, « were contending against him for the grace of Christ and the truth of the catholic faith. » Certainly this place called for the addition also of what the new defender of the grace of Christ had borne from the impious, if knowledge of so atrocious a crime had reached Africa. It is not to be doubted, however, that [0599B] that most keen conflict of Jerome against Pelagius was the true cause why he was dealt with so cruelly. Although, therefore, Eustochium and Paula had kept silent about the cause and the author of the violence brought upon their monastery, yet it was not difficult for Innocent, forewarned of what Pelagius was busy doing at Jerusalem, to scent this out.

## 3.

This pope rebuked John of Jerusalem with letters so threatening, not only because it was his part either to restrain or to punish such great crimes, but also because perhaps a suspicion was not lacking that he had favoured them. The old enmities between him and Jerome on the cause of Origen, although long since laid to rest, were not hidden. He had also heard, as report announced, how greatly Pelagius was loved by John. Wherefore Augustine, epist. 179, no. 1, addresses John himself thus: « But Pelagius our brother, thy son, whom I hear that thou lovest much; » and in no. 5: « If you love Pelagius, let him also love you, nay rather himself, [0599C] and not deceive you. »

## 4.

Lastly we observe that the letter of Innocent to this John savours of Greek phrasing: and therefore that this pope either wrote in Greek, or at least took care that what he had written in Greek should be turned into Latin, and that only the Latin interpretation of this Greek text, very tenacious of the letter, has come down to us. Certainly one or another place which at first sight appears obscure or even maimed, when brought to the Greek phrasing, at once comes out clear and whole.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 34; the one which was 33 is now 34.] XXXIII. *Innocent sends Aurelius a letter to be delivered to Jerome.*

[0600A]

*INNOCENT TO HIS MOST BELOVED BROTHER AURELIUS.*

Our fellow presbyter Jerome did well to believe in thy most pious affection for coming [nt: Some persuade themselves from this, following Baronius, that the letters of Jerome, as also of Eustochium and Paula, to Innocent were carried by Aurelius himself. Yet this Aurelius is mentioned here as having the affection for coming into the City, not as having come. Nor, if he had been present in person, would there have been need of these letters. We should therefore think it better that he took pains to have them carried by one of his own people.] to us. We suffer with a member of our flock, and what we thought should be done, or were able to do, we have speedily carried out. Let thy brotherhood, dearest brother, make haste to deliver the letter all the sooner to the aforementioned ( *Jerome* ).

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 32; the one which was 34 is now 33.] XXXIV. *Innocent consoles Jerome for what he has suffered, and declares what he has thought should be done for the present, and has already done.*

*INNOCENT TO HIS MOST BELOVED SON JEROME, PRESBYTER.* [0600B]

The Apostle testifies that contention has never done any good in the Church; and therefore he bids that the rebukes of heretics be made first rather [nt: So we had thought it should be corrected, because it had been published thus with no sense, nay with a sense contrary to Innocent's mind, as *magisque diuturna*, when our conjecture was confirmed by a re-examination of the Vatican archetype itself, and we learned that the fault had arisen from the mere unskilfulness of a copyist in reading old books. Innocent looks to that saying of Paul, Tit. III, 10: *A man that is a heretic after the first and second rebuke avoid*. And that pope teaches this chiefly of the obstinate and deceitful heretic, such as Pelagius was, who was more eager to deceive and to cover his heresy than to seek the truth.] than that they be drawn out in lengthy conference. While this rule is carelessly regarded, [nt: Baronius, having thrown *aspicitur* out to the margin of the book (which here is the same as *consideratur*), substituted *accipitur* in the text.] the evil which is to be guarded against is not avoided, but increased. Yet since thy grief and groaning so shake our bowels that [nt: In the Vatican manuscript *ratio* is lacking. Some other word is wanted for the completeness of the sentence. It will be more complete if one reads *ut nulla ratio*.] there is reason for not treating and consulting: first I address the faith of thy constancy. That everyone, for the truth, is struck with injury or, as thou sayest, with danger, [nt: In the edition of the Councils, after Baronius: *qui exspectat*. More truly the Roman, with the Vatican manuscript: *quia exspectet*. This reading will become clearer if construed thus: *Quisque* (as *quisquis*) *injuria aut periculo pro veritate percelletur, quia is beatitudinem exspectet, ipsi multis saepe narrastis*, that is, *exposuistis*. From which it follows that a man of this sort, since he is blessed in hope, is the less to be mourned.] because he awaits blessedness, you have often related in many words, and we remind [nt: The Roman edition: *et tu averte praedicationum*; the others: *et tuae verbis praedicationis*: they are corrected by the help of the Vatican manuscript, in which *tuarum te praedicationum* stands.] thee, well mindful of thy own preachings. And so, roused by so great a scene of evils, we hastened to take up the authority of the apostolic see for putting down all wickedness; [0600C] but against whom we should rise up we read neither named by name nor taxed with any account of the crime. What therefore we can, we grieve with thee. But if thou layest an open and manifest accusation against any men, I will either assign competent judges, or, if anything more urgent and careful can be done by us, I will not delay it, most beloved son. Yet I have written to my brother bishop John that he act more circumspectly: lest [nt: This place will be more complete if it is read thus: *ne si circa ecclesiam sibi creditam adhuc tale aliquid fiat, quale providere eum oportuit ne accideret, vel ne accidat*. Innocent here mentions what he writes to John at the beginning of the following letter: *It behoved thy brotherhood to keep guard, and to provide more carefully for his own flock, lest anything of this kind should arise*, and also the other things with which he closes that same letter. With what Innocent now writes are to be compared these words of Augustine, in the book On the Proceedings of Pelagius, no. 66, about the same evils: *For the impious doctrines of men of this kind are to be refuted by any catholics whatever, even those who are far away from those lands, lest they be able to do harm wherever they have been able to reach. But impious deeds, whose restraint belongs to episcopal discipline, are to be punished chiefly where they are committed, by those present or placed nearby, with pastoral diligence and godly severity. We therefore, placed so far away, ought to wish that such an end be given to these causes there that there be no need for them to be judged any further anywhere.* Where that doctor, distinguishing impious doctrines from impious deeds, teaches that impious doctrines can be refuted by any catholic and anywhere, but not likewise that impious deeds can be restrained by anyone. But Innocent, thinking otherwise of the power of his see, did not doubt that he had the right of punishing impious deeds committed in a territory no less distant from him than from Augustine, either by assigning competent judges, or even by some other means. Although he comes near to Augustine's opinion in this, that he teaches that competent judges are to be assigned by him. For by this name he may not undeservedly seem to understand bishops placed either in the same province or in the nearest one.] anything of the kind [0601A] should still happen with regard to the church entrusted to him, such as to provide against and drive off, lest it should happen, or lest it happen, would be most troublesome even to himself, and afterwards.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 32.] XXXV. *That John Jerome of Jerusalem ought to have taken care that Paula and Eustochium should not be overwhelmed by so many and so great evils, by which they were afflicted; and that, unless these are henceforth either corrected or checked, he will render account for them.*

*INNOCENT TO HIS MOST BELOVED BROTHER JOHN.*

Plunderings, slaughters, burnings, every crime, the most noble holy virgins Eustochium and Paula, of utmost [nt: It once pleased us, with Baronius and the edition of the Councils, to read *dementiae*: but afterwards it seemed to us rather that the holy virgins are praised here as of utmost clemency, because they kept silent the name of the hostile man and the cause of the hatreds: and so we must hold to the reading of the Roman edition.] clemency, have lamented that the devil has perpetrated in places of thy Church: for they kept silent the name of the man and the cause. And though it be not doubtful [0601B] by whom [nt: The suspicion of this crime fell upon Pelagius.] it was committed, yet it behoved thy brotherhood to keep guard, and to provide more carefully for his [nt: So Baronius. But the Roman edition: *gregis*. This is the first place from which we have conjectured that this letter was turned from the Greek. For what is in Greek τῷ ποιμνίῳ ἑαυτοῦ or αὐτοῦ, if it be rendered in Latin not word for word *gregi illius*, but as the Latin idiom requires, *gregi suo*, the course of the sentence, which at first appeared maimed and obscure, will become full and plain.] flock, lest anything of this kind should arise which, with danger to others, thy negligence provokes to admit [nt: The Roman edition and that of the Councils: *amittere gregem*, from a bad reading of the old books, in which *ammittere* is usually written for *admittere*: they are corrected from Baronius, in whom, however, a no lighter error then stands, where one reads *et tales agnos, quos incendio.... nudas*, as though the word *nudas* were the second person of the verb *nudo*. On which supposition John is proclaimed the author of these crimes, against the whole course of the letter.] into the flock of the Lord; and we have heard that such lambs, by fire, by arms and by persecutions, naked, weak, after the slaughters and deaths of their own people, scarcely live. Does nothing move that piety [nt: This other place too smells of a Greek speaking Latin after the manner of his own tongue, and one who, having had τὴν εὐσέβειαν before him in Greek, thought that the article τὴν should be expressed in Latin by the word *illam*. Then he would have said more Latinly *tanta... potestas* than *de tanta... potestate*.] of thy priesthood, concerning so great a power of the devil let loose against thee and against thine? Against thee, I say; for [nt: In Baronius: *sacerdotii*.] so great a wickedness having been accomplished in the Church altogether condemns the gravity of a priest. Where are thy provisions? where, at least, if the mischances had happened, the aids or consolations, [0602A] since they say that they still fear more than they complain of having suffered? I would judge more deeply if they had spoken with me somewhat more openly on this matter. See, brother, the snares of the ancient enemy, and watch with the spirit of a good ruler, so that these things which have been brought to us more by report than by manifest accusation may either be corrected or checked: lest the ecclesiastical law concerning things overthrown compel him who has not defended to answer for [nt: The editions: *causis*, for which word we had already conjectured that *causam*, that is, account, should be restored, when we learned that *causas* is read in the archetype. Further, *jus ecclesiasticum* is expressed here in the nominative case.] the causes.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 9. Among the decrees of Innocent, Dionysius Exiguus assigns the 37th place to this letter. From there the compiler of the Spanish collection took it, from whom Isidore too, as is his custom, copied the same. It is cited by Cresconius, c. 226. It bears no mark of the time at which it was written. Wherefore we are compelled to relegate it to the class of letters of uncertain date.] XXXVI. *If a husband whose wife had been carried off into captivity has taken another, when the first returns the second woman must be excluded.*

*INNOCENT TO [nt: This Probus seems to have been the son of Probus the praetorian prefect and of Proba Faltonia, that distinguished matron of whom and of whose children Jerome, epist. 8, to Demetrias, has this: *That Proba, the most illustrious name of every dignity and of all nobility in the Roman world, whose holiness and goodness toward all was venerable even among the barbarians, whom the ordinary consulships of her three children Probinus, Olybrius and Probus did not weary: and when, the houses having been burned and plundered, there is captivity in the City, she is now said to* renumdare *the possessions she has held.* Ursa is readily believed to have been carried off in that captivity which happened in the year 409, in the month of August, when the City was stormed by Alaric; but when she returned is not known.] PROBUS.*

[0602B]The turmoil of the barbarian storm has brought a mischance upon the power [nt: So the Roman edition, with the genuine Royal exemplar of Dionysius. But the other books: *legis*.] of the laws. For captivity's incursion had made a blemish in the well-established marriage between Fortunius and Ursa, had not the holy statutes of religion provided. For while the aforesaid woman Ursa was held in captivity, the aforementioned Fortunius is known to have entered into another marriage with Restituta [n: (34, q. 1 et 2, c. 2; Ivo p. 8, c. 245)]. But by the favour of the Lord Ursa returned and came to us, and, no one denying it, fully proved herself to be the wife of the aforementioned. Wherefore, lord son of deserved renown, we decree, the catholic faith giving its support, that that is the marriage which was from the first [nt: The marriage which the catholic faith judges to be founded by divine grace we rightly believe to be a sacrament. For a similar cause a similar answer was given afterwards in Leo, epist. 149, no. 2. Cresconius too indicates the answers of both pontiffs under the single canon 226. Before, Tertullian, book II To his Wife, no. 8, had proclaimed of Christian marriage: *Whence shall we suffice to set forth the happiness of that marriage which the Church joins, and the oblation confirms, and the blessing seals, angels announce, the Father holds ratified?*] founded by divine grace; [0603A] and that the union with the second woman, the former surviving and not cast out by divorce, can by no means be lawful.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 4. It was divided by Dionysius Exiguus into five titles or decrees, XXVIII, XXIX, XXX, XXXI and XXXII. It is cited by Cresconius more than once. It is found also in the Spanish, as in Isidore's collection.] XXXVII. 1. *If anyone of his own will has cut off part of his body, he cannot be a cleric; but if against his will, he can.* --II. *That digamists cannot be admitted to the clergy.* --III. *Which of the laity ought not to be promoted to the clergy.* -- IV. *Which of the laity can become clerics. Here a concubine is openly forbidden.* --V. *On the times to be spent in the orders of the clergy.*

*INNOCENT TO FELIX, bishop [nt: So the Royal codex, where in the others: *Nuceriano*.] of Nocera.*

1 We cannot wonder that thy love follows the institutions of the elders, and refers to us, as to the head and [0603B] summit of the episcopate, all things that can admit any doubt, so that the apostolic see, being consulted, may namely pronounce something certain out of the doubtful matters themselves [nt: The editions: *faciendum pronuntiet*, the conjunction being omitted, which is recalled from the manuscripts.] and to be done. Which we both gladly accept, and approve thy love as mindful of the canons.

## 2.

Thou hast written, then, that by the fervour of faith in which thou art strong, and by love of the holy people, thou hast either repaired the churches of God or built certain new ones: but that in these thou hast no clerics whom thou mightest appoint, and that some are mutilated, some are digamists. At which we were astonished, that a prudent man should have wished to consult about things which are known to all by certain reason. We therefore say this not as to men ignorant; but we say that you, occupied perhaps with other things, have forgotten this.

## CHAP. I.



## 3.

Whoever, then, of his own will has cut off part of any finger [0603C] from himself, him the canons do not admit to the clergy [n: (Dist. 55, c. 6; Concil. Tribur. c. 33, Nicaen. c. 1)] But whomsoever it has befallen [nt: The Royal manuscript: *labsu aliquo*.] by some chance, while he is either bestowing care on rustic work, or, doing something, has struck himself not of his own accord, these the canons direct both to be made clerics and, if they have been found in the clergy, not to be cast out. For in the former the will [nt: In the same codex: *voluntas est quae sibi ausa fuerit ferrum incidere*, *judicata* being omitted; which word is the same as *damnata*, inasmuch as *veniam meruit* is opposed to it just after. And the reason of this judgment is subjoined, that such a one would not hesitate to commit against others, that is, would commit without any scruple, what he had committed against himself. Hence *dubitari*, which then stands in the printed texts, does not please. Then in the Royal manuscript: *possint*; nothing prevents that word from being admitted, referred to those whose will is charged, not to the will itself.] has been judged, which has dared to lay the iron upon itself, because, namely, it could not hesitate to do [0604A] this to others as well: but in the latter the chance has earned pardon.

## CHAP. II.






## 4.

Concerning [nt: Labbe, as in the Royal manuscript: *bigamis*. The other books more elegantly: *digamis*.] digamists, however, there ought not even to have been a consultation, since the reading of the Apostle is manifest, that *a man of one wife* ought to be admitted to the priesthood or to the clerical state, and this very one only if he took her a virgin [n: (I Tim. III, 2)]. For she who has had a husband before, although he be dead, yet if she has afterwards been joined to a cleric, the cleric who has taken her cannot be one, because it is provided in the law that a priest cannot have as wife a widow, nor one cast off [n: (Levit. XXI, 13)].

## CHAP. III.



## 5.

But thy religion has consulted concerning laymen, whom the canons forbid to be ordained [n: (Vide concil. Sardic. c. 13)]. It is certain indeed that the ecclesiastical rules contain this: but it has not been so defined as to have been decreed of [0604B] all laymen. For clerics cannot be born and not be made: but [n: (Dist. 51, c. 2; Ivo. p. 6, c. 95)] the kinds have been marked out from which men cannot come to the clerical state, that is, if any of the faithful has served as a soldier, if any of the faithful has pleaded causes, that is, [nt: By this word is designated the advocate, who demands that penalties be decreed by the judges against the accused, just as by the word *administrantis* just after is indicated the judge, whose part it is to decree penalties and torments of this kind, or even to pronounce a capital sentence. About both of these Exsuperius, bishop of Toulouse, once doubted whether they were to be admitted even to communion. Innocent, consulted on this matter, answers him in epist. 6, chap. 3 and 5.] has prosecuted, if any of the faithful has held an administration. Concerning curials, moreover, the reason is manifest; since, even if men of this sort are found who ought to be made clerics, yet since they are rather often reclaimed to the curia, one must beware of them [n: (Vide supra epist. 2, n. 14)] on account of the trouble which often comes to the Church from them.

## CHAP. IV.



## 6.

But laymen who were baptized having wives, and have so ordered themselves that their reputation wavers in nothing, so that they have either been joined to clerics, or have clung to monasteries [0604C] from the time they were baptized, and if they have known not a [nt: So Dionysius, which was not changed even in the exemplars sent by Hadrian to Charlemagne. But the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections, as in the printed texts: *concubinam*. It is to be noted that Innocent so permits access to the clergy to those who have taken a wife that he wishes them to have the testimony of a life led innocently from the time they were baptized.] concubine, not a mistress, if they have been watchful in all [nt: The word *omnibus*, first struck out by Crabbe and not restored afterwards, is recalled from Merlin and the manuscripts.] good works, such men are not forbidden to be taken up into the lot of the clerical state. CHAP. V.--Yet so, that the times established by the elders be observed in their case [n: (V. Siric. epist. 1, n. 13, et Zosim. epist. 9, n. 5)]. Nor let anyone quickly become a lector, quickly an acolyte, quickly a deacon, quickly a priest: because if in [nt: The Royal exemplar: *in omnibus officiis*. From this place it is seen how frivolous is what an unskilled writer writes, who, having persuaded himself that the Roman pontiffs were accustomed to ordain only once a year, in the month of December, contends that those who had been ordained had to wait, by no law save that of necessity, until another year, in order to be advanced to a higher grade.] the lesser offices [0605A] they continue long, both their life and their services alike are approved, so that afterwards, having served out the merits of their stipends, they may come to the priesthood, and may not snatch beforehand what a proved life deserves to receive.

## 7.

Since, then, it has been shown by a certain definition who ought to be admitted, or who rejected: out of all those whom thy worthiness sees cannot be rejected thou shalt be bound to choose those whom thou wouldst make clerics. For if we are willing to show NO FAVOUR or benefit to men, we can find such men that, concerning their taking up, we may be able neither to incur scandal nor to blush.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 5. This letter holds a place in the Dionysian collection, whence it was transcribed into the Spanish and Isidorian, and it is entitled title or decree xxviii of Innocent.] XXXVIII. *That those who have begotten sons while in the presbyterate ought to be removed from office.*

*INNOCENT TO MAXIMUS and SEVERUS, bishops among the Bruttii.*

[0605B]The norm of the ecclesiastical canons ought to be unknown to none of the priests: because it is unbecoming enough for these things not to be known by a pontiff, especially since they are both known by religious lay men and held to be binding. Recently indeed [n: (Dist. 81, c. 6)]. Maximilian our son, an *agens in rebus*, [nt: The printed texts, with the manuscripts of the Spanish and Isidorian collections: *in rebus hujusmodi qualem querelam*: they are corrected from the exemplars of Dionysius and Hadrian.] the series of his petition, annexed, declares what sort of complaint he has brought. Who, led by zeal for faith and discipline, does not suffer the Church to be polluted by unworthy presbyters, who, he asserts, have begotten sons while in the presbyterate. That this is not lawful I would set forth, did I not know that your prudence has knowledge of the whole law. And therefore, dearest brethren, having looked into the tenor of the petition which is subjoined, you will order those who are said to have perpetrated such things to be placed in the midst; and the objections which are thrust upon these presbyters having been examined, [0605C] [nt: So the manuscripts of the collections of Dionysius and Hadrian. But others, with the printed texts: *si convicti fuerint*; and just after *tractare* for *tentare*. It does not escape me that the Africans, Code of the Canons of the African Church, c. 4, decreeing *that the bishop, the presbyter and the deacon, or those who HANDLE the sacraments, as guardians of chastity abstain from their wives*, seem to favour the latter reading. But we have thought that we must by no means depart from the manuscripts which we have found more genuine: especially since here the discourse is about presbyters alone, and by the name *sanctorum* are understood not only the sacraments of the body and blood of Christ, but whatever things belong to the ministry of presbyters. Wherefore the word *tentare*, the more general it is, the more apt it is also for expressing this.] if they can be convicted, let them be removed from the priestly office: because those who are not holy cannot handle holy things; and let them be made strangers to the ministry which they have polluted [nt: The Royal manuscript: *polluunt*.] by living unlawfully. We wonder, moreover, that their bishops overlook these things, so that they are judged either to connive or not to know them to be unlawful.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 6. It was inserted into the Spanish collection and Isidore's following Dionysius Exiguus, who placed it under title XXXIV of the decrees of Innocent.] XXXIX. *That after penance no one can be admitted to the clergy.*

[0606A]

*INNOCENT TO AGAPITUS, MACEDONIUS and [nt: In the printed texts, *Mauriano*; the Royal manuscript, *Martino*. The rest, *Mariano*.] MARIANUS, bishops of Apulia.*

That many things are usurped by very many in the province against the ecclesiastical canons and the decrees of the elders, both the reports of divers persons and most faithful suggestions have brought back: which indeed could easily be cut away if bishops were not found the authors in these things, who, while they strive to show favour either to friends or to those who are obsequious, violate religion and corrupt the orders. And so it comes about that all the unworthy take up ecclesiastical honours, and those are admitted to the clergy who would not deserve to have a worthy place even among the laity: just as has been shown in the petition now given to us, [0606B] that a certain Modestus, involved in many crimes, on account of which he is also said to have done penance, has not only been made a cleric, which is not lawful, but also is reaching toward the summit of the episcopate; whereas the canons established at Nicaea exclude penitents even from the lowest offices of the clergy [nt: Not indeed in express words, but because the fathers of this council decree that from which this is understood to follow. Nay, since in can. 10 they profess to follow the ecclesiastical rule on this matter, they set it down as already established before. And indeed that reasoning applies to all who are defiled by any stain of grave sin, by which Cyprian, epistle 72, with the common consent of the brethren, keeps back and removes from the clergy those who were returning to the Church from heresy or schism: *For priests and ministers who serve the altar and the sacrifices ought to be whole and spotless.* To this tend in truth the laws which the Church sanctioned from the beginning, that none should be promoted to the clergy save those who had led from baptism a life harmless and clean from every stain of any crime. But if anyone's fault, at first hidden, was afterwards discovered, the Nicene canons 2 and 10 will that he, when at length convicted, be deposed. See above, epist. 16, n. 11.] [n: (Dist. 50, c. 60; Nic. conc. c. 10)]. And therefore, dearest brethren, having looked into the tenor of the petition, you will order him to be presented: and if it has truly been established that he is such as the petition affirms, let him be removed not only from the ambition, [nt: Crabbe and the other editors after him: *ab episcopatus ambitione*; Merlin and all the manuscripts opposing. Nor indeed is Modestus kept only from the episcopate, but from any grade whatever which he had not yet attained, and from which he was reaching toward the summit of the episcopate.] but also from the office of the clerical state.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 8. It is presented in the collections of Isidore, the Spanish and Dionysius. By the last it is called decree xxxvi of Innocent.] XL. *That it is not lawful to invade another's parish, or to do anything in it without consulting its own bishop.*

*INNOCENT TO FLORENTINUS, [nt: So the old codices of the collections of Dionysius and Hadrian. But others: *Florentio*.] bishop of Tibur.*

[0606C]Not once but several times divine Scripture cries out that the bounds set by the fathers ought not to be moved: because it is wickedness if one should invade what another has always possessed, which our brother and fellow bishop Ursus asserts that thy goodness has perpetrated. For he has complained, not without grief, that thou didst invade the parish of Nomentum, or of Felicia, [nt: In the Royal manuscript: *Faciliensem*. Next, the word *paroecia* signifies not a collection of several churches subject to a bishop, in which sense it is commonly taken in early times, but a single church assigned to one presbyter. In the same sense it is used above too, epist. 25, n. 8, by Innocent himself.] belonging from of old to his [nt: Paschasius Quesnel, on Leo's first letter, note 4, laying it down as certain that the word DIOCESE *was hardly used in those times* (of Leo) *by the bishops of the Romans or of the Eastern parts for a provincial parish subject to some bishop*, raises this place of Innocent against himself, and says that in it *the word diocese seems to him to be taken for administration*, though he admits that in the use in which it is now frequent it was familiar both to the Africans at that time and, after Leo's time, to the Gauls. But since there were then only two dioceses in Italy in the sense in which this word signifies an administration, namely the Roman, over which the Roman prelate presided, and the Italian, which was under the bishop of Milan, in that sense the parish of Nomentum belonged to the diocese of the Roman prelate, not to that of Ursus or Florentinus. But we shall see that the word diocese was used for the episcopal territory by Hilarus, Leo's successor, as also by Gelasius, Vigilius, etc. The same is its use both in Augustine's epistle 209, n. 8, and in these words of the first council of Toledo, can. 20: *It has pleased that from this day no other but the bishop shall make chrism and send it through the* DIOCESE.] [0607A] diocese, and celebrated the divine mysteries there, without consulting him and without his knowledge. If this is true, know that thou hast not lightly [nt: Crabbe added *culpam* here of his own: a word which would require not *non leviter* but *non levem*. Yet it was not corrected afterwards. Further, *incurrere* and *offendere* are synonyms in Tully.] fallen into it. Wherefore, if thou desirest to decline the odium of so great a usurpation, it befits thee, admonished by our letter, to abstain. Certainly, if thou believest that any justice supports thee, all things remaining untouched and in their former state, thou must be present after the venerable days of Easter, so that thou mayest be able to answer the charges of the [nt: The printed texts, after Crabbe: *memoratis*. More fittingly Merlin and the manuscripts: *memorati*, namely of Ursus. Next, *intentionibus*, that is, the expostulation. For *intentio* was familiar to the ancients for a controversy and expostulation, or a suit brought.] aforementioned: and, the parties having been placed in the midst, we may inquire what antiquity or truth holds.

## EPISTLE [nt: In the edition of the Councils, 20. It has come down to us by the help of the three or four collections so often named. In Dionysius it is headed *decree XLIX of Innocent, on the Bonosians, that they are to be compared to the Jews*. This summary, copied thence by other compilers of canons, prevailed both in all the manuscripts and in the early editions of the Councils and the Roman. Nay, from this summary and another of the preceding epistle 16 joined together, Cresconius composed chapter 58 of his breviary in this manner: *On the Bonosian heretics, that they are to be compared to the Jews. And on receiving the clerics whom the same Bonosus ordained before he was condemned. From the decrees of Pope Innocent, tit. XLIX and L.* Hence it came about that Merlin, putting that twofold title before this letter, subjoined to it the preceding epistle 16 as though it were its second chapter. The heresies of Bonosus and Photinus were indeed akin, as we observed in the preliminary Notice to epistle 9 of Siricius, no. 7; yet there is no reason why we should depart from the letter of our epistle, in which mention is made of the followers of Photinus, not of Bonosus. Nay, if this letter was written to the bishop of Senia in Croatia, or, as they used to call it, in Liburnia, not to the bishop of Sena in Etruria, it will be beyond controversy that the partisans of Photinus, not of Bonosus, are noted in it. Moreover no one is unaware that Photinus, when he was bishop at Sirmium in Pannonia, to which Liburnia is neighbouring, infected those provinces with the poison of his heresy. Lastly, Innocent speaks of a heresy which he intimates was spread through almost the whole world, which certainly fits the Photinian rather than the recently arisen Bonosian. And the author of the heresy which Innocent rebukes is said to be Marcus, not because he devised it, but because he showed more zealous effort in propagating it.] XLI. *That Laurentius should take care that heretics following the poisons of Photinus be expelled.*

*[0607B]INNOCENT TO LAURENTIUS, bishop [nt: In some manuscripts: *Siniensi*. In Merlin: *Symensi*.] of Senia.*

We have long wondered, having read the letter of thy love, that heretics following the poisons of Photinus are not only in the territory of thy love, but also publicly prepare conventicles for themselves on the possessions of certain people; whereas nowhere from almost the whole world have so many chosen to dwell as among you. The author of whose nefarious doctrine, Marcus, driven out of the City some time ago, has been led by the audacity of such great rashness as to claim the first place among them for himself. But lest they have any further opportunity of raging, and drag with them into Gehenna, to which they are destined, the souls of the simple and of the rustic, [nt: The Roman edition: *rusticorum*. The other books more correctly: *rusticanorum*: by which word are designated not the inhabitants of the country, but men lacking learning.] action has been taken against them by the defenders [nt: The name and office of the defenders of the Roman church, if I remember well, we now read mentioned for the first time in the letters of the supreme pontiffs. But Innocent does not speak of that office as newly instituted. Mention of them will recur at the end of epistle 9, of Zosimus to Hesychius.] of our Church, by which they may be able to expel them: [0608A] so that those who deny that Christ God was begotten of the substance of the Father before the ages may have a share in the condemnation with the Jews, who denied his deity and deny it even until now. It is thy part, dearest brother, to carry out the things commanded not the more sluggishly: lest thou lose by dissimulation the people entrusted to thee, and begin to render account to God for the lost.

## EPISTLE XLII. OR FRAGMENTS OF THE LETTER OF PELAGIUS TO POPE INNOCENT, WRITTEN AFTER HIS DEATH, BUT WHEN HE DID NOT KNOW HIM TO BE DEAD.



## 1.

*Among these, as Augustine testifies [n: (Lib. de Gratia Christi, c. 30)]*, *Pelagius says* that there are things about which men try to defame him: one, that he denies to little ones the sacrament of baptism, and promises the kingdom of heaven to some without the redemption [0608B] of Christ: the other, that he says that man can so avoid sin as to exclude the help of God, and trusts so much in free will as to repudiate the aid of grace.

## 2.

*Augustine continues [n: (Ibid., c. 31)]*: *When he had mixed in with these invidious complaints about his enemies, he comes to the matter thus:* Behold, let this letter clear me before thy beatitude, in which purely and simply we say that we have whole free will for sinning and for not sinning, which in all good works is always helped by divine aid.

## 3.

*Then, says Augustine [n: (Ibid.,)]*, *having interposed certain things about the condition of man, and about his natural possibility of sinning and of not sinning, he added:* [0609A] Which power of free will we say is in all generally, in Christians, in Jews and in Gentiles. In all free will is equally by nature: but in Christians alone it is helped by grace. In the former the good of their condition is naked and unarmed: but in these who belong to Christ it is fortified by the help of Christ. The former are to be judged and condemned because, since they have free will, through which they could come to faith and merit [nt: On these words Augustine: "Plainly, he says, it is manifest that he says grace is given according to merits, whatever or whatsoever kind of grace he means; which however he does not openly express."] the grace of God, they make ill use of the liberty granted. But these are to be rewarded who, making good use of free will, merit the grace of the Lord and keep his commandments.



## 4.
